## default-s1 / auth-bug

> Our auth middleware rejects valid tokens at the exact expiry boundary (it uses currentTime > expiry). Find and fix the root cause.

### vanilla: 697 answer tokens, 1027 billed, 17 lines

~~~
Without the code I can't point to the exact line, but the description doesn't add up as stated.

**The mismatch:** `currentTime > expiry` as a rejection condition does *not* reject at the exact boundary. At `currentTime == expiry` it is false, so the token passes. If you're seeing rejections at equality, the failing check is probably not that expression, or it's being used differently than you think.

**Likely root causes:**

1. **Inverted or positive-form check.** The validity check is written as `isValid = currentTime < expiry`. That rejects at equality, because the token is invalid when `currentTime >= expiry`. Fix this only if you want the boundary instant to be valid:
   ```js
   const isValid = currentTime <= expiry;   // boundary inclusive
   ```
2. **Unit mismatch.** `exp` in JWTs is in seconds, while `Date.now()` is in milliseconds. This usually causes wholesale rejection, but if the values are rounded or truncated in one place (e.g. `Math.floor(Date.now()/1000)` in one path and not another), it can look like a boundary issue.
3. **Two checks in the stack.** A library such as `jsonwebtoken` or `jose` validates `exp` first, then your middleware checks again with different semantics. The library treats `now >= exp` as expired, per RFC 7519, so the token is rejected at the boundary before your code runs.
4. **Clock skew or a different time source** between the issuer and the verifier. Add a small tolerance, such as 30–60 s, instead of relying on exact-boundary behavior.

**Which behavior is correct?** RFC 7519 says the token must not be accepted on or after `exp`, so strictly the boundary instant is *expired*. If your tests expect it to be valid, the spec-compliant fix is usually to adjust the test or add clock tolerance, not to loosen the comparison. If you do want it inclusive, make the comparison `<=` everywhere and make sure only one layer enforces expiry.

If you paste the middleware (and any JWT library config), I can point to the exact line and give a precise patch.
~~~

### caveman: 524 answer tokens, 810 billed, 14 lines

~~~
Cannot find file: no codebase or tools here. But the description is contradictory, so check that first.

**`currentTime > expiry` does not reject at exact boundary.** At `currentTime == expiry` it is `false`, so token is accepted. It rejects only strictly after expiry. If tokens are really rejected at the boundary, the check running is probably not this one.

Likely real causes:
- **Other check runs**: `>=` somewhere else (another middleware, the JWT library, a gateway). Grep for `expiry`, `exp`, `expires`, `isExpired`.
- **Unit mismatch**: `exp` in seconds (JWT standard) vs `Date.now()` in ms. Then the token looks expired almost always, not only at the boundary.
- **Clock skew**: the issuer and verifier clocks differ, so "valid" tokens look expired near the edge. Fix with a small leeway (e.g. 30-60s), not by changing the operator.
- **Rounding**: `Math.floor`/`Math.ceil` on one side only shifts the boundary by up to 1s.
- **Type issue**: `exp` is a string or Date, so the comparison coerces oddly.

**Spec note:** RFC 7519 says the current time MUST be *before* `exp`. At `now == exp` the token is expired, so `>=` rejection is correct. `>` is the lenient variant. If you want inclusive expiry, `>` is right, but you need to see why something else rejects it.

Send the middleware code, or the grep results for those names, and I'll pin down the root cause and write the fix.

### ponytail: 515 answer tokens, 783 billed, 10 lines

~~~
The condition as quoted can't be what rejects the token. `currentTime > expiry` is false when `currentTime == expiry`, so at the exact boundary that check **accepts** the token. It only starts rejecting one tick later. (Strictly, RFC 7519 says the current time must be *before* `exp`, so the spec-correct check is `currentTime >= expiry`, which is stricter than yours.)

With no code or tool access here, I can't pinpoint the real cause, but these are the likely ones:

1. **Unit mismatch.** JWT `exp` is in seconds, while `Date.now()` and Java's `currentTimeMillis` are in milliseconds. This rejects every token, not just boundary ones. Fix: `Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000)`.
2. **Another check is doing the rejecting.** Look for a second comparison (`>=`, a `nbf`/`iat` check, or a cache TTL) in the library or a wrapper.
3. **Clock skew between issuer and verifier.** Fix once in the shared verify function: allow a small leeway (e.g. 30–60s). Most JWT libraries have an option for this, such as `clockTolerance` in jsonwebtoken or `leeway` in PyJWT.
4. **Mixed time sources.** Compare the issuer's and verifier's clocks, and check for a mix of local time and UTC.

If you paste the middleware, or run `grep -rn "expiry\|exp\b" .` in the repo, I can find the actual root cause and check every caller of the verify function.

### chisle: 530 answer tokens, 963 billed, 10 lines

~~~
Can't see the code, but `currentTime > expiry` as a reject condition does not reject at equality (`t == expiry` → false → accepted). So the boundary rejection is coming from somewhere else. Likely causes:

1. **Inverted or complementary check elsewhere:** an accept path like `if (currentTime < expiry) allow` (strict) rejects at equality. Fix: make both sides agree, using one helper, e.g. `isExpired = now >= exp`, and never write the accept side as its own comparison.
2. **Unit mismatch:** JWT `exp` is seconds, `Date.now()` is ms. This rejects everything, not just the boundary, but it can look boundary-only in tests with mocked clocks. Fix: `Math.floor(Date.now()/1000)`.
3. **Truncation or rounding:** the token expiry is truncated to seconds while `currentTime` keeps ms, so `now(ms) > exp(s*1000)` rejects within the final second. Fix: compare in the same unit and precision.
4. **Clock skew:** the issuer's clock is ahead of or behind the validator's. Fix: add a small leeway (30-60s), as most JWT libs do (`clockTolerance`).

Check also that the premise holds. RFC 7519 says `exp` is "on or after which the token MUST NOT be accepted", so a token at exactly `exp` is expired and the correct check is `now >= exp`. If the tests expect it valid at equality, the test is wrong, not the middleware.

Paste the middleware and the failing test and I'll pinpoint which one it is.

## default-s1 / cache

> Add a cache layer for our user profile API responses. Show the code.

### vanilla: 1498 answer tokens, 1621 billed, 97 lines

~~~
I don't know your stack, so this is a cache-aside layer in TypeScript (Node, Redis via `ioredis`). The pattern carries over to other languages. It has a TTL with jitter, in-process request coalescing to prevent stampedes, negative caching for 404s, and explicit invalidation on writes.

```ts
// profileCache.ts
import Redis from "ioredis";

export interface UserProfile {
  id: string;
  name: string;
  email: string;
  // ...
}

const redis = new Redis(process.env.REDIS_URL ?? "redis://localhost:6379");

const TTL_SECONDS = 300;        // positive entries
const NEGATIVE_TTL_SECONDS = 30; // "user not found" entries
const NOT_FOUND = "__NOT_FOUND__";

const key = (id: string) => `profile:v1:${id}`; // bump v1 to invalidate everything

// Coalesce concurrent misses for the same user into one DB call
const inflight = new Map<string, Promise<UserProfile | null>>();

// Spread expirations so entries don't all expire at once
const jitter = (ttl: number) => Math.floor(ttl * (0.9 + Math.random() * 0.2));

export async function getProfile(
  id: string,
  loader: (id: string) => Promise<UserProfile | null>
): Promise<UserProfile | null> {
  try {
    const cached = await redis.get(key(id));
    if (cached !== null) {
      return cached === NOT_FOUND ? null : (JSON.parse(cached) as UserProfile);
    }
  } catch (err) {
    console.warn("profile cache read failed, falling back to source", err);
  }

  const existing = inflight.get(id);
  if (existing) return existing;

  const p = (async () => {
    const profile = await loader(id);
    try {
      if (profile) {
        await redis.set(key(id), JSON.stringify(profile), "EX", jitter(TTL_SECONDS));
      } else {
        await redis.set(key(id), NOT_FOUND, "EX", NEGATIVE_TTL_SECONDS);
      }
    } catch (err) {
      console.warn("profile cache write failed", err);
    }
    return profile;
  })().finally(() => inflight.delete(id));

  inflight.set(id, p);
  return p;
}

export async function invalidateProfile(id: string): Promise<void> {
  try {
    await redis.del(key(id));
  } catch (err) {
    console.warn("profile cache invalidation failed", err);
  }
}
```

Usage in an Express handler:

```ts
import { getProfile, invalidateProfile } from "./profileCache";

app.get("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  const profile = await getProfile(req.params.id, db.users.findProfileById);
  if (!profile) return res.status(404).json({ error: "not found" });
  res.set("Cache-Control", "private, max-age=0, must-revalidate");
  res.json(profile);
});

app.patch("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  const updated = await db.users.updateProfile(req.params.id, req.body);
  await invalidateProfile(req.params.id); // invalidate after the DB write commits
  res.json(updated);
});
```

**Design notes**
- **Fail-open:** if Redis is down, requests go straight to the database instead of erroring.
- **Invalidate after commit:** deleting the key after the write is the standard approach. A small race remains where a concurrent reader can re-cache stale data. The TTL bounds that, and if it matters you can add a version field and compare on write.
- **Per-user data:** don't cache at a shared CDN or proxy. The `private` header above keeps responses out of shared caches. If profiles differ by viewer (for example, privacy settings), include the viewer's ID or role in the key.
- **Key versioning:** `profile:v1:` lets you change the profile shape and flush by bumping the version, with no need to scan keys.
- **Coalescing scope:** the `inflight` map only dedupes within one process. For cross-instance protection, use a short Redis lock (`SET NX PX`) around the loader.

If you tell me your language, framework, and datastore (and whether you already run Redis), I can adapt this to fit. For example, I could use an in-memory LRU, Python/Go, or `Cache-Control` and ETag-based HTTP caching instead.
~~~

### caveman: 1330 answer tokens, 1457 billed, 95 lines

~~~
Stack unknown, so assume Node/TypeScript + Redis (`ioredis`). Cache-aside pattern: read cache → miss → DB → write cache with TTL. Invalidate on update.

```ts
// profileCache.ts
import Redis from "ioredis";

const redis = new Redis(process.env.REDIS_URL ?? "redis://localhost:6379");

const TTL_SECONDS = 300;       // base TTL
const JITTER_SECONDS = 30;     // avoid mass expiry at same instant
const NEG_TTL_SECONDS = 30;    // cache "not found" briefly
const NOT_FOUND = "__null__";

const key = (id: string) => `profile:v1:${id}`; // bump v1 to flush on schema change

// In-process dedupe: concurrent misses for same id share one DB call
const inflight = new Map<string, Promise<Profile | null>>();

export interface Profile {
  id: string;
  name: string;
  email: string;
  // ...
}

export async function getProfile(
  id: string,
  loader: (id: string) => Promise<Profile | null>
): Promise<Profile | null> {
  try {
    const hit = await redis.get(key(id));
    if (hit !== null) return hit === NOT_FOUND ? null : (JSON.parse(hit) as Profile);
  } catch (err) {
    console.warn("cache read failed, falling back to DB", err); // fail open
  }

  const pending = inflight.get(id);
  if (pending) return pending;

  const p = (async () => {
    const profile = await loader(id);
    try {
      if (profile) {
        const ttl = TTL_SECONDS + Math.floor(Math.random() * JITTER_SECONDS);
        await redis.set(key(id), JSON.stringify(profile), "EX", ttl);
      } else {
        await redis.set(key(id), NOT_FOUND, "EX", NEG_TTL_SECONDS);
      }
    } catch (err) {
      console.warn("cache write failed", err);
    }
    return profile;
  })().finally(() => inflight.delete(id));

  inflight.set(id, p);
  return p;
}

export async function invalidateProfile(id: string): Promise<void> {
  try {
    await redis.del(key(id));
  } catch (err) {
    console.warn("cache invalidate failed", err);
  }
}
```

Usage (Express):

```ts
app.get("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  const profile = await getProfile(req.params.id, db.users.findProfileById);
  if (!profile) return res.status(404).json({ error: "not found" });
  res.set("Cache-Control", "private, max-age=0"); // server cache only; don't let CDN share per-user data
  res.json(profile);
});

app.put("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  const updated = await db.users.updateProfile(req.params.id, req.body);
  await invalidateProfile(req.params.id); // after DB commit
  res.json(updated);
});
```

Design notes:
- **Fail open**: Redis down → hit DB, no 500.
- **Jitter TTL**: stops synchronized expiry stampede.
- **Inflight map**: dedupes concurrent misses per process. Multi-instance needs Redis lock (`SET key val NX PX`) if DB load still hurts.
- **Negative caching**: short TTL stops repeated 404 lookups hammering DB.
- **Invalidate after commit**: delete, not set, avoids race writing stale data. Small race window remains; TTL bounds staleness.
- **Per-user data**: no shared CDN caching. Keep `private`.
- **Key version** `v1`: bump on shape change.
- **Sensitive fields**: strip from cached payload if Redis not trusted/encrypted.

Tell me stack (Python/Go/Java, other cache, framework) and I tailor it.

### ponytail: 722 answer tokens, 898 billed, 54 lines

~~~
Assuming Python, since I can't see your stack. `lru_cache` has no TTL or per-key invalidation, and profiles change, so this is a small TTL dict instead:

```python
import time
from functools import wraps

def ttl_cache(ttl=60, maxsize=10_000):
    store = {}  # key -> (expires_at, value)

    def deco(fn):
        @wraps(fn)
        def wrapper(user_id):
            now = time.monotonic()
            hit = store.get(user_id)
            if hit and hit[0] > now:
                return hit[1]
            val = fn(user_id)
            if len(store) >= maxsize:
                store.pop(next(iter(store)))  # ponytail: evicts oldest-inserted, not LRU
            store[user_id] = (now + ttl, val)
            return val
        wrapper.invalidate = lambda user_id: store.pop(user_id, None)
        return wrapper
    return deco


@ttl_cache(ttl=60)
def get_profile(user_id):
    return fetch_profile_from_db(user_id)  # your existing call


def update_profile(user_id, data):
    save_profile(user_id, data)            # your existing call
    get_profile.invalidate(user_id)        # call this on every write path


if __name__ == "__main__":
    calls = []

    @ttl_cache(ttl=0.05)
    def f(uid):
        calls.append(uid)
        return uid

    f(1); f(1)
    assert len(calls) == 1          # second call served from cache
    f.invalidate(1); f(1)
    assert len(calls) == 2          # invalidation forces refetch
    time.sleep(0.06); f(1)
    assert len(calls) == 3          # TTL expiry forces refetch
    print("ok")
```

→ Skipped: Redis, a cache class, thread locks, and stampede protection. It's per-process, so with multiple workers or servers, stale reads last up to `ttl` after a write on another instance. Move to Redis (`SETEX` plus `DEL` on update) when you run more than one instance. Tell me your language or framework if it isn't Python and I'll port it.

### chisle: 925 answer tokens, 1058 billed, 61 lines

~~~
Assumed stack: Node/TypeScript + Express, single process. In-memory TTL cache with in-flight dedup and invalidate-on-write.

```ts
// cache.ts
type Entry<T> = { value: T; expires: number };

export class TTLCache<T> {
  private store = new Map<string, Entry<T>>();
  private inflight = new Map<string, Promise<T>>();
  constructor(private ttlMs = 60_000, private max = 10_000) {}

  async get(key: string, load: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
    const hit = this.store.get(key);
    if (hit && hit.expires > Date.now()) return hit.value;

    // dedupe concurrent misses (stampede guard)
    let p = this.inflight.get(key);
    if (!p) {
      p = load()
        .then(v => { this.set(key, v); return v; })
        .finally(() => this.inflight.delete(key));
      this.inflight.set(key, p);
    }
    return p;
  }

  set(key: string, value: T) {
    if (this.store.size >= this.max) {
      this.store.delete(this.store.keys().next().value!); // evict oldest insert
    }
    this.store.set(key, { value, expires: Date.now() + this.ttlMs });
  }

  invalidate(key: string) { this.store.delete(key); this.inflight.delete(key); }
}
```

```ts
// routes/profile.ts
import { TTLCache } from "../cache";

const profiles = new TTLCache<Profile>(60_000);

router.get("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  const profile = await profiles.get(req.params.id, () => db.getProfile(req.params.id));
  res.set("Cache-Control", "private, max-age=30").json(profile);
});

router.put("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  const updated = await db.updateProfile(req.params.id, req.body);
  profiles.invalidate(req.params.id); // write → drop stale entry
  res.json(updated);
});
```

Gotchas:
- Failed loads aren't cached (`.then` only runs on success) → errors retry next call.
- Key by user id only if response is identical for all viewers. If it varies by requester (privacy fields, permissions), key on `${id}:${viewerId}` or the cache leaks data.
- In-memory is per-process: multiple instances → stale reads after writes on other nodes. Then swap to Redis (`GET`/`SET key EX 60`, `DEL` on update) behind the same `get/invalidate` interface.

skipped: LRU (FIFO eviction is enough until the cap is hit in practice), metrics, Redis variant. Add when multi-instance or hit-rate matters.
~~~

## default-s1 / debounce

> Add debounce to a search input that currently fires an API call on every keystroke. Show the code.

### vanilla: 1350 answer tokens, 1350 billed, 114 lines

~~~
Here's a React version: a reusable `useDebounce` hook, plus cancellation of stale requests so slow, out-of-order responses can't overwrite newer results.

```tsx
import { useState, useEffect } from "react";

// Returns `value` only after it has stopped changing for `delay` ms.
function useDebounce<T>(value: T, delay = 300): T {
  const [debounced, setDebounced] = useState(value);

  useEffect(() => {
    const id = setTimeout(() => setDebounced(value), delay);
    return () => clearTimeout(id); // reset timer on each keystroke / unmount
  }, [value, delay]);

  return debounced;
}

export function SearchBox() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState("");
  const [results, setResults] = useState<string[]>([]);
  const [loading, setLoading] = useState(false);
  const debouncedQuery = useDebounce(query, 300);

  useEffect(() => {
    const q = debouncedQuery.trim();
    if (!q) {
      setResults([]);
      return;
    }

    const controller = new AbortController();
    setLoading(true);

    fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(q)}`, { signal: controller.signal })
      .then((r) => {
        if (!r.ok) throw new Error(`HTTP ${r.status}`);
        return r.json();
      })
      .then((data) => {
        setResults(data);
        setLoading(false);
      })
      .catch((err) => {
        if (err.name !== "AbortError") {
          console.error(err);
          setLoading(false);
        }
      });

    // Abort the in-flight request if the debounced query changes again
    return () => controller.abort();
  }, [debouncedQuery]);

  return (
    <div>
      <input
        value={query}
        onChange={(e) => setQuery(e.target.value)}
        placeholder="Search..."
      />
      {loading && <p>Loading…</p>}
      <ul>
        {results.map((r) => (
          <li key={r}>{r}</li>
        ))}
      </ul>
    </div>
  );
}
```

**How it works**
- The input updates `query` immediately, so typing stays responsive.
- `useDebounce` only updates `debouncedQuery` after 300 ms with no new keystrokes. Each keystroke clears the previous timer.
- The fetch effect depends on `debouncedQuery`, so the API is called once per pause rather than once per keystroke.
- The effect cleanup calls `controller.abort()`, which cancels a stale in-flight request.

**Vanilla JS version** (no framework):

```js
function debounce(fn, delay = 300) {
  let timer;
  return (...args) => {
    clearTimeout(timer);
    timer = setTimeout(() => fn(...args), delay);
  };
}

let controller;
async function search(q) {
  controller?.abort();
  controller = new AbortController();
  try {
    const res = await fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(q)}`, {
      signal: controller.signal,
    });
    render(await res.json());
  } catch (e) {
    if (e.name !== "AbortError") console.error(e);
  }
}

document
  .querySelector("#search")
  .addEventListener("input", debounce((e) => search(e.target.value.trim()), 300));
```

**Tips**
- 250–400 ms is a typical delay. Shorter feels snappier, longer saves more requests.
- Skip empty or very short queries (e.g. under 2 characters) if your API is expensive.
- If you want the first keystroke to fire immediately, use a leading-edge debounce, but trailing-edge is usually what you want for search.
- If you already use lodash, `debounce` from `lodash/debounce` works too, but wrap it in `useMemo`/`useRef` in React so it isn't recreated on every render.

If you're on a different framework (Vue, Angular, Svelte), tell me and I'll adapt it.
~~~

### caveman: 965 answer tokens, 1024 billed, 82 lines

~~~
Wait delay after last keystroke, then fire. Cancel stale requests too.

**Vanilla JS**

```js
function debounce(fn, delay = 300) {
  let timer;
  const debounced = (...args) => {
    clearTimeout(timer);
    timer = setTimeout(() => fn(...args), delay);
  };
  debounced.cancel = () => clearTimeout(timer);
  return debounced;
}

let controller;
async function search(query) {
  controller?.abort();                 // kill in-flight request
  controller = new AbortController();
  try {
    const res = await fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`, {
      signal: controller.signal,
    });
    render(await res.json());
  } catch (e) {
    if (e.name !== "AbortError") throw e;
  }
}

const onInput = debounce((e) => {
  const q = e.target.value.trim();
  if (q) search(q);
}, 300);

document.querySelector("#search").addEventListener("input", onInput);
```

**React hook version**

```jsx
import { useState, useEffect } from "react";

function useDebouncedValue(value, delay = 300) {
  const [debounced, setDebounced] = useState(value);
  useEffect(() => {
    const id = setTimeout(() => setDebounced(value), delay);
    return () => clearTimeout(id);
  }, [value, delay]);
  return debounced;
}

function SearchBox() {
  const [input, setInput] = useState("");
  const [results, setResults] = useState([]);
  const query = useDebouncedValue(input, 300);

  useEffect(() => {
    if (!query.trim()) { setResults([]); return; }
    const controller = new AbortController();
    fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`, { signal: controller.signal })
      .then((r) => r.json())
      .then(setResults)
      .catch((e) => { if (e.name !== "AbortError") console.error(e); });
    return () => controller.abort();   // cleanup cancels stale call
  }, [query]);

  return (
    <>
      <input value={input} onChange={(e) => setInput(e.target.value)} />
      <ul>{results.map((r) => <li key={r.id}>{r.name}</li>)}</ul>
    </>
  );
}
```

Notes:
- Input state update instant (typing stay snappy). Only API call delayed.
- `AbortController` stop race: slow old response overwriting newer one.
- Delay 250–400ms typical. Lower = more calls, higher = feels laggy.
- Don't debounce inline in React render (`debounce(fn)` inside component recreates each render, timer lost). Use hook above, or `useMemo`/`useRef`.

Tell me framework (Vue, Angular, Svelte) if not these. Give matching version.

```js
function debounce(fn, ms = 300) {
  let t;
  return (...args) => {
    clearTimeout(t);
    t = setTimeout(() => fn(...args), ms);
  };
}

const input = document.querySelector('#search');
let ctrl;

const search = debounce(async (q) => {
  ctrl?.abort();                       // drop the stale in-flight request
  if (!q.trim()) return;
  ctrl = new AbortController();
  try {
    const res = await fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(q)}`, { signal: ctrl.signal });
    render(await res.json());
  } catch (e) {
    if (e.name !== 'AbortError') throw e;
  }
}, 300);

input.addEventListener('input', (e) => search(e.target.value));
```

If it's React, debounce the value instead of the handler:

```jsx
function useDebounced(value, ms = 300) {
  const [v, setV] = useState(value);
  useEffect(() => {
    const t = setTimeout(() => setV(value), ms);
    return () => clearTimeout(t);
  }, [value, ms]);
  return v;
}

// in the component
const debounced = useDebounced(query);
useEffect(() => {
  if (!debounced.trim()) return;
  const ctrl = new AbortController();
  fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(debounced)}`, { signal: ctrl.signal })
    .then(r => r.json()).then(setResults).catch(e => e.name !== 'AbortError' && console.error(e));
  return () => ctrl.abort();
}, [debounced]);
```

Skipped: leading-edge/cancel/flush options and lodash. Add lodash's `debounce` only if you need those. The `AbortController` is kept on purpose, since debounce alone doesn't stop an older response from overwriting a newer one.

### chisle: 513 answer tokens, 543 billed, 36 lines

~~~
Assuming React + `fetch`:

```tsx
import { useEffect, useState } from "react";

function useDebounce<T>(value: T, delay = 300): T {
  const [debounced, setDebounced] = useState(value);
  useEffect(() => {
    const t = setTimeout(() => setDebounced(value), delay);
    return () => clearTimeout(t);
  }, [value, delay]);
  return debounced;
}

export function Search() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState("");
  const [results, setResults] = useState<unknown[]>([]);
  const debounced = useDebounce(query.trim());

  useEffect(() => {
    if (!debounced) return setResults([]);
    const ctrl = new AbortController();
    fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(debounced)}`, { signal: ctrl.signal })
      .then((r) => r.json())
      .then(setResults)
      .catch((e) => e.name !== "AbortError" && console.error(e));
    return () => ctrl.abort();
  }, [debounced]);

  return <input value={query} onChange={(e) => setQuery(e.target.value)} />;
}
```

Input stays bound to `query` (instant typing); only `debounced` triggers the fetch. `AbortController` cancels in-flight requests → no out-of-order results overwriting newer ones.

skipped: loading/error UI, add when needed. Vanilla JS: wrap the handler in `setTimeout`/`clearTimeout` the same way.
~~~

## default-s1 / pooling

> Explain how database connection pooling works and why it helps.

### vanilla: 1498 answer tokens, 1498 billed, 57 lines

~~~
# Database connection pooling

## The problem it solves

Opening a database connection is expensive. Each new connection typically involves:

1. A TCP handshake (plus a TLS handshake if encrypted)
2. Authentication and authorization
3. Server-side setup: in PostgreSQL, a new backend process; in MySQL, a new thread; plus session state and memory allocation

This can cost anywhere from a few milliseconds to tens of milliseconds. If every request opens a connection, runs a 2 ms query, and closes it, most of the time goes to setup and teardown. The database also has a hard limit on concurrent connections, so a traffic spike can exhaust it.

## How a pool works

A connection pool is a cache of open, reusable connections managed by the application (or a proxy).

1. **Initialization**: The pool opens a minimum number of connections at startup (or lazily).
2. **Borrow**: When code needs the database, it asks the pool for a connection. If an idle one exists, it is handed over immediately, with no handshake.
3. **Use**: The code runs its queries or transaction.
4. **Return**: Instead of closing, the code releases the connection back to the pool. The pool resets its state (rolls back any open transaction, clears session variables) and marks it idle.
5. **Exhaustion**: If all connections are in use and the pool is below its maximum, it opens a new one. If it is at the maximum, the caller waits in a queue until one is returned or a timeout fires.

## Common configuration knobs

| Setting | Purpose |
|---|---|
| **Min / max size** | Bounds on open connections. Max protects the database. |
| **Acquire (checkout) timeout** | How long a caller waits for a connection before erroring. |
| **Idle timeout** | Closes connections unused for a while, shrinking the pool when load drops. |
| **Max lifetime** | Recycles connections periodically to avoid stale ones and to rebalance after failovers or DNS changes. |
| **Validation / health check** | Tests a connection (e.g. `SELECT 1` or a driver ping) before handing it out, so callers do not get dead connections. |

## Why it helps

- **Lower latency**: Skipping connection setup on most requests cuts per-request overhead.
- **Higher throughput**: Less CPU and network is spent on handshakes, on both the app and the database.
- **Protects the database**: A fixed cap prevents connection storms from overwhelming it. Excess demand queues in the application, which is far cheaper than excess connections on the server.
- **Predictable resource use**: Memory and process/thread counts on the database stay bounded.
- **Backpressure**: Timeouts on acquisition surface overload early instead of causing cascading failures.

## Pitfalls

- **Connection leaks**: Forgetting to return a connection (e.g. missing `close()` or `finally`) slowly drains the pool until everything hangs. Use context managers or try-with-resources.
- **Pool too large**: More connections do not mean more throughput. Past a point (roughly a small multiple of the database's CPU cores), contention makes things slower. A common starting point is a few dozen connections per database, not hundreds.
- **Multiplied pools**: Each app instance has its own pool. 50 instances × 20 connections = 1,000 connections to the database. Account for this when sizing.
- **Long transactions**: A connection held during slow external calls (HTTP requests, user input) is unavailable to others.
- **Session state leakage**: If the pool does not reset state, settings from one borrower can affect the next.
- **Transaction-mode proxies**: With external poolers like PgBouncer in transaction mode, session-level features (prepared statements, advisory locks, `SET`) may not behave as expected.

## Where pooling lives

- **In-process libraries**: HikariCP (Java), SQLAlchemy's pool (Python), `pgxpool` (Go), `pg-pool` (Node), ActiveRecord (Rails).
- **External poolers/proxies**: PgBouncer, pgcat, ProxySQL, RDS Proxy. These help when many app instances or serverless functions would otherwise overwhelm the database, since they multiplex many client connections onto a few server connections.

## Quick mental model

Think of a taxi stand: instead of building a new car for every passenger (opening a connection), a fixed fleet waits at the stand, each ride takes a free car, and the car returns when the ride ends. If every car is out, passengers queue.
~~~

### caveman: 741 answer tokens, 741 billed, 30 lines

~~~
Pool = set of pre-opened DB connections, shared by app. Reuse instead of open/close per request.

**How work:**
1. App start → pool open N connections (min size).
2. Request need DB → borrow idle connection from pool.
3. Run query.
4. Return connection to pool (not close). Next request reuse.
5. All busy → request wait in queue (with timeout) or pool grow up to max size.
6. Pool health-check idle connections, drop dead/stale ones, replace.

**Why help:**
- **Skip setup cost.** New connection = TCP handshake, TLS, auth, session init. Often tens of ms. Pool pay once.
- **Cap load on DB.** DB handle limited connections (Postgres each = process/memory). Max pool size stop app flood DB under spike.
- **Stable latency.** No connect storm during traffic burst.
- **Resource reuse.** Fewer sockets, less memory churn, fewer ephemeral ports in TIME_WAIT.
- **Backpressure.** Excess load queue in app, not crash DB.

**Key settings:**
- `min`/`max` size: max too big → DB overload. Too small → requests queue. Rule of thumb: small (roughly cores × 2 + disks), not huge.
- Acquire timeout: how long wait for free connection before error.
- Idle timeout / max lifetime: recycle connections, dodge firewall/DB drops.
- Validation query / ping: detect dead connection before hand to caller.

**Pitfalls:**
- **Connection leak:** borrow, never return (missing `close`/`release` in error path). Pool exhaust, app hang. Always release in `finally` / context manager.
- **Session state leak:** temp tables, `SET` vars, open transaction carry to next borrower. Good pools reset on return.
- **Long transactions** hold connection, starve others.
- **Many app instances × max size** can exceed DB total limit. Use external pooler (PgBouncer, ProxySQL) if so.

**Common impl:** HikariCP (Java), pgbouncer (Postgres proxy), SQLAlchemy pool (Python), `pg.Pool` (Node), `database/sql` (Go, built in).
~~~

### ponytail: 981 answer tokens, 1043 billed, 31 lines

~~~
# Database connection pooling

## The problem
Opening a DB connection is expensive. Each one involves a TCP handshake, usually TLS, authentication, and server-side session setup (a process or thread per connection in Postgres, for example). That can cost milliseconds to tens of milliseconds, often more than the query itself. Databases also cap concurrent connections, and each one uses server memory.

## How a pool works
1. **Startup:** the pool opens a small number of connections (e.g. `min_size`) and keeps them alive.
2. **Borrow:** when code needs the DB, it asks the pool for a connection. If one is idle, it is handed over immediately. If none is idle and the pool is below `max_size`, a new one is opened. If the pool is at max, the caller waits in a queue, up to a timeout.
3. **Use:** the caller runs its queries or transaction.
4. **Return:** the caller releases the connection. The pool does not close it. It resets session state (rolls back any open transaction, clears temp settings) and marks it idle for the next caller.
5. **Maintenance:** the pool health-checks connections (a ping on checkout or in the background), discards broken ones, and retires connections that exceed a max lifetime or idle time, so stale or firewall-killed sockets don't reach callers.

```python
# typical usage
with pool.connection() as conn:   # borrow
    conn.execute("SELECT ...")    # use
                                  # auto-return on exit
```

## Why it helps
- **Latency:** you pay the connection setup cost once, not per request.
- **Bounded load on the DB:** `max_size` caps concurrency, so a traffic spike queues in the app instead of overwhelming the database with thousands of connections.
- **Resource efficiency:** many short-lived requests share a few long-lived connections.
- **Resilience:** health checks and lifetimes handle dead connections and let you rotate credentials or fail over cleanly.

## Common gotchas
- **Leaks:** forgetting to return a connection (no `with`/`finally`) slowly drains the pool until everything blocks.
- **Sizing:** bigger isn't better. A common rule of thumb is a pool size near `cores × 2 + disk spindles` per DB server, and the sum across all app instances must stay under the DB's `max_connections`. Too many connections cause contention on the server.
- **Session state:** anything set per-connection (`SET search_path`, temp tables, advisory locks) can leak between borrowers if not reset.
- **Long transactions:** holding a connection while doing slow non-DB work (HTTP calls) starves other callers.
- **Many app instances:** each has its own pool, so total connections multiply. An external pooler (PgBouncer, RDS Proxy, ProxySQL) sits between apps and DB to multiplex many client connections onto few server connections. Note that in transaction-pooling mode, session-level features like prepared statements or `LISTEN` may not work.
~~~

### chisle: 694 answer tokens, 711 billed, 19 lines

~~~
Pool = set of pre-opened, reusable DB connections kept by the app (or a proxy like PgBouncer). Instead of connect → query → close per request, code borrows a connection, runs queries, returns it.

**Mechanics**
- On startup, pool opens `min` connections; grows lazily up to `max`.
- `acquire()`: idle connection available → hand it out. None idle and below `max` → open a new one. At `max` → caller blocks in a queue until one is released or a timeout fires.
- `release()`: connection goes back to idle, not closed. Pool typically resets session state (rollback open txn, clear temp settings).
- Housekeeping: health checks/validation query on borrow, idle timeout, max lifetime (to dodge server/firewall-side kills), eviction of broken connections.

**Why it helps**
- Connect cost is large: TCP handshake, TLS, auth, server-side process/session setup (Postgres forks a process per connection). Pooling pays it once, not per request → lower latency.
- Caps concurrency: the DB has a hard connection limit and degrades well before it (memory per connection, context switching, lock contention). Pool `max` acts as backpressure: excess requests queue in the app instead of overloading the DB.
- Stability: avoids connection storms and ephemeral port/FD exhaustion under bursty load.

**Gotchas**
- Pool size isn't "bigger is better". Rule of thumb: small, roughly `cores × 2 + disk spindles` per DB, shared across all app instances. Total = instances × `max` must stay under the server limit.
- Leaks: forgetting to release (e.g., exception path without `finally`/context manager) → pool exhausted → requests hang.
- Long transactions or slow queries hold connections and starve others.
- Session state (`SET`, temp tables, advisory locks, prepared statements) can leak across borrowers, and breaks under transaction-mode external poolers.
- Serverless/many-instance setups: per-instance pools multiply fast → use an external pooler (PgBouncer, RDS Proxy).
~~~

## default-s1 / regex-concept

> Explain what a regular expression backreference is, with one short example.

### vanilla: 339 answer tokens, 339 billed, 17 lines

~~~
A **backreference** is a regex feature that lets you match the same text that an earlier capturing group already matched. You refer to the group by number (`\1`, `\2`, ...) or, in many flavors, by name (`\k<name>`).

Note that it matches the *actual text* captured, not the group's pattern. So `(a|b)\1` matches `aa` or `bb`, but not `ab`.

**Example: finding repeated words**

```
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

- `(\w+)` captures a word into group 1.
- `\s+` matches whitespace.
- `\1` requires the exact same word to appear again.

Against the string `"this is is a test"`, it matches `is is`. It would not match `is a`, because the second word differs from what group 1 captured.

Backreferences are also commonly used in replacement strings (e.g., `$1` or `\1`) to reuse captured text, such as swapping `John Smith` to `Smith, John` with `(\w+) (\w+)` → `$2, $1`.
~~~

```

```

- `(\w+)` capture word into group 1
- `\s+` whitespace
- `\1` must match exact same text as group 1

Match "the the" in "this is the the end". No match "the cat".

Note: backreference match captured *text*, not the *pattern*. `(\d)\1` match "11", not "12".

### ponytail: 269 answer tokens, 269 billed, 15 lines

~~~
A **backreference** lets a regex match the same text that an earlier capture group already matched. You write `\1` for group 1, `\2` for group 2, and so on.

**Example:** find repeated words.

```
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

- `(\w+)` captures a word into group 1.
- `\s+` matches the whitespace after it.
- `\1` must match exactly the same text that group 1 captured.

Against `"this is is a test"`, it matches `is is`. It does not match `this is`, because `\1` requires the second word to be identical to the first, not merely another word.

Syntax varies slightly by flavor: some tools use `\1` in the replacement string, others use `$1`. Named groups work too, e.g. `(?<w>\w+)\s+\k<w>` in JavaScript.
~~~

### chisle: 259 answer tokens, 259 billed, 11 lines

~~~
Backreference: `\1`, `\2`, … inside a regex matches the same text that capture group 1, 2, … already matched (not the same pattern, the same literal text).

Example: find repeated words.

```
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

On `"this is is a test"` → matches `is is`. Group `(\w+)` captures `is`, `\1` requires `is` again. `"is it"` doesn't match: `it` ≠ `is`.

Gotchas: syntax varies (`\1` in most engines, `$1` in replacement strings, `\g<1>` or `(?P=name)` for named groups in Python). Backreferences make the regex non-regular and can cause catastrophic backtracking, and some engines (RE2, Rust `regex`) don't support them.
~~~

## default-s1 / rest-graphql

> Summarize the main tradeoffs between REST and GraphQL for a new API.

### vanilla: 1016 answer tokens, 1016 billed, 45 lines

~~~
# REST vs GraphQL: Main Tradeoffs

## Data fetching
- **REST:** Fixed endpoints return fixed shapes. This often leads to over-fetching (extra fields) or under-fetching (multiple round trips).
- **GraphQL:** Clients specify exactly the fields they need in one request. This is a big win for varied clients (mobile, web, third parties) and deeply nested data.

## Caching
- **REST:** Works naturally with HTTP caching (CDNs, browser cache, ETags) because resources have stable URLs and GET semantics.
- **GraphQL:** Typically uses a single POST endpoint, so HTTP caching is harder. You rely on client-side normalized caches (Apollo, Relay) or persisted queries with GET.

## Schema and typing
- **REST:** Typing is optional. OpenAPI gives you a contract, but it's a separate artifact that can drift.
- **GraphQL:** A strongly typed schema is built in, enabling introspection, codegen, and good tooling out of the box.

## Performance and security risks
- **REST:** Predictable per-endpoint cost, easy to rate limit by route.
- **GraphQL:** Clients can write expensive or deeply nested queries, and naive resolvers cause N+1 problems. You need query depth/complexity limits, DataLoader-style batching, and cost-aware rate limiting.

## Versioning and evolution
- **REST:** Usually versioned (`/v1`, `/v2`), which can mean maintaining parallel versions.
- **GraphQL:** Favors additive evolution and field deprecation, avoiding explicit versions. Breaking changes still need care.

## Error handling
- **REST:** Uses HTTP status codes, which work well with existing monitoring and tooling.
- **GraphQL:** Typically returns 200 with an `errors` array, and partial success is possible. Monitoring and client handling need more custom work.

## Operational complexity
- **REST:** Simple, widely understood, easy to debug with curl and browser tools. Lower learning curve.
- **GraphQL:** More upfront investment in schema design, resolvers, tooling, and observability. File uploads and streaming are less straightforward.

## Rules of thumb

**Choose REST when:**
- The API is resource-oriented and fairly simple
- You're building a public API where simplicity and cacheability matter
- Your team is small or new to GraphQL
- You need straightforward file transfer, webhooks, or CDN caching

**Choose GraphQL when:**
- Multiple clients have different data needs
- Data is highly relational or nested
- Frontend teams need to iterate without waiting on backend endpoint changes
- You're aggregating several backend services behind one gateway

**Recommendation:** If you have no strong need for flexible client-driven queries, start with REST. It's simpler to build, operate, and cache. Move to GraphQL (or add it as a layer over existing services) when over-fetching, round trips, or client diversity becomes a real pain point.
~~~

- Cons: over-fetch/under-fetch, many round trips for nested data, endpoint sprawl, versioning pain, no built-in schema (need OpenAPI).

**GraphQL**

- Cons: HTTP caching hard (mostly POST to one endpoint), N+1 resolver problem (need DataLoader), query cost/depth attack → need limits, complexity analysis, harder rate limiting, error handling non-standard (200 with errors array), file upload awkward, steeper learning curve, more server infra.

**Pick:**
- REST: simple CRUD, public API, caching matter, small team, few clients.
- GraphQL: many diverse clients, deeply nested/relational data, fast frontend iteration, aggregating multiple backends.

**Recommendation:** default REST + OpenAPI for new API unless clear client-driven data-shape pain. Can add GraphQL layer later as gateway over REST services.

**REST**
- **Pros:** Simple, universally understood; HTTP caching (CDN, ETags) works out of the box; easy to debug with curl; status codes and verbs map to standard semantics; straightforward rate limiting, auth, and monitoring per endpoint; huge tooling ecosystem (OpenAPI).
- **Cons:** Over-fetching and under-fetching (multiple round trips for related data); endpoint sprawl as clients diverge; versioning is manual; fixed response shapes couple the server to client needs.

**GraphQL**
- **Pros:** Clients request exactly the fields they need, one round trip for nested data; strongly typed schema doubles as documentation and enables codegen and introspection; evolves without versioning (add fields, deprecate old ones); good fit for many different clients (web, mobile) with different data needs.
- **Cons:** Caching is harder (usually POST to a single endpoint, so HTTP/CDN caching is lost; needs persisted queries or client-side caches like Apollo/Relay); N+1 query problem on the server (needs DataLoader-style batching); expensive or malicious queries require depth/complexity limits and cost analysis; rate limiting is by query cost, not by URL; file uploads, error handling (200 with an errors array), and monitoring are more awkward; steeper learning curve and more server-side complexity.

**Rule of thumb**
- Choose **REST** for simple or resource-oriented APIs, public APIs, small teams, or when HTTP caching and simplicity matter.
- Choose **GraphQL** when you have multiple client types with varied data needs, deeply relational data, or rapid frontend iteration against a stable backend.

**Default for a new API:** start with REST unless you have a concrete problem GraphQL solves. It's cheaper to own, and you can put a GraphQL layer on top later if over-fetching becomes real.

**REST:** simple, mature, resource-per-URL. HTTP caching (CDN, ETags) works for free, tooling is everywhere, and rate limiting, auth, and monitoring map cleanly to endpoints. Cost: over-fetching and under-fetching → many round trips or bloated payloads, especially for varied clients (web, mobile). Endpoint sprawl and versioning pain grow as client needs diverge.

**GraphQL:** client specifies exact fields, one request spans related resources, and a typed schema gives introspection, codegen, and self-documenting contracts. Evolves by deprecating fields instead of versioning. Cost: HTTP caching mostly lost (single POST endpoint → need persisted queries or client-side normalized caches), N+1 queries need dataloaders, and arbitrary queries need depth/complexity limits and cost-based rate limiting. Error handling is awkward (200 with an `errors` array), file uploads are clunky, and the learning curve is steeper for both server and client teams.

**Verdict:** default to REST for simple CRUD, public APIs, or small teams, where caching and simplicity win. Choose GraphQL when many distinct clients need different data shapes from a rich, interconnected domain, and you can afford the operational overhead (query cost controls, resolver performance). Hybrid is common: REST for public/simple endpoints, GraphQL as a BFF for your own frontends.

## default-s2 / auth-bug

> Our auth middleware rejects valid tokens at the exact expiry boundary (it uses currentTime > expiry). Find and fix the root cause.

### vanilla: 734 answer tokens, 1021 billed, 20 lines

~~~
The described behavior doesn't match the code you quoted. `currentTime > expiry` rejects a token only when the current time is strictly past expiry, so at the exact boundary (`currentTime == expiry`) it **accepts** the token. If your middleware really rejects at the boundary, the comparison isn't the cause, or the code isn't what you think it is.

Without the code, these are the likely root causes, most probable first:

1. **The check is inverted or written differently elsewhere.** Look for `currentTime >= expiry` in a second code path (a wrapper, a library like `jsonwebtoken`/`PyJWT`, a gateway or sidecar) that runs before your middleware.
2. **Unit mismatch.** JWT `exp` is in seconds, while `Date.now()` and Java's `System.currentTimeMillis()` are in milliseconds. A mix-up gives always-expired or never-expired tokens, not just boundary errors.
3. **Rounding or truncation.** If `currentTime` is a float and `expiry` is an integer, or one side is floored and the other isn't, the two can compare differently than you expect near the boundary.
4. **Clock skew between issuer and verifier.** Tokens appear to expire early if the verifier's clock is ahead. The usual fix is a small leeway (for example 30–60 s), which most JWT libraries support (`clockTolerance`, `leeway`).

**On the correct semantics:** RFC 7519 says the current time must be *before* `exp`. So a token is technically invalid at `currentTime == expiry`, and the strict rejection rule is `currentTime >= expiry`. If you want the boundary instant to be valid, `currentTime > expiry` is the right check, and it is what you already have.

**Suggested fix:**
```js
// seconds on both sides, explicit leeway, one place for the decision
const nowSec = Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000);
const LEEWAY_SEC = 30;
const isExpired = nowSec > exp + LEEWAY_SEC;   // boundary-inclusive, skew-tolerant
```

If you paste the middleware, or point me at the file in the working directory, I can find which of these is actually happening and fix it.
~~~

Can't see code, so no definitive fix. But the description has a snag.

**`currentTime > expiry` as a reject condition does not reject at the boundary.** At `currentTime == expiry` it's false, so the token passes. So one of these is true:

1. **Check is written as accept-condition inverted.** Real code is `valid = expiry > currentTime` (or `if (!(currentTime < expiry)) reject`). Equality → reject. Fix:
   ```js
   if (currentTime > expiry) reject();   // reject only when strictly past
   ```
   or `valid = currentTime <= expiry`.
2. **Unit mismatch.** JWT `exp` is seconds, `Date.now()` is ms. Then `currentTime > expiry` rejects nearly every token. Not just the boundary. Fix: `Math.floor(Date.now()/1000)`.
3. **Different check elsewhere.** Second `>=` in a helper, library (e.g. `jsonwebtoken`, `clockTolerance`), or cache layer. Grep for `exp`, `expiry`, `expiresAt`, `>=`.
4. **Not a bug.** RFC 7519 §4.1.4: `exp` means current time MUST be *before* it. Token at exactly `exp` is expired. If so, rejecting at equality is correct. Leave it.

Real root cause likely not the operator. Clock skew between issuer and verifier causes more "valid token rejected near expiry" reports. Add small leeway (30-60s) rather than fiddling with `>` vs `>=`.

I can't see your code, so I can't point to the exact line. But the symptom and the snippet don't line up, and that points to where the bug probably is.

**The mismatch:** `currentTime > expiry` as a *rejection* test is already inclusive. At `currentTime == expiry` it's false, so the token passes. A middleware that rejects at the exact boundary is therefore doing something else:

1. **The condition is inverted or negated.** Something like `if (!(currentTime > expiry)) reject()` or `valid = currentTime > expiry`. Fix: reject only when `currentTime > expiry`.
2. **Another check fires at the boundary.** Typical culprits are a `>=` in a helper, a library call (e.g. `jwt.verify`), or a second check on `iat`/`nbf`. Grep every comparison against `exp`/`expiry`, not just this one.

**Check the premise too:** RFC 7519 says the current time MUST be *before* `exp`. Under the spec, a token is already expired at `now == exp`, so `now >= exp` is the correct rejection rule. `currentTime > expiry` can't reject at the exact boundary: at `currentTime == expiry` it's false, so the token passes. Either the snippet isn't the code that's failing, or the root cause is elsewhere. Likely culprits:

- **Unit mismatch:** `exp` in seconds (JWT standard) vs `Date.now()` in ms. Then `currentTime > expiry` is true almost always, so rejections would be everywhere, not just at the boundary. Fix: `Math.floor(Date.now()/1000)`.
- **Truncation or rounding:** one side floored and the other not. Example: expiry floored to seconds, current time in fractional seconds → current is "later" by the fraction → rejected up to 1s before the real expiry. Fix: normalize both to the same unit and precision.
- **Different check:** a second validation (JWT library, gateway, cache TTL) using `>=`, or clock skew between issuer and verifier. Fix: add a small leeway (`clockTolerance` / `leeway`, 30-60s) instead of hand-rolling.
- **Inverted or wrong field:** comparing against `iat`/`nbf` or the wrong claim.

Spec note: RFC 7519 says the token must be rejected **at or after** `exp`, so the strict boundary is `currentTime >= expiry`. If you want a token valid at exactly `expiry`, that's a deliberate deviation. Use leeway rather than `>`.

## default-s2 / cache

> Add a cache layer for our user profile API responses. Show the code.

### vanilla: 1481 answer tokens, 1636 billed, 101 lines

~~~
I don't know your stack, so this assumes Node/TypeScript with Redis (ioredis). The pattern is cache-aside with TTL jitter, in-process request coalescing, negative caching, and fail-open behavior, and it ports to other stacks.

```ts
// profileCache.ts
import Redis from "ioredis";

const redis = new Redis(process.env.REDIS_URL!, {
  maxRetriesPerRequest: 1,
  enableOfflineQueue: false, // fail fast when Redis is down
});

export interface UserProfile {
  id: string;
  name: string;
  email: string;
  // ...
}

const TTL_SECONDS = 300;
const NEGATIVE_TTL_SECONDS = 30;
const NOT_FOUND = "__null__";
const key = (id: string) => `profile:v1:${id}`; // bump v1 when the shape changes

// Coalesce concurrent misses for the same user within this process
const inflight = new Map<string, Promise<UserProfile | null>>();

const jitter = (ttl: number) => Math.round(ttl * (0.9 + Math.random() * 0.2));

export async function getProfile(
  id: string,
  loadFromDb: (id: string) => Promise<UserProfile | null>
): Promise<UserProfile | null> {
  const k = key(id);

  // 1. Try cache (fail open on Redis errors)
  try {
    const hit = await redis.get(k);
    if (hit !== null) return hit === NOT_FOUND ? null : (JSON.parse(hit) as UserProfile);
  } catch (err) {
    console.warn("profile cache read failed", err);
  }

  // 2. Miss: load once per key, even under concurrency
  const existing = inflight.get(k);
  if (existing) return existing;

  const p = (async () => {
    const profile = await loadFromDb(id);
    try {
      if (profile) {
        await redis.set(k, JSON.stringify(profile), "EX", jitter(TTL_SECONDS));
      } else {
        await redis.set(k, NOT_FOUND, "EX", NEGATIVE_TTL_SECONDS);
      }
    } catch (err) {
      console.warn("profile cache write failed", err);
    }
    return profile;
  })().finally(() => inflight.delete(k));

  inflight.set(k, p);
  return p;
}

// Call after any profile update/delete
export async function invalidateProfile(id: string): Promise<void> {
  try {
    await redis.del(key(id));
  } catch (err) {
    console.warn("profile cache invalidate failed", err);
  }
}
```

Usage in an Express handler:

```ts
app.get("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  const profile = await getProfile(req.params.id, db.users.findProfileById);
  if (!profile) return res.status(404).json({ error: "not found" });
  res.set("Cache-Control", "private, max-age=0"); // don't let shared caches store per-user data
  res.json(profile);
});

app.put("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  const updated = await db.users.updateProfile(req.params.id, req.body);
  await invalidateProfile(req.params.id); // invalidate after the DB commit
  res.json(updated);
});
```

Design notes:
- **Invalidate on write, with a TTL as backstop.** If an invalidation fails, staleness is bounded to 5 minutes.
- **Jitter** keeps many keys from expiring at the same moment.
- **Negative caching** (30s) stops repeated lookups of nonexistent IDs from hitting the DB.
- **Fail open.** A Redis outage degrades to DB reads rather than errors.
- **Key versioning** (`v1`) lets you change the profile shape without serving stale-shaped data.
- **Cache only what's safe.** If the response varies by viewer (e.g. private fields for the owner vs. public view), include the viewer role in the key or cache the base profile and filter after.
- **Multiple instances.** Coalescing is per-process. For heavy hot keys, add a short Redis lock (`SET lock NX PX`) around the loader.

If you tell me your language, framework, and whether you already run Redis or Memcached, I can adapt this (e.g. Python/FastAPI, Go, or an in-memory LRU for a single instance).

No stack given. Assume Node/TypeScript + Redis (`ioredis`). Cache-aside, TTL, single-flight against stampede, invalidate on write.

```ts
// profileCache.ts
import Redis from "ioredis";

const redis = new Redis(process.env.REDIS_URL!);

const TTL_SECONDS = 300;
const key = (id: string) => `profile:v1:${id}`;

// in-process single-flight: concurrent misses for same id share one DB call
const inflight = new Map<string, Promise<Profile>>();

export interface Profile {
  id: string;
  name: string;
  email: string;
  // ...
}

export async function getProfile(
  id: string,
  loader: (id: string) => Promise<Profile>
): Promise<Profile> {
  try {
    const hit = await redis.get(key(id));
    if (hit) return JSON.parse(hit) as Profile;
  } catch (err) {
    // cache down → fall through to DB, don't fail request
    console.warn("cache read failed", err);
  }

  const pending = inflight.get(id);
  if (pending) return pending;

  const p = (async () => {
    try {
      const fresh = await loader(id);
      // jitter TTL ±10% to avoid mass expiry
      const ttl = Math.round(TTL_SECONDS * (0.9 + Math.random() * 0.2));
      redis.set(key(id), JSON.stringify(fresh), "EX", ttl).catch(() => {});
      return fresh;
    } finally {
      inflight.delete(id);
    }
  })();

  inflight.set(id, p);
  return p;
}

export async function invalidateProfile(id: string): Promise<void> {
  await redis.del(key(id));
}
```

Usage in Express:

```ts
app.get("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  const profile = await getProfile(req.params.id, db.users.findProfileById);
  res.set("Cache-Control", "private, max-age=0");
  res.json(profile);
});

app.put("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  const updated = await db.users.updateProfile(req.params.id, req.body);
  await invalidateProfile(req.params.id); // delete after commit
  res.json(updated);
});
```

Notes:
- **Key version** (`v1`): bump on schema change → old entries ignored.
- **Invalidate after DB commit**, not before. Else reader can re-cache stale row.
- **Cache-down safe**: reads fail open to DB.
- **Per-user data**: never cache by URL at CDN/shared proxy unless `Vary`/auth key included. Keep `private`.
- **Multi-instance**: single-flight only dedupes per process. Need cross-node lock (`SET lock NX PX`) if DB load matters.
- **Negative caching**: cache 404 briefly (e.g. 30s) if missing-user lookups hot.

```python
import time

TTL = 60  # seconds; tune to how stale a profile may be
_cache = {}  # user_id -> (expires_at, profile)

def get_profile(user_id):
    hit = _cache.get(user_id)
    if hit and hit[0] > time.monotonic():
        return hit[1]
    profile = fetch_profile(user_id)  # your existing API/DB call

    _cache[user_id] = (time.monotonic() + TTL, profile)
    return profile

def invalidate_profile(user_id):
    _cache.pop(user_id, None)  # call from every profile update/delete path

if __name__ == "__main__":
    calls = []
    def fetch_profile(uid):
        calls.append(uid)
        return {"id": uid}
    get_profile(1); get_profile(1)
    assert calls == [1]            # second call served from cache
    invalidate_profile(1); get_profile(1)
    assert calls == [1, 1]         # refetched after invalidation
    print("ok")
```

### chisle: 848 answer tokens, 956 billed, 57 lines

Assuming Node/TypeScript, in-process cache. No deps.

```ts
// cache.ts
type Entry<T> = { v: T; exp: number };

export class TTLCache<T> {
  private m = new Map<string, Entry<T>>();
  private inflight = new Map<string, Promise<T>>();
  constructor(private ttlMs = 60_000, private max = 10_000) {}

  async get(key: string, load: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
    const hit = this.m.get(key);
    if (hit && hit.exp > Date.now()) {
      this.m.delete(key); this.m.set(key, hit); // LRU bump
      return hit.v;
    }
    const pending = this.inflight.get(key); // dedupe concurrent misses
    if (pending) return pending;

    const p = load()
      .then((v) => {
        this.m.set(key, { v, exp: Date.now() + this.ttlMs });
        if (this.m.size > this.max) this.m.delete(this.m.keys().next().value!);
        return v;
      })
      .finally(() => this.inflight.delete(key));
    this.inflight.set(key, p);
    return p;
  }

  invalidate(key: string) { this.m.delete(key); }
}
```

```ts
// profile route
const profiles = new TTLCache<Profile>(60_000);

app.get("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  const profile = await profiles.get(req.params.id, () => db.getProfile(req.params.id));
  res.set("Cache-Control", "private, max-age=30").json(profile);
});

app.put("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  const updated = await db.updateProfile(req.params.id, req.body);
  profiles.invalidate(req.params.id); // write → drop stale entry
  res.json(updated);
});
```

Gotchas:
- In-memory is per-process → multiple instances serve stale data up to TTL after a write elsewhere. Multi-instance: swap the `Map` for Redis (`GET`/`SETEX`/`DEL`), same interface.
- Failed loads aren't cached (`.then` only on success).
- Key is user ID only; if the response varies by viewer (permissions, fields), include viewer in the key. Also `private` in Cache-Control is deliberate: profiles are per-user, so keep shared CDNs/proxies from storing them.

## default-s2 / debounce

> Add debounce to a search input that currently fires an API call on every keystroke. Show the code.

### vanilla: 1135 answer tokens, 1135 billed, 103 lines

~~~
Here's a React version with a reusable hook, plus a vanilla JS version. Both include cancellation of stale requests, which matters because debouncing alone doesn't prevent out-of-order responses.

## React

```jsx
import { useState, useEffect } from "react";

function useDebouncedValue(value, delay = 300) {
  const [debounced, setDebounced] = useState(value);

  useEffect(() => {
    const id = setTimeout(() => setDebounced(value), delay);
    return () => clearTimeout(id);
  }, [value, delay]);

  return debounced;
}

export default function Search() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState("");
  const [results, setResults] = useState([]);
  const debouncedQuery = useDebouncedValue(query, 300);

  useEffect(() => {
    if (!debouncedQuery.trim()) {
      setResults([]);
      return;
    }

    const controller = new AbortController();

    fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(debouncedQuery)}`, {
      signal: controller.signal,
    })
      .then((res) => res.json())
      .then(setResults)
      .catch((err) => {
        if (err.name !== "AbortError") console.error(err);
      });

    return () => controller.abort(); // cancel in-flight request if query changes
  }, [debouncedQuery]);

  return (
    <>
      <input
        value={query}
        onChange={(e) => setQuery(e.target.value)}
        placeholder="Search..."
      />
      <ul>
        {results.map((r) => (
          <li key={r.id}>{r.name}</li>
        ))}
      </ul>
    </>
  );
}
```

The input stays responsive because `query` updates on every keystroke. Only `debouncedQuery` lags, and it triggers the fetch.

## Vanilla JS

```js
function debounce(fn, delay = 300) {
  let timer;
  return (...args) => {
    clearTimeout(timer);
    timer = setTimeout(() => fn(...args), delay);
  };
}

let controller;

async function search(query) {
  controller?.abort();
  if (!query.trim()) return;

  controller = new AbortController();
  try {
    const res = await fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(query)}`, {
      signal: controller.signal,
    });
    render(await res.json());
  } catch (err) {
    if (err.name !== "AbortError") console.error(err);
  }
}

document
  .querySelector("#search")
  .addEventListener("input", debounce((e) => search(e.target.value), 300));
```

## Notes

- **Delay:** 250–400 ms is typical. Lower feels snappier but sends more requests.
- **Don't create the debounced function inside a render** (e.g. `debounce(...)` inline in a component body) without `useMemo`/`useRef`. It gets recreated each render and the timer is lost. The hook-based approach above avoids this.
- **Cleanup:** the `useEffect` return handles unmount, so no timer or request fires after the component is gone.
- **Alternatives:** `lodash.debounce` works if you already depend on it, and libraries like TanStack Query or SWR pair well with the debounced value as the query key.

If you're using a different framework (Vue, Angular, Svelte), tell me and I'll adapt it.
