## default-s2 / pooling

> Explain how database connection pooling works and why it helps.

### vanilla: 1334 answer tokens, 1334 billed, 55 lines

~~~
# Database Connection Pooling

## The problem it solves

Opening a database connection is expensive. Each new connection typically involves:

- A TCP handshake (and a TLS handshake if encrypted)
- Authentication and session setup on the server
- Server-side resources: in PostgreSQL, a whole OS process per connection; in MySQL, a thread plus memory buffers

If an application opens a fresh connection for every query or request and then closes it, this setup cost often exceeds the cost of the query itself. Databases also cap concurrent connections (e.g., `max_connections`), so many short-lived connections under load can exhaust the server.

## How a pool works

A connection pool is a cache of already-open, reusable connections managed by the application (or a proxy).

1. **Initialization:** The pool opens some minimum number of connections up front, or lazily as needed.
2. **Checkout:** When code needs the database, it asks the pool for a connection. If an idle one exists, it's handed over immediately. If none is idle and the pool is below its maximum size, a new one is opened. If the pool is at max, the request waits in a queue, usually with a timeout.
3. **Use:** The caller runs queries, possibly inside a transaction.
4. **Return:** When done, the caller "closes" the connection, but this actually returns it to the pool. The pool typically resets session state (rolls back uncommitted transactions, clears temp settings) so the next user gets a clean connection.
5. **Maintenance:** The pool validates connections (a health check or test query), evicts ones that have gone stale or exceeded a max lifetime, and shrinks back to the minimum after idle periods.

## Common configuration knobs

| Setting | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Min / max pool size | Bounds on open connections |
| Acquire (checkout) timeout | How long a caller waits before failing |
| Idle timeout | When to close unused connections |
| Max lifetime | Recycle connections periodically, avoiding stale ones and load balancer/firewall cutoffs |
| Validation / keepalive | Detect dead connections before handing them out |

## Why it helps

- **Lower latency:** Skips connect/auth/TLS on each request.
- **Higher throughput:** Less overhead per operation.
- **Protects the database:** The max size caps concurrency, so a traffic spike queues in the application rather than overwhelming the server.
- **Predictable resource use:** Connection counts stay bounded and stable.
- **Resilience:** Health checks and recycling handle dropped connections gracefully.

## Practical considerations

- **Bigger isn't better.** Databases perform best with a limited number of active connections, often only a small multiple of the CPU core count. An oversized pool causes contention. A well-tuned pool of 10-30 often outperforms one of hundreds.
- **Pool per process.** With many app instances, total connections = instances × pool size. This can exceed the server's limit, so account for it.
- **Always return connections.** Leaks (forgetting to release, especially on error paths) slowly drain the pool until requests time out. Use `try/finally` or context managers.
- **Transactions and session state.** Pinned state (session variables, prepared statements, advisory locks) can leak across users, or break entirely with transaction-level pooling.
- **External poolers.** Tools like PgBouncer, ProxySQL, or RDS Proxy pool at the proxy layer, which helps when you have many app instances or serverless functions that can't share an in-process pool. PgBouncer's modes (session, transaction, statement) trade off pooling efficiency against feature compatibility.

## Common implementations

HikariCP (Java), SQLAlchemy's pool and `psycopg_pool` (Python), `pgxpool` (Go), `pg.Pool` (Node), and built-in pooling in most ORMs and drivers.

## Short version

Connection pooling pays the expensive connection setup cost once, then reuses connections across many requests, while capping concurrency to protect the database.
~~~

### caveman: 1034 answer tokens, 1057 billed, 54 lines

~~~
## What it is

Pool = set of pre-opened DB connections, kept alive and shared. App borrows one, uses it, returns it. No open/close per request.

## How it works

1. **Startup:** pool opens N connections (min size), keeps idle.
2. **Request arrives:** app asks pool for connection (`getConnection()` / `acquire()`).
3. **Pool checks:**
   - Idle connection free → hand it out.
   - None free, below max size → open new one.
   - At max → caller waits in queue, until timeout → error.
4. **App runs queries**, then **releases** connection. Connection go back to pool, not closed.
5. **Pool housekeeping:**
   - Validate connection before lending (ping / `SELECT 1`).
   - Evict stale or broken ones, replace.
   - Close idle ones above min after idle timeout.
   - Reset session state (open transaction, temp tables, `SET` vars) on return.

## Why it help

- **Skip setup cost.** New connection = TCP handshake + TLS + auth + session init. Often tens of ms, more than the query itself. Pool pay once, reuse many.
- **Cap load on DB.** Each connection costs DB memory and often a process/thread (Postgres: one process per connection). Max size stop traffic spike from overwhelming DB with thousands of connections.
- **Backpressure.** Excess requests queue in app instead of crashing DB.
- **Better latency and throughput** under concurrency. Stable, predictable resource use.
- **Central control.** Timeouts, health checks, metrics in one place.

## Common pitfalls

- **Connection leak:** forget to release (missing `finally` / `close()` / context manager) → pool exhausted, app hang. Always release in `finally`.
- **Pool too big:** more connections ≠ faster. Past DB core/IO capacity, contention make it slower. Start small (rough rule: `cores * 2 + disk spindles`), then measure.
- **Pool too small:** requests queue, latency up.
- **Long transactions:** hold connection, starve others.
- **Many app instances:** total = instances × max size. Can exceed DB limit. Use external pooler (PgBouncer, ProxySQL, RDS Proxy) when so.
- **Stale connections:** firewall or DB drop idle ones silently. Set max lifetime below that limit, enable validation.
- **Session state leak:** one borrower's `SET` or open transaction affect next borrower if not reset.

## Typical libraries

- Java: HikariCP
- Python: SQLAlchemy pool, `psycopg_pool`
- Node: `pg.Pool`, `mysql2` pool
- Go: `database/sql` built-in pool
- External: PgBouncer, ProxySQL

## Key settings

| Setting | Meaning |
|---|---|
| min / max size | Floor and cap of connections |
| acquire timeout | How long caller waits for free connection |
| idle timeout | When to close unused extras |
| max lifetime | Recycle old connections |
| validation query | Health check before lend |
~~~

### ponytail: 908 answer tokens, 908 billed, 35 lines

~~~
**Connection pooling = reuse a fixed set of open DB connections instead of opening one per request.**

**How it works**

1. At startup, the pool opens a few connections (e.g. `min=5`) and keeps them alive.
2. A request calls `pool.acquire()`. The pool hands over an idle connection, or opens a new one if under `max`, or makes the caller wait (with a timeout) if the pool is exhausted.
3. The request runs its queries, then `release()`s the connection. The pool resets its state (rolls back open transactions, clears session settings) and marks it idle.
4. Background upkeep: health checks or validation queries drop dead connections, and idle ones past a TTL are closed down to `min`.

**Why it helps**

- **Cheaper requests:** A new connection costs a TCP handshake, TLS negotiation, authentication, and server-side process or session setup. That is often several ms to tens of ms, which is more than a simple query. Pooling pays it once.
- **Protects the database:** Databases cap concurrent connections, and each one uses memory (Postgres forks a process per connection). A pool's `max` bounds load, so a traffic spike queues in the app instead of overwhelming the DB.
- **Stable latency:** There's no connect storm under load, and the DB isn't thrashing through connection churn.
- **Backpressure:** Waiting with a timeout gives a clear failure mode (pool timeout) rather than DB-wide errors.

**Practical gotchas**

- **Sizing:** Bigger isn't better. A common starting point is roughly `cores × 2 + disk spindles` per DB, shared across all app instances. Total `max × instances` must stay under the DB's limit.
- **Leaks:** Forgetting to release (e.g. on an exception path) drains the pool. Use `with`/`try/finally` or context managers.
- **Stale connections:** Firewalls or the DB may silently drop idle connections. Use validation on borrow or a max lifetime.
- **Transaction state:** Session-level state (temp tables, `SET` variables, advisory locks) can leak between borrowers if not reset.
- **External poolers:** With many app instances or serverless, use a proxy like PgBouncer or RDS Proxy. Note that transaction-mode pooling breaks session features such as prepared statements and `SET`.

**Minimal example (Python, psycopg)**

```python
from psycopg_pool import ConnectionPool

pool = ConnectionPool("dbname=app", min_size=5, max_size=20, timeout=5)

with pool.connection() as conn:   # acquire
    conn.execute("SELECT 1")      # use
                                  # auto-release (commit/rollback) on exit
```
~~~

### chisle: 711 answer tokens, 730 billed, 23 lines

~~~
Pool = set of pre-opened, reusable DB connections held by the app. Request borrows one, runs queries, returns it instead of closing.

**Why opening per request is costly:** TCP handshake, TLS, auth, session setup, and on Postgres a forked backend process (~MBs of RAM each). That can cost more than the query itself. DBs also cap concurrent connections (`max_connections`), so one-connection-per-request → exhaustion under load.

**Mechanics:**
- Startup: pool opens `min` connections (or lazily).
- `acquire()`: hand out idle connection. None idle and below `max` → open new. At `max` → caller waits in queue until one is released or acquire timeout fires.
- `release()`: connection goes back to idle list. Pool typically resets session state (rollback open txn, clear temp settings).
- Background upkeep: health checks/validation query, evict connections past `max_lifetime` or `idle_timeout` (avoids stale sockets killed by firewalls/DB restarts).

**What it buys:**
- Latency: skip connect/auth cost per request.
- Bounded DB load: `max` acts as backpressure → queueing in app instead of overloading DB.
- Resource reuse: fewer backend processes, steadier memory.

**Gotchas:**
- Pool too big → DB thrashes on context switching; often small (≈ cores×2 + spindles) beats large. Total = pool size × app instances, so check against `max_connections`.
- Leaked connections (forgot to release, esp. on exceptions) → pool drains, requests hang. Always release in `finally`/context manager.
- Long transactions hold a connection the whole time.
- Session state (`SET`, advisory locks, temp tables) can leak between borrowers if not reset.
- Many app instances/serverless → use external pooler (PgBouncer, RDS Proxy). Transaction-mode pooling breaks session-level features like prepared statements and `LISTEN`.

Common implementations: HikariCP (Java), `pg.Pool` (Node), SQLAlchemy pool (Python), `database/sql` (Go, built in).
~~~

## default-s2 / regex-concept

> Explain what a regular expression backreference is, with one short example.

### vanilla: 397 answer tokens, 397 billed, 19 lines

~~~
A **backreference** is a regex feature that lets you match the same text that an earlier capture group already matched. You write it as `\1`, `\2`, etc., where the number refers to the capture group by its position (counting opening parentheses from left to right).

It's not "repeat the same pattern"; it's "repeat the exact text that was captured."

**Example: finding repeated words**

```
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

- `(\w+)` captures a word into group 1.
- `\s+` matches whitespace.
- `\1` matches the same word that group 1 captured.

Against the string `"this is is a test"`, it matches `is is`. It would not match `is it`, because `\1` must be literally `is`, not any word.

Another common use is matching paired quotes: `(['"]).*?\1` matches `'hi'` or `"hi"`, but not `'hi"`.

Syntax notes: some flavors also support named groups, such as `(?<word>\w+)\s+\k<word>` in JavaScript and .NET, or `(?P<word>\w+)\s+(?P=word)` in Python. In replacement strings, backreferences are often written `$1` or `\1` to reinsert captured text.
~~~

### caveman: 193 answer tokens, 193 billed, 15 lines

~~~
Backreference = pattern piece that match same text an earlier capture group already matched. Syntax: `\1`, `\2`, etc. Number = group position.

Example: find repeated word.

```
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

- `(\w+)` captures word → group 1
- `\s+` whitespace
- `\1` must match exact same text as group 1

Input `this is is a test` → match `is is`. Input `this is a test` → no match.

Note: backreference match captured *text*, not captured *pattern*. `(\d)\1` match `77`, not `78`.
~~~

### ponytail: 349 answer tokens, 349 billed, 17 lines

~~~
A **backreference** lets a regex pattern match the same text that an earlier capture group already matched. You write it as `\1`, `\2`, etc., where the number is the capture group's position (counting opening parentheses left to right).

**Example: find repeated words**

```
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

- `(\w+)` captures a word into group 1.
- `\s+` matches the whitespace.
- `\1` must match exactly what group 1 captured.

Against `"this is is a test"`, it matches `is is`. It won't match `is it`, because `\1` demands the literal text `is`, not just any word.

This is different from repeating the group's pattern (`(\w+)\s+(\w+)`), which would match any two words. A backreference matches the same *text*, not the same *pattern*.

Note: backreferences make a regex non-regular in the formal sense, so some engines (e.g., RE2, Rust's `regex`) don't support them. Named groups (`(?<word>\w+)` with `\k<word>`) are available in many flavors for readability.
~~~

### chisle: 226 answer tokens, 226 billed, 11 lines

~~~
Backreference: `\1`, `\2`, … inside a regex matches the same text that capture group 1, 2, … already matched. Not the same pattern, the same literal text.

Example: find repeated words.

```
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

On `"this is is a test"` → matches `is is`. Group 1 captures `is`, `\1` requires `is` again. `"is it"` doesn't match: `it` ≠ `is`.

Gotchas: replacement syntax differs by flavor (`$1` in JS, `\1` in sed/Python `re.sub`). Backreferences make the regex non-regular, so some engines (RE2, Go) don't support them.
~~~

## default-s2 / rest-graphql

> Summarize the main tradeoffs between REST and GraphQL for a new API.

### vanilla: 1083 answer tokens, 1083 billed, 39 lines

~~~
# REST vs GraphQL: Main Tradeoffs

## Data fetching
- **REST:** Fixed endpoints return fixed shapes. This often means over-fetching (extra fields) or under-fetching (multiple round trips to assemble a view).
- **GraphQL:** Clients ask for exactly the fields they need, across related resources, in one request. This is especially valuable for varied clients (web, mobile, third parties).

## Caching
- **REST:** Works naturally with HTTP caching (CDNs, browser cache, ETags) because resources have URLs and GET is cacheable.
- **GraphQL:** Typically a single POST endpoint, so HTTP caching is harder. You need persisted queries, client-side normalized caches (Apollo, Relay), or CDN-specific support.

## Schema and typing
- **REST:** Typing is optional and comes from OpenAPI, which is good but separate from the runtime.
- **GraphQL:** A strongly typed schema is built in and introspectable. This enables codegen, IDE tooling, and self-documentation out of the box.

## Evolution and versioning
- **REST:** Usually versioned (`/v2/`), which means maintaining parallel versions.
- **GraphQL:** Encourages additive change and field deprecation without versions. Removing fields still needs usage tracking.

## Performance and server risk
- **REST:** Predictable cost per endpoint, so rate limiting and monitoring are straightforward.
- **GraphQL:** Clients can write expensive or deeply nested queries. You need depth/complexity limits, cost analysis, and dataloader-style batching to avoid N+1 problems. Rate limiting by request count is not enough.

## Complexity and tooling
- **REST:** Simple, universally understood, and easy to debug with curl and browser tools. Low barrier for consumers.
- **GraphQL:** More upfront work (schema design, resolvers, batching, authorization per field) and a steeper learning curve for both server and client teams.

## Security and authorization
- **REST:** Authorization maps cleanly to endpoints and methods.
- **GraphQL:** Authorization must be enforced at the field/object level. Introspection and query flexibility widen the attack surface if left unchecked.

## Other considerations
- **File uploads, streaming, webhooks:** Simpler in REST. GraphQL needs extensions or subscriptions.
- **Error handling:** REST uses HTTP status codes. GraphQL often returns 200 with an `errors` array, which can confuse monitoring.
- **Ecosystem fit:** REST is the default for public APIs and simple CRUD. GraphQL shines in aggregation layers over many services.

## Recommendation
- **Choose REST** if the API is public, resource-oriented, CRUD-heavy, cache-sensitive, or your team is small and wants low operational overhead.
- **Choose GraphQL** if you have multiple clients with differing data needs, deeply related data, rapid frontend iteration, or a backend-for-frontend/aggregation role.
- **Default for a new API with no strong signal:** start with REST (documented with OpenAPI). Add GraphQL later as a layer if client data-shaping pain actually appears.
~~~

### caveman: 620 answer tokens, 620 billed, 37 lines

~~~
**REST**

Pros:
- Simple, familiar. Wide tooling, easy onboarding.
- HTTP caching works free (CDN, ETag, browser).
- Easy to rate limit, monitor, secure per endpoint.
- Predictable server load per endpoint.
- File upload/download, streaming straightforward.

Cons:
- Over-fetch / under-fetch. Clients make many round trips or get bloated payloads.
- Endpoint sprawl as client needs diverge (BFF, `?fields=`, `?include=`).
- Versioning pain over time.
- No built-in typed schema (need OpenAPI, extra work).

**GraphQL**

Pros:
- Client picks exact fields. One request for nested data. Good for mobile, varied clients.
- Strong typed schema, introspection, codegen, good dev tooling.
- Schema evolves by deprecation, less versioning.
- Good fit for aggregating many backends behind one graph.

Cons:
- Caching hard. Mostly POST to one endpoint, need client cache (Apollo/Relay) or persisted queries.
- N+1 query problem. Need DataLoader/batching.
- Expensive or abusive queries. Need depth/complexity limits, cost analysis, persisted queries.
- Auth at field level, more complex.
- Error handling odd: HTTP 200 with `errors` array.
- Steeper learning curve, more server infra.
- File upload, streaming less natural.

**Pick**

- REST: simple CRUD, public API, cache-heavy, small team, few client types.
- GraphQL: many clients with different data needs, deeply relational data, fast-moving frontend, multiple backend services to unify.
- Unsure: start REST + OpenAPI. Add GraphQL later as a layer if over-fetch pain appears. Easier than reverse.
~~~

### ponytail: 512 answer tokens, 512 billed, 15 lines

~~~
**Default to REST unless you have a concrete reason for GraphQL.**

**REST**
- Pros: simple, HTTP caching works out of the box (CDN, ETags), easy to debug with curl, mature tooling, straightforward auth/rate limiting per endpoint, easy for third parties to adopt.
- Cons: over-fetching and under-fetching, multiple round trips for nested data, endpoint sprawl as clients diverge, versioning pain.

**GraphQL**
- Pros: clients ask for exactly the fields they need, one round trip for nested data, strongly typed schema with introspection and good codegen, evolves without versioning (deprecate fields), great for many differing clients (web, mobile, partners).
- Cons: HTTP caching is harder (mostly POST to one endpoint), N+1 queries need DataLoader-style batching, expensive or malicious queries require depth/complexity limits, rate limiting is harder (cost-based, not per-request), file uploads and error semantics are awkward, steeper learning curve and more server-side machinery.

**Pick GraphQL when:** multiple clients with varied data needs, deeply relational data, or a frontend team that iterates faster than the backend can add endpoints.

**Pick REST when:** the API is simple CRUD, public or cache-heavy, consumed by third parties, or the team is small and wants the least moving parts.

Skipped: hybrid setups (e.g. a GraphQL gateway over REST services). Add that when you already have REST services and a client-aggregation problem.
~~~

### chisle: 545 answer tokens, 587 billed, 5 lines

~~~
**REST wins on simplicity and ops.** Resource URLs + HTTP verbs map onto HTTP caching (CDN, ETag, `Cache-Control`), status codes, and standard tooling (curl, OpenAPI, gateways, rate limiting per endpoint). Low learning curve, easy to debug, easy for third parties to consume. Costs: over-fetching and under-fetching (multiple round trips to assemble a view), endpoint sprawl as clients diverge, and versioning pain.

**GraphQL wins on client flexibility.** One endpoint, typed schema, client asks for exactly the fields it needs → fewer round trips, good for many clients (web, mobile) with different data shapes, strong introspection and codegen. Costs: HTTP caching mostly lost (everything is `POST /graphql`, so you need persisted queries or client-side caches), N+1 resolver problems (need DataLoader), harder rate limiting (cost analysis, depth limits), a larger security surface (query complexity, introspection exposure, per-field authz), errors returned as 200 with an `errors` array, and more server-side complexity.

**Verdict:** default to REST for a new API unless you have a concrete problem GraphQL solves: multiple heterogeneous clients, deeply nested/relational data, or a frontend team iterating faster than the backend can ship endpoints. Public APIs, simple CRUD, file uploads, and cache-heavy workloads favor REST. Internal product APIs with a rich UI favor GraphQL. Adding GraphQL later as a layer over existing services is feasible, so don't pay the complexity up front without a need.
~~~

## default-s3 / auth-bug

> Our auth middleware rejects valid tokens at the exact expiry boundary (it uses currentTime > expiry). Find and fix the root cause.

### vanilla: 649 answer tokens, 893 billed, 13 lines

~~~
I can't point to a specific line without seeing the code, but the premise doesn't hold: `currentTime > expiry` doesn't reject a token at the exact expiry boundary. When `currentTime == expiry`, that expression is false, so the token is accepted. If the middleware rejects at the boundary, the rejection is coming from somewhere else.

Also, RFC 7519 says the current time must be *before* `exp`, so the strict spec-correct check is `currentTime >= expiry` (reject at the boundary). Changing `>` to `>=` would make boundary tokens fail, not fix them.

Likely real causes of valid tokens being rejected near expiry:

1. **Unit mismatch**: JWT `exp` is in seconds, while `Date.now()` / `System.currentTimeMillis()` is in milliseconds. Comparing the two directly makes every token look expired. The fix is to normalize both to the same unit, e.g. `Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000)`.
2. **Clock skew between issuer and verifier**: tokens issued by another server can look expired a few seconds early. Fix by adding a small leeway (e.g. 30-60s), which most JWT libraries support, such as `clockTolerance` in `jsonwebtoken`.
3. **A second check elsewhere**: the library's own verification (e.g. `jwt.verify`), a gateway, or a cache TTL may use `>=` or a stricter rule than your middleware does.
4. **Type or timezone issues**: `expiry` parsed as a string, or as a local-time `Date` instead of UTC.
5. **Refresh-window logic**: a "token about to expire" check that treats tokens within N seconds of expiry as invalid.

If you paste the middleware (and where `expiry` and `currentTime` come from), I can pinpoint which of these it is. If you run a repro, the values of `currentTime` and `expiry` at the point of rejection will almost certainly show it.
~~~

- Check inverted: `if (!(currentTime < expiry))` or `valid = currentTime > expiry`.
- Different check in another layer (gateway, library, second middleware) using `>=`.
- Unit mismatch: `exp` in seconds, `Date.now()` in ms. Compare `Math.floor(Date.now()/1000)`.

**Likely fix** (if tokens fail slightly before or at expiry from skew):
```js
const nowSec = Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000);
const LEEWAY = 30; // seconds
if (nowSec >= claims.exp + LEEWAY) reject();
```
Use leeway, not boundary tweak. Most JWT libs have this option (`clockTolerance` in jsonwebtoken, `leeway` in PyJWT).

## default-s3 / cache

> Add a cache layer for our user profile API responses. Show the code.

### vanilla: 1917 answer tokens, 2052 billed, 126 lines

~~~
I don't know your stack, so this is a TypeScript cache-aside layer with a pluggable store (in-memory for one instance, Redis for several). It includes TTLs, in-flight request coalescing against stampedes, and invalidation on writes.

```ts
// cache.ts
export interface CacheStore {
  get(key: string): Promise<string | null>;
  set(key: string, value: string, ttlSeconds: number): Promise<void>;
  del(key: string): Promise<void>;
}

// In-memory store with TTL and a size cap (simple LRU via Map insertion order)
export class MemoryStore implements CacheStore {
  private map = new Map<string, { v: string; exp: number }>();
  constructor(private maxEntries = 10_000) {}

  async get(key: string) {
    const e = this.map.get(key);
    if (!e) return null;
    if (e.exp < Date.now()) { this.map.delete(key); return null; }
    this.map.delete(key); this.map.set(key, e); // refresh recency
    return e.v;
  }
  async set(key: string, value: string, ttl: number) {
    this.map.delete(key);
    this.map.set(key, { v: value, exp: Date.now() + ttl * 1000 });
    if (this.map.size > this.maxEntries) {
      this.map.delete(this.map.keys().next().value!);
    }
  }
  async del(key: string) { this.map.delete(key); }
}

// Redis store (ioredis)
import type { Redis } from "ioredis";
export class RedisStore implements CacheStore {
  constructor(private redis: Redis) {}
  get(key: string) { return this.redis.get(key); }
  async set(key: string, value: string, ttl: number) {
    await this.redis.set(key, value, "EX", ttl);
  }
  async del(key: string) { await this.redis.del(key); }
}
```

```ts
// profileCache.ts
import { CacheStore } from "./cache";

export interface Profile { id: string; name: string; /* ... */ }

const TTL_SECONDS = 300;
const NEGATIVE_TTL_SECONDS = 30; // cache "not found" briefly
const key = (id: string) => `profile:v1:${id}`; // bump version on schema changes

export class ProfileService {
  private inflight = new Map<string, Promise<Profile | null>>();

  constructor(
    private store: CacheStore,
    private db: { findProfile(id: string): Promise<Profile | null>;
                  updateProfile(id: string, patch: Partial<Profile>): Promise<Profile> },
  ) {}

  async getProfile(id: string): Promise<Profile | null> {
    const k = key(id);

    try {
      const hit = await this.store.get(k);
      if (hit !== null) return JSON.parse(hit); // "null" => cached miss
    } catch { /* cache failure must not break the API: fall through to DB */ }

    // Coalesce concurrent misses for the same id into one DB query
    const pending = this.inflight.get(k);
    if (pending) return pending;

    const p = (async () => {
      const profile = await this.db.findProfile(id);
      try {
        await this.store.set(
          k, JSON.stringify(profile),
          profile ? TTL_SECONDS + Math.floor(Math.random() * 30) // jitter
                  : NEGATIVE_TTL_SECONDS,
        );
      } catch { /* ignore */ }
      return profile;
    })().finally(() => this.inflight.delete(k));

    this.inflight.set(k, p);
    return p;
  }

  async updateProfile(id: string, patch: Partial<Profile>): Promise<Profile> {
    const updated = await this.db.updateProfile(id, patch);
    try { await this.store.del(key(id)); } catch { /* TTL will expire it */ }
    return updated;
  }
}
```

```ts
// Express wiring
app.get("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  const profile = await profiles.getProfile(req.params.id);
  if (!profile) return res.sendStatus(404);
  res.set("Cache-Control", "private, max-age=60"); // optional client-side caching
  res.json(profile);
});

app.patch("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  res.json(await profiles.updateProfile(req.params.id, req.body));
});
```

**Design choices:**
- **Cache-aside:** the DB stays the source of truth, and cache errors degrade to DB reads.
- **Invalidate on write (delete, not set):** this avoids races where a stale value overwrites a newer one.
- **TTL with jitter:** this bounds staleness even if an invalidation is missed, and keeps keys from expiring all at once.
- **Negative caching:** short-lived entries for missing users protect the DB from repeated lookups of nonexistent IDs.
- **Coalescing:** the `inflight` map is per-process. For cross-instance protection, add a Redis lock (`SET NX`) or serve stale data while refreshing.
- **Key versioning:** `profile:v1:` lets you invalidate everything by bumping the version.

**Caveats:**
- If profiles contain per-viewer fields (privacy settings, "is following"), cache only the viewer-independent part, or include the viewer ID in the key. Don't set `Cache-Control: public` on personalized responses.
- If other services write to the profile table, they also need to invalidate the key, or you'll need to publish change events.

If you tell me your language, framework, and whether you run multiple instances, I can tailor this, for example to Python/FastAPI, Go, or a Redis-only setup.
~~~

### caveman: 1053 answer tokens, 1190 billed, 80 lines

~~~
No stack given. Assume Node/TypeScript + Redis (`ioredis`). Pattern: cache-aside, TTL with jitter, in-flight dedup, invalidate on write.

```ts
// profileCache.ts
import Redis from "ioredis";

const redis = new Redis(process.env.REDIS_URL!);

const TTL_SECONDS = 300;
const key = (userId: string) => `profile:v1:${userId}`; // bump v1 on schema change

// Dedup concurrent misses for same user (stampede guard, per process)
const inflight = new Map<string, Promise<Profile>>();

export interface Profile {
  id: string;
  name: string;
  // ...
}

export async function getProfile(
  userId: string,
  loader: (id: string) => Promise<Profile>, // your DB call
): Promise<Profile> {
  const k = key(userId);

  try {
    const hit = await redis.get(k);
    if (hit) return JSON.parse(hit) as Profile;
  } catch (err) {
    console.warn("cache read failed, falling through", err); // cache down != API down
  }

  const pending = inflight.get(k);
  if (pending) return pending;

  const p = (async () => {
    const profile = await loader(userId);
    const ttl = TTL_SECONDS + Math.floor(Math.random() * 30); // jitter
    redis.set(k, JSON.stringify(profile), "EX", ttl).catch((err) =>
      console.warn("cache write failed", err),
    );
    return profile;
  })().finally(() => inflight.delete(k));

  inflight.set(k, p);
  return p;
}

export async function invalidateProfile(userId: string): Promise<void> {
  await redis.del(key(userId));
}
```

Usage:

```ts
// GET /users/:id
app.get("/users/:id", async (req, res) => {
  const profile = await getProfile(req.params.id, db.users.findById);
  res.set("Cache-Control", "private, max-age=0");
  res.json(profile);
});

// PUT /users/:id
app.put("/users/:id", async (req, res) => {
  const updated = await db.users.update(req.params.id, req.body);
  await invalidateProfile(req.params.id); // after DB commit
  res.json(updated);
});
```

Notes:
- **Invalidate after commit**, not before. Else concurrent read can repopulate stale data.
- **Don't cache per-viewer fields** (e.g. "isFollowing") under shared key. Add viewer to key or cache only the viewer-independent part.
- **Other writers** (admin tools, jobs, other services) must call `invalidateProfile` too, or rely on the 5-min TTL.
- **Negative caching**: not-found not cached here. Add short TTL (~30s) sentinel if 404 floods are a concern.
- **PII**: profiles in Redis → use auth/TLS, and delete on account deletion.

### ponytail: 685 answer tokens, 1309 billed, 56 lines

~~~
Assuming Python, since there's no codebase to check. This is an in-process TTL cache keyed by user id:

```python
import time
from functools import wraps

def ttl_cache(ttl=60, maxsize=1000):
    def deco(fn):
        store = {}  # user_id -> (expires_at, value)

        @wraps(fn)
        def wrapper(user_id):
            now = time.monotonic()
            hit = store.get(user_id)
            if hit and hit[0] > now:
                return hit[1]
            val = fn(user_id)  # exceptions propagate, so failures are never cached
            if len(store) >= maxsize:
                store.pop(next(iter(store)))  # ponytail: evicts oldest-inserted, not LRU
            store[user_id] = (now + ttl, val)
            return val

        wrapper.invalidate = lambda user_id: store.pop(user_id, None)
        return wrapper
    return deco


# Usage
@ttl_cache(ttl=60)
def get_profile(user_id):
    return fetch_profile_from_api(user_id)

# On any profile write:
#   update_profile(user_id, data); get_profile.invalidate(user_id)


def demo():
    calls = []
    @ttl_cache(ttl=0.05)
    def f(uid):
        calls.append(uid)
        return uid
    f(1); f(1)
    assert calls == [1]            # second call cached
    f.invalidate(1); f(1)
    assert calls == [1, 1]         # invalidate forces refetch
    time.sleep(0.06); f(1)
    assert calls == [1, 1, 1]      # TTL expiry forces refetch

if __name__ == "__main__":
    demo()
```

→ Skipped: Redis, LRU, and thread locks. Add Redis if you run multiple workers, since each process has its own cache and invalidation won't cross them. Add `cachetools.TTLCache` if you need true LRU or thread safety.

Assumed: Node/TypeScript, single instance, in-memory. Cache-aside with TTL, in-flight dedupe, invalidate on write.

```ts
// profileCache.ts
type Entry<T> = { value: T; expires: number };

const TTL_MS = 60_000;
const MAX = 10_000;
const store = new Map<string, Entry<Profile>>();
const inflight = new Map<string, Promise<Profile>>();

export async function getProfile(id: string): Promise<Profile> {
  const hit = store.get(id);
  if (hit && hit.expires > Date.now()) return hit.value;

  let p = inflight.get(id);
  if (!p) {
    p = fetchProfileFromDb(id)
      .then((value) => {
        if (store.size >= MAX) store.delete(store.keys().next().value!); // evict oldest
        store.set(id, { value, expires: Date.now() + TTL_MS });
        return value;
      })
      .finally(() => inflight.delete(id));
    inflight.set(id, p);
  }
  return p;
}

export function invalidateProfile(id: string) {
  store.delete(id);
}
```

Wire-up:

```ts
app.get("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => res.json(await getProfile(req.params.id)));

app.put("/users/:id/profile", async (req, res) => {
  const updated = await updateProfileInDb(req.params.id, req.body);
  invalidateProfile(req.params.id);
  res.json(updated);
});
```

skipped: Redis. Needed once you run >1 instance, since invalidation here is per-process → stale reads on other nodes (swap `store` for Redis `GET`/`SETEX`/`DEL`, same shape).
Race: a read in flight during a write can re-cache old data for up to 60s. Acceptable at this TTL; otherwise add a version check.
Don't cache per-viewer fields (e.g. "is following") under the same key.

## default-s3 / debounce

> Add debounce to a search input that currently fires an API call on every keystroke. Show the code.

### vanilla: 1075 answer tokens, 1075 billed, 90 lines

~~~
Here's a React version, since that's the most common setup. It debounces the API call and cancels stale requests, so out-of-order responses can't overwrite newer results.

```tsx
import { useEffect, useState } from "react";

function useDebouncedValue<T>(value: T, delay = 300): T {
  const [debounced, setDebounced] = useState(value);

  useEffect(() => {
    const id = setTimeout(() => setDebounced(value), delay);
    return () => clearTimeout(id); // reset timer on every keystroke
  }, [value, delay]);

  return debounced;
}

export function Search() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState("");
  const [results, setResults] = useState<any[]>([]);
  const debouncedQuery = useDebouncedValue(query, 300);

  useEffect(() => {
    const q = debouncedQuery.trim();
    if (!q) {
      setResults([]);
      return;
    }

    const controller = new AbortController();

    fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(q)}`, { signal: controller.signal })
      .then((res) => res.json())
      .then(setResults)
      .catch((err) => {
        if (err.name !== "AbortError") console.error(err);
      });

    return () => controller.abort(); // cancel in-flight request if query changes
  }, [debouncedQuery]);

  return (
    <input
      value={query}
      onChange={(e) => setQuery(e.target.value)}
      placeholder="Search..."
    />
  );
}
```

**How it works**
- `query` updates on every keystroke, so the input stays responsive.
- `debouncedQuery` only updates after 300 ms of no typing.
- The fetch effect depends on `debouncedQuery`, so the API is called once per pause rather than per keystroke.
- `AbortController` cancels any in-flight request when a new one starts or the component unmounts.

**Vanilla JS equivalent**

```js
function debounce(fn, delay = 300) {
  let timer;
  return (...args) => {
    clearTimeout(timer);
    timer = setTimeout(() => fn(...args), delay);
  };
}

let controller;
const search = debounce(async (q) => {
  controller?.abort();
  controller = new AbortController();
  try {
    const res = await fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(q)}`, {
      signal: controller.signal,
    });
    render(await res.json());
  } catch (e) {
    if (e.name !== "AbortError") console.error(e);
  }
}, 300);

document.querySelector("#search").addEventListener("input", (e) => search(e.target.value));
```

**Tips**
- 250–400 ms is a typical delay. Shorter feels snappier, longer saves more requests.
- Skip empty or very short queries (e.g. under 2 characters) to avoid useless calls.
- If you use a library, `lodash.debounce` or `useDeferredValue` (React 18+) are alternatives, but the custom hook above avoids extra dependencies and gives you explicit control over the delay.

If you tell me your framework (Vue, Angular, Svelte, etc.), I can adapt it.
~~~

## default-s3 / pooling

> Explain how database connection pooling works and why it helps.

### vanilla: 1427 answer tokens, 1427 billed, 56 lines

~~~
# Database Connection Pooling

## The problem it solves

Opening a database connection is expensive. Each new connection typically involves:

- A TCP handshake (plus a TLS handshake if encrypted)
- Authentication and session setup
- Server-side allocation (a process or thread, memory buffers, session state)

For a query that takes 1-2 ms, spending 20-100+ ms on connection setup is wasteful. And if every request opens and closes its own connection, the database spends much of its time on setup and teardown rather than queries.

## How a pool works

A connection pool is a cache of open, ready-to-use connections maintained by the application (or a proxy in front of the database).

1. **Initialization**: The pool opens some minimum number of connections up front (or lazily).
2. **Checkout**: When code needs the database, it asks the pool for a connection. If an idle one is available, it's handed over immediately. If not, and the pool is under its maximum size, a new connection is created. If the pool is at its maximum, the caller waits in a queue (usually with a timeout).
3. **Use**: The caller runs its queries or transaction on that connection exclusively.
4. **Return**: When done, the caller "closes" the connection, but this actually resets its state (rolls back open transactions, clears session variables) and returns it to the pool rather than tearing down the socket.
5. **Maintenance**: The pool runs background housekeeping: validating connections (health checks or test queries), evicting ones idle too long or older than a max lifetime, and replacing broken ones.

## Key configuration knobs

| Setting | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Min/max pool size | Bounds on open connections |
| Acquire/checkout timeout | How long a caller waits before erroring |
| Idle timeout | Close connections unused for a while |
| Max lifetime | Recycle connections periodically (avoids stale connections, firewall/LB cutoffs) |
| Validation/keepalive | Detect dead connections before handing them out |

## Why it helps

- **Lower latency**: Skips connection setup on most requests.
- **Higher throughput**: The database does more real work and less setup/teardown.
- **Protects the database**: Databases handle a limited number of concurrent connections well (PostgreSQL, for instance, uses a process per connection, so thousands get costly). A pool caps concurrency, and excess requests queue in the application rather than overwhelming the server.
- **Resource predictability**: Connection counts stay bounded even under traffic spikes.
- **Resilience**: Health checks and recycling hide transient connection failures from callers.

## Common pitfalls

- **Pool too large**: More connections do not mean more speed. Beyond what the database's CPU and disk can service in parallel, extra connections just add contention. A small pool (often on the order of tens, not hundreds) frequently outperforms a large one.
- **Connection leaks**: Forgetting to return a connection (e.g., not closing it in a `finally` block or context manager) slowly exhausts the pool, and requests start timing out.
- **Long transactions**: Holding a connection while doing slow non-database work (HTTP calls, etc.) starves other callers.
- **Multiplied pools**: Total connections = pool size × number of app instances. Ten instances with a max of 50 each can mean 500 connections hitting the database.
- **Session state leakage**: Session-level settings (temp tables, `SET` variables, advisory locks) can bleed between callers if the pool doesn't reset them.

## In-app pools vs. external poolers

- **In-app pools** (HikariCP for Java, SQLAlchemy's pool for Python, `pgx` pool for Go, `pg-pool` for Node) live inside each application process.
- **External poolers** (PgBouncer, ProxySQL, RDS Proxy) sit between many clients and the database, multiplexing many client connections onto fewer server connections. This is especially useful with many app instances, serverless functions, or short-lived processes. Transaction-level pooling modes give the best multiplexing but restrict session-level features.

## Rule of thumb

Size the pool to what the database can actually execute in parallel (roughly related to its core count and disk characteristics), keep transactions short, always return connections, and let excess demand queue rather than open more connections.
~~~

### caveman: 774 answer tokens, 774 billed, 32 lines

~~~
Pool = set of pre-opened DB connections, shared by app threads/requests.

**How it work:**
1. App start → pool open N connections (min size), keep alive.
2. Request need DB → **borrow** idle connection from pool.
3. Run query.
4. Done → **return** connection to pool. Not closed. Reset state (rollback open txn, clear session vars).
5. All connections busy → request wait (with timeout) or pool grow up to max size.
6. Pool also validate connections (ping/test query), evict dead or old ones, replace.

**Why help:**
- **Skip setup cost.** New connection = TCP handshake + TLS + auth + session init. Often tens of ms. Pool pay once, reuse many times.
- **Cap load on DB.** DB connections cost memory/process each (Postgres = process per connection). Max pool size stop app flooding DB with thousands of connections.
- **Better latency under load.** No connect per request. Predictable.
- **Backpressure.** Excess requests queue instead of crash DB.
- **Resilience.** Health checks drop stale connections after DB restart or network drop.

**Key settings:**
- `min`/`max` size: max too big → DB overload. Too small → requests queue.
- Acquire timeout: how long wait for free connection before error.
- Idle timeout / max lifetime: recycle connections, dodge firewall/server kills.
- Validation query: detect dead connections.

**Common pitfalls:**
- **Connection leak:** borrow, never return (missed `close()` on error path). Pool exhaust, app hang. Use `try/finally` or context manager.
- **Long transactions** hold connection, starve others.
- **Pool × instances:** 20 app replicas × max 50 = 1000 DB connections. Size per total, not per instance.
- Many replicas/serverless → use external pooler (PgBouncer, RDS Proxy).

**Sizing rule of thumb:** small pool often wins. Start near `(CPU cores × 2) + disk spindles` for DB, tune by measuring. More connections ≠ more throughput; past DB capacity, contention slows everything.

**Examples:** HikariCP (Java), `pg-pool` (Node), SQLAlchemy `QueuePool` (Python), `database/sql` built-in pool (Go).
~~~

### ponytail: 809 answer tokens, 809 billed, 21 lines

~~~
**Connection pooling = reuse open DB connections instead of opening a new one per request.**

**How it works**
1. At startup, the pool opens a few connections (e.g. `min` of them) and keeps them alive.
2. When code needs the DB, it **borrows** a connection from the pool (`pool.acquire()`).
3. It runs its queries, then **returns** the connection (`release()`). The connection is not closed; it goes back to the idle set.
4. If all connections are busy, the caller waits in a queue (up to a timeout) or the pool opens a new one, up to `max`.
5. Background upkeep: health checks/validation on borrow, closing connections idle too long or past a max lifetime, and resetting session state (open transactions, `SET` variables) on return.

**Why it helps**
- **Setup cost is large.** A new connection means a TCP handshake, TLS negotiation, authentication, and server-side session/process setup. That is often several ms to tens of ms, which can exceed the query itself.
- **The database has a hard ceiling.** Each connection costs server memory (Postgres forks a process per connection). Without a pool, a traffic spike of 5,000 requests tries to open 5,000 connections and the DB falls over. A pool caps concurrency at `max` and queues the rest, which is graceful backpressure.
- **Steady throughput.** Keeping a bounded number of warm connections avoids connect/disconnect churn (and ephemeral port exhaustion or `TIME_WAIT` buildup on the client).

**Practical tuning notes**
- Bigger isn't better. Past the DB's core and I/O capacity, more connections just add contention. A common starting point is a small multiple of the DB's CPU cores, then measure.
- Always release connections (use `try/finally` or a context manager). Leaks are the classic pool failure: the pool exhausts and every request hangs.
- Set an acquire timeout so exhaustion fails loudly instead of hanging.
- Keep max lifetime below any idle timeout enforced by firewalls, load balancers, or the DB, so you don't borrow silently dead connections.
- With many app instances, total connections = instances × `max`. If that exceeds the DB limit, put an external pooler such as PgBouncer or RDS Proxy in front.
- Transaction-level poolers (PgBouncer in transaction mode) break session-scoped features like prepared statements, advisory locks, and `SET`.
~~~

### chisle: 686 answer tokens, 703 billed, 18 lines

~~~
Pool = set of pre-opened, reusable DB connections kept by the app (or a proxy like PgBouncer). Requests borrow one, run queries, return it. No close.

**Why it helps:** opening a connection is expensive: TCP handshake, TLS, auth, server-side session/process setup (Postgres forks a process per connection). Often several ms to tens of ms, more than a simple query. Pooling pays that once, amortized across many requests.

It also caps concurrency. DB servers degrade with too many connections (memory per connection, context switching, `max_connections` limit). Pool size bounds load on the DB; excess requests queue in the app instead of crashing the DB.

**Lifecycle:**
1. Startup: pool opens `min` connections (or lazily).
2. Request calls `acquire()`: idle connection handed out; if none idle and below `max`, open new one; if at `max`, wait (with timeout).
3. Caller runs queries, then `release()`: connection returns to idle set, not closed.
4. Background housekeeping: health checks (validation query/ping), evict connections idle too long or past `max_lifetime` (avoids stale connections killed by firewalls/DB restarts).

**Gotchas:**
- Leaked connections (acquired, never released, e.g. missing `finally`/context manager) → pool exhausted → everything hangs. Most common pooling bug.
- Session state (temp tables, `SET`, open transactions, advisory locks) leaks to next borrower unless the pool resets it. Transaction-mode proxies (PgBouncer) break session-level features like prepared statements and `SET`.
- Pool too big is as bad as none: N app instances × `max` must stay under the DB's `max_connections`. Start small (roughly `cores × 2 + disk spindles` is the classic heuristic); throughput usually peaks at a modest size.
- Acquire timeout needed, else a slow query backlog turns into unbounded request waits.
- Serverless/many short-lived processes: per-process pools don't share, so use an external pooler/proxy.
~~~

## default-s3 / regex-concept

> Explain what a regular expression backreference is, with one short example.

### vanilla: 320 answer tokens, 320 billed, 17 lines

~~~
A **backreference** in a regular expression lets you refer back to text that an earlier capture group already matched, so the pattern can require that the same text appear again.

You write it as `\1`, `\2`, etc. (the number is the capture group's position, counted by opening parentheses). Some flavors also support named forms like `\k<name>`.

**Example: finding repeated words**

```
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

- `(\w+)` captures a word into group 1.
- `\s+` matches the whitespace after it.
- `\1` matches *exactly the same text* that group 1 captured.

Against `"this is is a test"`, it matches `is is`. It would not match `is a`, because `\1` must repeat the literal text `is`, not just any word.

Backreferences are also commonly used in replacements (e.g., `$1` or `\1` in a substitution string) to reuse captured text, such as swapping `(\w+) (\w+)` to `\2 \1`.
~~~

### caveman: 190 answer tokens, 190 billed, 15 lines

~~~
Backreference = regex token that match same text an earlier capture group already matched. Written `\1`, `\2`, etc. (number = group index).

Example: find repeated words.

```regex
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

- `(\w+)` captures word → group 1.
- `\s+` whitespace.
- `\1` must match exact same text as group 1.

Matches `the the` in "I saw the the cat". No match on `the cat`.

Note: backreference match captured *text*, not the *pattern*. `(\d)\1` match `77`, not `78`.
~~~

### ponytail: 227 answer tokens, 227 billed, 14 lines

~~~
A **backreference** is a regex token (`\1`, `\2`, …) that matches the *same text* that an earlier capture group matched, not just the same pattern.

**Example:** find repeated words.

```
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

- `(\w+)` captures a word into group 1.
- `\1` must then match exactly that captured text.

On `"this is is a test"`, it matches `"is is"`. It would not match `"is it"`, even though `\w+` could match both words, because `\1` requires the identical string `"is"`.

Many engines also support named groups (`(?<w>\w+)\s+\k<w>`), which is the same idea with a label instead of a number.
~~~

### chisle: 236 answer tokens, 236 billed, 14 lines

~~~
Backreference: regex token (`\1`, `\2`, ...) matching the **same text** that capture group N already matched, not just the same pattern.

Example: find repeated words.

```
\b(\w+)\s+\1\b
```

- `(\w+)` captures a word, e.g. `the`
- `\1` must match that exact text again

Matches `the the` in "I saw the the cat"; doesn't match `the cat`, though `\w+\s+\w+` would.

Gotchas: syntax varies (`\1` in most engines, `$1` is for replacement strings, `\k<name>` for named groups). Backreferences make matching non-regular, so some engines (RE2, Rust `regex`) don't support them.
~~~

## default-s3 / rest-graphql

> Summarize the main tradeoffs between REST and GraphQL for a new API.

### vanilla: 919 answer tokens, 919 billed, 30 lines

~~~
# REST vs GraphQL for a new API

**REST strengths**
- **Simplicity and familiarity**: Maps to HTTP verbs and resources; nearly every developer and tool understands it.
- **Caching**: HTTP caching (CDNs, browser, ETags) works out of the box because resources have stable URLs.
- **Operational clarity**: Per-endpoint monitoring, rate limiting, auth, and logging are straightforward.
- **Tooling**: OpenAPI gives docs, codegen, and validation. File uploads, streaming, and webhooks are natural.

**REST weaknesses**
- **Over-fetching and under-fetching**: Fixed response shapes mean clients get too much or must make multiple round trips.
- **Versioning and endpoint sprawl**: Diverse clients (web, mobile) often drive custom endpoints or breaking changes.

**GraphQL strengths**
- **Client-driven queries**: Clients request exactly the fields they need, in one round trip, which helps mobile and complex UIs.
- **Strongly typed schema**: Introspection, self-documenting, great codegen and IDE support.
- **Evolution without versions**: Add fields and deprecate old ones gradually.
- **Good aggregation layer**: Works well as a front for multiple backend services.

**GraphQL weaknesses**
- **Caching is harder**: Typically a single POST endpoint, so you rely on client-side normalized caches or persisted queries rather than plain HTTP caching.
- **Performance risks**: N+1 resolver problems (need DataLoader/batching), and expensive or deeply nested queries require depth/complexity limits and cost analysis.
- **Security and ops complexity**: Authorization must be enforced per field, rate limiting is not per-endpoint, and error handling and observability are less standard (often HTTP 200 with an errors body).
- **Learning curve and infrastructure**: More upfront design, schema governance, and server tooling. File uploads and streaming are less standardized.

**How to choose**
- **Pick REST** if your API is resource-oriented, public or third-party facing, cache-heavy, or your team is small and wants the lowest complexity.
- **Pick GraphQL** if you have multiple diverse clients, deeply relational data, rapidly changing frontend needs, or need to aggregate many services.
- **Hybrid** is common: REST for simple, cacheable, or public endpoints, and GraphQL as a BFF (backend-for-frontend) for your own rich clients.

**Default recommendation**: For a new API with no strong client-diversity pressure, start with REST plus OpenAPI. Adopt GraphQL when over-fetching or round-trip costs become real problems, not preemptively.
~~~

### caveman: 650 answer tokens, 650 billed, 36 lines

~~~
**REST**

Pros:
- Simple. Familiar. Huge tooling/ecosystem.
- HTTP caching work free (CDN, ETag, browser).
- Easy to debug (curl, logs, status codes).
- Per-endpoint auth, rate limit, monitoring easy.
- File upload/download, streaming natural.

Cons:
- Over-fetch / under-fetch. Many round trips for nested data.
- Endpoint sprawl as clients diverge (`/users/:id/summary`, `/users/:id/full`).
- Versioning pain (`/v1`, `/v2`).
- Schema/docs optional (need OpenAPI discipline).

**GraphQL**

Pros:
- Client ask exact fields. One request, many resources. Good for mobile, varied clients.
- Strong typed schema. Introspection, codegen, self-documenting.
- Evolve schema by adding fields, deprecating. Less versioning.
- Good fit for aggregating many backends (federation/gateway).

Cons:
- HTTP caching hard (mostly POST to one endpoint). Need persisted queries, client cache (Apollo/Relay).
- N+1 query problem. Need DataLoader/batching.
- Expensive/malicious queries. Need depth/complexity limits, cost analysis, timeouts.
- Harder rate limiting, authz (per-field), observability.
- Bigger learning curve, more server infra. File upload awkward.
- Errors: often 200 OK with `errors` array. Monitoring need care.

**Pick**

- REST: simple CRUD, public API, small team, cache-heavy, few client types.
- GraphQL: many clients with different data needs, deeply nested/relational data, fast frontend iteration, aggregating services.
- Unsure → start REST + OpenAPI. Add GraphQL later as BFF layer if over-fetch pain real. Don't pick GraphQL for hype.
~~~

**REST**
- **Pros:** Simple, universally understood; HTTP caching (CDN, ETags) works out of the box; easy to debug with curl; mature tooling (OpenAPI); straightforward rate limiting, auth, and monitoring per endpoint.
- **Cons:** Over-fetching and under-fetching; multiple round trips for related data; endpoint sprawl as clients diversify; versioning is manual.

**GraphQL**
- **Pros:** Clients request exactly the fields they need; one round trip for nested data; strongly typed schema doubles as documentation and enables codegen; good fit for many different clients (web, mobile) with differing needs; schema evolution via deprecation rather than versions.
- **Cons:** Harder caching (mostly POST to one endpoint, so you need client-side caches or persisted queries); N+1 query problem needs DataLoader-style batching; need query depth/complexity limits to avoid abusive queries; per-field authorization is more work; file uploads and error semantics (200 with an `errors` array) are awkward; steeper learning curve and heavier server tooling.

**Rule of thumb**
- Pick **REST** for simple or resource-oriented CRUD APIs, public APIs, or small teams where caching and simplicity matter.
- Pick **GraphQL** when you have multiple clients with divergent data needs, deeply relational data, or a frontend team that iterates faster than the backend can ship endpoints.

For a new API with no strong requirement pushing toward GraphQL, start with REST. You can add a GraphQL layer later if over-fetching becomes a real problem.

~~~
**REST:** simple, resource-per-URL, uses HTTP natively (GET caching, CDNs, status codes, standard tooling). Weak spots: over-fetching, under-fetching (multiple round trips for related data), and versioning/endpoint sprawl as clients diversify.

**GraphQL:** client picks exact fields, fetches nested data in one request, and has a typed schema with introspection → great codegen and docs. Best when many different clients (web, mobile, third parties) need different shapes. Costs: HTTP caching mostly lost (everything is POST to one endpoint), so you need client-side or persisted-query caching. N+1 queries need DataLoader-style batching. Unbounded queries need depth/complexity limits and rate limiting by cost, not request count. File uploads, error handling (200 with `errors`), and observability are all clunkier. Higher learning curve and more server infrastructure.

**Verdict:** default to REST (plus OpenAPI) for a new API with few clients, public/cacheable resources, or a small team. Choose GraphQL if you already have multiple frontends with divergent data needs, deeply relational data, or rapid UI iteration where backend changes are a bottleneck. You can also start with REST and add a GraphQL gateway later if the need appears.
