## large-s1 / apidesign

> Design the REST API for a multi-tenant document store: resources, auth model, pagination, versioning and error format. Explain the decisions.

### vanilla: 3550 answer tokens, 3550 billed, 148 lines

~~~
# Multi-Tenant Document Store REST API

## 1. Tenancy and resource model

**Decision: tenant is derived from the credential, not (only) the URL.** Every request is scoped to one tenant, resolved from the token. The URL still carries it for clarity and cacheability, and the server rejects mismatches.

```
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/...
```

Why in the URL at all: it makes logs, rate limits, and routing legible, and prevents "confused deputy" bugs where a client forgets which tenant it is acting on. Why verified against the token: a path parameter alone is user-controlled, and trusting it is the classic cross-tenant leak. Return `404` (not `403`) for resources in other tenants so existence is not disclosed.

### Resources

```
/v1/tenants/{t}
/v1/tenants/{t}/users                       members and roles
/v1/tenants/{t}/api-keys                    service credentials
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections                 logical grouping, schema, default ACL
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents   the documents
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}/versions
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}/versions/{n}
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}/content     blob/body for large payloads
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}/permissions
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents:search          (POST, custom method)
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents:batchGet        (POST)
/v1/tenants/{t}/usage                       quotas and metering
/v1/tenants/{t}/audit-events                read-only
```

Key choices:
- **Collections as a middle layer** give a unit for schema validation, retention policy, and default permissions, without nesting arbitrarily deep.
- **Document versions are a sub-resource**, immutable and addressed by integer. `GET .../documents/{d}` returns the latest; `GET .../versions/3` returns history. Restore is `POST .../versions/3:restore`, which creates a new version rather than rewriting history.
- **Metadata vs. content split.** The document resource holds JSON metadata; `/content` handles large or binary bodies with `Content-Type` passthrough, range requests, and (for big files) a pre-signed upload URL so bytes bypass the API tier.
- **IDs are opaque, server-generated (ULID/UUIDv7)**, so they are sortable and non-guessable. Tenant IDs are never sequential integers.
- **Verbs:** standard `GET/POST/PUT/PATCH/DELETE`. `PATCH` uses JSON Merge Patch (`application/merge-patch+json`). Non-CRUD actions use the `:action` suffix instead of inventing pseudo-resources.
- **Soft delete:** `DELETE` sets a tombstone, retained per collection policy. `POST :undelete` within the window; `DELETE ...?purge=true` requires a higher scope.

## 2. Auth model

**Authentication:** OAuth 2.0 / OIDC bearer tokens (JWT, short-lived, ~15 min) for humans via the tenant's IdP (SSO/SAML federation where needed). **API keys** for service-to-service, stored hashed, shown once, prefix-identifiable (`dk_live_ab12…`) so they can be found in leak scans, and individually revocable and rotatable.

Token claims include `tenant_id`, `sub`, `scopes`, and `exp`. Middleware compares `tenant_id` with the path and rejects on mismatch.

**Authorization: RBAC with resource-level ACLs.**
- Scopes at the coarse level: `documents:read`, `documents:write`, `collections:admin`, `tenant:admin`, `audit:read`.
- Roles map to scope bundles (viewer, editor, admin).
- Per-collection and per-document ACLs refine this (grant principal X read on document Y). Effective permission is the intersection of token scope and ACL.
- Default deny.

**Isolation defense in depth** (not an API-surface thing, but it shapes the design): tenant ID is mandatory in every storage key and query, enforced in a data-access layer or row-level security, not reliant on each handler remembering a `WHERE`. Consider per-tenant encryption keys (BYOK for enterprise).

**Operational:** per-tenant rate limits and quotas, with headers `RateLimit-Limit`, `RateLimit-Remaining`, `RateLimit-Reset`, and `Retry-After` on `429`. Cross-tenant admin (support staff) uses a separate audience and issuer, with every access written to the tenant's audit log.

## 3. Pagination

**Decision: cursor-based, not offset.** Documents are written concurrently, so offset pagination skips or duplicates items and gets slow at depth.

```
GET /v1/tenants/t1/collections/c1/documents?page_size=50&page_token=eyJ...
```
```json
{
  "data": [ ... ],
  "next_page_token": "eyJpZCI6...",
  "has_more": true
}
```

- `page_size` default 50, max 200; server clamps.
- The token is opaque (signed/encrypted, base64), encoding the sort key plus a filter hash. Clients can't forge or tamper with it, and the server can change its internals freely. Tokens are bound to the tenant and expire (e.g. 24h).
- Stable ordering: sort key plus ID as tiebreaker (`created_at, id`), so ties never cause skips.
- Filtering and sorting: `filter=` (a small documented grammar, e.g. `status:active AND created_at>2026-01-01`), `order_by=updated_at desc`. Changing filter or sort invalidates the token (`400`).
- No total count by default (expensive). Offer `?include_total=true` as an approximate count.
- Also emit `Link: <...>; rel="next"` for generic clients.

## 4. Versioning

Two distinct concepts:

**API versioning: major version in the URL path (`/v1/`).** Chosen over header versioning because it is visible, easy to route, curl-friendly, and cacheable. Within a major version, only **additive, backward-compatible** changes are allowed (new optional fields, new endpoints, new enum values; clients must ignore unknown fields). Breaking changes require `/v2`, with a published deprecation policy (e.g. 12 months), plus `Deprecation` and `Sunset` headers on old endpoints. For fine-grained opt-ins, an optional date header (`API-Version: 2026-11-01`) can gate individual behavior changes without a whole new major.

**Document versioning: optimistic concurrency.**
- Each document has a `version` integer and an `ETag`.
- `PUT/PATCH/DELETE` require `If-Match: "<etag>"`. Mismatch returns `412 Precondition Failed`; a missing header returns `428 Precondition Required`. This prevents lost updates in a store where many clients edit concurrently.
- `POST` creation supports an `Idempotency-Key` header (retained 24h) so retries after network failure don't create duplicates.
- `GET` supports `If-None-Match` → `304`.

## 5. Error format

**Decision: RFC 9457 Problem Details (`application/problem+json`)**, extended with a few fields. It is a standard, has client library support, and avoids inventing a bespoke envelope.

```json
{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/errors/version-conflict",
  "title": "Version conflict",
  "status": 412,
  "code": "DOCUMENT_VERSION_MISMATCH",
  "detail": "Document d_01J... is at version 7; request was based on version 5.",
  "instance": "/v1/tenants/t1/collections/c1/documents/d_01J...",
  "request_id": "req_8f3a...",
  "errors": [
    { "field": "metadata.title", "code": "TOO_LONG", "message": "Max 256 characters" }
  ]
}
```

- `code` is a stable, machine-readable string clients switch on; `title`/`detail` are for humans and may change.
- `errors[]` is used for validation failures so all problems are reported at once (`422` for semantic validation, `400` for malformed requests).
- `request_id` is also returned as a header (`X-Request-Id`) and logged, for support correlation.
- Never leak internals (stack traces, SQL, other tenants' existence).

**Status code usage:**

| Code | Use |
|---|---|
| 400 | Malformed request, bad page token |
| 401 | Missing/invalid/expired credential |
| 403 | Authenticated, but lacking scope (within own tenant) |
| 404 | Not found, **or** belongs to another tenant |
| 409 | State conflict (duplicate name, collection not empty) |
| 412 / 428 | ETag mismatch / missing `If-Match` |
| 413 | Payload too large |
| 415 | Unsupported content type |
| 422 | Schema validation failure |
| 429 | Rate limit or quota exceeded |
| 5xx | Server fault; `503` includes `Retry-After` |

## Example

```http
PATCH /v1/tenants/acme/collections/contracts/documents/d_01JABC
Authorization: Bearer eyJ...
If-Match: "7"
Idempotency-Key: 3b1f...
Content-Type: application/merge-patch+json

{"metadata": {"status": "signed"}}
```
→ `200 OK`, `ETag: "8"`, body with the updated document.

## Trade-offs worth flagging

- **Tenant in the path** adds verbosity but makes isolation auditable. If you prefer shorter URLs, use a subdomain (`acme.api.example.com`) and keep the same token check.
- **Cursor pagination** loses random page access ("jump to page 40"); this is acceptable for a document store and the right trade for consistency.
- **Immutable version history** costs storage; mitigate with per-collection retention and compaction policies.
- **Per-document ACLs** are powerful but expensive to evaluate on list endpoints. Filter in the query layer (or cap ACL granularity to collections initially) to avoid N+1 permission checks.
~~~

### caveman: 3037 answer tokens, 3079 billed, 159 lines

~~~
# Multi-tenant Document Store REST API

## 1. Tenancy model

Tenant = **organization**. Tenant ID in path, not header or body.

```
/v1/orgs/{org_id}/...
```

**Why:** Path scoping make isolation visible in logs, cache keys, rate limits, and authz checks. Header tenancy easy to forget or spoof. Cross-tenant ID guess return `404`, not `403`, so no existence leak.

## 2. Resources

```
/v1/orgs/{org}/collections                        GET, POST
/v1/orgs/{org}/collections/{coll}                 GET, PATCH, DELETE
/v1/orgs/{org}/collections/{coll}/documents       GET, POST
/v1/orgs/{org}/collections/{coll}/documents/{id}  GET, PUT, PATCH, DELETE
/v1/orgs/{org}/collections/{coll}/documents/{id}/versions        GET
/v1/orgs/{org}/collections/{coll}/documents/{id}/versions/{n}    GET
/v1/orgs/{org}/collections/{coll}/documents/{id}/content         GET, PUT  (blob/binary body)
/v1/orgs/{org}/collections/{coll}/documents:search               POST
/v1/orgs/{org}/members                            GET, POST
/v1/orgs/{org}/members/{user}                     GET, PATCH, DELETE
/v1/orgs/{org}/api-keys                           GET, POST
/v1/orgs/{org}/api-keys/{id}                      DELETE
/v1/orgs/{org}/audit-events                       GET (read-only)
```

Decisions:
- **Collection layer** group docs, carry schema, retention, and ACL defaults. Cheaper than per-doc config.
- **Metadata vs content split.** JSON metadata small and cacheable. Large bodies go via `/content` with streaming, `Content-Type` passthrough, optionally presigned upload URL.
- **Search as `POST documents:search`.** Complex filters overflow URL length. Safe and idempotent, so document it as such.
- **Document versions** are read-only sub-resource. Every write make new immutable version. Restore = `POST .../versions/{n}:restore`.
- Soft delete by default (`DELETE` sets `deleted_at`, recoverable for retention window). `?permanent=true` needs admin scope.

## 3. Auth model

**AuthN**
- Humans: OAuth2/OIDC, short-lived JWT access token (≤15 min) plus refresh token.
- Machines: org-scoped API keys, prefixed (`sk_live_…`), hashed at rest, shown once, rotatable, optional expiry.
- All via `Authorization: Bearer <token>`. TLS only.

**AuthZ**
- RBAC at org level: `owner`, `admin`, `editor`, `viewer`.
- Scopes on tokens and keys: `docs:read`, `docs:write`, `docs:delete`, `members:manage`, `keys:manage`.
- Optional per-collection ACL overriding org role.
- Effective permission = role ∩ token scope ∩ collection ACL.

**Isolation enforcement**
- Token carries `org_id` claim (or key bound to one org). Gateway reject if path `{org}` ≠ claim. Users in many orgs get one token per org via token exchange.
- Defence in depth: DB row-level security on `org_id`, every query filtered by tenant. Never trust path alone.
- Per-tenant rate limits and quotas (storage, req/s). Return `429` with `Retry-After`.

## 4. Pagination, filtering, sorting

**Cursor-based**, not offset.

```
GET /v1/orgs/acme/collections/contracts/documents?limit=50&cursor=eyJ...
```
```json
{
  "data": [ ... ],
  "page": { "next_cursor": "eyJ...", "has_more": true }
}
```

- `limit` default 25, max 100.
- Cursor opaque, base64 of (sort key, id), signed so clients can't tamper.
- **Why:** Offset slow on deep pages and skip/duplicate rows under concurrent writes. Cursor stable and index-friendly.
- No total count by default (expensive). `?include=total_count` opt-in, can be approximate.
- Filtering: `?filter[status]=active&filter[created_after]=2026-01-01`. Sort: `?sort=-updated_at`. Whitelist sortable fields, each backed by index.
- Sparse fields: `?fields=id,title,updated_at`.

## 5. Versioning

Two layers:

**API version:** major in URL path (`/v1`).
- **Why:** Visible, cacheable, easy to route and sunset. Only bump for breaking changes. Additive changes (new fields, endpoints, enum values) ship in-place; clients must ignore unknown fields.
- Deprecation: `Deprecation` and `Sunset` headers, plus changelog, minimum 12 months overlap.

**Document version:** optimistic concurrency with ETag.
```
GET  → ETag: "v7"
PUT  → If-Match: "v7"
```
- Mismatch → `412 Precondition Failed`. Missing `If-Match` on `PUT`/`PATCH`/`DELETE` → `428 Precondition Required`.
- **Why:** Lost updates are the main correctness risk in a shared document store. ETag standard, works with HTTP caches.

## 6. Writes, idempotency, async

- `POST` creates accept `Idempotency-Key` header. Server store key + response 24h. Safe retries.
- `PUT` full replace. `PATCH` use JSON Merge Patch (`application/merge-patch+json`); JSON Patch for array edits if needed.
- `POST` create return `201` + `Location` header.
- Slow ops (bulk import, reindex, export) return `202` + `/v1/orgs/{org}/operations/{id}`, pollable, optional webhook.
- Bulk: `POST documents:batch`, max 100 items, per-item status in `207`-style body.

## 7. Error format

RFC 9457 `application/problem+json`:

```json
{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/errors/version-conflict",
  "title": "Version conflict",
  "status": 412,
  "detail": "Document is at version 8, request was based on version 7.",
  "instance": "/v1/orgs/acme/collections/contracts/documents/doc_91",
  "code": "version_conflict",
  "request_id": "req_8f2c1a",
  "errors": [
    { "pointer": "/title", "code": "too_long", "message": "Max 200 chars" }
  ]
}
```

- `code` stable machine string; `title`/`detail` human text, may change.
- `errors[]` for field-level validation, with JSON Pointer.
- `request_id` also in `X-Request-Id` response header, for support.
- Never leak stack traces or other tenants' info.

Status map:

| Code | Use |
|------|-----|
| 400 | Malformed request |
| 401 | Missing/invalid credentials |
| 403 | Authenticated, lacks permission (within own tenant) |
| 404 | Not found, **or** belongs to other tenant |
| 409 | Duplicate/state conflict |
| 412 / 428 | ETag mismatch / missing |
| 413 | Body too large |
| 422 | Semantic validation failed |
| 429 | Rate/quota exceeded |
| 5xx | Server; retryable with backoff |

## 8. Cross-cutting

- JSON `snake_case`, RFC 3339 UTC timestamps, prefixed opaque IDs (`doc_…`, `col_…`), ULID/UUIDv7 inside for sortability.
- `X-RateLimit-Limit/Remaining/Reset` headers.
- Audit every write with actor, org, request_id.
- Publish OpenAPI 3.1 spec as source of truth; generate SDKs.
- Webhooks (`document.created/updated/deleted`) signed with HMAC, per-org secret.

## Key tradeoffs

| Choice | Alternative | Why this |
|--------|-------------|----------|
| Org in path | Subdomain / header | Simple routing, explicit, easy authz check |
| 404 for cross-tenant | 403 | No ID enumeration |
| Cursor pagination | Offset | Stable, scales |
| URL major version | Header/media-type | Discoverable, simple ops |
| ETag concurrency | Last-write-wins | Prevent silent data loss |
| Problem+JSON | Custom envelope | Standard, tooling support |

Open questions that change design: need per-doc ACLs (not just collection)? Need full-text search (then separate search service and eventual consistency note)? Need data residency per tenant (then region in org config, region-specific base URL)?
~~~

### ponytail: 2191 answer tokens, 2787 billed, 99 lines

~~~
# Multi-tenant document store: REST API

## Resources

```
/v1/collections                     GET, POST
/v1/collections/{cid}               GET, PATCH, DELETE
/v1/documents                       GET (filter: collection, tag, q, updated_since), POST
/v1/documents/{did}                 GET, PATCH (JSON Merge Patch), DELETE
/v1/documents/{did}/content         GET, PUT            # raw bytes, Content-Type preserved
/v1/documents/{did}/revisions       GET                 # immutable history
/v1/documents/{did}/revisions/{n}   GET
/v1/documents/{did}/revisions/{n}:restore   POST
/v1/documents/{did}/shares          GET, POST           # per-document ACL entries
/v1/members                         GET, POST, PATCH, DELETE
/v1/api-keys                        GET, POST, DELETE
```

- **Metadata and content are separate.** `/documents/{did}` is small JSON (title, tags, size, `etag`, timestamps). `/content` carries the bytes. Listings stay cheap, and large uploads can later redirect to a presigned URL without changing the model.
- **Revisions are read-only.** Every successful content write creates one. Restoring creates a new revision rather than rewriting history.
- **Documents are a top-level collection**, not nested under collections. A document's collection is an attribute, so moving it is a PATCH rather than a URL change.
- **Custom verbs use `:restore`** instead of pretending an action is a CRUD resource.

## Tenancy and auth

- **Tenant comes from the credential, not the URL.** The token or API key is bound to one tenant, and every query is scoped by it server-side. There is no `/tenants/{id}/…` for a client to tamper with, which removes the classic IDOR class. Enforce it again at the storage layer (a `tenant_id` column with row-level security, or per-tenant key prefixes).
- **Users** authenticate with OIDC/OAuth2 and send a bearer JWT. It carries `sub`, `tenant`, and scopes. A user in several tenants gets a token per tenant via the token exchange, so a leaked token never spans tenants.
- **Services** use API keys (`Authorization: Bearer sk_…`). They are tenant-bound, scoped (`documents:read`, `documents:write`, `admin`), stored hashed, and shown only once at creation.
- **Authorization** combines tenant roles (`viewer`, `editor`, `admin`) with per-document shares that can only grant access, never exceed the token's scopes.
- **Cross-tenant and unauthorized lookups return `404`, not `403`**, so IDs don't leak existence. Use `403` only when the caller can already see the resource but lacks the permission.
- **Per-tenant rate limits and quotas** return `429` with `Retry-After`. This stops one noisy tenant from affecting the others.
- **Platform-operator endpoints** (tenant provisioning) live on a separate `/admin` host with separate credentials, never mixed into this API.

## Pagination

Cursor-based:

```
GET /v1/documents?limit=50&cursor=eyJ0IjoiLi4uIn0&sort=-updated_at
→ { "data": [...], "next_cursor": "eyJ...", "has_more": true }
```

- Offset pagination skips or duplicates rows under concurrent writes and gets slow on deep pages. Cursors don't.
- The cursor is opaque (encoded sort key plus ID as a tiebreaker), so the internals can change.
- `limit` defaults to 50 with a max of 100. There's no total count by default because it's expensive. Offer `?include=total` if clients really need it.
- Filters and sort are fixed per cursor, and a mismatch returns `400`.

## Versioning

- **Major version in the path (`/v1`).** It's visible in logs, easy to route, and easy to curl.
- **Additive changes are not breaking.** New fields, endpoints, and optional params ship within v1, and clients must ignore unknown fields. Breaking changes mean `/v2`, with v1 kept for a published window.
- **Deprecation is signaled by headers** (`Deprecation`, `Sunset`, and a `Link` to the migration guide) rather than silent removal.
- **Don't confuse this with document revisions.** API version is the contract. Revision is data.
- **Concurrency control uses ETags.** `GET` returns `ETag`. `PATCH`, `PUT`, and `DELETE` accept `If-Match`, and a stale value returns `412`. Requiring `If-Match` on content `PUT` (`428` if missing) prevents lost updates.
- **Idempotency:** `POST` accepts `Idempotency-Key` so retries after timeouts don't create duplicates.

## Error format

RFC 9457 `application/problem+json`:

```json
{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/errors/validation-failed",
  "title": "Validation failed",
  "status": 422,
  "code": "validation_failed",
  "detail": "2 fields are invalid.",
  "request_id": "req_8f3a1c",
  "errors": [
    { "field": "title", "code": "too_long", "message": "Max 256 characters." }
  ]
}
```

- It's a standard, so client libraries and gateways already understand it.
- `code` is a stable machine-readable string that clients branch on. `title` and `detail` are for humans and may change.
- `request_id` matches the `X-Request-Id` response header, so support can trace any failure.
- **Status codes:**

| Status | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `400` | malformed request |
| `401` | missing or invalid credentials |
| `403` | permission denied |
| `404` | not found, or in another tenant |
| `409` | conflict (e.g. duplicate name) |
| `412` / `428` | ETag mismatch / missing `If-Match` |
| `413` | content too large |
| `415` | unsupported content type |
| `422` | semantic validation failure |
| `429` | rate limit or quota exceeded |

- Error messages never echo other tenants' data or reveal whether a resource exists elsewhere.

## Skipped (add when needed)

- Webhooks or event feeds: add when clients need change notifications. Until then, `updated_since` polling works.
- Bulk endpoints and batch operations.
- Full-text search as its own resource: the `q` param on `/documents` covers it for now.
~~~

### chisle: 1883 answer tokens, 3108 billed, 94 lines

~~~
## Resources

Tenant is derived from the credential, never from the URL. A path like `/tenants/{id}/...` invites cross-tenant IDOR bugs.

```
# data plane (tenant-scoped)
GET/POST          /v1/collections
GET/PATCH/DELETE  /v1/collections/{cid}
GET/POST          /v1/collections/{cid}/documents
GET/PUT/PATCH/DELETE /v1/collections/{cid}/documents/{did}        # metadata + JSON body
GET/PUT           /v1/collections/{cid}/documents/{did}/content   # blob; large uploads via presigned URL
GET               /v1/collections/{cid}/documents/{did}/revisions
GET               /v1/collections/{cid}/documents/{did}/revisions/{rev}
POST              /v1/collections/{cid}/documents/{did}/revisions/{rev}:restore
POST              /v1/search                                      # query in body, not URL
GET/POST/DELETE   /v1/api-keys, /v1/members, /v1/webhooks

# control plane (platform operators only, separate audience)
/admin/v1/tenants, /admin/v1/tenants/{tid}/quotas
```

- Document history is called `revisions`, not `versions`, to avoid clashing with API versioning.
- Content is split from metadata so listing stays cheap and blobs can bypass the API tier.
- `:restore` is a custom action because restore is not CRUD. It creates a new revision and never rewrites history.
- Search uses POST because queries are large and structured, and this keeps them out of access logs.

## Auth

- **Users:** OIDC/OAuth2 bearer JWTs, short-lived, with `tid` (tenant), `sub` and `scope` claims.
- **Services:** API keys, scoped, stored hashed, with a visible prefix for identification and revocation. They exchange for the same token shape (client-credentials) or are sent directly as `Authorization: Bearer`.
- **Authorization:** RBAC per tenant plus optional per-collection ACLs. Scopes look like `documents:read` and `collections:write`.
- **Isolation:** the `tid` claim is injected into every query, enforced below the handler layer (Postgres RLS or tenant-prefixed keys). Handlers can't forget it.
- **Existence leaks:** cross-tenant or unauthorized resources return `404`, not `403`.
- **Admin separation:** the platform-admin API uses a different audience and issuer, so a tenant token can never reach it.
- **Rate limits and quotas:** per tenant, so one noisy tenant can't starve the rest. Responses carry `RateLimit-*` headers and `429` includes `Retry-After`.

## Concurrency and retries

- Documents return an `ETag`. `PUT`/`PATCH`/`DELETE` require `If-Match` → `412` on mismatch, `428` if the header is missing. This prevents lost updates.
- `POST` accepts an `Idempotency-Key`, so client retries don't create duplicates.

## Pagination

Cursor-based:

```
GET /v1/collections/c1/documents?limit=50&cursor=eyJ...&sort=-updated_at&filter[status]=active
→ { "data": [...], "page": { "next_cursor": "eyJ...", "has_more": true } }
```

- The cursor is opaque, signed, and bound to tenant, sort and filters, so it can't be forged or replayed elsewhere.
- Offset pagination is rejected: pages drift under concurrent writes, and deep offsets scan and discard rows.
- Default limit is 50, max 200. Sort fields are an allowlist, with a unique tiebreaker (`id`) appended.
- No total count by default (`?include=total` opts in), because counts are expensive on large tenants.

## Versioning

- Major version in the URL path (`/v1`). It's visible, cacheable, and easy to route and log.
- Additive changes (new fields, endpoints, optional params) don't bump the version, so clients must ignore unknown fields.
- Breaking changes go to `/v2`, with `/v1` kept running alongside and announced via `Deprecation` and `Sunset` headers.
- Don't use header or media-type versioning. It's hard to debug and test with curl.

## Errors

RFC 9457 `application/problem+json`, with a stable machine code and a request ID:

```json
{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/problems/validation-error",
  "title": "Validation failed",
  "status": 422,
  "code": "validation_error",
  "detail": "2 fields invalid",
  "request_id": "req_8f2c1a",
  "errors": [
    {"pointer": "/title", "code": "required"},
    {"pointer": "/tags/3", "code": "too_long", "detail": "max 64 chars"}
  ]
}
```

| Status | Use |
|---|---|
| 400 | malformed request |
| 401 | missing or invalid credential |
| 403 | authenticated but lacks scope (only when the resource's existence isn't secret) |
| 404 | missing, or not visible to the tenant |
| 409 | state conflict (duplicate name) |
| 412 / 428 | ETag mismatch / `If-Match` missing |
| 413 | body too large (point to the upload URL flow) |
| 422 | semantic validation |
| 429 | rate or quota exceeded |

Clients branch on `code`, never on `title` or `detail`, which are free to change. Never leak internals or other tenants' data in `detail`.
~~~

## large-s1 / architecture

> Explain how you would migrate a monolith to services without a big-bang rewrite, covering ordering, data ownership, and how to keep it shippable throughout.

### vanilla: 2170 answer tokens, 2170 billed, 75 lines

~~~
# Migrating a monolith to services incrementally

The core idea is the strangler fig pattern: grow new services around the monolith, route traffic to them one slice at a time, and let the monolith shrink until it's small enough to keep or delete. At every step, production works and you can roll back.

## 0. Prerequisites (do these first)

- **Make the monolith modular internally.** Enforce module boundaries inside the codebase (package visibility rules, dependency-lint, architecture tests). If you can't draw clean seams in-process, you will draw bad ones across the network.
- **Observability and CI/CD.** Tracing, structured logs, per-endpoint metrics, and a fast, reliable deploy pipeline. You need a baseline to prove a migrated slice behaves the same.
- **A routing layer.** An API gateway or reverse proxy in front of the monolith, so traffic can be redirected per route, per tenant, or by percentage without client changes.
- **Decide why.** Pick the real driver (independent deploys, scaling hotspot, team autonomy, tech change). It determines what to extract first and when to stop.

## 1. Ordering: what to extract and when

Choose candidates by value versus coupling:

1. **Start with a low-risk, loosely coupled edge capability** (notifications, document generation, search, image processing). It proves the platform: pipelines, auth between services, tracing, on-call. Mistakes here are cheap.
2. **Then extract what the driver demands**: the scaling hotspot, the component that changes most often, or the one blocking a team.
3. **Leave the tangled core (often billing/orders/user identity) for last**, and only if it's justified. Some of it may stay in the monolith permanently, and that's a fine outcome.
4. **Prefer vertical slices** (a business capability with its logic and data) over technical layers. Extracting "the database layer" or "the UI" yields a distributed monolith.

Use change-frequency analysis from git history and the dependency graph to find seams. Components that change together belong together.

## 2. Per-service extraction procedure

Repeat this loop for each slice:

1. **Define the interface** in the monolith first: a module API that all callers use instead of reaching into internals.
2. **Build the service behind that interface**, initially with the monolith's code or a thin adapter.
3. **Branch by abstraction / feature flag**: the interface can call the in-process implementation or the remote service, switchable at runtime.
4. **Shadow/dark launch**: send copies of real traffic to the new service, compare responses (parallel run), and don't serve its results yet.
5. **Canary**: shift 1% → 10% → 50% → 100% at the gateway, watching error rate, latency, and correctness diffs.
6. **Keep the fallback** for a defined window, then delete the old in-monolith code. Deleting is part of the migration; otherwise you get two implementations forever.

## 3. Data ownership

Data is the hard part. The rule: **each table has exactly one owning service, and others access it only through that service's API or events.**

Stepwise approach:

1. **Map table ownership** within the shared database. Find cross-module joins and foreign keys, which are the real coupling.
2. **Separate schemas first, same database.** Give the future service its own schema and restrict access by DB permissions. This is cheap and reveals hidden dependencies.
3. **Replace cross-boundary joins** with API calls, or with locally held read models populated from events (denormalize what you need: store the customer name in the order rather than joining to customers).
4. **Move data to the service's own database** only when the service is stable. Common sequence:
   - Dual-write or, better, change-data-capture (CDC/outbox) to replicate into the new store.
   - Backfill, then verify with reconciliation jobs.
   - Switch reads to the new store, then writes (the service becomes the source of truth).
   - Stop replication, revoke the monolith's access, drop old tables later.
5. **Accept the consequences**: no cross-service transactions. Use sagas or compensating actions for multi-step workflows, idempotent consumers, and the transactional outbox pattern so state change and event publication are atomic. Decide per case where eventual consistency is acceptable; keep strongly consistent invariants inside one service.
6. **Reference data and shared IDs**: use stable identifiers, never expose internal DB keys, and avoid shared "common" tables.

## 4. Keeping it shippable throughout

- **Trunk-based development, small increments.** No long-lived migration branch. Every change merges behind a flag and deploys.
- **Expand/contract schema changes** (add new, dual-write, migrate, remove old) so any version of the code works against the schema during rollout.
- **Backward-compatible contracts.** Version APIs and events; use consumer-driven contract tests so a service can't break callers silently.
- **Feature flags and gateway routing as the rollback mechanism.** Rolling back is a config change, not a deploy.
- **Automated end-to-end and contract tests in CI,** plus characterization tests around legacy behavior before extracting it, so you capture what the code actually does, quirks included.
- **Keep feature work flowing.** Teams continue delivering product features; migration work is interleaved, and new features go into new services (or the module being extracted), never deeper into the legacy tangle.
- **Limit work in progress.** One or two extractions at a time. Each should finish (including deleting the old code) before the next starts.

## 5. Common pitfalls

- **Distributed monolith**: services that must deploy together or share a database. Check by asking whether one can be released independently today.
- **Chatty synchronous call chains** that multiply latency and failure. Prefer coarser APIs, caching, or events.
- **Skipping the platform investment** (service templates, shared auth, tracing), which leaves every team reinventing it.
- **Too many too-small services** (nanoservices). Size by team ownership and business capability.
- **No stopping rule.** Define done: when the original driver is satisfied, stop. The remaining monolith can be a perfectly good "core service."

## Summary sequence

1. Modularize in place and build the gateway and observability.
2. Extract an easy edge service to prove the platform.
3. Extract slices that serve the actual driver, each via interface → flag → shadow → canary → delete.
4. Split data progressively: ownership map → separate schemas → events/CDC → separate database.
5. Merge small, flag everything, keep rollback a config flip, and stop when the goal is met.
~~~

### caveman: 1477 answer tokens, 1536 billed, 59 lines

~~~
## Core approach: strangler fig

Never rewrite. Grow services around the monolith, route traffic piece by piece, shrink the monolith until it's gone or small enough to leave alone.

## Step 0: Prep inside the monolith

- **Modularize first.** Carve modules with enforced boundaries (modular monolith). Cheap, reversible, ships daily.
- **Find seams.** Use dependency graph, change-frequency from git history, and team ownership. Seam = few inbound calls, clear domain.
- **Add facade/gateway** in front. All traffic goes through it so routing can flip per route or per tenant.
- **Build baseline:** CI/CD, observability (tracing, structured logs), feature flags. Without these, services = pain.

## Ordering

Pick by value and risk, not by what's interesting.

1. **First: easy, low-risk, leaf-like.** Few dependencies, little shared data (notifications, email, PDF generation, search, auth edge). Goal is to build the platform muscle: deploy pipeline, service template, tracing, on-call.
2. **Next: high-pain seams.** Parts that need independent scaling, have different release cadence, or are the team bottleneck.
3. **Last: the tangled core.** Heavily shared tables, transactional hot paths (orders/billing/ledger). May stay in the monolith. Fine.
4. **Don't extract** things that change together. If two modules always change in the same PR, they're one service.

## Data ownership

This is the hard part. Code is easy to move; data is not.

- **One owner per table.** Owner service alone writes. Others read via API or events, never direct SQL.
- **Sequence per extraction:**
  1. Inside monolith, route all access to that data through one module/interface.
  2. Stand up new service, still reading/writing the **shared DB**. Verify behavior.
  3. Move tables to service-owned schema, then to separate DB. Use **dual-write or CDC** (e.g. Debezium) to sync during transition.
  4. **Shadow-read / compare** old vs new, fix diffs.
  5. Cut writes over, keep old path as rollback for a window, then delete.
- **Break cross-service joins:** replace with API calls, replicated read models (events), or accept denormalized copies with clear source of truth.
- **No distributed transactions.** Use sagas / outbox pattern + idempotent consumers. Outbox gives atomic "write + publish."
- **Foreign keys across boundaries die.** Replace with ID references + eventual consistency + reconciliation jobs.
- **Expect to drop some consistency guarantees.** Decide explicitly with product, don't discover in prod.

## Keeping it shippable

- **Every step is a deployable, reversible change.** Small PRs, no long-lived branches.
- **Feature flags / gateway routing** per route, tenant, or percentage. Rollback = flip flag.
- **Expand/contract migrations:** add new, dual-run, switch, remove old. Never rename-in-place.
- **Parallel run / shadow traffic:** send copy of requests to new service, compare responses, don't serve them yet.
- **Canary then ramp:** 1% → 10% → 50% → 100%, with SLO-based auto-rollback.
- **Contract tests** (e.g. Pact) between monolith and service so either side can deploy independently.
- **Anti-corruption layer** at the boundary so the monolith's messy model doesn't leak into new service.
- **Keep feature work flowing.** New features go in the new service if in that domain, otherwise monolith. Don't freeze product.
- **Delete aggressively.** After cutover bakes, remove dead monolith code. Otherwise you run two systems forever.

## Failure modes to avoid

- **Distributed monolith:** services share a DB or must deploy together. Worse than before.
- **Too fine-grained** too early. Start coarse, split later.
- **No platform investment:** every team reinvents deploy/observability.
- **Chatty sync calls** creating latency chains. Prefer coarse APIs and async events.
- **No exit criteria.** Define "done" per extraction (traffic 100%, old code deleted, data owned) and for the whole program. Stopping at "good enough" is valid.

## Sanity check per extraction

Ask: independent deploy? Own data? Own on-call owner? Rollback in minutes? If any "no", not done.
~~~

# Migrating a monolith to services without a big-bang rewrite

**Core approach:** strangler fig. Build new behavior beside the monolith, route traffic to it gradually, and delete the old code once it's dead. The monolith stays deployable the whole time.

## 0. Before extracting anything
- **Ask whether you need services at all.** A modular monolith, with enforced module boundaries inside one deployable, often delivers 80% of the benefit. Extract only for a concrete reason: independent scaling, a team that is blocked by shared releases, a different runtime need, or a fault-isolation requirement.
- **Get the prerequisites in place:** CI/CD that deploys in minutes, centralized logging, tracing, and metrics, and a feature-flag system. Without observability you can't tell whether a cutover broke anything.
- **Add characterization tests** around the seam you plan to cut. They pin current behavior so you can prove parity later.

## 1. Ordering: what to extract first
1. **Modularize inside the monolith first.** Find seams (domain boundaries, low-coupling clusters) and put an in-process interface in front of each. Enforce it with lint or dependency rules so nothing reaches around it.
2. **Pick an easy, valuable first candidate.** Good choices are leaf-like capabilities with few inbound dependencies and clear ownership: notifications, search, PDF or report generation, auth or identity, or media processing. Avoid the tangled core (orders, billing) first. Use the early extractions to build the platform muscle (deploy pipeline, service template, tracing).
3. **Then move toward the core,** ordered by pain: the module that most blocks deploys or most needs independent scaling goes next.
4. **Leave the last tangled piece alone if it's cheap to keep.** A smaller monolith that is just "the core service" is a fine end state.

## 2. The mechanics of one extraction
1. **Define the contract** (API or events) at the existing in-process seam.
2. **Build the service** and run it in shadow mode: the monolith still serves the request, but also sends a copy to the new service. Compare the responses and log diffs.
3. **Route behind a flag** or at the proxy/gateway, starting at 1% of traffic, then ramping up. Rollback is flipping the flag.
4. **Delete the old module** only after a soak period at 100%. Skipping this step is how you end up with two implementations that diverge.

Put an **anti-corruption layer** between the new service and the monolith's model so the old schema's quirks don't leak into the new design.

## 3. Data ownership (the hard part)
Rule: **each table has exactly one owning service, and nobody else reads or writes it directly.** Getting there is a sequence of safe steps:

1. **Find the cross-module coupling:** joins, shared tables, foreign keys, and transactions that span boundaries.
2. **Cut access first, data second.** Make other modules call the owner through its interface, still in-process and still against one database. Then you can move the data without changing behavior.
3. **Separate the schema,** then the database. Start with a separate schema in the same DB and move to a separate instance once access is clean.
4. **Migrate data with dual-write or CDC:**
   - Backfill the new store.
   - Keep it in sync with change-data-capture from the old DB (preferred) or dual-writes behind a flag.
   - Verify with reconciliation jobs.
   - Flip reads, then flip writes, then stop syncing.
5. **Replace joins and FKs with:**
   - API calls for current data.
   - Replicated read models fed by events for data you need locally, accepting that they are eventually consistent.
   - Stored IDs only (no cross-service FKs).
6. **Replace cross-boundary transactions** with sagas or the outbox pattern (write the event in the same local transaction as the state change, then publish it). Try first to redraw the boundary so the transaction doesn't cross it at all. If two things must be atomic, they probably belong in one service.

## 4. Keeping it shippable throughout
- **Trunk-based development.** No long-lived migration branch. Every step merges to main behind a flag and ships.
- **Each step is independently reversible:** flag-based routing, expand/contract schema changes (add new, dual-write, migrate, remove old), and backward-compatible API changes with versioning.
- **Keep one deployable truth.** Don't freeze feature work. New features go in the new service if they belong to an extracted domain, otherwise in the monolith, and teams never build in code that's scheduled for deletion.
- **Contract tests** (consumer-driven) between the monolith and each service catch breakage before deploy.
- **Guardrails for the distributed-systems tax:** timeouts, retries with backoff and idempotency keys, circuit breakers, and per-service SLOs. Each new network hop introduces failure modes the in-process call never had.
- **Track progress with measurable outcomes:** share of traffic served by new services, number of cross-boundary table accesses (should trend to zero), deploy frequency, and lead time. Stop when the original reason for migrating is met.

## Common failure modes
- **Distributed monolith:** services that must deploy together or share a DB. This is worse than what you started with.
- **Splitting by technical layer** (a "database service," a "validation service") instead of by business capability.
- **Extracting the data last.** If the DB is still shared, you haven't really extracted anything.
- **No deletion step,** leaving two versions of the logic indefinitely.
~~~

### chisle: 1390 answer tokens, 1438 billed, 36 lines

~~~
Strangler fig: new services grow around the monolith, traffic shifts one slice at a time, monolith shrinks until it's deleted or small enough to keep. Every step is a normal deploy and reversible.

**Prep first, in the monolith.** Make it a modular monolith: enforce module boundaries (no cross-module table access, calls through interfaces), add tests around the seams, and get CI/CD, observability (tracing, per-endpoint metrics) and feature flags working. Putting a routing layer (gateway/proxy) in front lets you redirect per-route later. Skipping this → you distribute a ball of mud.

**Ordering.** Pick slices by value/risk, not by layer:
- First: something low-risk, loosely coupled, with a clear boundary (notifications, search, auth/profile, reporting). Goal is to prove the pipeline: deploy, routing, monitoring, rollback.
- Then: parts with real pain (different scaling profile, high change rate, team bottleneck). Extract along business capabilities (billing, catalog), not technical layers.
- Last: the core, highly-coupled domain (orders, ledger). Often stays as the "remaining monolith", which is fine.
- Dependency rule: extract leaves of the call graph first; avoid extracting something whose callers you'd have to rewrite all at once.

**Per-slice recipe.**
1. Define the contract (API/events) inside the monolith; route monolith callers through it.
2. Build the service behind it, reimplementing or lifting the code.
3. Shadow/dark-launch: mirror traffic, diff responses, no user impact.
4. Canary via gateway/flag: 1% → 10% → 100%; rollback = flip flag.
5. Delete the old code path only after a soak period. Skipping deletion → permanent duplication.

**Data ownership.** The hard part. Rule: each table has exactly one owning service; others get data via API or events, never direct reads. Sequence:
1. Within the monolith, stop cross-module joins and shared-table writes; replace with calls.
2. Service initially reads/writes the monolith DB (same schema, owned logically by it). Lowest risk, gets code out first.
3. Split the schema: move tables to the service's own DB. Migrate with expand/contract: dual-write or CDC (e.g. Debezium) to the new store, backfill, verify with reconciliation, flip reads, then flip writes, then drop old.
4. Replace joins with API composition, or local read-replicas fed by events (accept eventual consistency). Cross-service transactions → sagas/outbox pattern, not distributed 2PC. Use the transactional outbox so DB write + event publish are atomic.
5. Shared reference data (users, tenants): one owner, others cache by ID plus events, never copy-and-diverge.

Foreign keys across the boundary die; design for that (IDs only, tolerate dangling refs, idempotent consumers).

**Staying shippable.**
- Trunk-based, small PRs; monolith keeps releasing throughout, no long-lived migration branch.
- Every cutover behind a flag with a tested rollback; keep the old path alive until the new one has soaked.
- Contract tests (consumer-driven) between monolith and services so independent deploys don't break each other.
- Backward-compatible changes only (expand/contract for schemas and APIs); versioned events.
- SLO-based gates: automatic rollback on error-rate/latency regression; compare against the monolith baseline.
- Anti-corruption layer where the new service must talk to legacy models, so legacy shapes don't leak into the new design.
- Limit work in progress: one or two extractions at a time; each must reach "old code deleted" before starting the next.

**Gotchas.** Distributed monolith (services that deploy together or share a DB) is worse than the monolith. Network calls add latency and failure modes: timeouts, retries with idempotency keys, circuit breakers from day one. Operational cost (tracing, per-service pipelines, on-call) is real; stop extracting when the remaining pain no longer justifies it. Measure the goal (deploy frequency, lead time, scaling cost) so you know when you're done.
~~~

## large-s1 / authflow

> Implement email-and-password signup and login with password hashing, session issuing, and rate limiting on failed attempts. Show the code.

### vanilla: 3488 answer tokens, 3831 billed, 196 lines

~~~
Here is a single-file implementation using Node, Express, SQLite (`better-sqlite3`), and Argon2id. I haven't run it.

```bash
npm i express better-sqlite3 argon2 cookie-parser
```

```js
// auth.js
const express = require("express");
const cookieParser = require("cookie-parser");
const Database = require("better-sqlite3");
const argon2 = require("argon2");
const crypto = require("crypto");

const db = new Database("auth.db");
db.exec(`
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  password_hash TEXT NOT NULL,
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sessions (
  token_hash TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  expires_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS login_attempts (
  key TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  failures INTEGER NOT NULL,
  window_start INTEGER NOT NULL,
  locked_until INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0
);
`);

// ---- config ----
const SESSION_TTL_MS = 7 * 24 * 3600 * 1000;
const MAX_FAILURES = 5;               // per key per window
const WINDOW_MS = 15 * 60 * 1000;
const BASE_LOCK_MS = 15 * 60 * 1000;
const ARGON_OPTS = { type: argon2.argon2id, memoryCost: 19456, timeCost: 2, parallelism: 1 };

// Verified against when the email doesn't exist, to equalize timing.
let DUMMY_HASH;
argon2.hash("dummy-password", ARGON_OPTS).then(h => (DUMMY_HASH = h));

// ---- helpers ----
const sha256 = s => crypto.createHash("sha256").update(s).digest("hex");
const normEmail = e => String(e || "").trim().toLowerCase();
const validEmail = e => e.length <= 254 && /^[^\s@]+@[^\s@]+\.[^\s@]+$/.test(e);
const validPassword = p => typeof p === "string" && p.length >= 12 && p.length <= 128;

function issueSession(res, userId) {
  const token = crypto.randomBytes(32).toString("base64url");
  db.prepare("INSERT INTO sessions VALUES (?,?,?)")
    .run(sha256(token), userId, Date.now() + SESSION_TTL_MS); // store only the hash
  res.cookie("sid", token, {
    httpOnly: true,
    secure: process.env.NODE_ENV === "production",
    sameSite: "lax",
    maxAge: SESSION_TTL_MS,
    path: "/",
  });
}

// ---- rate limiting (keyed by email+IP and by IP alone) ----
function limitKeys(email, ip) {
  return [`ip:${ip}`, `acct:${sha256(email)}:${ip}`];
}
function lockedFor(keys) {
  const now = Date.now();
  let wait = 0;
  for (const k of keys) {
    const r = db.prepare("SELECT locked_until FROM login_attempts WHERE key=?").get(k);
    if (r && r.locked_until > now) wait = Math.max(wait, r.locked_until - now);
  }
  return wait;
}
function recordFailure(keys) {
  const now = Date.now();
  for (const k of keys) {
    const r = db.prepare("SELECT * FROM login_attempts WHERE key=?").get(k);
    let failures = 1, start = now, lock = 0;
    if (r && now - r.window_start < WINDOW_MS) { failures = r.failures + 1; start = r.window_start; }
    if (failures >= MAX_FAILURES) {
      // exponential backoff: 15m, 30m, 60m ... capped at 24h
      const over = failures - MAX_FAILURES;
      lock = now + Math.min(BASE_LOCK_MS * 2 ** over, 24 * 3600 * 1000);
    }
    db.prepare(`INSERT INTO login_attempts (key,failures,window_start,locked_until) VALUES (?,?,?,?)
                ON CONFLICT(key) DO UPDATE SET failures=?, window_start=?, locked_until=?`)
      .run(k, failures, start, lock, failures, start, lock);
  }
}
function clearFailures(keys) {
  // Only clear the per-account key; the IP key keeps counting so one valid
  // login can't be used to reset an attacker's budget.
  db.prepare("DELETE FROM login_attempts WHERE key=?").run(keys[1]);
}

// ---- app ----
const app = express();
app.set("trust proxy", false); // set appropriately if behind a reverse proxy
app.use(express.json({ limit: "10kb" }));
app.use(cookieParser());

app.post("/signup", async (req, res) => {
  const email = normEmail(req.body.email);
  const { password } = req.body;
  if (!validEmail(email)) return res.status(400).json({ error: "Invalid email" });
  if (!validPassword(password))
    return res.status(400).json({ error: "Password must be 12-128 characters" });

  const hash = await argon2.hash(password, ARGON_OPTS);
  try {
    const info = db.prepare("INSERT INTO users (email,password_hash,created_at) VALUES (?,?,?)")
      .run(email, hash, Date.now());
    issueSession(res, info.lastInsertRowid);
    res.status(201).json({ ok: true });
  } catch (e) {
    if (String(e.code).startsWith("SQLITE_CONSTRAINT"))
      return res.status(409).json({ error: "Could not create account" });
    throw e;
  }
});

app.post("/login", async (req, res) => {
  const email = normEmail(req.body.email);
  const password = typeof req.body.password === "string" ? req.body.password.slice(0, 128) : "";
  const keys = limitKeys(email, req.ip);

  const wait = lockedFor(keys);
  if (wait > 0) {
    res.set("Retry-After", String(Math.ceil(wait / 1000)));
    return res.status(429).json({ error: "Too many attempts. Try again later." });
  }

  const user = db.prepare("SELECT id,password_hash FROM users WHERE email=?").get(email);
  const ok = await argon2.verify(user ? user.password_hash : DUMMY_HASH, password).catch(() => false);

  if (!user || !ok) {
    recordFailure(keys);
    return res.status(401).json({ error: "Invalid email or password" });
  }

  clearFailures(keys);
  // Opportunistic rehash if parameters have been strengthened
  if (argon2.needsRehash(user.password_hash, ARGON_OPTS)) {
    db.prepare("UPDATE users SET password_hash=? WHERE id=?")
      .run(await argon2.hash(password, ARGON_OPTS), user.id);
  }
  issueSession(res, user.id); // always a fresh token (prevents session fixation)
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

function requireAuth(req, res, next) {
  const token = req.cookies.sid;
  if (!token) return res.status(401).json({ error: "Unauthenticated" });
  const row = db.prepare("SELECT user_id, expires_at FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=?").get(sha256(token));
  if (!row || row.expires_at < Date.now()) return res.status(401).json({ error: "Unauthenticated" });
  req.userId = row.user_id;
  next();
}

app.post("/logout", requireAuth, (req, res) => {
  db.prepare("DELETE FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=?").run(sha256(req.cookies.sid));
  res.clearCookie("sid");
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

app.get("/me", requireAuth, (req, res) => {
  const u = db.prepare("SELECT id,email FROM users WHERE id=?").get(req.userId);
  res.json(u);
});

// purge expired sessions hourly
setInterval(() => db.prepare("DELETE FROM sessions WHERE expires_at < ?").run(Date.now()), 3600_000).unref();

app.listen(3000, () => console.log("listening on :3000"));
```

**Design notes**

- **Hashing:** Argon2id with per-hash random salts (handled by the library). `needsRehash` upgrades hashes on login when you raise the cost.
- **Sessions:** 256-bit random tokens in an `HttpOnly`, `Secure`, `SameSite=Lax` cookie. Only the SHA-256 of the token is stored, so a database leak doesn't yield usable sessions.
- **Rate limiting:** Two counters, one per IP and one per email+IP, with a lockout and exponential backoff after 5 failures in 15 minutes. The lock check happens before the expensive hash verification. Keying on email+IP rather than email alone stops an attacker from locking a victim out of their own account.
- **Enumeration resistance:** Login returns the same error and does similar work (dummy hash) whether or not the email exists. Signup still reveals duplicates via 409. To hide that too, you'd need an email-verification flow that always responds "check your inbox."

**Gaps for production**

- Add CSRF protection for state-changing routes. `SameSite=Lax` helps but isn't complete.
- Run it behind HTTPS and set `trust proxy` correctly so `req.ip` is real.
- Use a shared store such as Redis if you run multiple instances, since SQLite here is single-node.
- Add email verification and password reset, and invalidate all sessions on password change.
- Consider checking passwords against a breached-password list.
- Add a global signup rate limit.
~~~

```js
// auth.js
import express from "express";
import argon2 from "argon2";
import Database from "better-sqlite3";
import crypto from "node:crypto";

const db = new Database("app.db");
db.exec(`
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  password_hash TEXT NOT NULL,
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sessions (
  token_hash TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  expires_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS failed_logins (
  key TEXT NOT NULL,          -- "email|ip"
  at INTEGER NOT NULL
);
CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS idx_failed_key ON failed_logins(key, at);
`);

const SESSION_TTL_MS = 7 * 24 * 3600 * 1000;
const MAX_FAILS = 5;
const WINDOW_MS = 15 * 60 * 1000;

const ARGON_OPTS = { type: argon2.argon2id, memoryCost: 19456, timeCost: 2, parallelism: 1 };
// Dummy hash so unknown-email path costs same as real path (timing).
const DUMMY_HASH = await argon2.hash("dummy-password", ARGON_OPTS);

const sha256 = (s) => crypto.createHash("sha256").update(s).digest("hex");
const normEmail = (e) => String(e ?? "").trim().toLowerCase();
const validEmail = (e) => e.length <= 254 && /^[^\s@]+@[^\s@]+\.[^\s@]+$/.test(e);
const validPassword = (p) => typeof p === "string" && p.length >= 10 && p.length <= 128;

// ---------- sessions ----------
function issueSession(res, userId) {
  const token = crypto.randomBytes(32).toString("base64url");
  db.prepare("INSERT INTO sessions VALUES (?,?,?)")
    .run(sha256(token), userId, Date.now() + SESSION_TTL_MS); // store hash only
  res.cookie("sid", token, {
    httpOnly: true,
    secure: process.env.NODE_ENV === "production",
    sameSite: "lax",
    maxAge: SESSION_TTL_MS,
    path: "/",
  });
}

function requireAuth(req, res, next) {
  const token = req.cookies?.sid;
  if (!token) return res.status(401).json({ error: "unauthenticated" });
  const row = db.prepare(
    "SELECT user_id, expires_at FROM sessions WHERE token_hash = ?"
  ).get(sha256(token));
  if (!row || row.expires_at < Date.now()) return res.status(401).json({ error: "unauthenticated" });
  req.userId = row.user_id;
  next();
}

// ---------- rate limiting ----------
function recentFails(key) {
  return db.prepare("SELECT COUNT(*) c, MIN(at) oldest FROM failed_logins WHERE key=? AND at>?")
    .get(key, Date.now() - WINDOW_MS);
}

// ---------- app ----------
const app = express();
app.use(express.json({ limit: "10kb" }));
app.use((req, _res, next) => { // tiny cookie parser (or use cookie-parser)
  req.cookies = Object.fromEntries(
    (req.headers.cookie ?? "").split(";").map((c) => c.trim().split("=").map(decodeURIComponent)).filter((p) => p[0])
  );
  next();
});

app.post("/signup", async (req, res) => {
  const email = normEmail(req.body?.email);
  const { password } = req.body ?? {};
  if (!validEmail(email)) return res.status(400).json({ error: "invalid email" });
  if (!validPassword(password)) return res.status(400).json({ error: "password must be 10-128 chars" });

  const hash = await argon2.hash(password, ARGON_OPTS);
  try {
    const { lastInsertRowid } = db
      .prepare("INSERT INTO users (email, password_hash, created_at) VALUES (?,?,?)")
      .run(email, hash, Date.now());
    issueSession(res, Number(lastInsertRowid));
    res.status(201).json({ ok: true });
  } catch (e) {
    if (e.code === "SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_UNIQUE") return res.status(409).json({ error: "email already registered" });
    throw e;
  }
});

app.post("/login", async (req, res) => {
  const email = normEmail(req.body?.email);
  const password = req.body?.password;
  if (!email || typeof password !== "string" || password.length > 128)
    return res.status(400).json({ error: "bad request" });

  const key = `${email}|${req.ip}`;
  const { c, oldest } = recentFails(key);
  if (c >= MAX_FAILS) {
    const retry = Math.ceil((oldest + WINDOW_MS - Date.now()) / 1000);
    res.set("Retry-After", String(Math.max(retry, 1)));
    return res.status(429).json({ error: "too many attempts" });
  }

  const user = db.prepare("SELECT id, password_hash FROM users WHERE email=?").get(email);
  const ok = await argon2.verify(user?.password_hash ?? DUMMY_HASH, password).catch(() => false);

  if (!user || !ok) {
    db.prepare("INSERT INTO failed_logins VALUES (?,?)").run(key, Date.now());
    return res.status(401).json({ error: "invalid email or password" }); // generic
  }

  db.prepare("DELETE FROM failed_logins WHERE key=?").run(key);
  issueSession(res, user.id);
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

app.post("/logout", requireAuth, (req, res) => {
  db.prepare("DELETE FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=?").run(sha256(req.cookies.sid));
  res.clearCookie("sid");
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

app.get("/me", requireAuth, (req, res) => {
  const u = db.prepare("SELECT id, email FROM users WHERE id=?").get(req.userId);
  res.json(u);
});

// housekeeping
setInterval(() => {
  db.prepare("DELETE FROM sessions WHERE expires_at < ?").run(Date.now());
  db.prepare("DELETE FROM failed_logins WHERE at < ?").run(Date.now() - WINDOW_MS);
}, 10 * 60 * 1000).unref();

app.set("trust proxy", false); // set true/hop count ONLY behind a real proxy, else req.ip spoofable
app.listen(3000);
```

Setup: `npm i express argon2 better-sqlite3`, `"type": "module"` in package.json.

Design notes:
- **Hashing**: argon2id, per-hash random salt built into the encoded string. Min 10 chars, max 128 (caps hashing DoS).
- **Sessions**: 256-bit random token in `httpOnly` cookie. DB stores SHA-256 only → DB leak doesn't yield live sessions. Logout deletes server-side.
- **Rate limit**: key = `email|ip`, 5 fails per 15 min → 429 + `Retry-After`. Success clears counter. Per-pair key stops attacker locking out the victim from elsewhere, but spread-IP attack on one email still passes. Add per-IP global limit (e.g. `express-rate-limit`) and/or per-email higher ceiling with CAPTCHA/step-up.
- **Enumeration**: login returns the same error and does a dummy hash for unknown emails. Signup still reveals 409. Fix by always replying "check your email" and sending verification mail.
- **Missing (add for prod)**: CSRF token or `Origin` check on POSTs (SameSite=lax helps but isn't enough alone), email verification, password reset, session rotation on privilege change, HTTPS, breached-password check, `helmet`. Multi-instance deploy → move rate limit counters to Redis.

### ponytail: 1798 answer tokens, 3771 billed, 91 lines

~~~
```python
# auth.py: stdlib only (scrypt, sqlite3, secrets). Run self-check: AUTH_DB=:memory: python auth.py
import hashlib, hmac, os, secrets, sqlite3, time

MAX_FAILS, WINDOW, SESSION_TTL = 5, 15 * 60, 7 * 24 * 3600

db = sqlite3.connect(os.environ.get("AUTH_DB", "auth.db"))
db.executescript("""
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users(email TEXT PRIMARY KEY, pw BLOB, salt BLOB);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sessions(token_hash BLOB PRIMARY KEY, email TEXT, expires REAL);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS fails(key TEXT, ts REAL);
CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS fails_key ON fails(key, ts);
""")

_sha = lambda s: hashlib.sha256(s.encode()).digest()
_hash = lambda pw, salt: hashlib.scrypt(pw.encode(), salt=salt, n=2**14, r=8, p=1)


def signup(email, pw):
    email = email.strip().lower()
    if "@" not in email or not 8 <= len(pw) <= 256:
        raise ValueError("invalid email or password (8-256 chars)")
    salt = secrets.token_bytes(16)
    try:
        with db:
            db.execute("INSERT INTO users VALUES(?,?,?)", (email, _hash(pw, salt), salt))
    except sqlite3.IntegrityError:
        raise ValueError("email already registered")


def _blocked(keys):
    since = time.time() - WINDOW
    return any(
        db.execute("SELECT COUNT(*) FROM fails WHERE key=? AND ts>?", (k, since)).fetchone()[0] >= MAX_FAILS
        for k in keys
    )


def login(email, pw, ip):
    """Returns a session token (put it in an HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Lax cookie)."""
    email = email.strip().lower()
    keys = ("e:" + email, "i:" + ip)  # limit per account AND per client IP
    if _blocked(keys):
        raise PermissionError("too many attempts, try later")
    row = db.execute("SELECT pw, salt FROM users WHERE email=?", (email,)).fetchone()
    stored, salt = row or (b"", bytes(16))  # unknown email still pays the hash cost (no timing oracle)
    ok = hmac.compare_digest(_hash(pw, salt), stored) and row is not None
    if not ok:
        with db:
            db.executemany("INSERT INTO fails VALUES(?,?)", [(k, time.time()) for k in keys])
        raise PermissionError("invalid credentials")
    token = secrets.token_urlsafe(32)
    with db:
        db.execute("DELETE FROM fails WHERE key=?", (keys[0],))
        db.execute("INSERT INTO sessions VALUES(?,?,?)", (_sha(token), email, time.time() + SESSION_TTL))
    return token  # only its hash is stored, so a DB leak doesn't leak live sessions


def user_for(token):
    r = db.execute("SELECT email FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=? AND expires>?", (_sha(token), time.time())).fetchone()
    return r and r[0]


def logout(token):
    with db:
        db.execute("DELETE FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=?", (_sha(token),))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    signup("a@b.co", "hunter2hunter2")
    t = login("a@b.co", "hunter2hunter2", "1.1.1.1")
    assert user_for(t) == "a@b.co"
    logout(t); assert user_for(t) is None
    for _ in range(MAX_FAILS):
        try: login("a@b.co", "wrong", "2.2.2.2")
        except PermissionError: pass
    try:
        login("a@b.co", "hunter2hunter2", "3.3.3.3")  # right password, new IP: still locked via email key
        raise SystemExit("rate limit failed")
    except PermissionError as e:
        assert "too many" in str(e)
    print("ok")
```

Skipped, with add-when:
- **Web framework wiring:** call these from any handler, passing the client IP (the real one behind your proxy).
- **Signup enumeration:** "email already registered" reveals accounts. Switch to email verification if that matters.
- **Account lockout DoS:** the per-email limit lets someone lock a victim out for 15 minutes. Add CAPTCHA or backoff if that becomes a problem.
- **Housekeeping:** there's no cleanup of expired sessions or old `fails` rows. Add a periodic `DELETE` when the tables grow.
- **Multi-process deployments:** sqlite is fine for one box. Move the rate-limit counters to Redis when you scale out.
- **Transport and CSRF:** serve over HTTPS only. Add CSRF tokens if you use cookie sessions with state-changing POSTs.

### chisle: 2559 answer tokens, 3145 billed, 129 lines

~~~
```js
// npm i express cookie-parser better-sqlite3 argon2
const express = require('express');
const cookieParser = require('cookie-parser');
const Database = require('better-sqlite3');
const argon2 = require('argon2');
const crypto = require('crypto');

const db = new Database('auth.db');
db.exec(`
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, email TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL, pw_hash TEXT NOT NULL,
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sessions (
  token_hash TEXT PRIMARY KEY, user_id INTEGER NOT NULL, expires_at INTEGER NOT NULL);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS failures (key TEXT NOT NULL, ts INTEGER NOT NULL);
CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS failures_key ON failures(key, ts);
`);

const SESSION_MS = 7 * 24 * 3600e3;
const WINDOW_MS = 15 * 60e3;
const LIMITS = { e: 5, i: 20 };          // failures per window: per email, per IP
const sha = s => crypto.createHash('sha256').update(s).digest('hex');
const now = () => Date.now();

// Verified against when user doesn't exist → equalizes timing, no user enumeration
const DUMMY = argon2.hash('dummy-password-for-timing');

// ---- rate limiting ----
function blockedFor(keys) {
  const since = now() - WINDOW_MS;
  for (const k of keys) {
    const { n, oldest } = db.prepare(
      'SELECT COUNT(*) n, MIN(ts) oldest FROM failures WHERE key=? AND ts>?').get(k, since);
    if (n >= LIMITS[k[0]]) return Math.ceil((oldest + WINDOW_MS - now()) / 1000);
  }
  return 0;
}
const recordFailure = keys =>
  keys.forEach(k => db.prepare('INSERT INTO failures VALUES (?,?)').run(k, now()));

// ---- sessions ----
function issueSession(res, userId) {
  const token = crypto.randomBytes(32).toString('base64url');
  db.prepare('INSERT INTO sessions VALUES (?,?,?)')
    .run(sha(token), userId, now() + SESSION_MS);          // store only the hash
  res.cookie('sid', token, {
    httpOnly: true, sameSite: 'lax', maxAge: SESSION_MS,
    secure: process.env.NODE_ENV === 'production', path: '/',
  });
}

function requireAuth(req, res, next) {
  const t = req.cookies.sid;
  const s = t && db.prepare('SELECT user_id, expires_at FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=?').get(sha(t));
  if (!s || s.expires_at < now()) return res.status(401).json({ error: 'unauthenticated' });
  req.userId = s.user_id;
  next();
}

// ---- validation ----
const normEmail = e => (typeof e === 'string' ? e.trim().toLowerCase() : '');
const validEmail = e => e.length <= 254 && /^[^\s@]+@[^\s@]+\.[^\s@]+$/.test(e);
const validPw = p => typeof p === 'string' && p.length >= 12 && p.length <= 128; // cap → no argon2 DoS

const app = express();
app.set('trust proxy', 1);                // only if behind exactly one proxy; else req.ip is spoofable
app.use(express.json({ limit: '10kb' }), cookieParser());

app.post('/signup', async (req, res) => {
  const email = normEmail(req.body?.email), pw = req.body?.password;
  if (!validEmail(email)) return res.status(400).json({ error: 'invalid email' });
  if (!validPw(pw)) return res.status(400).json({ error: 'password must be 12-128 chars' });
  const hash = await argon2.hash(pw, { type: argon2.argon2id });
  try {
    const { lastInsertRowid } = db.prepare(
      'INSERT INTO users (email, pw_hash, created_at) VALUES (?,?,?)').run(email, hash, now());
    issueSession(res, lastInsertRowid);
    res.status(201).json({ ok: true });
  } catch (e) {
    if (e.code === 'SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_UNIQUE') return res.status(409).json({ error: 'email taken' });
    throw e;
  }
});

app.post('/login', async (req, res) => {
  const email = normEmail(req.body?.email), pw = req.body?.password;
  if (!validEmail(email) || !validPw(pw)) return res.status(401).json({ error: 'invalid credentials' });

  const keys = [`e:${email}`, `i:${req.ip}`];
  const wait = blockedFor(keys);
  if (wait > 0) return res.set('Retry-After', wait).status(429).json({ error: 'too many attempts' });

  const user = db.prepare('SELECT id, pw_hash FROM users WHERE email=?').get(email);
  const ok = await argon2.verify(user ? user.pw_hash : await DUMMY, pw);
  if (!user || !ok) {
    recordFailure(keys);
    return res.status(401).json({ error: 'invalid credentials' });
  }
  db.prepare('DELETE FROM failures WHERE key=?').run(keys[0]);   // reset per-email counter only
  issueSession(res, user.id);
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

app.post('/logout', (req, res) => {
  if (req.cookies.sid) db.prepare('DELETE FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=?').run(sha(req.cookies.sid));
  res.clearCookie('sid').json({ ok: true });
});

app.get('/me', requireAuth, (req, res) => {
  res.json(db.prepare('SELECT id, email FROM users WHERE id=?').get(req.userId));
});

// housekeeping
setInterval(() => {
  db.prepare('DELETE FROM sessions WHERE expires_at<?').run(now());
  db.prepare('DELETE FROM failures WHERE ts<?').run(now() - WINDOW_MS);
}, 10 * 60e3).unref();

app.listen(3000);
```

Design notes:
- argon2id with library defaults; password length capped at 128 so oversized inputs can't burn CPU.
- Session tokens are 256-bit random, stored as SHA-256 → DB leak doesn't yield usable cookies.
- Login failures are uniformly `invalid credentials`, with a dummy-hash verify for unknown emails → no enumeration via message or timing.
- Rate limit: 5 failures/15 min per email, 20 per IP, `429` + `Retry-After`. Success clears only the email counter, so one IP can't reset its own budget by logging into an account it owns.

skipped: CSRF tokens (`sameSite=lax` covers basic cases; add for cross-site POST needs), signup enumeration protection (needs email verification), signup rate limiting by IP, session rotation on password change, HTTPS (required in prod; `secure` cookie is set when `NODE_ENV=production`).
~~~

## large-s1 / csvpipeline

> Write a script that reads a large CSV of transactions, validates each row, aggregates totals per customer, and writes a report, handling malformed rows without dying. Show the code.

### vanilla: 2042 answer tokens, 2091 billed, 139 lines

~~~
Here's a streaming Python script. It uses constant memory, `Decimal` for money, and sends bad rows to a rejects file with a reason instead of crashing.

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Aggregate per-customer transaction totals from a large CSV.

Usage:
    python aggregate.py transactions.csv report.csv [--rejects rejects.csv]

Expected columns: transaction_id, customer_id, amount, date (YYYY-MM-DD)
"""
import argparse
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
from datetime import datetime
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation

REQUIRED = ["transaction_id", "customer_id", "amount", "date"]


class RowError(ValueError):
    pass


def validate(row):
    """Return (customer_id, amount, txn_id) or raise RowError."""
    if None in row:  # DictReader puts extra fields under key None
        raise RowError("too many fields")
    if any(row.get(c) is None for c in REQUIRED):
        raise RowError("too few fields")

    txn_id = row["transaction_id"].strip()
    cust = row["customer_id"].strip()
    if not txn_id:
        raise RowError("empty transaction_id")
    if not cust:
        raise RowError("empty customer_id")

    try:
        amount = Decimal(row["amount"].strip())
    except InvalidOperation:
        raise RowError(f"bad amount: {row['amount']!r}")
    if not amount.is_finite():
        raise RowError(f"non-finite amount: {row['amount']!r}")

    try:
        datetime.strptime(row["date"].strip(), "%Y-%m-%d")
    except ValueError:
        raise RowError(f"bad date: {row['date']!r}")

    return cust, amount, txn_id


def main():
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    ap.add_argument("input")
    ap.add_argument("report")
    ap.add_argument("--rejects", default="rejects.csv")
    args = ap.parse_args()

    totals = defaultdict(lambda: Decimal("0"))
    counts = defaultdict(int)
    seen_ids = set()  # drop this if ids are too numerous for memory
    good = bad = dupes = 0

    try:
        with open(args.input, newline="", encoding="utf-8", errors="replace") as fin, \
             open(args.rejects, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as frej:

            reader = csv.DictReader(fin)
            missing = [c for c in REQUIRED if c not in (reader.fieldnames or [])]
            if missing:
                sys.exit(f"Missing required columns: {missing}")

            rej = csv.writer(frej)
            rej.writerow(["line", "reason", "raw_row"])

            while True:
                try:
                    row = next(reader)
                except StopIteration:
                    break
                except csv.Error as e:  # e.g. unterminated quote, NUL byte
                    bad += 1
                    rej.writerow([reader.line_num, f"csv error: {e}", ""])
                    continue

                try:
                    cust, amount, txn_id = validate(row)
                    if txn_id in seen_ids:
                        dupes += 1
                        raise RowError(f"duplicate transaction_id {txn_id}")
                    seen_ids.add(txn_id)
                except RowError as e:
                    bad += 1
                    rej.writerow([reader.line_num, str(e), repr(row)])
                    continue

                totals[cust] += amount
                counts[cust] += 1
                good += 1

                if (good + bad) % 1_000_000 == 0:
                    print(f"...{good + bad:,} rows processed", file=sys.stderr)
    except FileNotFoundError as e:
        sys.exit(f"File error: {e}")

    with open(args.report, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as fout:
        w = csv.writer(fout)
        w.writerow(["customer_id", "transaction_count", "total_amount"])
        for cust in sorted(totals):
            w.writerow([cust, counts[cust], f"{totals[cust]:.2f}"])

    print(f"Valid: {good:,}  Rejected: {bad:,} (duplicates: {dupes:,})  "
          f"Customers: {len(totals):,}", file=sys.stderr)
    print(f"Report: {args.report}  Rejects: {args.rejects}", file=sys.stderr)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

**Design choices**

- **Streaming:** the file is read row by row, so memory scales with the number of customers (and transaction IDs, if deduplicating), not file size.
- **Malformed rows:** three failure types are handled separately.
  - Structural problems (too many or too few fields) are caught in `validate`.
  - Value problems (bad amount, bad date, empty ID) raise `RowError`.
  - Parser-level `csv.Error` is caught around `next(reader)`. A plain `for row in reader` loop can't recover from that, which is why the loop is manual.
- **Rejects file:** each bad row is logged with its line number and reason, so nothing is silently lost and you can fix and reprocess.
- **`Decimal`:** avoids floating-point drift in sums. `Decimal("NaN")` and `Infinity` parse successfully, so they're rejected explicitly.
- **Encoding:** `errors="replace"` keeps invalid bytes from killing the run.

**Adjustments you may want**

- If there are tens of millions of unique IDs, remove `seen_ids` or replace it with a Bloom filter or a database-backed check.
- Change `REQUIRED` and the date format to match your schema. If negative amounts (refunds) are invalid, add a check in `validate`.
- For multi-GB files where speed matters, the same logic ports to `polars` or `pandas` in chunks, but per-row rejection reasons are easier with the stdlib approach above.
~~~
