```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Aggregate transaction totals per customer.

Usage: python report.py transactions.csv report.csv [rejects.csv]

Expected columns: transaction_id, customer_id, date (YYYY-MM-DD), amount
"""
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
from datetime import datetime
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation

REQUIRED = ["transaction_id", "customer_id", "date", "amount"]


class RowError(ValueError):
    pass


def validate(row):
    """Return (txn_id, customer_id, amount) or raise RowError."""
    if None in row:  # DictReader puts extra fields under key None
        raise RowError("too many fields")
    if any(row.get(c) is None for c in REQUIRED):
        raise RowError("too few fields")

    txn_id = row["transaction_id"].strip()
    cust = row["customer_id"].strip()
    if not txn_id:
        raise RowError("empty transaction_id")
    if not cust:
        raise RowError("empty customer_id")

    try:
        datetime.strptime(row["date"].strip(), "%Y-%m-%d")
    except ValueError:
        raise RowError(f"bad date: {row['date']!r}")

    try:
        amount = Decimal(row["amount"].strip())
    except InvalidOperation:
        raise RowError(f"bad amount: {row['amount']!r}")
    if not amount.is_finite():  # rejects NaN / Infinity
        raise RowError(f"non-finite amount: {row['amount']!r}")

    return txn_id, cust, amount


def main(src, dst, rejects_path="rejects.csv"):
    totals = defaultdict(lambda: [Decimal("0"), 0])  # cust -> [sum, count]
    seen = set()  # dedupe txn ids (memory grows with unique ids)
    good = bad = 0

    # errors="replace": bad bytes become U+FFFD instead of crashing
    # newline="": required by csv module
    with open(src, newline="", encoding="utf-8", errors="replace") as fin, \
         open(rejects_path, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as frej:

        reader = csv.DictReader(fin)
        missing = [c for c in REQUIRED if c not in (reader.fieldnames or [])]
        if missing:
            sys.exit(f"fatal: missing columns: {missing}")

        rej = csv.writer(frej)
        rej.writerow(["line", "reason", "raw"])

        while True:
            try:
                row = next(reader)
            except StopIteration:
                break
            except csv.Error as e:  # e.g. NUL byte, field too large
                bad += 1
                rej.writerow([reader.line_num, f"csv error: {e}", ""])
                continue

            try:
                txn_id, cust, amount = validate(row)
                if txn_id in seen:
                    raise RowError(f"duplicate transaction_id: {txn_id}")
            except RowError as e:
                bad += 1
                rej.writerow([reader.line_num, str(e), repr(dict(row))])
                continue

            seen.add(txn_id)
            totals[cust][0] += amount
            totals[cust][1] += 1
            good += 1

    with open(dst, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as fout:
        w = csv.writer(fout)
        w.writerow(["customer_id", "transaction_count", "total_amount"])
        for cust in sorted(totals):
            total, n = totals[cust]
            w.writerow([cust, n, f"{total:.2f}"])

    print(f"valid rows:    {good}")
    print(f"rejected rows: {bad} (see {rejects_path})")
    print(f"customers:     {len(totals)}")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) not in (3, 4):
        sys.exit(__doc__)
    main(*sys.argv[1:])
```

Key choices:

- **Streaming:** `csv.DictReader` iterates lazily. File size no matter. Only per-customer totals + `seen` set in RAM.
- **`Decimal` not `float`:** money sums exact, no drift.
- **Malformed handling:** three layers. `validate()` raises `RowError` for bad data. `csv.Error` caught around `next(reader)` for structural breakage. `errors="replace"` for bad encoding. Each → reject row, continue.
- **Fatal only on missing header columns.** No point processing then.
- **Reject file:** line number + reason + raw row. Fix and rerun.

Tweaks:

- `seen` set blows memory at billions of unique IDs. Drop dedupe, or dedupe downstream (sort/DB).
- Negative amounts (refunds) allowed now. Add `if amount < 0: raise RowError(...)` to forbid.
- Multi-currency: key totals by `(customer, currency)`.

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Usage: report.py transactions.csv report.csv rejects.csv
Input columns: txn_id,customer_id,amount,date (ISO date)."""
import csv, io, sys
from collections import defaultdict
from datetime import date
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation

COLS = ["txn_id", "customer_id", "amount", "date"]


def validate(row):
    """Return (customer_id, amount) or raise ValueError with a reason."""
    if None in row or None in row.values():  # extra or missing fields
        raise ValueError("wrong column count")
    cust = row["customer_id"].strip()
    if not cust:
        raise ValueError("empty customer_id")
    try:
        amt = Decimal(row["amount"].strip())
    except InvalidOperation:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount {row['amount']!r}")
    if not amt.is_finite():  # rejects NaN/Infinity
        raise ValueError(f"non-finite amount {row['amount']!r}")
    try:
        date.fromisoformat(row["date"].strip())
    except ValueError:
        raise ValueError(f"bad date {row['date']!r}")
    return cust, amt


def process(src, report, rejects):
    totals = defaultdict(lambda: [0, Decimal(0)])
    rj = csv.writer(rejects)
    rj.writerow(["line", "reason", "raw"])
    reader = csv.DictReader(src, fieldnames=COLS)  # we own the header, see below
    next(reader, None)  # skip header row
    ok = bad = 0
    while True:  # manual loop so csv.Error on a row doesn't kill the run
        try:
            row = next(reader)
        except StopIteration:
            break
        except csv.Error as e:
            bad += 1
            rj.writerow([reader.line_num, f"csv error: {e}", ""])
            continue
        try:
            cust, amt = validate(row)
        except ValueError as e:
            bad += 1
            rj.writerow([reader.line_num, e, "|".join(str(v) for v in row.values())])
            continue
        t = totals[cust]
        t[0] += 1
        t[1] += amt
        ok += 1

    w = csv.writer(report)
    w.writerow(["customer_id", "txn_count", "total"])
    for cust in sorted(totals):
        n, tot = totals[cust]
        w.writerow([cust, n, tot])
    return ok, bad


def selftest():
    data = ("txn_id,customer_id,amount,date\n"
            "1,a,10.50,2026-01-01\n"
            "2,a,4.50,2026-01-02\n"
            "3,b,abc,2026-01-02\n"      # bad amount
            "4,,5,2026-01-02\n"         # empty customer
            "5,b,5,2026-13-45\n"        # bad date
            "6,b\n"                     # short row
            "7,b,NaN,2026-01-01\n"      # non-finite
            "8,b,-2,2026-01-03\n")      # refund is valid
    rep, rej = io.StringIO(), io.StringIO()
    assert process(io.StringIO(data), rep, rej) == (3, 5)
    assert "a,2,15.00" in rep.getvalue() and "b,1,-2" in rep.getvalue()


if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) == 2 and sys.argv[1] == "--selftest":
        selftest(); print("ok"); sys.exit()
    src_path, rep_path, rej_path = sys.argv[1:4]
    # errors="replace": bad bytes become U+FFFD instead of crashing mid-file
    with open(src_path, newline="", encoding="utf-8", errors="replace") as src, \
         open(rep_path, "w", newline="") as rep, \
         open(rej_path, "w", newline="") as rej:
        ok, bad = process(src, rep, rej)
    print(f"{ok} ok, {bad} rejected -> {rej_path}", file=sys.stderr)
```

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""usage: report.py transactions.csv report.csv [rejects.csv]"""
import csv, sys
from collections import defaultdict
from datetime import date
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation

REQUIRED = ("transaction_id", "customer_id", "amount", "date")

def validate(row):
    if None in row or any(row.get(k) is None for k in REQUIRED):
        raise ValueError("wrong column count")
    cid = row["customer_id"].strip()
    if not cid:
        raise ValueError("empty customer_id")
    if not row["transaction_id"].strip():
        raise ValueError("empty transaction_id")
    try:
        amt = Decimal(row["amount"].strip())
    except InvalidOperation:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount {row['amount']!r}")
    if not amt.is_finite():
        raise ValueError("non-finite amount")
    try:
        date.fromisoformat(row["date"].strip())
    except ValueError:
        raise ValueError(f"bad date {row['date']!r}")
    return cid, amt

def main(src, out, rej_path="rejects.csv"):
    totals = defaultdict(lambda: [Decimal(0), 0])
    seen = set()
    ok = bad = 0
    with open(src, newline="", encoding="utf-8-sig", errors="replace") as f, \
         open(rej_path, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as rj:
        rd = csv.DictReader(f)
        missing = set(REQUIRED) - set(rd.fieldnames or [])
        if missing:
            sys.exit(f"missing columns: {sorted(missing)}")
        rw = csv.writer(rj)
        rw.writerow(["line", "error", "raw"])
        while True:
            try:
                row = next(rd)
            except StopIteration:
                break
            except csv.Error as e:  # malformed quoting etc.; reader continues
                bad += 1
                rw.writerow([rd.line_num, f"csv error: {e}", ""])
                continue
            try:
                cid, amt = validate(row)
                tid = row["transaction_id"].strip()
                if tid in seen:
                    raise ValueError("duplicate transaction_id")
                seen.add(tid)
            except ValueError as e:
                bad += 1
                rw.writerow([rd.line_num, str(e), "|".join(str(v) for v in row.values())])
                continue
            t = totals[cid]
            t[0] += amt
            t[1] += 1
            ok += 1
    with open(out, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        w = csv.writer(f)
        w.writerow(["customer_id", "total", "count"])
        for cid in sorted(totals):
            w.writerow([cid, totals[cid][0], totals[cid][1]])
    print(f"ok={ok} rejected={bad} customers={len(totals)}", file=sys.stderr)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) < 3:
        sys.exit(__doc__)
    main(*sys.argv[1:4])
```

Notes:
- `Decimal` → no float drift on money.
- Bad rows → `rejects.csv` with line number + reason; run never aborts on them. Missing header columns abort (whole file unusable).
- `seen` set for dedup grows with row count (~100 bytes/ID). At hundreds of millions of rows, drop it or move to sqlite.
- skipped: negative-amount policy (refunds?), currency handling. Add when rules are known.

## large-s1 / migration

> Write a database migration script that moves a users table from a single full_name column to first_name/last_name, backfills existing rows, and is safely re-runnable. Show the code.

### vanilla: 1642 answer tokens, 2527 billed, 85 lines

~~~
This is PostgreSQL, split into two re-runnable scripts (expand and backfill, then contract), so you can deploy app code between them.

## `001_split_full_name.sql` (expand and backfill)

```sql
BEGIN;

-- 1. Add the new columns (no-op if they already exist)
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS first_name text;
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS last_name  text;

-- 2. Backfill, only if full_name still exists, and only rows not yet migrated
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF EXISTS (
    SELECT 1
    FROM information_schema.columns
    WHERE table_schema = current_schema()
      AND table_name   = 'users'
      AND column_name  = 'full_name'
  ) THEN
    UPDATE users u
    SET first_name = substring(n.name from '^\S+'),
        last_name  = NULLIF(btrim(substring(n.name from '^\S+\s+(.*)$')), '')
    FROM (
      SELECT id, regexp_replace(btrim(full_name), '\s+', ' ', 'g') AS name
      FROM users
      WHERE full_name IS NOT NULL
    ) n
    WHERE u.id = n.id
      AND u.first_name IS NULL
      AND u.last_name  IS NULL;
  END IF;
END $$;

COMMIT;
```

## `002_drop_full_name.sql` (contract, run after the app no longer uses `full_name`)

```sql
BEGIN;

DO $$
BEGIN
  IF EXISTS (
    SELECT 1
    FROM information_schema.columns
    WHERE table_schema = current_schema()
      AND table_name   = 'users'
      AND column_name  = 'full_name'
  ) THEN
    -- Safety net: catch rows written since 001 ran
    UPDATE users u
    SET first_name = substring(n.name from '^\S+'),
        last_name  = NULLIF(btrim(substring(n.name from '^\S+\s+(.*)$')), '')
    FROM (
      SELECT id, regexp_replace(btrim(full_name), '\s+', ' ', 'g') AS name
      FROM users
      WHERE full_name IS NOT NULL
    ) n
    WHERE u.id = n.id
      AND u.first_name IS NULL
      AND u.last_name  IS NULL;

    ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN full_name;
  END IF;
END $$;

COMMIT;
```

## Why it's safe to re-run

- **Schema changes:** `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` and the `information_schema` guard make each step a no-op once done.
- **Backfill:** the `first_name IS NULL AND last_name IS NULL` filter means it never overwrites a value, including edits made after migration. A second run changes nothing, and a run after a partial or failed attempt picks up the remaining rows.
- **Transactions:** each script runs in one transaction, so a failure leaves nothing half-applied.
- **Dropping the column:** it's in a separate script so you can re-run 001 as often as you like while old and new app versions overlap.

## Notes and caveats

- **Splitting rule:** the first whitespace-delimited token becomes `first_name` and the rest becomes `last_name`. `"Ada Lovelace"` gives `Ada` / `Lovelace`, `"Mary Ann Smith"` gives `Mary` / `Ann Smith`, and `"Madonna"` gives `Madonna` / `NULL`. Name splitting is lossy for many cultures (for example, a multi-word given name), so spot-check the output and consider keeping `full_name` until you're confident.
- **Large tables:** a single `UPDATE` holds locks and creates bloat. For millions of rows, run the backfill in batches, for example by adding `AND u.id BETWEEN :lo AND :hi` and looping from a script, committing per batch. The idempotent filter makes that resumable too.
- **Rollback:** reverse with `ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS full_name text; UPDATE users SET full_name = btrim(concat_ws(' ', first_name, last_name)) WHERE full_name IS NULL;`. This only helps if you haven't dropped data you can't reconstruct.
- **MySQL or SQLite:** the logic carries over, but you'd swap in `SUBSTRING_INDEX` (MySQL) or `instr`/`substr` (SQLite). MySQL also has no `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` before 8.0.29/MariaDB, so you'd check `information_schema` first. Tell me which database you use and I'll adapt it.
~~~

## large-s1 / postmortem

> Write an incident postmortem for a four-hour outage caused by a connection pool exhausted by a slow downstream dependency, including timeline, root cause and remediations.

### vanilla: 2920 answer tokens, 2920 billed, 122 lines

~~~
# Incident Postmortem: Checkout API Outage Caused by Connection Pool Exhaustion

*Template with illustrative details. Replace bracketed values and example figures with your real data.*

**Status:** Resolved
**Severity:** SEV-1
**Date:** [YYYY-MM-DD]
**Duration:** 4 hours 5 minutes (10:12 to 14:17 UTC)
**Authors:** [names]
**Review meeting:** [date]

---

## 1. Summary

For about four hours, the Orders API returned errors or timed out for roughly 85% of requests. The cause was a degradation in a downstream dependency, the Inventory Service, which began responding in 20-30 seconds instead of ~80 ms. Orders API threads held database and HTTP connections while waiting on those calls. The connection pool was exhausted within minutes, and every request, including those that never needed Inventory, queued for a connection and then failed.

Recovery took four hours because the trigger was misdiagnosed as a database problem, and because restarting the service only gave temporary relief: the pool refilled with stuck calls within minutes.

**Impact**
- ~85% of API requests failed at peak (HTTP 503/504).
- ~[N] customers were unable to place orders; estimated [$X] in lost or delayed revenue.
- [N] support tickets; [N] delayed webhook deliveries to partners.
- No data loss or corruption. Orders accepted before the incident were processed normally.

---

## 2. Timeline (UTC)

| Time | Event |
|---|---|
| 09:40 | Inventory Service deploy begins, including a new query on an unindexed column. |
| 10:05 | Inventory p99 latency rises from 120 ms to 2 s. No alert fires (threshold is on error rate only). |
| 10:12 | Inventory latency reaches 20-30 s. Orders API pool utilization hits 100%. **Impact begins.** |
| 10:18 | Alert: Orders API 5xx rate > 5%. On-call engineer paged. |
| 10:25 | On-call acknowledges. Logs show "timeout waiting for connection from pool." Suspects database. |
| 10:40 | DB dashboards show low CPU and few active queries, but this is read as "DB is fine, app is misbehaving." Pool size increased from 50 to 100. |
| 10:55 | Pool fills again within 3 minutes. Errors continue. |
| 11:10 | Orders API instances restarted. Brief recovery (~4 min), then errors return. |
| 11:30 | Incident escalated to SEV-1; incident commander assigned. Second responder starts examining thread dumps. |
| 12:05 | Thread dumps show most threads blocked in `InventoryClient.reserve()`. Inventory identified as the bottleneck. |
| 12:20 | Inventory team engaged. They had not been alerted because their own error rate was low (slow, not failing). |
| 12:50 | Inventory team correlates slowness with the 09:40 deploy. |
| 13:30 | Rollback of the Inventory deploy started (delayed by a failed canary check on the rollback artifact). |
| 14:00 | Rollback complete. Inventory latency returns to normal. |
| 14:10 | Orders API pool utilization recovers; error rate drops below 1%. |
| 14:17 | Incident declared resolved after sustained monitoring. |

---

## 3. Root Cause

**Direct cause:** The Inventory deploy introduced a query that did a full table scan, driving latency from ~80 ms to 20-30 s.

**Why it became an outage:** Orders API had several design gaps that let one slow dependency take down the entire service:

1. **No client-side timeout** on Inventory calls beyond a 60 s default, far longer than any useful response time.
2. **Shared connection pool.** Inventory calls and unrelated endpoints (order lookup, health checks) drew from the same pool, so one slow dependency starved everything.
3. **No circuit breaker or load shedding.** Orders API kept sending calls to a failing dependency and kept accepting requests it could not serve.
4. **Retries amplified load.** The client retried timed-out calls 3 times, tripling demand on the already-degraded Inventory Service and holding connections longer.
5. **Blocking calls inside open DB transactions.** The reservation call was made while a DB connection was held, so each slow call pinned both an HTTP and a DB connection.

**Why detection and diagnosis were slow:**
- Alerts keyed on errors, not latency. A dependency that is slow but not erroring is invisible to this.
- No dependency-level dashboard showing per-dependency latency and pool wait time.
- Raising pool size and restarting are treated as default first steps, but both mask this failure mode.
- The Inventory deploy was not linked to the incident until 2.5 hours in. There was no deploy-event overlay on dashboards.

---

## 4. What Went Well

- The 5xx alert paged within 6 minutes of impact.
- Once thread dumps were taken, the cause was identified within 35 minutes.
- No data corruption; the system recovered cleanly after the rollback.
- Communication to the status page and support was timely.

## 5. What Went Poorly

- Two hours to identify the true cause, driven by anchoring on the database.
- Inventory deploy went out with no latency regression check and no performance test against production-sized data.
- Rollback was delayed ~40 minutes by a canary check that wasn't designed for emergency rollbacks.
- Inventory team was not paged because their alerts were error-based.

---

## 6. Remediation Items

| # | Action | Type | Owner | Priority | Due |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Set explicit timeouts on all Inventory calls (connect 200 ms, total 1 s) and audit other outbound clients | Prevent | [Orders] | P0 | [date] |
| 2 | Add circuit breaker with fallback behavior (e.g. fail fast or degrade to cached availability) | Prevent | [Orders] | P0 | [date] |
| 3 | Isolate pools via bulkheads: separate pool/concurrency limit per dependency; keep health checks and unrelated endpoints off the shared pool | Prevent | [Orders] | P0 | [date] |
| 4 | Cap retries to 1 with jittered backoff and a retry budget | Prevent | [Orders] | P1 | [date] |
| 5 | Move external calls outside DB transactions | Prevent | [Orders] | P1 | [date] |
| 6 | Add latency-based (p95/p99) alerts for every dependency, plus pool wait-time and saturation alerts | Detect | [SRE] | P0 | [date] |
| 7 | Build a dependency health dashboard with deploy markers | Detect | [SRE] | P1 | [date] |
| 8 | Add the missing index; add query-plan review and a latency regression gate to Inventory's CI/deploy pipeline | Prevent | [Inventory] | P0 | [date] |
| 9 | Add a "fast rollback" path that bypasses non-essential canary gates | Mitigate | [Platform] | P1 | [date] |
| 10 | Update runbook: "pool exhaustion" should direct responders to take thread dumps and check downstream latency before resizing or restarting | Respond | [SRE] | P1 | [date] |
| 11 | Run a game day injecting dependency latency to validate timeouts, breakers and alerts | Verify | [SRE] | P2 | [date] |
| 12 | Add automatic "recent deploys" lookup to the incident channel template | Respond | [SRE] | P2 | [date] |

---

## 7. Lessons Learned

- **Slow is worse than down.** A failing dependency returns quickly and frees resources; a slow one holds them. Timeouts and breakers must treat latency as failure.
- **Shared resources spread failures.** Without isolation, a problem in one dependency becomes a problem in every endpoint.
- **Scaling a pool or restarting treats the symptom.** If the pool fills again within minutes, look at what is holding connections.
- **Alert on latency, not just errors,** and make deploys visible in every dashboard used during incidents.

---

## 8. Appendix

- Graphs: Orders API pool utilization, Inventory p99 latency, 5xx rate [attach]
- Thread dump excerpt [attach]
- Relevant PRs / deploy IDs [link]
- Incident channel log [link]

*Blameless format: this review examines systems and processes, not individuals.*
~~~

# Incident Postmortem: Checkout API Outage (Connection Pool Exhaustion)

**Status:** Draft | **Severity:** SEV-1 | **Duration:** 4h 02m | **Owner:** `<name>`
*Template with illustrative details. Replace bracketed values with real data.*

## Summary

Slow downstream dependency (`inventory-service`) caused `orders-api` HTTP client connection pool to exhaust. All worker threads blocked waiting for pool slots. Health checks failed, load balancer pulled instances, remaining instances took more load and failed too. Full outage of order placement for 4h 02m. ~38% of all requests failed during window. No data loss.

## Impact

- Order placement: 100% failure for ~3h 20m, partial (~40% errors) for remaining ~40m.
- ~[N] failed orders, est. revenue impact $[X].
- ~[N] support tickets.
- No data corruption. Retried orders deduplicated by idempotency key.

## Timeline (UTC)

| Time | Event |
|------|-------|
| 09:12 | `inventory-service` deploys DB migration. Query p99 goes 80ms → 12s (missing index). |
| 09:20 | `orders-api` latency p99 rises. No alert; threshold is on error rate, not latency. |
| 09:34 | Pool in `orders-api` hits 100% utilization. Requests queue for slots. |
| 09:41 | Health checks time out (share pool). LB marks instances unhealthy. |
| 09:44 | **Alert fires:** 5xx rate > 5%. On-call paged. |
| 09:52 | On-call acks. Suspects `orders-api` bug. Checks recent deploys: none. |
| 10:15 | Restart of `orders-api` fleet. Recovers ~3 min, then pool exhausts again. |
| 10:40 | Incident declared SEV-1. IC assigned. Comms posted to status page. |
| 11:05 | Thread dumps show all threads blocked in `inventory-service` client call. |
| 11:25 | `inventory-service` identified as slow. Its team joins. |
| 11:50 | Team suspects load, scales `inventory-service` 3x. No improvement (DB-bound). |
| 12:35 | `inventory-service` DB slow-query log reveals the unindexed query from the 09:12 migration. |
| 12:50 | Decision: roll back migration vs add index. Rollback chosen (index build on live table risky). |
| 13:10 | Migration rolled back. `inventory-service` latency normal. |
| 13:14 | `orders-api` pool drains. Errors drop to ~40% (retry storm, cold caches). |
| 13:30 | Traffic shed at LB, instances restarted in batches. |
| 13:54 | Error rate baseline. Incident resolved. |

## Root Cause

**Trigger:** `inventory-service` migration dropped an index the stock-check query relied on. Query latency rose ~150x.

**Why it became an outage (contributing causes):**

1. **No client timeout.** `orders-api` call to `inventory-service` used the library default (infinite read timeout). Slow calls held connections indefinitely.
2. **Shared, unbounded blocking.** Single pool and thread set served all routes. Stock-check slowness starved routes that never needed inventory, including health checks.
3. **No circuit breaker or bulkhead.** Nothing stopped calls to a known-bad dependency.
4. **Health check coupled to pool.** Check needed a pool slot, so a degraded instance looked dead. LB ejection shifted load onto survivors → cascading failure.
5. **Retries without backoff or budget.** Clients and `orders-api` retried immediately, amplifying load and prolonging recovery.
6. **Alert gap.** Alerts on errors, not latency or pool saturation. ~24 min lost before page.
7. **Slow diagnosis.** No dependency dashboard. Restart attempted before checking what threads were waiting on. ~2h 40m from page to correct dependency identified... wait, correct figure: page 09:44 → culprit service identified 11:25 (1h 41m); root query found 12:35.
8. **Migration review gap.** Migration not load-tested, no query-plan check, no canary.

## What Went Well

- Idempotency keys prevented duplicate orders.
- Rollback of migration was fast once chosen.
- Status page updated within an hour of declaration.

## What Went Poorly

- Restarts treated as fix; masked the cause and wasted ~25 min.
- Scaling `inventory-service` without confirming bottleneck.
- Incident declared 56 min after page.
- No ownership map; took 1h+ to loop in the right team.

## Remediation Items

| # | Action | Type | Owner | Priority | Due |
|---|--------|------|-------|----------|-----|
| 1 | Set connect (≤500ms) and read (≤2s) timeouts on all `orders-api` outbound clients | Prevent | `<>` | P0 | 1 wk |
| 2 | Add circuit breaker + fallback (degrade: accept order, reserve stock async) for inventory calls | Mitigate | `<>` | P0 | 3 wk |
| 3 | Separate pools per dependency (bulkheads); dedicated pool/thread for health checks | Prevent | `<>` | P0 | 3 wk |
| 4 | Make health check shallow (process liveness only); separate deep readiness check | Prevent | `<>` | P1 | 2 wk |
| 5 | Retries: exponential backoff + jitter, retry budget (≤10% of traffic) | Prevent | `<>` | P1 | 4 wk |
| 6 | Alerts: pool utilization >80%, dependency p99 latency, queue wait time | Detect | `<>` | P0 | 1 wk |
| 7 | Dependency dashboard (latency, errors, pool use per downstream) | Detect | `<>` | P1 | 3 wk |
| 8 | Runbook: "high latency / pool exhaustion" with thread-dump-first step | Respond | `<>` | P1 | 2 wk |
| 9 | Service ownership map + on-call escalation links in alert payloads | Respond | `<>` | P2 | 6 wk |
| 10 | Migration checks: `EXPLAIN` on hot queries in CI, staging load test, canary rollout for DB changes | Prevent | `<inventory team>` | P0 | 4 wk |
| 11 | Auto-declare SEV-1 if page unresolved after 15 min and error rate >20% | Respond | `<>` | P2 | 6 wk |
| 12 | Game-day: inject 10s latency into each critical dependency, verify degradation | Verify | `<>` | P1 | Next quarter |

## Lessons Learned

- Every outbound call needs timeout. Default "infinite" is outage waiting.
- Slow is worse than down: down fails fast, slow holds resources.
- Health checks must not depend on shared resources that can saturate.
- Check what threads are waiting on before restarting.

## Appendix (to fill)

- Graphs: pool utilization, p99 latency per dependency, 5xx rate.
- Thread dump excerpt.
- Slow query and `EXPLAIN` output.
- Migration diff.

**Note:** Item 7 in root cause has a clumsy phrase ("wait, correct figure"). Clean up before publishing: page 09:44 → culprit service identified 11:25 (1h 41m), root query found 12:35 (2h 51m).

# Incident Postmortem: Checkout API Outage from Connection Pool Exhaustion

*Illustrative draft. Names, times, and numbers are placeholders; replace them with real data.*

**Status:** Resolved | **Severity:** SEV-1 | **Duration:** 4h 05m (09:12-13:17 UTC) | **Authors:** [on-call lead] | **Review date:** [date]

## Summary

A latency regression in a downstream dependency (the `pricing-service`) caused request threads in `orders-api` to hold database and HTTP connections far longer than normal. The connection pool filled, new requests queued and then timed out, and `orders-api` stopped serving traffic. Retries from clients and an upstream gateway amplified the load and prevented self-recovery. Roughly 62% of checkout requests failed during the window.

## Impact

- **Customer:** ~62% of checkout requests returned 5xx or timed out for 4h 05m. Est. [N] failed orders, [$X] in delayed or lost revenue.
- **Internal:** Support ticket volume rose [N]x. Two dependent batch jobs were delayed.
- **Data loss:** None confirmed. [N] orders were left in a `pending` state and reconciled manually.

## Timeline (UTC)

| Time | Event |
|---|---|
| 08:55 | `pricing-service` deploys a release that adds an unindexed query. p99 latency rises from 80 ms to ~9 s. |
| 09:12 | `orders-api` connection pool (max 50) reaches 100% utilization. Request queueing begins. |
| 09:15 | Error-rate alert fires (5xx > 5%). Pages the on-call engineer. |
| 09:22 | On-call acknowledges. Initial hypothesis: `orders-api` database problem, because the pool-exhausted errors appear there. |
| 09:40 | DB checked and found healthy (low CPU, few active queries). Hypothesis dropped. |
| 09:55 | `orders-api` restarted. Recovers for about 3 minutes, then pool saturates again. |
| 10:20 | Incident declared SEV-1. Incident commander assigned. |
| 10:45 | Traces show most pool-held time is spent waiting on `pricing-service`. |
| 11:05 | `pricing-service` owners paged. They did not know of the regression. Their dashboards showed latency but had no alerting on it. |
| 11:30 | Gateway retry storm identified (3 retries per request), tripling load on the degraded path. |
| 11:50 | Pool size raised to 150 as a mitigation. This failed because `pricing-service` itself saturated. |
| 12:25 | `pricing-service` rollback decided. Delayed by unclear ownership of the deploy and a rollback runbook that was out of date. |
| 12:50 | Rollback completes. `pricing-service` p99 returns to normal. |
| 13:05 | Gateway retries disabled temporarily. `orders-api` pool drains. |
| 13:17 | Error rate below 0.5%. Incident resolved. |
| 14:00 | Pending orders reconciled. |

## Root Cause

**Trigger:** A `pricing-service` release introduced a slow, unindexed query, raising latency roughly 100x.

**Root cause:** `orders-api` called `pricing-service` while holding a pooled connection, with no effective timeout and no isolation. Slow downstream calls therefore translated directly into pool occupancy. By Little's law, with request rate λ and hold time W, the connections in use are λ·W. A 100x increase in W exhausted the pool regardless of its size.

## Contributing Factors

1. **No client-side timeout:** the HTTP client used a 60 s default, far above the 2 s SLO budget.
2. **No circuit breaker or bulkhead:** one failing dependency could consume the shared pool for all endpoints, including ones that don't need pricing.
3. **Connection held across a network call:** the DB connection was acquired before the pricing call and released after it.
4. **Retry amplification:** gateway and client retries without backoff or budgets tripled load.
5. **Monitoring gaps:** no alert on `pricing-service` latency, and no alert on pool utilization in `orders-api`.
6. **Misleading symptom:** "pool exhausted" errors pointed at the DB, costing ~25 min.
7. **Slow rollback:** unclear deploy ownership and a stale runbook added ~1h 20m between diagnosis and remediation.
8. **Restart and pool-size changes** treated the symptom and did not address hold time.

## What Went Well

- The error-rate alert fired within 3 minutes of impact.
- Distributed tracing, once consulted, identified the bottleneck quickly.
- Cross-team communication was clear once the right owners were engaged.

## What Went Poorly

- Diagnosis anchored on the database for ~1.5 hours.
- The dependency owner was not alerted automatically.
- No fast "disable retries" or "degrade gracefully" switch existed.

## Remediation / Action Items

| # | Action | Type | Owner | Priority | Due |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Set explicit timeouts on all outbound calls (connect 200 ms, total 2 s) | Prevent | orders team | P0 | [date] |
| 2 | Release DB connections before outbound network calls; stop holding them across I/O | Prevent | orders team | P0 | [date] |
| 3 | Add circuit breaker and per-dependency bulkheads (separate pools or concurrency limits) | Mitigate | orders team | P0 | [date] |
| 4 | Fallback for pricing outages (cached last-known price or a clear degraded response) | Mitigate | orders + pricing | P1 | [date] |
| 5 | Retry policy: exponential backoff with jitter, retry budget (≤10% extra load), none on timeouts at the gateway | Prevent | platform | P0 | [date] |
| 6 | Alerts on pool utilization (>80% for 2 min) and on per-dependency p99 latency | Detect | orders + pricing | P0 | [date] |
| 7 | Dashboard and runbook entry: "pool exhausted → check downstream latency first" | Detect | SRE | P1 | [date] |
| 8 | Canary and automated rollback on latency regression for `pricing-service` deploys | Prevent | pricing team | P1 | [date] |
| 9 | Load test with an injected slow dependency (chaos or latency fault) in staging | Prevent | SRE | P2 | [date] |
| 10 | Update rollback runbooks and define deploy ownership on the service catalog | Process | eng mgmt | P1 | [date] |
| 11 | Review other services for the same acquire-connection-then-call-out pattern | Prevent | all teams | P2 | [date] |

## Lessons Learned

- Pool size is not a defense against slow dependencies. Bounded hold time is.
- Every network call needs a timeout shorter than the caller's own budget.
- Retries are load multipliers and need budgets.
- Errors surface where resources run out, not necessarily where the fault is. Start from traces.

## Appendix (to fill in)

- Graphs: pool utilization, `pricing-service` p99, error rate, retry volume
- Deploy diff for the `pricing-service` release
- Support ticket and revenue-impact calculations

This postmortem is blameless. The focus is on system and process gaps, not individuals.

# Postmortem: Checkout API outage, 4h 05m

**Status:** Draft | **Severity:** SEV-1 | **Date:** 2026-11-18 | **Authors:** [names]

`orders-api` returned 5xx/timeouts for 4h 05m (14:02-18:07 UTC). `orders-api` blocks on `inventory-svc`. A latency regression in `inventory-svc` held DB/HTTP connections open until `orders-api`'s pool (size 50/pod) was exhausted. All requests queued, health checks failed, pods restarted, and the restarts amplified load. About 38% of checkout attempts failed overall; ~100% during peak impact.

## Impact
- Checkout unavailable for ~4h; est. [N] failed orders, [$X] lost revenue.
- Support tickets: [N]. No data loss or corruption.
- Unaffected: browse/search (separate service).

## Timeline (UTC)
| Time | Event |
|---|---|
| 13:40 | `inventory-svc` deploy v2.31 (adds uncached per-SKU stock query) |

| 14:09 | Alert: `orders-api` 5xx > 5%. On-call paged |
| 14:20 | On-call sees pool exhaustion, suspects `orders-api` itself, scales pods 6 → 12 |

| 14:50 | Liveness probes fail (probe shares the saturated pool) → crash-loop restarts |
| 15:30 | Incident escalated; `inventory-svc` team engaged |

## Root cause
1. **Trigger:** v2.31 added an unindexed/uncached query, making `inventory-svc` ~75x slower.
2. **Why it became an outage:** `orders-api` called `inventory-svc` with a 60s client timeout, no circuit breaker, and no bulkhead. Each slow call held a pool connection; at ~800 req/s × 6s the 50-connection pool was exhausted in seconds.
3. **Why it lasted 4h:**
   - Detection was by symptom (5xx), not cause (downstream latency); no latency alert.
   - Scaling out was the wrong mitigation: more pods → more load on the slow dependency.
   - Liveness probe depended on the same pool → restarts instead of load shedding.
   - Client retries (3x, no backoff/jitter) kept the pool saturated after the fix.
   - Rollback delayed ~35 min by approval and a stale runbook.

## What went well
- Alert fired within 7 min of impact.
- No data loss; rollback itself was clean.

## What went badly
- Misdiagnosis for ~2h; no dependency dashboard on the `orders-api` overview.
- Canary for v2.31 had no latency gate and ran 15 min, too short to show the regression at traffic scale.

## Remediations
| # | Action | Type | Owner | Due |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Cut `inventory-svc` client timeout 60s → 1.5s; add circuit breaker with fallback (allow order, reserve async) | Prevent | [orders] | [date] |
| 2 | Separate bounded pool/bulkhead per downstream | Prevent | [orders] | [date] |
| 3 | Retries: max 1, exponential backoff + jitter, retry budget | Prevent | [orders] | [date] |
| 4 | Liveness probe independent of pool; readiness sheds load | Prevent | [orders] | [date] |
| 5 | Alert on downstream p99 latency and pool utilization > 80% | Detect | [SRE] | [date] |
| 6 | Add the per-dependency latency/pool panel to the service dashboard | Detect | [SRE] | [date] |
| 7 | Canary gate on p99 latency; extend to ≥ 1h or traffic-proportional | Prevent | [inventory] | [date] |
| 8 | Index/cache the stock query; load test before re-release | Fix | [inventory] | [date] |
| 9 | Pre-approved rollback for SEV-1; update runbook ("check dependency latency before scaling") | Mitigate | [SRE] | [date] |
| 10 | Game day: inject 5s latency into a dependency, verify breaker/bulkhead | Verify | [SRE] | [date] |

## Lessons
- Slow is worse than down: a dependency that fails fast is survivable, one that hangs consumes your capacity.
- Scaling out into a saturated dependency makes things worse.
- Health checks must not share the resource they're meant to protect.

## large-s1 / statemachine

> Implement an order state machine covering created, paid, shipped, delivered, cancelled and refunded, with the legal transitions enforced and invalid ones rejected. Show the code.

### vanilla: 1793 answer tokens, 2036 billed, 117 lines

~~~
I used Python with no dependencies. The transition table is the single source of truth, and every state change goes through one method.

```python
from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from datetime import datetime, timezone
from enum import Enum


class OrderState(str, Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"


S = OrderState

# Legal transitions: current state -> set of allowed next states.
TRANSITIONS: dict[OrderState, frozenset[OrderState]] = {
    S.CREATED:   frozenset({S.PAID, S.CANCELLED}),
    S.PAID:      frozenset({S.SHIPPED, S.CANCELLED, S.REFUNDED}),
    S.SHIPPED:   frozenset({S.DELIVERED}),
    S.DELIVERED: frozenset({S.REFUNDED}),
    S.CANCELLED: frozenset(),   # terminal
    S.REFUNDED:  frozenset(),   # terminal
}


class InvalidTransitionError(Exception):
    def __init__(self, current: OrderState, target: OrderState):
        self.current = current
        self.target = target
        allowed = sorted(s.value for s in TRANSITIONS[current])
        super().__init__(
            f"Illegal transition {current.value} -> {target.value}; "
            f"allowed from {current.value}: {allowed or 'none (terminal state)'}"
        )


@dataclass
class Order:
    id: str
    state: OrderState = S.CREATED
    history: list[tuple[OrderState, OrderState, datetime]] = field(default_factory=list)

    def can_transition(self, target: OrderState) -> bool:
        return target in TRANSITIONS[self.state]

    def transition(self, target: OrderState) -> None:
        target = OrderState(target)  # also rejects unknown values/strings
        if not self.can_transition(target):
            raise InvalidTransitionError(self.state, target)
        self.history.append((self.state, target, datetime.now(timezone.utc)))
        self.state = target

    @property
    def is_terminal(self) -> bool:
        return not TRANSITIONS[self.state]

    # Intent-revealing helpers
    def pay(self):      self.transition(S.PAID)
    def ship(self):     self.transition(S.SHIPPED)
    def deliver(self):  self.transition(S.DELIVERED)
    def cancel(self):   self.transition(S.CANCELLED)
    def refund(self):   self.transition(S.REFUNDED)
```

Tests (pytest):

```python
import pytest

def test_happy_path():
    o = Order("A1")
    o.pay(); o.ship(); o.deliver(); o.refund()
    assert o.state is S.REFUNDED and o.is_terminal
    assert len(o.history) == 4

def test_cancel_before_payment():
    o = Order("A2"); o.cancel()
    assert o.state is S.CANCELLED

@pytest.mark.parametrize("setup,bad", [
    ([],                         S.SHIPPED),    # created -> shipped
    ([S.PAID, S.SHIPPED],        S.CANCELLED),  # can't cancel once shipped
    ([S.PAID, S.SHIPPED],        S.REFUNDED),   # must be delivered first
    ([S.CANCELLED],              S.PAID),       # terminal
    ([S.PAID, S.REFUNDED],       S.SHIPPED),    # terminal
    ([S.PAID],                   S.PAID),       # no self-transitions
])
def test_invalid_transitions_rejected(setup, bad):
    o = Order("X")
    for s in setup:
        o.transition(s)
    before = (o.state, len(o.history))
    with pytest.raises(InvalidTransitionError):
        o.transition(bad)
    assert (o.state, len(o.history)) == before  # failed attempts change nothing

def test_unknown_state_rejected():
    with pytest.raises(ValueError):
        Order("Y").transition("teleported")
```

Design notes:

- **Transition map:** Allowed moves are data, not scattered `if` statements, so changing a rule means editing one line, and the map is easy to review or render as a diagram.
- **Atomic failure:** The check runs before any mutation, so a rejected transition leaves the state and history untouched.
- **Business decisions to confirm:**
  - `paid -> cancelled` is allowed. If it should always trigger a refund, remove it and route those orders through `refunded`.
  - `shipped -> cancelled` is not allowed. A return flow would need extra states such as `return_requested`.
  - Refunds are allowed only from `paid` or `delivered`.
- **Concurrency:** In a real service, persist the state and update it with a compare-and-set, for example `UPDATE orders SET state=:new WHERE id=:id AND state=:expected`, and treat zero affected rows as a conflict. Otherwise two concurrent requests, such as `ship` and `cancel`, can both pass the in-memory check.
~~~

Python, stdlib only. Transition table = single source of truth. Terminal states have empty set.

```python
from __future__ import annotations
from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from datetime import datetime, timezone
from enum import Enum


class OrderState(str, Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"


S = OrderState

TRANSITIONS: dict[OrderState, frozenset[OrderState]] = {
    S.CREATED:   frozenset({S.PAID, S.CANCELLED}),
    S.PAID:      frozenset({S.SHIPPED, S.CANCELLED, S.REFUNDED}),
    S.SHIPPED:   frozenset({S.DELIVERED}),
    S.DELIVERED: frozenset({S.REFUNDED}),
    S.CANCELLED: frozenset(),  # terminal
    S.REFUNDED:  frozenset(),  # terminal
}


class InvalidTransition(Exception):
    def __init__(self, current: OrderState, target: OrderState):
        self.current, self.target = current, target
        allowed = ", ".join(s.value for s in TRANSITIONS[current]) or "none (terminal)"
        super().__init__(
            f"Illegal transition {current.value} -> {target.value}; allowed: {allowed}"
        )


@dataclass
class Order:
    id: str
    state: OrderState = S.CREATED
    history: list[tuple[OrderState, OrderState, datetime]] = field(default_factory=list)

    def can_transition(self, target: OrderState) -> bool:
        return target in TRANSITIONS[self.state]

    def _transition(self, target: OrderState) -> None:
        if not self.can_transition(target):
            raise InvalidTransition(self.state, target)
        self.history.append((self.state, target, datetime.now(timezone.utc)))
        self.state = target

    # Named actions
    def pay(self):     self._transition(S.PAID)
    def ship(self):    self._transition(S.SHIPPED)
    def deliver(self): self._transition(S.DELIVERED)
    def cancel(self):  self._transition(S.CANCELLED)
    def refund(self):  self._transition(S.REFUNDED)

    @property
    def is_terminal(self) -> bool:
        return not TRANSITIONS[self.state]
```

Tests (pytest):

```python
import itertools
import pytest

LEGAL = {(a, b) for a, targets in TRANSITIONS.items() for b in targets}

@pytest.mark.parametrize("a,b", sorted(LEGAL))
def test_legal(a, b):
    o = Order("1", state=a)
    o._transition(b)
    assert o.state == b and len(o.history) == 1

@pytest.mark.parametrize("a,b", [p for p in itertools.product(OrderState, OrderState) if p not in LEGAL])
def test_illegal(a, b):
    o = Order("1", state=a)
    with pytest.raises(InvalidTransition):
        o._transition(b)
    assert o.state == a and not o.history  # state unchanged on failure

def test_happy_path():
    o = Order("1"); o.pay(); o.ship(); o.deliver(); o.refund()
    assert o.state is S.REFUNDED and o.is_terminal
```

Design notes:
- **Illegal = exception, no mutation.** Check happens before state change.
- **Same-state transition** (e.g. paid → paid) rejected. Good for idempotency bugs; if you want idempotent retries, catch and ignore at caller.
- **Assumptions you may want to change:**
  - `paid → cancelled` and `paid → refunded` both allowed (cancel before ship, refund = money back). Merge if you want only one.
  - `shipped → refunded` blocked (must deliver first). Add edge for lost-in-transit if needed.
  - `delivered → refunded` only; no partial refunds.
- **Concurrency:** in real DB, make transition atomic: `UPDATE orders SET state=:new WHERE id=:id AND state=:expected`; check rowcount = 1. Else two requests race past in-memory check.
- **Side effects** (charge, email, inventory): hook after successful `_transition`, ideally in same DB transaction or via outbox.
~~~

```python
from enum import Enum


class State(Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"


S = State
TRANSITIONS = {
    S.CREATED:   {S.PAID, S.CANCELLED},
    S.PAID:      {S.SHIPPED, S.CANCELLED, S.REFUNDED},  # cancel-after-pay implies refund; see note
    S.SHIPPED:   {S.DELIVERED},
    S.DELIVERED: {S.REFUNDED},
    S.CANCELLED: set(),   # terminal
    S.REFUNDED:  set(),   # terminal
}


class InvalidTransition(Exception):
    pass


class Order:
    def __init__(self):
        self.state = S.CREATED

    def transition(self, to: State) -> None:
        if to not in TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise InvalidTransition(f"{self.state.value} -> {to.value}")
        self.state = to


def demo():
    o = Order()
    o.transition(S.PAID)
    o.transition(S.SHIPPED)
    o.transition(S.DELIVERED)
    o.transition(S.REFUNDED)
    for bad in (S.PAID, S.CANCELLED):  # terminal: nothing allowed
        try:
            o.transition(bad)
            assert False, "should have raised"
        except InvalidTransition:
            pass
    o2 = Order()
    try:
        o2.transition(S.SHIPPED)  # can't skip payment
        assert False
    except InvalidTransition:
        pass
    print("ok")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    demo()
```

The whole machine is one transition table plus a single guarded `transition()` method, so the legal moves are visible in one place.

Assumptions I made (change the table if they're wrong):
- Paid orders can be cancelled or refunded; shipped orders can't be cancelled, only delivered (returns after that go via `DELIVERED → REFUNDED`).
- `CANCELLED` and `REFUNDED` are terminal.

Skipped: transition history/audit log, hooks/side effects, persistence and locking. Add them when orders are stored in a DB (use a conditional `UPDATE ... WHERE state = ?` to avoid races).

```python
from enum import Enum


class State(Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"


S = State
TRANSITIONS = {
    S.CREATED:   {S.PAID, S.CANCELLED},
    S.PAID:      {S.SHIPPED, S.CANCELLED, S.REFUNDED},
    S.SHIPPED:   {S.DELIVERED},
    S.DELIVERED: {S.REFUNDED},
    S.CANCELLED: set(),  # terminal
    S.REFUNDED:  set(),  # terminal
}


class InvalidTransition(Exception):
    pass


class Order:
    def __init__(self):
        self.state = S.CREATED

    def transition(self, new: State) -> None:
        if not isinstance(new, State):
            raise TypeError(f"expected State, got {type(new).__name__}")
        if new not in TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise InvalidTransition(f"{self.state.value} -> {new.value}")
        self.state = new
```

Usage:

```python
o = Order()
o.transition(S.PAID)
o.transition(S.SHIPPED)
o.transition(S.CREATED)   # raises InvalidTransition: shipped -> created
```

Assumptions: paid → cancelled/refunded allowed (pre-shipment); shipped → refunded not allowed (must deliver first); cancelled and refunded are terminal.

Skipped: persistence and concurrency. When storing in a DB, make the update atomic (`UPDATE ... WHERE id=? AND state=?`, check rowcount) so concurrent requests can't both pass the check. Also skipped: transition side effects (payment capture, stock release), added when needed.

## large-s2 / apidesign

> Design the REST API for a multi-tenant document store: resources, auth model, pagination, versioning and error format. Explain the decisions.

### vanilla: 3384 answer tokens, 3384 billed, 141 lines

~~~
# Multi-tenant Document Store REST API

## 1. Tenancy model

**Decision: tenant is derived from the credential, not (only) the URL.** Every token is bound to exactly one tenant (or an explicit set for operators). The URL still carries the tenant for clarity and cacheability:

```
/v1/tenants/{tenantId}/...
```

The server checks that `{tenantId}` matches the token's tenant claim and returns `404` (not `403`) on mismatch, so tenant existence isn't leaked. Putting the tenant in the path makes logs, rate limits, and routing simple. Deriving it from the token means a bug in path handling can't silently cross tenants. Enforce at the data layer too (row-level security or a mandatory tenant-scoped query wrapper), not just in the handler.

## 2. Resources

```
/v1/tenants/{t}
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections                       # logical grouping (like buckets/folders)
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}/versions
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}/versions/{n}
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}/content   # binary/blob body
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents:search        # custom method
/v1/tenants/{t}/api-keys
/v1/tenants/{t}/members
/v1/tenants/{t}/roles
/v1/tenants/{t}/audit-events                      # read-only
/v1/tenants/{t}/usage                             # read-only quotas/metering
```

Key choices:

- **Metadata vs. content split.** `documents/{d}` is JSON metadata (id, name, content type, size, labels, timestamps, etag, current version). `/content` is the payload. This lets listings stay small and lets large uploads use streaming, range requests, or signed URLs to object storage (`POST .../documents/{d}/content:initiateUpload` returns a pre-signed URL for >N MB).
- **Immutable versions.** Writing content creates a new version; `versions/{n}` is read-only. Restore is `POST .../versions/{n}:restore`.
- **Custom actions** use `:verb` suffixes (`:search`, `:restore`, `:move`) rather than overloading PUT/PATCH.
- **IDs** are opaque, server-generated (ULID/UUIDv7: sortable, non-guessable enough combined with authz). Human names are a mutable field, not an identifier.

### Verbs

| Operation | Method | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Create | `POST /documents` | Returns `201` + `Location`. Accepts `Idempotency-Key`. |
| Read | `GET` | Supports `ETag`, `If-None-Match` → `304`. |
| Update metadata | `PATCH` (JSON Merge Patch) | Requires `If-Match`; `412` on stale. |
| Replace content | `PUT .../content` | Requires `If-Match`; creates new version. |
| Delete | `DELETE` | Soft delete by default (`204`); `?permanent=true` needs elevated scope. |

Optimistic concurrency via `ETag`/`If-Match` is required on mutations to prevent lost updates, which is the most common correctness bug in document stores.

## 3. Auth model

**Authentication**
- **Humans / apps:** OAuth 2.0 / OIDC, with short-lived JWT access tokens (5–15 min) containing `sub`, `tenant_id`, `scope`, and `roles`.
- **Machines:** tenant-scoped API keys (prefix + secret, e.g. `dk_live_abc123.…`), stored hashed, individually revocable, with optional expiry and scope restrictions. Exchanged for a short-lived token, or accepted directly as `Authorization: Bearer`.
- Always TLS; never accept credentials in query strings.

**Authorization**
- RBAC at the tenant level (`owner`, `admin`, `editor`, `viewer`) plus **scopes** on tokens (`documents:read`, `documents:write`, `members:admin`), where effective permission is the intersection of role and scope.
- Optional per-collection ACLs for finer grain. Start with collection-level rather than per-document to keep permission checks and list queries cheap.
- Cross-tenant sharing, if needed, is an explicit grant resource (`/shares`), never a relaxation of tenant checks.

**Tenant isolation extras:** per-tenant rate limits and quotas (`429` with `Retry-After`, `RateLimit-*` headers), per-tenant encryption keys (optional BYOK), and audit events for all auth-relevant actions.

## 4. Pagination

**Decision: cursor-based, not offset.** Offsets get slow and inconsistent on large, changing collections.

```
GET /v1/tenants/t1/collections/c1/documents?page_size=50&page_token=eyJ...
```

```json
{
  "data": [ ... ],
  "next_page_token": "eyJ...",
  "has_more": true
}
```

- `page_size` default 50, max 200.
- The token is opaque (base64 of last sort key + filter hash, signed so clients can't tamper or reuse it with different filters, and bound to the tenant).
- Ordering must be total and stable: `(sort_field, id)`.
- Filtering and sorting: `?filter=label:invoice AND created_at>2026-01-01`, `?order_by=-updated_at`. Whitelist sortable fields so each is backed by an index.
- No total count by default (expensive); offer `?include_total=true` with an approximate count.
- Use `Link` headers in addition if you want standards-based navigation.

## 5. Versioning

Two distinct things:

1. **API version:** major version in the URL path (`/v1/`). It's the most visible, cache-friendly, and easy to route. Within a major version, only **additive** changes are allowed (new fields, new optional params, new endpoints); clients must ignore unknown fields. Breaking changes go to `/v2/`, with a deprecation policy: `Deprecation` and `Sunset` headers, a minimum 12-month overlap, and per-tenant usage reports of who still calls old versions.
2. **Document versions:** a resource (`/versions/{n}`) as above, not tied to the API version.

If you need finer-grained opt-in to behavior changes within a major version, use a date-pinned header (`API-Version: 2026-11-01`) per tenant, as Stripe does. Probably overkill initially.

## 6. Error format

Use **RFC 9457 Problem Details** (`application/problem+json`) with a few extensions:

```json
{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/problems/version-conflict",
  "title": "Version conflict",
  "status": 412,
  "detail": "The document was modified since the ETag you supplied.",
  "instance": "/v1/tenants/t1/collections/c1/documents/d9",
  "code": "DOCUMENT_VERSION_CONFLICT",
  "request_id": "req_01HZX...",
  "errors": [
    { "field": "labels[2]", "code": "TOO_LONG", "message": "Max 64 characters." }
  ]
}
```

- `type` (stable URI) and `code` (stable machine string) are what clients branch on; `title`/`detail` are for humans and may change.
- `errors[]` for field-level validation failures (`422`), so all problems are reported at once.
- `request_id` is also returned in an `X-Request-Id` header and logged, to support debugging.
- Status code usage: `400` malformed, `401` unauthenticated, `403` authenticated but not permitted (within your own tenant), `404` not found or cross-tenant, `409` state conflict (name already exists), `412` failed precondition (ETag), `413` too large, `422` semantic validation, `429` rate/quota limited, `5xx` server fault. Never leak internals or stack traces.

## 7. Cross-cutting details

- **Idempotency:** `Idempotency-Key` on POSTs, stored per tenant for 24h.
- **Bulk operations:** `POST .../documents:batchGet` / `:batchCreate` (cap at 100 items, per-item status in response) rather than N round trips.
- **Async work** (large exports, reindex): return `202` with an `/operations/{id}` resource to poll, plus optional webhooks (signed with HMAC, per-tenant secret).
- **Field selection:** `?fields=id,name,updated_at` to cut payloads.
- **Caching:** ETags on everything; `Cache-Control: private` for tenant data.
- **Observability:** per-tenant metrics, audit log of all writes, with `actor`, `tenant`, `action`, `target`, `request_id`.

## Summary of the main trade-offs

| Decision | Chosen | Alternative | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tenant location | Path + token-bound | Header or subdomain only | Explicit, loggable, but verified against the credential |
| Pagination | Opaque cursors | Offset/limit | Stable and fast on large, mutating sets |
| Versioning | URL major, additive within | Header/media type | Simple, visible, cache-friendly |
| Concurrency | ETag + `If-Match` | Last-write-wins | Prevents silent lost updates |
| Errors | RFC 9457 + stable `code` | Ad-hoc JSON | Standard, tooling-friendly, evolvable |
| Content | Separate from metadata | Inline in JSON | Streaming, big files, cheap listings |

If useful, I can turn this into an OpenAPI 3.1 spec skeleton or go deeper on any one area (e.g., the ACL model or the search endpoint).
~~~

~~~
# Multi-tenant Document Store API

## Resources

Tenant in path. Explicit, cacheable, easy to route and rate-limit.

```
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/users
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/api-keys
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/collections
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/collections/{collection_id}
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/collections/{collection_id}/documents
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}/versions
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}/versions/{n}
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}/content
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents/{doc_id}/permissions
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents:search        (POST, custom verb)
/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/documents:batchGet      (POST)
```

Decisions:
- **Tenant in path, not header.** Header-only tenant is easy to forget, and it breaks logging, caching and URL-based authz. Server must check token's tenant == path tenant. Mismatch → `404`, not `403`, so tenant existence does not leak.
- **Docs addressable flat by `doc_id`** (globally unique ULID/UUIDv7) so moving between collections keeps URL stable. Collection is a filter/attribute: `POST .../collections/{id}/documents` creates, `PATCH .../documents/{id}` with `collection_id` moves.
- **Metadata separate from `/content`.** Metadata is small JSON. Content may be big/binary: support `Range`, streaming, and pre-signed upload (`POST .../documents` returns `upload_url` for blobs over a size limit).
- **Versions are read-only subresource.** Every write makes a new immutable version. Restore = `POST .../versions/{n}:restore`.
- **Verbs via `:action` suffix** only where CRUD does not fit (search, batch, restore).

### Methods

| Op | Request | Success |
|---|---|---|
| Create | `POST .../documents` | `201` + `Location` + `ETag` |
| Read | `GET .../documents/{id}` | `200` + `ETag` |
| Partial update | `PATCH` (JSON Merge Patch) | `200` |
| Replace | `PUT` | `200` |
| Delete | `DELETE` | `204` (soft delete, `deleted_at`, purge job later) |
| Create with retry safety | `POST` + `Idempotency-Key` | same response on replay |

## Auth model

Two layers: **authentication** (who) and **authorization** (what, within tenant).

- **Humans / apps acting for users:** OAuth 2.1 / OIDC, short-lived JWT access tokens (5–15 min) + refresh tokens. Authorization Code + PKCE.
- **Service-to-service:** OAuth client credentials, or tenant-scoped API keys (`Authorization: Bearer sk_...`). Keys stored hashed, shown once, with scopes, expiry, rotation, and revoke.
- **Token claims:** `sub`, `tenant_id`, `scope`, `roles`. Server compares `tenant_id` claim to path. Users in many tenants get one token per tenant (or token exchange), never a wildcard token.
- **Authorization:** RBAC for coarse (`tenant_admin`, `editor`, `viewer`) plus per-document/collection ACLs via `/permissions`. Scopes (`documents:read`, `documents:write`, `admin`) cap what any token can do regardless of role. Effective permission = scope ∩ role/ACL.
- **Isolation in depth:** do not trust app-layer checks alone. Enforce `tenant_id` in the data layer (Postgres RLS, or mandatory tenant-prefixed keys/partitions). Every query takes tenant from the authenticated context, never from request body.
- **Extras:** per-tenant rate limits and quotas (`429` + `Retry-After`, `RateLimit-*` headers), audit log of all mutations, TLS only, optional per-tenant encryption keys (BYOK).

## Pagination

**Cursor-based**, not offset. Offset gets slow and skips/duplicates rows under concurrent writes.

```
GET /v1/tenants/t1/collections/c1/documents?limit=50&page_token=eyJ...
```

```json
{
  "data": [ ... ],
  "next_page_token": "eyJpZCI6...",
  "has_more": true
}
```

- `limit` default 50, max 200. Server clamps.
- Token is opaque (base64 of sort key + id, signed). Clients must not parse it. Lets you change internals later.
- Stable sort: `(sort_field, id)` tiebreaker. Default `created_at desc`.
- Filtering: `?filter=` simple fields (`status=published`, `updated_after=`), sort with `?sort=-updated_at`. Complex queries go to `POST :search` so query bodies do not hit URL limits.
- No total count by default (expensive). Optional `?include_total=true`, approximate.
- Optional `Link` header (`rel="next"`) as well as body field.

## Versioning

Two separate things:

1. **API version:** major in URL (`/v1/`). Simple, visible, easy to route and cache. Within a major, only additive changes (new fields, new optional params, new endpoints). Clients must ignore unknown fields. Breaking change → `/v2`, with `/v1` kept for a published deprecation window (≥12 months), signalled by `Deprecation` and `Sunset` headers. Optional date-pinned minor behaviour via `API-Version: 2026-11-01` header if finer control is needed. Skip it initially.
2. **Document version:** integer `version` field + `ETag`. Gives optimistic concurrency:
   - `GET` returns `ETag: "7"`.
   - `PUT`/`PATCH`/`DELETE` require `If-Match: "7"`. Stale → `412 Precondition Failed`. Missing → `428 Precondition Required`.
   - Prevents lost updates between concurrent editors without locks.

## Error format

RFC 9457 `application/problem+json`, plus a machine code and field errors.

```json
{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/errors/validation-failed",
  "title": "Validation failed",
  "status": 422,
  "code": "validation_failed",
  "detail": "2 fields are invalid.",
  "instance": "/v1/tenants/t1/documents",
  "request_id": "req_01HZX...",
  "errors": [
    { "field": "title", "code": "required", "message": "title is required" },
    { "field": "tags[2]", "code": "too_long", "message": "max 32 chars" }
  ]
}
```

Rules:
- Standard problem fields, plus `code` (stable, enum, for client branching), `request_id` (matches `X-Request-Id` header and server logs), and `errors[]` for per-field detail.
- Never leak internals (stack traces, SQL, other tenants' ids).
- Status mapping:

| Status | Use |
|---|---|
| 400 | Malformed JSON / bad params |
| 401 | Missing/invalid/expired token (`WWW-Authenticate`) |
| 403 | Authenticated, lacks permission within own tenant |
| 404 | Not found **or** cross-tenant access |
| 409 | Conflict (duplicate name, state conflict) |
| 412 / 428 | ETag mismatch / missing `If-Match` |
| 413 | Content too large |
| 415 | Unsupported media type |
| 422 | Semantic validation failure |
| 429 | Rate/quota exceeded + `Retry-After` |
| 5xx | Server fault. `503` + `Retry-After` when shedding load |

- Idempotent retry of `POST` with same `Idempotency-Key` but different body → `422` `idempotency_key_reuse`.

## Other notes

- Timestamps RFC 3339 UTC. IDs are opaque prefixed strings (`doc_01H...`).
- Async long ops (bulk import, reindex, export): `202 Accepted` + `Location: /v1/tenants/{t}/operations/{op_id}`, poll or webhook.
- Webhooks per tenant for `document.created|updated|deleted`, HMAC-signed.
- Publish OpenAPI 3.1 spec as source of truth, generate SDKs and contract tests from it.
~~~

### ponytail: 2178 answer tokens, 2926 billed, 91 lines

~~~
# Multi-tenant document store REST API

## Resources

Tenant identity comes from the credential, not the URL. Clients never send a tenant ID on the data plane, so they can't ask for another tenant's data by mistake or on purpose.

| Resource | Endpoints |
|---|---|
| Collections | `POST/GET /v1/collections`, `GET/PATCH/DELETE /v1/collections/{id}` |
| Documents | `POST/GET /v1/documents` (filter with `?collection=`), `GET/PATCH/DELETE /v1/documents/{id}` |
| Content | `PUT/GET /v1/documents/{id}/content` (raw bytes, with its own `Content-Type`) |
| Revisions | `GET /v1/documents/{id}/revisions`, `GET /v1/documents/{id}/revisions/{rev}`, `POST /v1/documents/{id}/revisions/{rev}:restore` |
| Permissions | `GET/PUT/DELETE /v1/collections/{id}/permissions/{principal}` |
| API keys | `POST/GET/DELETE /v1/api-keys` |
| Tenants (platform operators only) | `POST/GET /v1/admin/tenants`, `PATCH /v1/admin/tenants/{id}` |

**Decisions**
- **Metadata and content are separate.** Metadata is small JSON, which is cheap to list and patch. Content can be large and non-JSON, so it gets its own endpoint, `Range` support, and optionally a presigned upload.
- **Revisions are a sub-resource.** Every content write creates an immutable revision, and "restore" creates a new revision rather than rewriting history.
- **Permissions sit on collections, not documents.** Per-document ACLs multiply quickly, so documents inherit from their collection.
- **The admin plane is under `/v1/admin`.** It uses a different credential type, so a tenant token can never reach it.
- **Soft delete.** `DELETE` marks the document deleted. `?include_deleted=true` shows it to admins, and a retention job purges it later.

## Auth model

- **Users:** OIDC/OAuth2 bearer JWTs. The token carries `tid` (tenant), `sub`, and scopes such as `documents:read`, `documents:write`, and `admin`.
- **Services:** API keys (`Authorization: Bearer sk_...`). Each key is bound to one tenant and a scope set. Only a hash is stored, the secret is shown once, and keys are revocable and can expire.
- **Two checks on every request:**
  1. **Tenant isolation:** `tenant_id` is a mandatory column on every table. Enforce it in the data layer (Postgres row-level security, or a scoped repository that can't run an unscoped query), not in handlers someone might forget to filter.
  2. **Authorization:** the scope must allow the verb, and the principal needs a role (viewer, editor, owner) on the collection.
- **Cross-tenant IDs return `404`, not `403`.** This avoids leaking which IDs exist.
- **Rate limits and quotas are per tenant.** One noisy tenant gets `429` with `Retry-After` without affecting the others.

## Pagination

Cursor-based:

```
GET /v1/documents?limit=50&cursor=eyJ...&sort=-created_at
→ { "data": [...], "next_cursor": "eyJ..." | null }
```

- **Why not offset:** offsets skip or duplicate rows under concurrent writes and get slow on deep pages.
- **Cursors are opaque** (base64 of the last sort key plus `id` as a tiebreaker), so the encoding can change without breaking clients.
- **Limits:** default `limit` of 50, maximum 200. Over the maximum, clamp it or return `400`, but be consistent.
- **Filtering** uses plain query params (`?collection=`, `?updated_after=`, `?content_type=`). There is no total count by default, because it's expensive. Offer `?include_total=true` if a UI needs it.

## Versioning

- **Major version in the path (`/v1`).** It is visible in logs and curl commands and easy to route, which matters more than header purity.
- **Within a major version, only additive changes:** new optional fields, endpoints, and enum values. Clients must ignore unknown fields.
- **Breaking changes go to `/v2`.** The old version gets `Deprecation` and `Sunset` headers and runs in parallel for a published window.
- **Document revisions are a separate concern, handled with ETags.** Reads return `ETag: "<rev>"`. `PATCH` and `PUT` require `If-Match`, otherwise `428`. A stale value returns `412`. This prevents lost updates between concurrent editors.

## Error format

RFC 9457 `application/problem+json`:

```json
{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/errors/validation-failed",
  "title": "Validation failed",
  "status": 422,
  "code": "validation_failed",
  "detail": "2 fields are invalid.",
  "request_id": "req_8f3a...",
  "errors": [{ "field": "name", "message": "must be 1-255 characters" }]
}
```

| Status | When |
|---|---|
| 400 | Malformed request or bad cursor |
| 401 | Missing or invalid credential |
| 403 | Authenticated but lacking scope or role |
| 404 | Not found, or belongs to another tenant |
| 409 | Name conflict, or an idempotency key reused with a different body |
| 412 / 428 | `If-Match` mismatch / missing |
| 413 | Content too large |
| 422 | Semantic validation failure |
| 429 | Rate or quota limit, with `Retry-After` |

**Decisions**
- **A standard format** means off-the-shelf clients can parse it.
- **`code` is a stable machine-readable string.** Clients branch on `code`, never on `title` or `detail`.
- **`request_id` is also returned as a header** so support can trace a failure.
- **`POST` accepts `Idempotency-Key`,** so retries after a network failure don't create duplicates. Store the key and response per tenant for 24 hours.

## Deliberately left out

GraphQL, a per-document ACL, bulk endpoints, and full-text search. Add search as `GET /v1/documents:search` when it's needed, and bulk endpoints when clients show they're hitting rate limits with per-item calls.
~~~

### chisle: 2231 answer tokens, 2301 billed, 87 lines

~~~
## Tenancy

Tenant comes from the credential, never from a client-supplied ID alone. Path carries it for readability and cacheability: `/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/...`. Server checks that the path tenant matches the token's tenant claim, else `404` (not `403`, so tenant existence doesn't leak). Enforce at the DB too: `tenant_id` on every row, Postgres RLS or a mandatory query scope. Never rely on handlers remembering the filter.

## Resources

```
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections                     GET, POST
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}                 GET, PATCH, DELETE
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents       GET (list/filter), POST (create)
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}   GET, PUT (replace), PATCH (merge), DELETE
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}/versions        GET
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}/versions/{n}    GET
/v1/tenants/{t}/documents:batchGet              POST
/v1/tenants/{t}/documents:search                POST (complex query body)
/v1/tenants/{t}/api-keys                        GET, POST, DELETE /{id}
/v1/tenants/{t}/members                         GET, POST, PATCH, DELETE
```

- Collections group documents and are the unit for schema, quota, and ACL.
- Versions are a read-only subresource. The history is immutable, and restoring creates a new version.
- Custom verbs (`:search`, `:batchGet`) cover what doesn't fit CRUD. Search uses POST because the query is too large or structured for a URL.
- Large binary content: `POST .../documents/{d}/content` returns a pre-signed upload URL. Keep blobs out of the JSON API.
- Concurrency: every document returns an `ETag` (the version). `PUT`/`PATCH`/`DELETE` require `If-Match`, otherwise `412`. Missing header → `428`. This prevents lost updates, which matter most in multi-writer stores.
- Idempotency: `POST` accepts an `Idempotency-Key` header (stored 24h). Safe retries without duplicate documents.

## Auth

- **Humans**: OAuth2/OIDC (auth code + PKCE) → short-lived JWT (≤15 min) with `sub`, `tenant_id`, `scopes`/`roles`.
- **Machines**: tenant-scoped API keys (`Authorization: Bearer sk_...`). Store only a hash, show the key once, support rotation and per-key scopes and expiry.
- **Authorization**: RBAC per tenant (`owner`, `admin`, `editor`, `viewer`), plus optional per-collection ACLs. Scopes like `documents:read`, `documents:write`, `collections:admin`.
- Rate limit and quota per tenant, to contain noisy neighbors. Return `429` with `Retry-After` and `RateLimit-*` headers.
- Cross-tenant access goes through explicit sharing grants, not an ID swap.

## Pagination

Cursor-based. Offset breaks under concurrent writes and gets slow at depth.

```
GET .../documents?limit=50&cursor=eyJ...&sort=-updated_at
→ { "data": [...], "next_cursor": "eyJ...", "has_more": true }
```

- Cursor is an opaque, signed token encoding the last sort key plus a unique tiebreaker (`id`). Stable ordering requires the tiebreaker.
- `limit` default 50, max 200, with an enforced cap.
- No total count by default (expensive). Offer `?include=total` or a separate `:count`.
- Filters use `filter[field]=value` or a `q` param. Reject unindexed sort fields with `400`.

## Versioning

- Major version in the URL path (`/v1`). It is visible, cacheable, and easy to route.
- Within a major version, only additive changes are allowed: new fields, endpoints, and optional params. Clients must ignore unknown fields.
- Breaking change → `/v2`, with `Deprecation` and `Sunset` headers on `/v1` and at least 12 months of overlap.
- Document *content* versions are separate from API versions (the `versions` subresource and the `ETag`).
- Optional date-pinned behavior tweaks via an `API-Version: 2026-11-01` header, only if you need finer granularity than majors.

## Error format

RFC 9457 `application/problem+json`:

```json
{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/errors/version-conflict",
  "title": "Version conflict",
  "status": 412,
  "detail": "Document changed since ETag \"7\"; current is \"8\".",
  "instance": "/v1/tenants/acme/collections/inv/documents/d_123",
  "request_id": "req_8fK2...",
  "errors": [{ "pointer": "/title", "code": "too_long", "message": "max 200 chars" }]
}
```

- `type` is a stable, machine-readable code that clients branch on. `title` and `detail` are for humans.
- `errors[]` holds per-field validation failures using JSON Pointers.
- `request_id` is also returned in an `X-Request-Id` header and logged, so support can trace it.
- Status mapping: `400` malformed, `401` no or bad credentials, `403` authenticated but not allowed, `404` missing or cross-tenant, `409` unique conflict, `412` ETag mismatch, `413` too large, `422` semantic validation, `428` precondition required, `429` rate/quota.
- Never leak internals (stack traces, SQL, other tenants' IDs).

## Rationale

- **Path-based tenant plus token claim**: the path makes routing, logs, and rate limits obvious, and the token claim is the real authority. Mismatch is a hard `404`.
- **Optimistic concurrency (ETag)**: cheaper than locks, and it fits a document store where read-modify-write races are the main data-loss risk.
- **Cursor pagination**: correct under writes and constant-cost at any depth, at the price of no random page access.
- **URL major versions**: easiest for clients and gateways. Additive-only evolution keeps `v2` rare.
- **Problem+JSON**: it's a standard, so client libraries and tooling already understand it.

Skipped: webhooks, bulk import/export jobs, and field-level encryption. Add them once you have concrete requirements.
~~~

## large-s2 / architecture

> Explain how you would migrate a monolith to services without a big-bang rewrite, covering ordering, data ownership, and how to keep it shippable throughout.

### vanilla: 2216 answer tokens, 2216 billed, 66 lines
