# Migrating a monolith to services incrementally

The approach is the strangler fig: build new capability around the monolith, route traffic to it piece by piece, and shrink the monolith until it's gone or small enough to leave alone. Every step must be independently releasable and reversible.

## 0. Preconditions (do these first)

- **Modularize inside the monolith first.** If you can't draw clean boundaries in-process, you'll just build a distributed monolith. Enforce module boundaries with lint rules or build-level dependency checks, and make modules talk through explicit interfaces.
- **Observability and tests.** You need tracing, per-endpoint latency/error metrics, and characterization tests around the area you'll extract. Without a baseline you can't tell whether the new service is correct.
- **Deployment basics.** CI/CD, feature flags, and a routing layer (gateway or reverse proxy) in front of the monolith. The routing layer is what lets you shift traffic gradually.

## 1. Ordering: what to extract and when

Choose candidates by value and by coupling, not by what's easiest to describe.

1. **Start with a low-risk, loosely coupled edge capability** (notifications, search, file/report generation, auth proxying). It teaches you the pipeline, deployment, and ops patterns at low stakes.
2. **Then extract what hurts**: components with different scaling profiles, a high change rate that blocks other teams, or distinct reliability or compliance requirements.
3. **Leave the tangled core (often billing, orders, or the central domain model) for last**, or never. Extract it only once boundaries are proven and the team is practiced.
4. **Prefer vertical slices** (UI/API + logic + data for one business capability) over horizontal layers. Extracting "the data access layer" or "the business logic layer" gives no independent deployability.
5. **Use dependency analysis**: look at call graphs and table access to find seams. Components with few inbound dependencies and clear data ownership go first. Leaf dependencies before hubs.

## 2. Data ownership (the hard part)

Code is easy to move; data is where migrations fail. Rule: **each table has exactly one owning service, and everyone else goes through its API or events.**

Stage it:

1. **Logical separation in the shared DB.** Assign tables to modules, give the module its own schema, and ban cross-schema joins and foreign keys in code review or via DB permissions. Fix violations while still in one process.
2. **Extract the service reading/writing the same DB** (a short-lived intermediate state). This de-risks the code move separately from the data move. Don't stay here long, since it's a shared-database coupling.
3. **Move the data.** Options:
   - **Dual-write with verification**: write to old and new, compare, then flip reads, then stop writing the old. Risk: partial failures cause divergence, so prefer the options below when possible.
   - **CDC / outbox pattern**: the owner writes state plus an outbox row in one transaction; a relay publishes events. Consumers and the new store replicate from the stream. This is more reliable than dual-writes.
   - **Backfill + sync + cutover**: bulk copy, keep in sync via CDC, run shadow reads comparing results, then cut over reads, then writes, then drop the old tables after a soak period.
4. **Replace joins** with API calls, local read-model replicas fed by events, or denormalized copies. Accept eventual consistency where the business allows it.
5. **Transactions across boundaries**: avoid them by drawing boundaries around consistency needs. Where unavoidable, use sagas with compensating actions and idempotent handlers. If two things must be atomically consistent, that's a signal they belong in the same service.
6. **Reference data and IDs**: keep stable IDs across systems; use the owner's ID, don't re-key.

## 3. Keeping it shippable throughout

- **Route, don't replace.** Put a gateway/proxy in front. Per-route or per-tenant flags send a percentage of traffic to the new service, and rollback is a config change.
- **Staged rollout per capability**: shadow traffic (new service receives copies, responses discarded but compared) → internal users → 1% → 10% → 50% → 100% → delete the old code path. Deleting old code is part of "done"; otherwise you accumulate two systems.
- **Parallel runs / diff testing** for logic-heavy areas (pricing, calculations): run both, return the old result, log mismatches, and fix until the mismatch rate is acceptable.
- **Anti-corruption layer**: a translation layer between the new service's clean model and the monolith's legacy model, so legacy quirks don't leak into new code.
- **Contract tests** (consumer-driven) between the monolith and each service, plus backward-compatible API/schema changes (expand → migrate → contract).
- **Small increments, trunk-based**: each PR leaves main deployable. Extraction is many small steps (add the interface, add the flagged client, move the code, shift traffic, delete), not a long-lived branch.
- **Keep shipping features.** Make new features in the area being extracted land in the new service when possible, and don't freeze the monolith. Agree on rules so teams don't build new features in code scheduled for removal.
- **Reversibility checkpoints**: each phase has a defined rollback. After data cutover, rollback requires reverse sync, so keep the old store updated for a soak period before dropping it.

## 4. Operational concerns to budget for

- Timeouts, retries with backoff, circuit breakers, and idempotency on every new network call (a former function call is now a failure-prone RPC).
- Distributed tracing and correlation IDs from day one.
- Per-service ownership, on-call, SLOs, and a paved road (templates, CI, deploy tooling) so each new service isn't bespoke.
- Auth propagation and service-to-service security.

## 5. Knowing when to stop

Success isn't "zero monolith." Stop when the remaining monolith is stable, modular, and not blocking teams. Track outcomes (deploy frequency, lead time, incident rate, team independence), not number of services. If the modularized monolith already gives you most of the benefit, extracting further may only add cost.

## Typical sequence

1. Add gateway, observability, flags; modularize internally.
2. Extract one edge service end to end (pipeline learning).
3. Separate data ownership logically, then physically, for the next candidates.
4. Introduce events/outbox for cross-service data flow.
5. Extract pain-point capabilities with shadow → canary → cutover → delete.
6. Reassess the core; extract or keep as a well-factored modular monolith.
~~~

### caveman: 1873 answer tokens, 1928 billed, 76 lines

~~~
## Core approach: strangler fig

Monolith stay live. Carve out one capability at a time behind a seam. Old path and new path coexist until old path dies. Every step is a normal deploy, not a project milestone.

## 0. Prep (before any extraction)

- **Modularize inside monolith first.** Enforce boundaries in-process (packages, module visibility rules, import linting). If you can't separate it as modules, you can't separate it as services.
- **Find seams.** Use domain boundaries (bounded contexts), not tech layers. Use change-coupling from git history and call graphs to see what really moves together.
- **Build platform basics:** CI/CD per service, tracing, structured logs, health checks, config/secrets. Cheap now, painful later.
- **Add a routing layer** (gateway/proxy or in-app facade) so traffic can switch per route or per feature flag.

## 1. Ordering: what to extract first

Pick by **low coupling + high value + low data entanglement**.

1. **First: easy, edge, stateless-ish.** Notifications, email, PDF generation, search, auth-adjacent. Teach the team the pipeline with low blast radius.
2. **Next: high-churn or high-scale parts.** Pull out what's blocking team independence or needs separate scaling.
3. **Last: the core.** Heavily shared domain (orders, accounts, pricing) with tangled tables. By then you have tooling and scar tissue.
4. **Leave alone:** stable, rarely changed, no pain. Monolith core may stay forever. That's fine.

Rule: extract **leaves of the dependency graph first**, those with many inbound callers but few outbound ones are easier to wrap behind API. Things that call everything else go late.

## 2. Per-extraction playbook

1. **Define contract** (API/events) at the seam, inside the monolith. Monolith code calls it through the interface.
2. **Build the service** behind the same interface. Initially it may just wrap monolith logic or call back in.
3. **Shadow/dark launch.** Send copy of traffic to the new service, compare outputs, discard new result.
4. **Canary** with feature flag: 1% → 10% → 100%. Instant rollback = flip flag.
5. **Delete old code** from monolith once stable. Skip this step and you get a distributed monolith plus the old one.

## 3. Data ownership (hardest part)

Principle: **one service owns each table; everyone else goes through its API or events.** No shared-DB writes across services.

Stages:

1. **Shared DB, owned by code.** Service gets the tables logically. Others stop touching them directly. Enforce via DB roles/permissions, then lint for cross-module queries.
2. **Break joins.** Replace cross-domain SQL joins with API calls, or with local read models fed by events. Store IDs not foreign keys across boundaries. Drop cross-boundary FK constraints.
3. **Split the schema.** Separate schema in same DB first, then separate DB instance.
4. **Migrate data live.** Expand/contract:
   - Dual-write (or CDC/outbox) to old and new store
   - Backfill history
   - Verify via reconciliation job
   - Switch reads
   - Switch writes, stop dual-write
   - Drop old
5. **Consistency.** Lose cross-service ACID transactions. Use:
   - **Outbox pattern** so DB write and event publish are atomic
   - **Sagas** with compensating actions for multi-service workflows
   - Idempotent consumers (events arrive at-least-once)
   - Accept eventual consistency where business allows; keep strongly-consistent things in one service

Reference data and shared concepts (e.g. "customer"): one owner, others hold cached/replicated copy with ID.

## 4. Keeping it shippable throughout

- **Trunk-based, small steps.** Each PR deployable. No long-lived migration branch.
- **Feature flags** for every routing/read/write switch. Rollback in seconds, no redeploy.
- **Expand/contract schema changes**: additive first, remove later, never breaking in one step.
- **Backward-compatible contracts.** Version APIs/events; consumer-driven contract tests catch breaks in CI.
- **Parallel run + reconciliation** before cutting reads/writes.
- **Keep feature work flowing.** Teams change code in the monolith during migration. Rule: new features go in the new service if that area is already extracted; otherwise in the monolith's module behind the seam.
- **Observability per step.** Compare latency, error rate, business metrics (orders/min) old vs new. Define rollback triggers before the canary.
- **Time-box each extraction** (weeks, not quarters). If one drags, stop at "well-modularized in monolith" and reassess.

## 5. Pitfalls

- **Distributed monolith:** services that must deploy together or share a DB. Worse than before.
- **Too-fine services early.** Start coarse; split further only when pain proves it.
- **Chatty sync calls** across boundary → latency and cascading failure. Use timeouts, retries with backoff, circuit breakers, or async events.
- **Skipping deletion** of old code.
- **No clear done criteria.** Define the target: which pain (deploy speed, scaling, team autonomy) are you fixing? Stop when fixed.

## Summary

Modularize in place → build routing seam → extract leaves first → move data in stages (logical ownership, break joins, split schema, expand/contract live migration) → flag, shadow, canary, delete old. Each step is small, reversible, and releasable.
~~~

# Migrating a monolith to services without a rewrite

The approach is the strangler fig: new behavior grows around the monolith, traffic shifts piece by piece, and the old code is deleted last. Every step is a normal, releasable change.

## 0. Before extracting anything

- **Put a routing seam in front.** A gateway, reverse proxy, or in-process interface lets you send a given call to old or new code. It is also your rollback switch.
- **Make the monolith observable.** You need request tracing, per-endpoint latency and error rates, and a map of which modules call which. Without a baseline you can't tell whether an extraction made things worse.
- **Modularize inside the monolith first.** Enforce module boundaries with lint rules or architecture tests, and have modules talk through explicit interfaces rather than reaching into each other's tables. This is the cheapest step, and many teams find a modular monolith is enough. If a boundary can't hold in-process, it won't survive a network.

## 1. Ordering: what to extract, and when

Pick candidates by these criteria, in roughly this order:

1. **First extraction is a low-risk learning slice.** It should have few inbound dependencies and be non-critical, such as notifications, document rendering, or search. The goal is to build the platform muscle: CI/CD, deploys, tracing, on-call, and service templates. Don't make the checkout flow your first service.
2. **Then extract where it hurts.** Good candidates are modules with a different scaling profile, a different release cadence, a team that is blocked by the shared deploy, or a need for different tech or compliance isolation. Extract for a reason you can name.
3. **Leave the tangled core for last.** Shared-entity modules like "User" or "Order" are hard, so tackle them once the tooling and patterns are proven. Often you hollow them out gradually and what remains is a smaller core.
4. **Extract leaves before roots.** Prefer modules that others call but that call little themselves. Pulling out something with many outbound calls into the monolith creates chatty, circular network traffic.

Don't extract for resume or fashion reasons. If a module has no scaling, team, or release-cadence pain, leave it.

## 2. Data ownership

Data is the hard part, and code extraction without data extraction gives you a distributed monolith.

**Principle: one owner per table.** Only the owning service reads or writes it directly. Everyone else uses its API or consumes its events.

**Sequence for each extraction:**

1. **Identify the data the module owns** and find every other module that touches it, through joins, foreign keys, or direct queries.
2. **Cut the cross-module access in the monolith first.** Replace joins and direct reads with calls through the module's interface. Cross-module joins become application-level composition. Foreign keys across the boundary become plain IDs, with integrity enforced by the owner.
3. **Stand up the service against the shared database**, still reading its own tables only. This is a transitional state that lets you ship and verify behavior before moving data.
4. **Move the data.** Create the service's own schema or database and sync with change data capture or dual writes. Backfill, then verify by comparing the two sides. Cut reads over, then writes, then stop the sync.
5. **Drop the old tables** from the monolith only after a soak period.

**Handling what the split breaks:**

- **Transactions across the boundary** can't be ACID anymore. Use sagas or an outbox pattern with idempotent consumers, and design for eventual consistency. If two things truly must be atomic, that's a signal they belong in the same service.
- **Reference data other services need** (e.g. user names) gets replicated through events into local read models, rather than fetched synchronously on every request.
- **Reporting and analytics** that used to join everything should move to a warehouse fed by events or CDC, not live cross-service queries.

## 3. Keeping it shippable throughout

- **Branch by abstraction.** Put an interface in front of the module in the monolith, add a client implementation that calls the new service, and switch between them with a flag. Both paths ship to production the whole time.
- **Shift traffic gradually.** Use shadow traffic first (call the new service, discard the result, compare outputs), then canary at 1%, 10%, 50%, 100%, with the flag as an instant rollback. Keep the old code path alive until the new one has run at 100% for a full business cycle.
- **Small, reversible steps.** Each PR leaves main deployable. Never have a long-lived "migration branch."
- **Contract tests at every new seam.** These catch API drift between the monolith and the service without needing full end-to-end environments.
- **Make the network safe.** Add timeouts, retries with backoff, circuit breakers, and idempotency keys on every new remote call. A call that was a function a week ago is now a failure mode.
- **Feature work continues.** Freeze nothing. New features in an extracted area go in the service. Features elsewhere go in the monolith, which keeps the business supportive of the migration.
- **Delete aggressively.** After each cutover, remove the old code, flag, and tables. Otherwise you carry both systems forever, which is the most common way these migrations stall.

## 4. Guardrails and stop conditions

- **Set a service-per-team limit.** Don't create more services than you can staff and operate. Too fine-grained a split is worse than the monolith.
- **Track outcomes, not service count.** Watch deploy frequency, lead time, incident rate, and the pain you set out to fix. If they aren't improving after a couple of extractions, stop and reassess.
- **Be willing to stop early.** A smaller monolith plus three or four well-chosen services is a perfectly good end state. The migration is done when the pain is gone, not when the monolith is empty.

## Summary

Add a routing seam and observability, tidy boundaries inside the monolith, extract a low-risk service first to build the platform, then extract by pain, leaves first. Give every table a single owner and cut cross-module data access before moving data. Keep everything shippable with flags, shadow traffic, canaries, and aggressive deletion of the old path.
~~~

Strangler fig: new capability grows around the monolith, traffic shifts slice by slice, old code gets deleted only after it's dead. Main stays deployable every step.

**Prep (before extracting anything)**
- Put a routing seam in front: gateway/reverse proxy, or an in-process interface. Without it nothing can be shifted incrementally.
- Get CI, one-command deploy, observability (tracing, per-endpoint metrics) and feature flags working first. Services multiply ops cost; if deploys are already painful, fix that before splitting.
- Make the monolith modular internally (enforced package boundaries, no cross-module table access). This is often 70% of the value and is fully reversible. Some modules never need to leave.

**Ordering**
- Pick seams by business domain (bounded context), not technical layer. Look for low coupling, clear API, independent change/scale pressure.
- First extraction: low-risk, loosely coupled, but real (notifications, search, auth, file/media, reporting). Goal is to build the pipeline and learn, not to win big.
- Then extract by pain: highest change rate, different scaling profile, team ownership conflicts.
- Leave the tangled core (orders, billing, user hub) for last, or never. Use git history and call-graph/coupling analysis to find real seams, not the org-chart guess.
- One extraction at a time per team. Finish (old code deleted) before starting the next, or you end up with a distributed monolith plus a monolith.

**Per-slice recipe**
1. Define the contract (API/events) around existing behavior; wrap monolith calls behind that interface first.
2. Build the service behind it; run in shadow/dark mode: mirror traffic, diff responses, don't serve them.
3. Flag-based canary: 1% → 10% → 100%, per-tenant or per-endpoint, instant rollback by flipping the flag.
4. Delete the monolith code path and its tables after a soak period. Skipping this step is the usual failure.

**Data ownership**
- Rule: each table has exactly one owning service; others go through API or events, never shared-DB reads/writes. Shared DB = coupled deploys, the "distributed monolith".
- Sequence per slice: (a) service reads/writes the monolith's DB through its own schema/views, with ownership declared; (b) split into its own database; (c) migrate data.
- Data migration without downtime: expand/contract. Dual-write or CDC (Debezium-style) from old → new, backfill, verify with reconciliation jobs, flip reads, flip writes, stop old writes. Keep the old copy until verified.
- Replace joins with: API composition, local read-model replicas fed by events, or denormalized copies of the few fields you need (name, status). Accept eventual consistency, and decide per case where it isn't acceptable (then the boundary is probably wrong).
- Cross-service transactions: avoid by choosing boundaries well; otherwise sagas with compensations, idempotent handlers, and the outbox pattern for reliable event publishing.
- Hardest parts: the shared `users`/`accounts` table, reports that join everything, and foreign keys. Reporting → separate warehouse fed by CDC.

**Staying shippable**
- No long-lived migration branch; trunk-based, small PRs, every merge deployable.
- Every change backward-compatible: additive schema changes, versioned APIs/events, tolerant readers, expand → migrate → contract.
- Contract tests (e.g. Pact) between monolith and service so either side can deploy independently.
- Flags on every cutover; rollback = flag flip, not redeploy.
- Feature work continues in parallel; new features go into the new service if the domain is already extracted, otherwise in the monolith's module, never in a "temporary" new place.
- Track metrics: lead time, change failure rate, p99 latency (network hops add up), % traffic on the new path, lines/tables remaining in the monolith.

**Pitfalls**
- Chatty sync calls between services → latency and cascading failures: use timeouts, retries with backoff, circuit breakers, and async events where possible.
- Splitting too fine too early: start coarse, split further only on evidence.
- Auth, config, logging, and tracing as shared concerns: standardize them early (shared library or platform) so each service doesn't reinvent them.
- Stopping halfway is a valid outcome if the remaining monolith is healthy. The goal is independent deployability where it pays off, not service count.
~~~

## large-s2 / authflow

> Implement email-and-password signup and login with password hashing, session issuing, and rate limiting on failed attempts. Show the code.

### vanilla: 3369 answer tokens, 4246 billed, 193 lines

~~~
Here's a self-contained Express + SQLite implementation using argon2id for hashing. I haven't run it.

```bash
npm i express cookie-parser better-sqlite3 argon2
```

```js
// auth.mjs
import express from "express";
import cookieParser from "cookie-parser";
import Database from "better-sqlite3";
import argon2 from "argon2";
import crypto from "node:crypto";

const db = new Database("app.db");
db.pragma("journal_mode = WAL");
db.exec(`
  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
    password_hash TEXT NOT NULL,
    created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
  );
  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sessions (
    token_hash TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    user_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    expires_at INTEGER NOT NULL
  );
  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS attempts (
    key TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    failures INTEGER NOT NULL,
    window_start INTEGER NOT NULL
  );
`);

// ---- config ----
const SESSION_TTL_MS = 7 * 24 * 3600 * 1000;
const WINDOW_MS = 15 * 60 * 1000;
const MAX_FAILS_PER_ACCOUNT_IP = 5;
const MAX_FAILS_PER_IP = 20;
const ARGON_OPTS = { type: argon2.argon2id, memoryCost: 19456, timeCost: 2, parallelism: 1 };

// Used to equalize timing when the email doesn't exist.
const DUMMY_HASH = await argon2.hash("dummy-password", ARGON_OPTS);

// ---- helpers ----
const sha256 = (s) => crypto.createHash("sha256").update(s).digest("hex");
const normEmail = (e) => String(e ?? "").trim().toLowerCase();
const validEmail = (e) => e.length <= 254 && /^[^\s@]+@[^\s@]+\.[^\s@]+$/.test(e);
const validPassword = (p) => typeof p === "string" && p.length >= 10 && p.length <= 128;

// ---- rate limiting (failed attempts only) ----
const getAttempt = db.prepare("SELECT failures, window_start FROM attempts WHERE key = ?");
const bumpAttempt = db.prepare(`
  INSERT INTO attempts (key, failures, window_start) VALUES (?, 1, ?)
  ON CONFLICT(key) DO UPDATE SET
    failures = CASE WHEN ? - window_start > ? THEN 1 ELSE failures + 1 END,
    window_start = CASE WHEN ? - window_start > ? THEN ? ELSE window_start END`);
const clearAttempt = db.prepare("DELETE FROM attempts WHERE key = ?");

function retryAfterSec(key, max) {
  const row = getAttempt.get(key);
  if (!row) return 0;
  const now = Date.now();
  if (now - row.window_start > WINDOW_MS) return 0;
  return row.failures >= max ? Math.ceil((row.window_start + WINDOW_MS - now) / 1000) : 0;
}
function recordFailure(key) {
  const now = Date.now();
  bumpAttempt.run(key, now, now, WINDOW_MS, now, WINDOW_MS, now);
}

// ---- sessions ----
function issueSession(res, userId) {
  const token = crypto.randomBytes32?.() ?? crypto.randomBytes(32).toString("base64url");
  db.prepare("INSERT INTO sessions (token_hash, user_id, expires_at) VALUES (?, ?, ?)")
    .run(sha256(token), userId, Date.now() + SESSION_TTL_MS);
  res.cookie("sid", token, {
    httpOnly: true,
    secure: process.env.NODE_ENV === "production",
    sameSite: "lax",
    maxAge: SESSION_TTL_MS,
    path: "/",
  });
}

function requireAuth(req, res, next) {
  const token = req.cookies.sid;
  if (!token) return res.status(401).json({ error: "Not authenticated" });
  const row = db.prepare(`
    SELECT u.id, u.email FROM sessions s JOIN users u ON u.id = s.user_id
    WHERE s.token_hash = ? AND s.expires_at > ?`).get(sha256(token), Date.now());
  if (!row) return res.status(401).json({ error: "Not authenticated" });
  req.user = row;
  next();
}

// ---- app ----
const app = express();
app.set("trust proxy", 1); // set correctly for your deployment, or req.ip is spoofable
app.use(express.json({ limit: "10kb" }));
app.use(cookieParser());

app.post("/signup", async (req, res) => {
  const email = normEmail(req.body?.email);
  const { password } = req.body ?? {};
  if (!validEmail(email)) return res.status(400).json({ error: "Invalid email" });
  if (!validPassword(password))
    return res.status(400).json({ error: "Password must be 10-128 characters" });

  const hash = await argon2.hash(password, ARGON_OPTS);
  try {
    const info = db.prepare("INSERT INTO users (email, password_hash, created_at) VALUES (?, ?, ?)")
      .run(email, hash, Date.now());
    issueSession(res, info.lastInsertRowid);
    res.status(201).json({ id: info.lastInsertRowid, email });
  } catch (e) {
    if (e.code === "SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_UNIQUE")
      return res.status(409).json({ error: "Email already registered" });
    throw e;
  }
});

app.post("/login", async (req, res) => {
  const email = normEmail(req.body?.email);
  const password = req.body?.password;
  const ip = req.ip;
  const acctKey = `acct:${email}|${ip}`;
  const ipKey = `ip:${ip}`;

  const wait = Math.max(
    retryAfterSec(acctKey, MAX_FAILS_PER_ACCOUNT_IP),
    retryAfterSec(ipKey, MAX_FAILS_PER_IP),
  );
  if (wait > 0) {
    res.set("Retry-After", String(wait));
    return res.status(429).json({ error: "Too many failed attempts. Try again later." });
  }

  const user = typeof password === "string" && password.length <= 128
    ? db.prepare("SELECT id, email, password_hash FROM users WHERE email = ?").get(email)
    : undefined;

  // Always run a verify so response time doesn't reveal whether the email exists.
  const ok = await argon2
    .verify(user?.password_hash ?? DUMMY_HASH, typeof password === "string" ? password : "")
    .catch(() => false);

  if (!user || !ok) {
    recordFailure(acctKey);
    recordFailure(ipKey);
    return res.status(401).json({ error: "Invalid email or password" });
  }

  clearAttempt.run(acctKey);
  issueSession(res, user.id);
  res.json({ id: user.id, email: user.email });
});

app.post("/logout", (req, res) => {
  if (req.cookies.sid) db.prepare("DELETE FROM sessions WHERE token_hash = ?").run(sha256(req.cookies.sid));
  res.clearCookie("sid", { path: "/" });
  res.status(204).end();
});

app.get("/me", requireAuth, (req, res) => res.json(req.user));

// Periodic cleanup
setInterval(() => {
  db.prepare("DELETE FROM sessions WHERE expires_at < ?").run(Date.now());
  db.prepare("DELETE FROM attempts WHERE ? - window_start > ?").run(Date.now(), WINDOW_MS);
}, 3600_000).unref();

app.listen(3000, () => console.log("listening on :3000"));
```

One line is a leftover slip: in `issueSession`, replace the token line with the plain version:

```js
const token = crypto.randomBytes(32).toString("base64url");
```

**Design notes**
- **Hashing:** argon2id with per-hash random salt (built into the library). Parameters follow OWASP's minimum recommendation, so raise them if your hardware allows.
- **Sessions:** the cookie holds a 256-bit random token and the DB stores only its SHA-256. A DB leak doesn't yield usable sessions. The cookie is `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax`, and `Secure` in production.
- **Rate limiting:** failures are tracked per email+IP (5 per 15 min) and per IP (20 per 15 min). Keying on email+IP stops an attacker from locking a victim out from elsewhere, and the IP-wide cap limits password spraying across many emails. A successful login clears the account counter. It returns `429` with `Retry-After`.
- **Enumeration:** login returns one generic error and does equal-cost hashing. Signup still reveals whether an email is registered (409). To avoid that, use an email-verification flow.
- **Not included, but you'll want them:**
  - CSRF tokens for state-changing routes if you rely on cross-site cookies.
  - Email verification and password reset.
  - Session rotation on password change.
  - Checking passwords against a breached list.
  - A shared store such as Redis instead of SQLite if you run multiple instances.
~~~

~~~
Stack: Node + Express, `better-sqlite3`, `argon2`. Install: `npm i express better-sqlite3 argon2 cookie-parser`.

```js
// auth.js
const express = require("express");
const Database = require("better-sqlite3");
const argon2 = require("argon2");
const crypto = require("crypto");
const cookieParser = require("cookie-parser");

const db = new Database("app.db");
db.exec(`
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  password_hash TEXT NOT NULL,
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sessions (
  token_hash TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id),
  expires_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS login_attempts (
  key TEXT PRIMARY KEY,          -- "ip|email"
  failures INTEGER NOT NULL,
  window_start INTEGER NOT NULL,
  locked_until INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0
);
`);

const SESSION_TTL_MS = 7 * 24 * 3600 * 1000;
const MAX_FAILS = 5;
const WINDOW_MS = 15 * 60 * 1000;
const LOCK_MS = 15 * 60 * 1000;

const ARGON_OPTS = { type: argon2.argon2id, memoryCost: 19456, timeCost: 2, parallelism: 1 };
let DUMMY_HASH; // for constant-ish timing when user missing
argon2.hash("dummy-password", ARGON_OPTS).then(h => (DUMMY_HASH = h));

const sha256 = s => crypto.createHash("sha256").update(s).digest("hex");
const normEmail = e => String(e || "").trim().toLowerCase();
const validEmail = e => e.length <= 254 && /^[^\s@]+@[^\s@]+\.[^\s@]+$/.test(e);
const validPassword = p => typeof p === "string" && p.length >= 10 && p.length <= 128;

// ---------- rate limiting ----------
function checkLock(key) {
  const row = db.prepare("SELECT * FROM login_attempts WHERE key=?").get(key);
  const now = Date.now();
  if (row && row.locked_until > now) return Math.ceil((row.locked_until - now) / 1000);
  return 0;
}
function recordFailure(key) {
  const now = Date.now();
  const row = db.prepare("SELECT * FROM login_attempts WHERE key=?").get(key);
  if (!row || now - row.window_start > WINDOW_MS) {
    db.prepare("INSERT OR REPLACE INTO login_attempts(key,failures,window_start,locked_until) VALUES(?,?,?,0)")
      .run(key, 1, now);
    return;
  }
  const failures = row.failures + 1;
  const lockedUntil = failures >= MAX_FAILS ? now + LOCK_MS : 0;
  db.prepare("UPDATE login_attempts SET failures=?, locked_until=? WHERE key=?")
    .run(failures, lockedUntil, key);
}
const clearFailures = key => db.prepare("DELETE FROM login_attempts WHERE key=?").run(key);

// ---------- sessions ----------
function issueSession(res, userId) {
  const token = crypto.randomBytes(32).toString("base64url");
  db.prepare("INSERT INTO sessions(token_hash,user_id,expires_at) VALUES(?,?,?)")
    .run(sha256(token), userId, Date.now() + SESSION_TTL_MS);
  res.cookie("sid", token, {
    httpOnly: true,
    secure: process.env.NODE_ENV === "production",
    sameSite: "lax",
    maxAge: SESSION_TTL_MS,
    path: "/",
  });
}

function requireAuth(req, res, next) {
  const token = req.cookies.sid;
  if (!token) return res.status(401).json({ error: "unauthenticated" });
  const row = db.prepare(
    "SELECT user_id, expires_at FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=?"
  ).get(sha256(token));
  if (!row || row.expires_at < Date.now()) return res.status(401).json({ error: "unauthenticated" });
  req.userId = row.user_id;
  next();
}

// ---------- routes ----------
const app = express();
app.use(express.json({ limit: "10kb" }));
app.use(cookieParser());

app.post("/signup", async (req, res) => {
  const email = normEmail(req.body.email);
  const { password } = req.body;
  if (!validEmail(email)) return res.status(400).json({ error: "invalid email" });
  if (!validPassword(password))
    return res.status(400).json({ error: "password must be 10-128 chars" });

  const hash = await argon2.hash(password, ARGON_OPTS);
  try {
    const info = db.prepare("INSERT INTO users(email,password_hash,created_at) VALUES(?,?,?)")
      .run(email, hash, Date.now());
    issueSession(res, info.lastInsertRowid);
    res.status(201).json({ ok: true });
  } catch (e) {
    if (String(e.code).startsWith("SQLITE_CONSTRAINT"))
      return res.status(409).json({ error: "email unavailable" });
    throw e;
  }
});

app.post("/login", async (req, res) => {
  const email = normEmail(req.body.email);
  const password = typeof req.body.password === "string" ? req.body.password : "";
  const key = `${req.ip}|${email}`;

  const wait = checkLock(key);
  if (wait) {
    res.set("Retry-After", String(wait));
    return res.status(429).json({ error: "too many attempts" });
  }

  const user = db.prepare("SELECT id,password_hash FROM users WHERE email=?").get(email);
  // always run a verify so timing doesn't reveal whether email exists
  const ok = await argon2
    .verify(user ? user.password_hash : DUMMY_HASH, password.slice(0, 128))
    .catch(() => false);

  if (!user || !ok) {
    recordFailure(key);
    return res.status(401).json({ error: "invalid email or password" });
  }

  clearFailures(key);
  if (argon2.needsRehash(user.password_hash, ARGON_OPTS)) {
    const newHash = await argon2.hash(password, ARGON_OPTS);
    db.prepare("UPDATE users SET password_hash=? WHERE id=?").run(newHash, user.id);
  }
  issueSession(res, user.id);
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

app.post("/logout", requireAuth, (req, res) => {
  db.prepare("DELETE FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=?").run(sha256(req.cookies.sid));
  res.clearCookie("sid");
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

app.get("/me", requireAuth, (req, res) => {
  const u = db.prepare("SELECT id,email FROM users WHERE id=?").get(req.userId);
  res.json(u);
});

// purge expired sessions hourly
setInterval(() => db.prepare("DELETE FROM sessions WHERE expires_at<?").run(Date.now()), 3600e3).unref();

app.listen(3000);
```

Design notes:

- **Hashing**: argon2id, random salt built in. `needsRehash` upgrades old hashes on login.
- **Sessions**: 256-bit random token in `httpOnly` cookie. DB stores only SHA-256 of token, so DB leak ≠ usable sessions.
- **Rate limit**: key = IP + email. 5 fails in 15 min → 15 min lock, returns `429` + `Retry-After`. Success clears counter. Per-IP+email key stops attacker locking out victim from all IPs, but distributed attack on one email still gets 5 tries per IP. Add a global per-email limit or CAPTCHA if that matters.
- **Enumeration**: login gives same error + dummy hash verify when user missing. Signup returns `409` and so leaks email existence. Fix: always return "check your email" and do email verification.
- **Behind proxy**: set `app.set("trust proxy", 1)` or `req.ip` is the proxy's IP.

Missing for production:

- CSRF token on state-changing routes (`sameSite: lax` helps, not full).
- Email verification and password reset.
- HTTPS.
- Coarse per-IP limit on `/signup` and `/login` (e.g. `express-rate-limit`), since argon2 is CPU-heavy and a DoS vector.
- Session rotation or revocation on password change.
- Redis instead of SQLite if running multiple instances.
~~~

### ponytail: 2684 answer tokens, 3236 billed, 145 lines

~~~
```python
# app.py: pip install flask
import hashlib, hmac, os, re, secrets, sqlite3, time
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify, g

DB = os.environ.get("DB", "auth.db")
SESSION_TTL = 7 * 86400
MAX_FAILS, WINDOW = 5, 15 * 60          # 5 failures per 15 min, per email and per IP

app = Flask(__name__)

def db():
    if "db" not in g:
        g.db = sqlite3.connect(DB)
    return g.db

@app.teardown_appcontext
def close(_):
    d = g.pop("db", None)
    if d: d.close()

def init():
    with sqlite3.connect(DB) as c:
        c.executescript("""
        CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users(id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, email TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL, pw TEXT NOT NULL);
        CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sessions(token_hash TEXT PRIMARY KEY, user_id INT NOT NULL, expires REAL NOT NULL);
        CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS fails(key TEXT NOT NULL, ts REAL NOT NULL);
        CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS fails_key ON fails(key, ts);""")

# --- password hashing (stdlib scrypt, per-user random salt) ---
def hash_pw(pw: str) -> str:
    salt = os.urandom(16)
    h = hashlib.scrypt(pw.encode(), salt=salt, n=2**14, r=8, p=1)
    return salt.hex() + "$" + h.hex()

def check_pw(pw: str, stored: str) -> bool:
    salt, h = stored.split("$")
    cand = hashlib.scrypt(pw.encode(), salt=bytes.fromhex(salt), n=2**14, r=8, p=1)
    return hmac.compare_digest(cand.hex(), h)

DUMMY = hash_pw("dummy")  # equalizes timing when the email doesn't exist

# --- rate limiting ---
def keys(email):
    return [f"email:{email}", f"ip:{request.remote_addr}"]

def limited(email) -> bool:
    cutoff = time.time() - WINDOW
    return any(
        db().execute("SELECT COUNT(*) FROM fails WHERE key=? AND ts>?", (k, cutoff)).fetchone()[0] >= MAX_FAILS
        for k in keys(email))

def record_fail(email):
    now = time.time()
    db().executemany("INSERT INTO fails VALUES(?,?)", [(k, now) for k in keys(email)])
    db().execute("DELETE FROM fails WHERE ts<?", (now - WINDOW,))
    db().commit()

# --- sessions: random token to client, only its hash stored server-side ---
def issue_session(user_id):
    token = secrets.token_urlsafe(32)
    th = hashlib.sha256(token.encode()).hexdigest()
    db().execute("INSERT INTO sessions VALUES(?,?,?)", (th, user_id, time.time() + SESSION_TTL))
    db().commit()
    r = jsonify(ok=True)
    r.set_cookie("sid", token, max_age=SESSION_TTL, httponly=True,
                 secure=not app.debug, samesite="Lax")
    return r

def current_user():
    t = request.cookies.get("sid")
    if not t: return None
    row = db().execute("SELECT user_id FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=? AND expires>?",
                       (hashlib.sha256(t.encode()).hexdigest(), time.time())).fetchone()
    return row[0] if row else None

# --- routes ---
def creds():
    j = request.get_json(silent=True) or {}
    return str(j.get("email", "")).strip().lower(), str(j.get("password", ""))

@app.post("/signup")
def signup():
    email, pw = creds()
    if not re.fullmatch(r"[^@\s]+@[^@\s]+\.[^@\s]+", email) or len(email) > 254:
        return jsonify(error="invalid email"), 400
    if not 10 <= len(pw) <= 128:
        return jsonify(error="password must be 10-128 chars"), 400
    try:
        cur = db().execute("INSERT INTO users(email,pw) VALUES(?,?)", (email, hash_pw(pw)))
        db().commit()
    except sqlite3.IntegrityError:
        return jsonify(error="email already registered"), 409
    return issue_session(cur.lastrowid), 201

@app.post("/login")
def login():
    email, pw = creds()
    if limited(email):
        return jsonify(error="too many attempts, try later"), 429
    row = db().execute("SELECT id, pw FROM users WHERE email=?", (email,)).fetchone()
    ok = check_pw(pw, row[1] if row else DUMMY) and row is not None
    if not ok:
        record_fail(email)
        return jsonify(error="invalid email or password"), 401
    db().execute("DELETE FROM fails WHERE key=?", (f"email:{email}",))
    return issue_session(row[0])

@app.post("/logout")
def logout():
    t = request.cookies.get("sid")
    if t:
        db().execute("DELETE FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=?", (hashlib.sha256(t.encode()).hexdigest(),))
        db().commit()
    r = jsonify(ok=True); r.delete_cookie("sid"); return r

@app.get("/me")
def me():
    uid = current_user()
    return (jsonify(user_id=uid), 200) if uid else (jsonify(error="unauthenticated"), 401)

# --- self-check: python app.py ---
if __name__ == "__main__":
    DB = ":memory:" if False else "test_auth.db"
    if os.path.exists(DB): os.remove(DB)
    init()
    c = app.test_client()
    assert c.post("/signup", json={"email": "a@b.co", "password": "correct horse"}).status_code == 201
    assert c.get("/me").status_code == 200                      # signup cookie works
    assert c.post("/signup", json={"email": "a@b.co", "password": "correct horse"}).status_code == 409
    c2 = app.test_client()
    for _ in range(5):
        assert c2.post("/login", json={"email": "a@b.co", "password": "wrong-password"}).status_code == 401
    assert c2.post("/login", json={"email": "a@b.co", "password": "correct horse"}).status_code == 429  # locked out
    os.remove(DB); print("ok")
```

Run `python app.py` for the self-check. For real use, call `init()` at startup and serve behind HTTPS (the cookie is `Secure`).

Skipped, with the point to add each:
- **Proxy IPs:** behind a reverse proxy, `request.remote_addr` is the proxy's address. Add `ProxyFix` when you deploy that way, or the per-IP limit hits everyone at once.
- **CSRF tokens:** `SameSite=Lax` plus JSON-only POSTs covers the basics. Add tokens if you accept form posts or cross-site requests.
- **Email verification and password reset:** add when you need them.
- **Account enumeration:** signup returns 409 for a taken email, which reveals that the account exists. Login is uniform. Switch signup to email-confirmation if that matters.
- **Lockout as DoS:** the per-email limit lets an attacker lock a victim out for 15 minutes. That is the usual tradeoff. Add CAPTCHA or per-(email, IP) keys if it becomes a problem.
~~~

### chisle: 2539 answer tokens, 2917 billed, 126 lines

Assumed stack: Node + Express + `better-sqlite3` (`npm i express better-sqlite3`). Hashing uses stdlib `crypto.scrypt`, so no extra dependency.

```js
// auth.js
const express = require("express");
const Database = require("better-sqlite3");
const crypto = require("crypto");
const { promisify } = require("util");
const scrypt = promisify(crypto.scrypt);

const db = new Database("app.db");
db.exec(`
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users(
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, email TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL, pw TEXT NOT NULL);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sessions(
  token_hash TEXT PRIMARY KEY, user_id INTEGER NOT NULL, expires INTEGER NOT NULL);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS fails(
  key TEXT NOT NULL, at INTEGER NOT NULL);
CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS fails_key ON fails(key, at);
`);

const MAX_FAILS = 5, WINDOW = 15 * 60e3, SESSION_TTL = 7 * 864e5;
const sha = (s) => crypto.createHash("sha256").update(s).digest("hex");

async function hashPw(pw) {
  const salt = crypto.randomBytes(16);
  const h = await scrypt(pw, salt, 64);
  return `${salt.toString("hex")}:${h.toString("hex")}`;
}
async function verifyPw(pw, stored) {
  const [s, h] = stored.split(":");
  const calc = await scrypt(pw, Buffer.from(s, "hex"), 64);
  return crypto.timingSafeEqual(calc, Buffer.from(h, "hex"));
}
// dummy hash: unknown email costs same time as wrong password
const DUMMY = hashPw("dummy-password");

function issueSession(res, userId) {
  const token = crypto.randomBytes(32).toString("base64url");
  db.prepare("INSERT INTO sessions VALUES(?,?,?)")
    .run(sha(token), userId, Date.now() + SESSION_TTL);
  res.cookie?.("sid", token); // overridden below; see setCookie
  res.setHeader("Set-Cookie",
    `sid=${token}; HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax; Path=/; Max-Age=${SESSION_TTL / 1000}`);
}

function sessionUser(req) {
  const m = /(?:^|;\s*)sid=([^;]+)/.exec(req.headers.cookie || "");
  if (!m) return null;
  return db.prepare(
    `SELECT u.id, u.email FROM sessions s JOIN users u ON u.id=s.user_id
     WHERE s.token_hash=? AND s.expires>?`).get(sha(m[1]), Date.now());
}

const validEmail = (e) => typeof e === "string" && e.length <= 254 && /^[^@\s]+@[^@\s]+\.[^@\s]+$/.test(e);
const validPw = (p) => typeof p === "string" && p.length >= 10 && p.length <= 128;

const app = express();
app.use(express.json({ limit: "10kb" }));

app.post("/signup", async (req, res) => {
  const email = String(req.body.email ?? "").trim().toLowerCase();
  const { password } = req.body;
  if (!validEmail(email)) return res.status(400).json({ error: "invalid email" });
  if (!validPw(password)) return res.status(400).json({ error: "password must be 10-128 chars" });
  try {
    const { lastInsertRowid } = db.prepare("INSERT INTO users(email,pw) VALUES(?,?)")
      .run(email, await hashPw(password));
    issueSession(res, lastInsertRowid);
    res.status(201).json({ ok: true });
  } catch (e) {
    if (e.code === "SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_UNIQUE") return res.status(409).json({ error: "email taken" });
    throw e;
  }
});

app.post("/login", async (req, res) => {
  const email = String(req.body.email ?? "").trim().toLowerCase();
  const { password } = req.body;
  if (!validEmail(email) || !validPw(password))
    return res.status(401).json({ error: "invalid credentials" });

  const key = `${email}|${req.ip}`;
  const now = Date.now();
  db.prepare("DELETE FROM fails WHERE at<?").run(now - WINDOW);
  const { n, oldest } = db.prepare("SELECT COUNT(*) n, MIN(at) oldest FROM fails WHERE key=?").get(key);
  if (n >= MAX_FAILS) {
    const wait = Math.ceil((oldest + WINDOW - now) / 1000);
    return res.set("Retry-After", String(wait)).status(429).json({ error: "too many attempts" });
  }

  const user = db.prepare("SELECT id, pw FROM users WHERE email=?").get(email);
  const ok = await verifyPw(password, user ? user.pw : await DUMMY) && !!user;
  if (!ok) {
    db.prepare("INSERT INTO fails VALUES(?,?)").run(key, now);
    return res.status(401).json({ error: "invalid credentials" });
  }
  db.prepare("DELETE FROM fails WHERE key=?").run(key);
  issueSession(res, user.id);
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

app.post("/logout", (req, res) => {
  const m = /(?:^|;\s*)sid=([^;]+)/.exec(req.headers.cookie || "");
  if (m) db.prepare("DELETE FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=?").run(sha(m[1]));
  res.setHeader("Set-Cookie", "sid=; HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax; Path=/; Max-Age=0");
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

app.get("/me", (req, res) => {
  const u = sessionUser(req);
  u ? res.json(u) : res.status(401).json({ error: "unauthenticated" });
});

app.set("trust proxy", false); // set to proxy hop count if behind one, else req.ip is wrong
app.listen(3000);
```

Remove the stray `res.cookie?.("sid", token);` line in `issueSession`; it's redundant with the `Set-Cookie` header below it.

Design notes:
- Session tokens stored as SHA-256 → DB leak doesn't yield usable sessions.
- Rate limit keyed on `email|ip`: stops brute force on one account from one IP, and a remote attacker can't lock out the real user from other IPs. Doesn't stop distributed attacks across many IPs; add a per-email global cap (higher threshold) if that matters.
- Cookie is `Secure` → needs HTTPS (browsers exempt `localhost`).

skipped: CSRF tokens (SameSite=Lax covers most cases; add for cross-site POST needs), email verification, password reset, signup rate limiting, signup enumeration (409 reveals registered emails; fix with email-verification flow), session rotation/cleanup job for expired rows. Add when going to production.
~~~

## large-s2 / csvpipeline

> Write a script that reads a large CSV of transactions, validates each row, aggregates totals per customer, and writes a report, handling malformed rows without dying. Show the code.

### vanilla: 2385 answer tokens, 2438 billed, 163 lines

~~~
# Transaction CSV aggregator

This is a streaming Python script using only the standard library. It reads one row at a time, so memory use depends on the number of customers rather than the file size. It uses `Decimal` for money, writes rejected rows to a separate file with the reason, and exits non-zero only on fatal problems such as a missing file or header.

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Aggregate per-customer transaction totals from a large CSV.

Usage:
    python aggregate.py transactions.csv -o report.csv -r rejects.csv

Expected columns: transaction_id, customer_id, amount, date (YYYY-MM-DD)
"""
import argparse
import csv
import logging
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
from datetime import datetime
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation

REQUIRED = ["transaction_id", "customer_id", "amount", "date"]

log = logging.getLogger("aggregate")


class RowError(ValueError):
    """Raised when a single row fails validation."""


def validate(row: dict) -> tuple[str, str, Decimal]:
    """Return (transaction_id, customer_id, amount) or raise RowError."""
    # DictReader puts overflow fields under key None and fills missing ones with None.
    if None in row:
        raise RowError("too many fields")
    if any(row[c] is None for c in REQUIRED):
        raise RowError("too few fields")

    txn_id = row["transaction_id"].strip()
    cust = row["customer_id"].strip()
    if not txn_id:
        raise RowError("empty transaction_id")
    if not cust:
        raise RowError("empty customer_id")

    try:
        amount = Decimal(row["amount"].strip())
    except InvalidOperation:
        raise RowError(f"invalid amount {row['amount']!r}")
    if not amount.is_finite():
        raise RowError(f"non-finite amount {row['amount']!r}")

    try:
        datetime.strptime(row["date"].strip(), "%Y-%m-%d")
    except ValueError:
        raise RowError(f"invalid date {row['date']!r}")

    return txn_id, cust, amount


def process(in_path, rejects_path, dedupe):
    totals = defaultdict(lambda: [Decimal("0"), 0])  # customer -> [sum, count]
    seen = set() if dedupe else None
    n_total = n_ok = n_bad = 0

    with open(in_path, newline="", encoding="utf-8-sig", errors="replace") as fin, \
         open(rejects_path, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as frej:

        reader = csv.DictReader(fin)
        missing = [c for c in REQUIRED if c not in (reader.fieldnames or [])]
        if missing:
            raise SystemExit(f"Missing required columns: {missing}")

        rej = csv.writer(frej)
        rej.writerow(["line", "reason", "raw"])

        while True:
            try:
                row = next(reader)
            except StopIteration:
                break
            except csv.Error as e:
                # Malformed CSV structure (e.g. bad quoting); reader can continue.
                n_total += 1
                n_bad += 1
                rej.writerow([reader.line_num, f"csv error: {e}", ""])
                continue

            n_total += 1
            try:
                txn_id, cust, amount = validate(row)
                if seen is not None:
                    if txn_id in seen:
                        raise RowError(f"duplicate transaction_id {txn_id!r}")
                    seen.add(txn_id)
            except RowError as e:
                n_bad += 1
                rej.writerow([reader.line_num, str(e), "|".join(str(v) for v in row.values())])
                continue

            t = totals[cust]
            t[0] += amount
            t[1] += 1
            n_ok += 1

            if n_total % 1_000_000 == 0:
                log.info("processed %d rows (%d rejected)", n_total, n_bad)

    return totals, n_total, n_ok, n_bad


def write_report(path, totals):
    with open(path, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        w = csv.writer(f)
        w.writerow(["customer_id", "transaction_count", "total_amount"])
        for cust in sorted(totals):
            total, count = totals[cust]
            w.writerow([cust, count, f"{total:.2f}"])


def main():
    p = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=__doc__,
                                formatter_class=argparse.RawDescriptionHelpFormatter)
    p.add_argument("input")
    p.add_argument("-o", "--output", default="report.csv")
    p.add_argument("-r", "--rejects", default="rejects.csv")
    p.add_argument("--dedupe", action="store_true",
                   help="reject repeated transaction_ids (uses extra memory)")
    args = p.parse_args()

    logging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO, format="%(asctime)s %(message)s")

    try:
        totals, n_total, n_ok, n_bad = process(args.input, args.rejects, args.dedupe)
    except OSError as e:
        sys.exit(f"I/O error: {e}")

    write_report(args.output, totals)
    log.info("done: %d rows, %d valid, %d rejected, %d customers",
             n_total, n_ok, n_bad, len(totals))
    log.info("report: %s | rejects: %s", args.output, args.rejects)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

## Design notes

- **Streaming:** `csv.DictReader` iterates lazily, so a multi-GB file is fine. Memory grows only with distinct customers, plus transaction IDs if you pass `--dedupe`.
- **Malformed rows:**
  - Wrong field counts, bad amounts, bad dates and empty IDs raise `RowError`.
  - Each such row is logged to `rejects.csv` with its line number and reason, and processing continues.
  - Structural CSV errors from `csv.Error` are caught around `next(reader)`, so one bad row doesn't abort the run.
- **Money:** `Decimal` avoids float drift. `NaN` and `Infinity` parse as valid `Decimal`s, so they are rejected explicitly.
- **Encoding:** `utf-8-sig` strips a BOM, and `errors="replace"` keeps bad bytes from crashing the run.
- **Fatal errors:** A missing file or required column exits with a clear message instead of a traceback.

## Adjustments to consider

- Negative amounts (refunds) are accepted. Add a check in `validate` if they should be rejected.
- For very large inputs where speed matters, pandas with `chunksize` or Polars' lazy scan will be faster, but they make row-level reject reporting more awkward.
- If the customer count itself is too large for memory, aggregate in chunks to temporary files, or load into SQLite and use `GROUP BY`.
~~~

### caveman: 1932 answer tokens, 2007 billed, 124 lines

~~~
Python script. Stream row by row (constant memory), `Decimal` for money, bad rows go to rejects file with reason.

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Aggregate transaction totals per customer.

Usage: python aggregate.py transactions.csv report.csv [rejects.csv]

Expected columns: transaction_id, customer_id, amount, currency, timestamp
"""
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
from datetime import datetime
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation

REQUIRED = ["transaction_id", "customer_id", "amount", "currency", "timestamp"]
VALID_CURRENCIES = {"USD", "EUR", "GBP", "INR"}


class RowError(Exception):
    pass


def validate(row):
    """Return cleaned (customer_id, currency, amount, txn_id) or raise RowError."""
    if None in row:  # DictReader puts extra fields under key None
        raise RowError("too many fields")
    if any(row.get(c) is None for c in REQUIRED):
        raise RowError("too few fields")

    txn_id = row["transaction_id"].strip()
    cust = row["customer_id"].strip()
    cur = row["currency"].strip().upper()
    if not txn_id:
        raise RowError("empty transaction_id")
    if not cust:
        raise RowError("empty customer_id")
    if cur not in VALID_CURRENCIES:
        raise RowError(f"bad currency {cur!r}")

    try:
        amount = Decimal(row["amount"].strip())
    except InvalidOperation:
        raise RowError(f"bad amount {row['amount']!r}")
    if not amount.is_finite():  # rejects NaN / Infinity
        raise RowError(f"non-finite amount {row['amount']!r}")

    try:
        datetime.fromisoformat(row["timestamp"].strip())
    except ValueError:
        raise RowError(f"bad timestamp {row['timestamp']!r}")

    return cust, cur, amount, txn_id


def main(src, report_path, rejects_path):
    # totals[(customer, currency)] = [sum, count]
    totals = defaultdict(lambda: [Decimal("0"), 0])
    seen_ids = set()  # drop if txn ids too many for RAM; then dedupe downstream
    good = bad = 0

    # newline="" required by csv module; errors="replace" survives bad bytes
    with open(src, newline="", encoding="utf-8", errors="replace") as f, \
         open(rejects_path, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as rej:
        reader = csv.DictReader(f)
        missing = [c for c in REQUIRED if c not in (reader.fieldnames or [])]
        if missing:
            sys.exit(f"Missing required columns: {missing}")

        rej_writer = csv.writer(rej)
        rej_writer.writerow(["line", "reason", "raw_row"])

        while True:
            try:
                row = next(reader)
            except StopIteration:
                break
            except csv.Error as e:  # unbalanced quotes, NUL bytes, etc.
                bad += 1
                rej_writer.writerow([reader.line_num, f"csv error: {e}", ""])
                continue

            try:
                cust, cur, amount, txn_id = validate(row)
                if txn_id in seen_ids:
                    raise RowError(f"duplicate transaction_id {txn_id}")
            except RowError as e:
                bad += 1
                rej_writer.writerow([reader.line_num, str(e), str(row)])
                continue

            seen_ids.add(txn_id)
            t = totals[(cust, cur)]
            t[0] += amount
            t[1] += 1
            good += 1

    with open(report_path, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as out:
        w = csv.writer(out)
        w.writerow(["customer_id", "currency", "total", "txn_count"])
        for (cust, cur), (total, n) in sorted(totals.items()):
            w.writerow([cust, cur, f"{total:.2f}", n])

    print(f"valid rows:    {good}")
    print(f"rejected rows: {bad}  -> {rejects_path}")
    print(f"customers:     {len({c for c, _ in totals})}  -> {report_path}")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) < 3:
        sys.exit(__doc__)
    main(sys.argv[1], sys.argv[2], sys.argv[3] if len(sys.argv) > 3 else "rejects.csv")
```

Key choices:
- **Streaming**: `DictReader` read one row at a time. File size irrelevant. Only totals and ID set held in RAM.
- **Never dies on bad row**: `validate` raises `RowError`; loop catches, logs to rejects, continues. `csv.Error` caught around `next(reader)` too, since parser itself can fail on broken quoting.
- **`Decimal` not `float`**: no rounding drift in money sums. NaN/Infinity rejected (`Decimal("NaN")` parses fine, would poison totals).
- **Per (customer, currency)**: no summing USD with EUR.
- **Fatal only on**: missing header columns. Nothing useful to do then.
- **Memory caveat**: `seen_ids` grows per row. For hundreds of millions of rows, remove dedupe or do it in sort/DB step.

Tweak `REQUIRED`, `VALID_CURRENCIES`, and rules in `validate` to fit your schema. Want negative amounts (refunds) rejected? Add check in `validate`.
~~~

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""usage: report.py transactions.csv report.csv rejects.csv
Expects columns: customer_id, amount, date (ISO yyyy-mm-dd)."""
import csv, io, sys
from collections import defaultdict
from datetime import date
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation

REQUIRED = {"customer_id", "amount", "date"}

def validate(row):
    """Return (customer, amount) or raise ValueError."""
    if None in row or None in row.values():  # extra / missing fields
        raise ValueError("wrong column count")
    cust = row["customer_id"].strip()
    if not cust:
        raise ValueError("empty customer_id")
    try:
        amt = Decimal(row["amount"].strip())
    except InvalidOperation:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount {row['amount']!r}")
    if not amt.is_finite():
        raise ValueError(f"non-finite amount {row['amount']!r}")
    date.fromisoformat(row["date"].strip())  # raises ValueError if bad
    return cust, amt

def run(src, report, rejects):
    reader = csv.DictReader(src)
    if not REQUIRED <= set(reader.fieldnames or ()):
        sys.exit(f"missing columns: {REQUIRED - set(reader.fieldnames or ())}")
    rej = csv.writer(rejects)
    rej.writerow(["line", "reason", "raw"])
    totals = defaultdict(lambda: [0, Decimal(0)])  # cust -> [count, sum]
    good = bad = 0

    it = iter(reader)
    while True:  # manual loop so a csv.Error doesn't end the run
        try:
            row = next(it)
        except StopIteration:
            break
        except csv.Error as e:
            bad += 1
            rej.writerow([reader.line_num, f"csv error: {e}", ""])
            continue
        try:
            cust, amt = validate(row)
        except ValueError as e:
            bad += 1
            rej.writerow([reader.line_num, e, row])
            continue
        t = totals[cust]
        t[0] += 1
        t[1] += amt
        good += 1

    out = csv.writer(report)
    out.writerow(["customer_id", "transactions", "total"])
    for cust in sorted(totals):
        n, s = totals[cust]
        out.writerow([cust, n, s])
    return good, bad

def demo():
    data = ("customer_id,amount,date\n"
            "a,10.50,2026-01-01\n"
            "a,4.50,2026-01-02\n"
            "b,abc,2026-01-01\n"       # bad amount
            "c,1,2026-13-40\n"         # bad date
            ",5,2026-01-01\n"          # empty customer
            "d,1\n"                    # short row
            "e,2,2026-01-01,extra\n"   # long row
            "b,7,2026-01-03\n")
    rep, rej = io.StringIO(), io.StringIO()
    good, bad = run(io.StringIO(data), rep, rej)
    assert (good, bad) == (3, 5), (good, bad)
    assert "a,2,15.00" in rep.getvalue() and "b,1,7" in rep.getvalue()

if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) == 1:
        demo(); print("ok"); sys.exit()
    src, rpt, rjt = sys.argv[1:4]
    with open(src, newline="", encoding="utf-8", errors="replace") as s, \
         open(rpt, "w", newline="") as r, open(rjt, "w", newline="") as j:
        good, bad = run(s, r, j)
    print(f"{good} ok, {bad} rejected -> {rjt}", file=sys.stderr)
```

Run it with `python report.py in.csv report.csv rejects.csv`, or with no args to run the self-check.

- **Streaming:** it reads one row at a time, so memory scales with the number of customers rather than rows.
- **Money:** it uses `Decimal`, because floats drift when summing currency.
- **Bad rows:** each one is logged to the rejects file with its line number and reason, and the run continues. Only a missing required column aborts, since nothing can be parsed then.
- **Skipped:** duplicate-transaction detection, negative-amount policy, and multi-currency handling. Add them when your data requires it. The columns are my assumption, so adjust `REQUIRED` and `validate` to your schema.

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Usage: report.py transactions.csv report.csv rejects.csv
Expected header: transaction_id,customer_id,amount,date (ISO YYYY-MM-DD)"""
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
from datetime import date
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation

COLS = ["transaction_id", "customer_id", "amount", "date"]


def parse(row):
    if len(row) != len(COLS):
        raise ValueError(f"expected {len(COLS)} fields, got {len(row)}")
    tid, cust, amt, d = (f.strip() for f in row)
    if not tid:
        raise ValueError("empty transaction_id")
    if not cust:
        raise ValueError("empty customer_id")
    try:
        amount = Decimal(amt)
    except InvalidOperation:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount {amt!r}")
    if not amount.is_finite():
        raise ValueError(f"non-finite amount {amt!r}")
    try:
        date.fromisoformat(d)
    except ValueError:
        raise ValueError(f"bad date {d!r}")
    return cust, amount


def main(src, out, rej):
    totals = defaultdict(lambda: [0, Decimal(0)])
    good = bad = 0
    with open(src, newline="", encoding="utf-8", errors="replace") as f, \
         open(rej, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as rf:
        reader = csv.reader(f)
        rw = csv.writer(rf)
        rw.writerow(["line", "reason", "raw"])

        header = next(reader, None)
        if header is None or [h.strip().lower() for h in header] != COLS:
            sys.exit(f"bad header: expected {COLS}, got {header}")

        while True:
            try:
                row = next(reader)
            except StopIteration:
                break
            except csv.Error as e:          # malformed quoting etc.
                bad += 1
                rw.writerow([reader.line_num, f"csv error: {e}", ""])
                continue
            if not row:                      # blank line
                continue
            try:
                cust, amount = parse(row)
            except ValueError as e:
                bad += 1
                rw.writerow([reader.line_num, str(e), ",".join(row)])
                continue
            t = totals[cust]
            t[0] += 1
            t[1] += amount
            good += 1

    with open(out, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as of:
        w = csv.writer(of)
        w.writerow(["customer_id", "transactions", "total"])
        for cust, (n, total) in sorted(totals.items(), key=lambda kv: -kv[1][1]):
            w.writerow([cust, n, total])

    print(f"valid={good} rejected={bad} customers={len(totals)}", file=sys.stderr)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) != 4:
        sys.exit(__doc__)
    main(*sys.argv[1:])
```

Design notes:
- Streams row by row; memory scales with customer count, not file size.
- `Decimal` for money, no float drift. Rejects `NaN`/`Infinity`.
- Bad rows → `rejects.csv` with line number, reason, raw text. A `csv.Error` is caught per-row, so the loop continues.
- Header mismatch aborts, since every row would be misread.

skipped: duplicate `transaction_id` detection (needs a set of all IDs, which costs memory at scale; use sqlite or sort-based dedup if needed), currency handling, negative-amount policy (refunds are currently summed as-is). Column names are assumed, so adjust `COLS` to match your file.

## large-s2 / migration

> Write a database migration script that moves a users table from a single full_name column to first_name/last_name, backfills existing rows, and is safely re-runnable. Show the code.

### vanilla: 1513 answer tokens, 2429 billed, 81 lines

~~~
Here is a PostgreSQL migration in three phases (expand, backfill, contract). Every step checks current state first, so you can run it repeatedly, including after the old column is dropped.

```sql
-- migrate_full_name_split.sql
-- Usage: psql -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1 -v drop_old=0 -f migrate_full_name_split.sql
-- Set -v drop_old=1 only after the app no longer reads or writes full_name.

\set ON_ERROR_STOP on

-- Phase 1: expand (idempotent, metadata-only, fast)
BEGIN;
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS first_name text;
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS last_name  text;
COMMIT;

-- Phase 2: backfill (only touches rows not yet migrated)
-- Skipped automatically if full_name has already been dropped.
DO $$
DECLARE
  batch_size constant int := 10000;
  rows_done  int;
BEGIN
  IF NOT EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns
    WHERE table_schema = current_schema()
      AND table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'full_name'
  ) THEN
    RAISE NOTICE 'full_name already removed; skipping backfill';
    RETURN;
  END IF;

  LOOP
    WITH todo AS (
      SELECT id,
             regexp_match(btrim(full_name), '^(\S+)\s*(.*)$') AS parts
      FROM users
      WHERE full_name IS NOT NULL
        AND btrim(full_name) <> ''
        AND first_name IS NULL
        AND last_name  IS NULL
      ORDER BY id
      LIMIT batch_size
      FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED
    )
    UPDATE users u
    SET first_name = t.parts[1],
        last_name  = NULLIF(regexp_replace(t.parts[2], '\s+', ' ', 'g'), '')
    FROM todo t
    WHERE u.id = t.id;

    GET DIAGNOSTICS rows_done = ROW_COUNT;
    EXIT WHEN rows_done = 0;
    RAISE NOTICE 'backfilled % rows', rows_done;
  END LOOP;
END $$;

-- Verification: should be 0 before you contract
SELECT count(*) AS unmigrated_rows
FROM users
WHERE full_name IS NOT NULL AND btrim(full_name) <> ''
  AND first_name IS NULL
  AND to_regclass('users') IS NOT NULL;

-- Phase 3: contract (opt-in, guarded)
SELECT :'drop_old' = '1' AS do_drop \gset
\if :do_drop
  BEGIN;
  ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN IF EXISTS full_name;
  COMMIT;
\endif
```

**Caveats**

- **Re-runnability:** `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` and the `first_name IS NULL AND last_name IS NULL` filter make repeats no-ops. Rows inserted between runs, for example by old app code during a rolling deploy, get picked up on the next run.
- **Splitting rule:** the first whitespace-delimited token becomes `first_name` and the rest becomes `last_name`, so "Mary Jane Watson" becomes `Mary` / `Jane Watson`. A single-token name gets a `NULL` last name. Blank or `NULL` names are left alone. This is lossy for names like "van der Berg" or single-name cultures, so spot-check the output.
- **Locking and batching:** the `DO` block is a single transaction, so row locks and WAL are held until it finishes. For very large tables (tens of millions of rows), run the batch `UPDATE` from a driver script that commits per batch. The SQL is the same.
- **Final step:** if you want the verification query to fail the migration on leftovers, add a check that raises an exception when the count is non-zero before the drop.
- **Rollback:** nothing destructive happens until `drop_old=1`, so you can roll back by ignoring the new columns. Take a backup before dropping.

If you're on MySQL, SQLite, or an ORM migration tool (Alembic, Django, Rails, Knex), tell me which and I'll port it.
~~~

### caveman: 1219 answer tokens, 1434 billed, 83 lines

~~~
Assume PostgreSQL. Expand → backfill → (later) contract. Every step guarded, so re-run is safe.

```sql
-- migrate_full_name_split.sql
BEGIN;

-- 1. Expand: add new columns (no-op if present)
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS first_name text;
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS last_name  text;

-- 2. Backfill: only rows not yet migrated, only if full_name still exists
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns
    WHERE table_schema = current_schema()
      AND table_name = 'users'
      AND column_name = 'full_name'
  ) THEN
    UPDATE users
    SET first_name = COALESCE(NULLIF(split_part(btrim(full_name), ' ', 1), ''), ''),
        last_name  = btrim(substr(btrim(full_name),
                           length(split_part(btrim(full_name), ' ', 1)) + 1))
    WHERE first_name IS NULL
      AND last_name  IS NULL
      AND full_name  IS NOT NULL;
  END IF;
END $$;

COMMIT;
```

Behavior:
- `"Ada Lovelace"` → `Ada` / `Lovelace`
- `"Mary Jane Watson"` → `Mary` / `Jane Watson`
- `"Cher"` → `Cher` / `''`
- `NULL` full_name → both stay NULL, skipped each run

Re-run safe because:
- `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` no-op second time.
- `WHERE first_name IS NULL AND last_name IS NULL` skips done rows. Never overwrites later manual edits.
- `information_schema` check means script still works after `full_name` dropped.

Contract step (separate migration, run only after app deploys using new columns and you verified data):

```sql
-- migrate_drop_full_name.sql
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns
    WHERE table_schema = current_schema()
      AND table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'full_name'
  ) THEN
    ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN full_name;
  END IF;
END $$;
```

Warning: drop is irreversible. Back up first.

Big table notes:
- Single `UPDATE` locks many rows and bloats. For millions of rows, batch instead:

```sql
-- run repeatedly until it reports UPDATE 0
UPDATE users
SET first_name = split_part(btrim(full_name), ' ', 1),
    last_name  = btrim(substr(btrim(full_name),
                       length(split_part(btrim(full_name), ' ', 1)) + 1))
WHERE id IN (
  SELECT id FROM users
  WHERE first_name IS NULL AND last_name IS NULL AND full_name IS NOT NULL
  ORDER BY id
  LIMIT 5000
  FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED
);
```

- During rollout, new writes need to fill both old and new columns (app dual-write or trigger), else rows inserted mid-migration miss backfill. Re-run script after deploy to catch them.
- Add `NOT NULL` on new columns only after backfill verified.

Other DB (MySQL/SQLite)? Say which. `IF NOT EXISTS` on columns and `split_part` differ.
~~~

This assumes Postgres. The SQL is below, and you can run it with `psql -f`.

```sql
-- migrate_split_name.sql
BEGIN;

-- Idempotent: no-op if the columns already exist.
ALTER TABLE users
  ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS first_name text,
  ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS last_name  text;

DO $$
BEGIN
  -- Skip if full_name was already dropped (re-run after the contract step).
  IF EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns
             WHERE table_schema = current_schema()
               AND table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'full_name') THEN

    -- Only touch rows not yet migrated, so re-runs never overwrite
    -- edits made to first_name/last_name after the first run.
    -- ponytail: split on first space; "Mary Ann Smith" -> "Mary" / "Ann Smith".
    -- Single-word names get last_name = ''. Swap in a smarter rule if you need one.
    UPDATE users
    SET first_name = split_part(btrim(full_name), ' ', 1),
        last_name  = btrim(substr(btrim(full_name),
                           length(split_part(btrim(full_name), ' ', 1)) + 1))
    WHERE first_name IS NULL
      AND last_name  IS NULL
      AND full_name IS NOT NULL;

    -- Check: fail (and roll back) if any row with a name was left unmigrated.
    IF EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM users
               WHERE full_name IS NOT NULL AND first_name IS NULL) THEN
      RAISE EXCEPTION 'backfill incomplete';
    END IF;
  END IF;
END $$;

COMMIT;

-- Contract step, run separately once the app no longer reads full_name:
-- ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN full_name;
```

Skipped:
- **Dropping `full_name`:** it's left in place so you can roll back and deploy the app change safely. Drop it later using the commented line.
- **Batching:** the single `UPDATE` locks all affected rows. For tables with tens of millions of rows, loop over id ranges instead.
- **`NOT NULL` constraints:** I didn't add them. Add them after the backfill if you want them enforced.

Assuming PostgreSQL:

```sql
-- migrate_full_name_split.sql
BEGIN;

ALTER TABLE users
  ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS first_name text,
  ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS last_name  text;

-- Backfill only rows not yet migrated → re-runs are no-ops.
-- Split on first whitespace run: "Mary Ann Smith" → first "Mary", last "Ann Smith".
-- Single token "Cher" → first "Cher", last NULL.
UPDATE users
SET first_name = split_part(btrim(full_name), ' ', 1),
    last_name  = NULLIF(btrim(substr(btrim(full_name),
                         length(split_part(btrim(full_name), ' ', 1)) + 1)), '')
WHERE first_name IS NULL
  AND last_name  IS NULL
  AND NULLIF(btrim(full_name), '') IS NOT NULL;

COMMIT;
```

Rollback (separate file):

```sql
ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN IF EXISTS first_name, DROP COLUMN IF EXISTS last_name;
```

Notes:
- `full_name` is deliberately kept (expand/contract). Deploy app writing both/new columns, verify, then drop `full_name` in a later migration.
- Re-run safe: `IF NOT EXISTS` + `WHERE first_name IS NULL AND last_name IS NULL`. Caveat: rows whose names were already split are never overwritten, even if `full_name` changed afterwards.
- Naive split is wrong for multi-word first names ("Mary Ann") and non-Western name orders; review samples before dropping the old column.
- Large table: the single `UPDATE` holds locks and bloats. Loop in batches instead, e.g. add `AND id IN (SELECT id FROM users WHERE first_name IS NULL AND last_name IS NULL AND full_name IS NOT NULL LIMIT 10000)` and repeat until 0 rows are updated.
- Add `NOT NULL` on `first_name` only after the app writes it for all new rows.
