### vanilla: 2895 answer tokens, 2895 billed, 107 lines

~~~
# Incident Postmortem: Order Service Outage Due to Connection Pool Exhaustion

> Note: Details below (service names, times, numbers) are illustrative placeholders. Replace them with your real data.

**Status:** Resolved
**Severity:** SEV-1
**Date:** 2026-11-18
**Duration:** 4 hours 5 minutes (09:12 – 13:17 UTC)
**Author:** [Name] | **Reviewers:** [Names]
**Blameless format:** This review focuses on systems and processes, not individuals.

---

## 1. Summary

On 2026-11-18, the Checkout API became unavailable for most users for roughly four hours. A latency regression in a downstream dependency, the Inventory Service, caused requests to hold database and HTTP connections far longer than normal. The Checkout API's connection pool became exhausted, new requests queued and timed out, and health checks began failing. Automatic restarts and retries amplified the load and prolonged recovery. Service was restored by shedding load, reducing timeouts, and rolling back the Inventory Service change.

## 2. Impact

- **User impact:** ~85% of checkout requests failed (HTTP 503/504) at peak; ~42,000 failed orders.
- **Business impact:** Estimated [$X] in lost revenue; [N] support tickets.
- **Data impact:** No data loss. ~300 orders were charged but not confirmed and required reconciliation.
- **SLO impact:** Monthly availability SLO (99.9%) breached; error budget fully consumed.

## 3. Timeline (UTC)

| Time | Event |
|---|---|
| 08:40 | Inventory Service deploys v2.14, which includes a new query lacking an index. p99 latency rises from 80 ms to ~6 s. No alert fires (alert threshold: p99 > 10 s). |
| 09:05 | Checkout API in-flight request count and pool utilization begin climbing. |
| 09:12 | Checkout connection pool reaches 100% utilization. Request queueing begins. **Outage begins.** |
| 09:18 | Alert "Checkout 5xx > 5%" fires and pages on-call. |
| 09:25 | On-call acknowledges; suspects database because of pool errors. Database CPU appears normal. |
| 09:50 | Rolling restart of Checkout pods. Pools refill within minutes. Brief recovery, then relapse. |
| 10:15 | Client retries (3x, no backoff) identified as amplifying load. Incident escalated; incident commander assigned. |
| 10:40 | Trace sampling shows time spent waiting on Inventory Service calls. Inventory identified as the slow dependency. |
| 11:05 | Inventory team engaged. Recent deploy v2.14 identified as a candidate cause. |
| 11:30 | Mitigation: Checkout's Inventory call timeout reduced from 30 s to 2 s via config flag; retries disabled. Partial recovery (~40% success). |
| 12:10 | Inventory rollback to v2.13 started; delayed by a failing canary check. |
| 12:55 | Rollback complete. Inventory p99 returns to normal. |
| 13:17 | Checkout error rate below 0.5% and pools healthy. **Incident resolved.** |
| 13:45 | Order reconciliation job started for charged-but-unconfirmed orders. |

## 4. Root Cause

**Direct cause:** The Checkout API called Inventory Service synchronously on the request path with a 30 s timeout, while holding a connection from a shared, bounded pool (size 100). When Inventory latency rose from ~80 ms to ~6 s, each request held its connection ~75x longer. By Little's Law, concurrency needed rose beyond pool capacity, and the pool was exhausted.

**Trigger:** Inventory v2.14 introduced a query without a supporting index, causing full table scans under production data volumes. This was not caught in staging, whose dataset was ~1% of production size.

## 5. Contributing Factors

1. **Excessive timeouts:** 30 s timeout was far above the dependency's normal p99 (~100 ms).
2. **No bulkheading:** Inventory calls shared a pool with critical paths (payment, order writes), so one slow dependency starved everything.
3. **Retry amplification:** Clients retried three times without backoff or jitter, tripling load during degradation.
4. **No circuit breaker:** Checkout kept sending traffic to a failing dependency.
5. **Alerting gaps:** No alert on pool saturation or on dependency latency relative to baseline. The first alert came 6 minutes after impact started, and it was a symptom (5xx), not a cause.
6. **Misleading signals:** Pool errors pointed responders toward the database, costing ~75 minutes.
7. **Restart as mitigation:** Restarting pods briefly cleared pools but increased cold-start load and did not address the cause.
8. **Slow rollback:** A flaky canary check delayed the Inventory rollback by ~40 minutes.
9. **Deploy correlation not visible:** No deploy markers on dashboards, so the 08:40 deploy was not linked until 11:05.

## 6. What Went Well

- Paging worked and the incident commander role was filled once escalated.
- The config flag for timeouts allowed mitigation without a deploy.
- No data loss; reconciliation tooling existed.

## 7. What Went Poorly

- Time to identify the failing dependency was about 90 minutes.
- Retries and restarts worsened the situation.
- Cross-team engagement was late.

## 8. Remediation Actions

| # | Action | Type | Owner | Priority | Due |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Add the missing index; add query-plan review to Inventory's PR checklist | Prevent | Inventory team | P0 | 2026-11-25 |
| 2 | Set per-dependency timeouts based on p99 (e.g., 500 ms for Inventory) | Mitigate | Checkout team | P0 | 2026-11-27 |
| 3 | Isolate connection pools per dependency (bulkheads); reserve capacity for payment and order writes | Mitigate | Checkout team | P0 | 2026-12-05 |
| 4 | Add circuit breakers on all synchronous downstream calls | Mitigate | Checkout team | P1 | 2026-12-12 |
| 5 | Enforce exponential backoff with jitter and retry budgets in the shared client library | Prevent | Platform | P1 | 2026-12-12 |
| 6 | Add alerts: pool utilization > 80%, dependency p99 > 3x baseline, queue wait time | Detect | SRE | P0 | 2026-11-30 |
| 7 | Add deploy markers to all service dashboards | Detect | SRE | P1 | 2026-12-05 |
| 8 | Add a fallback for Inventory (cached stock levels or optimistic accept) so checkout degrades gracefully | Mitigate | Checkout/Inventory | P1 | 2027-01-15 |
| 9 | Load test staging with production-scale data; add latency regression gate in CI | Prevent | Inventory/QA | P2 | 2027-01-31 |
| 10 | Fix the flaky canary check; define a fast-rollback path that bypasses non-critical gates | Mitigate | Release eng | P1 | 2026-12-05 |
| 11 | Update runbook: "pool exhaustion: check downstream latency first"; run a game day | Respond | SRE | P2 | 2026-12-19 |
| 12 | Make order confirmation idempotent and add automatic reconciliation for charged-unconfirmed orders | Mitigate | Payments | P1 | 2026-12-19 |

## 9. Lessons Learned

- A slow dependency is often more dangerous than a failed one, because it ties up resources instead of failing fast.
- Timeouts, bulkheads, circuit breakers, and bounded retries should be treated together as a baseline for every synchronous call.
- Alert on causes and saturation, not only on user-facing symptoms.
- Pool size, timeout, and latency are linked: required concurrency ≈ request rate × time held.

## 10. Appendix

- Dashboards: [link]
- Traces: [link]
- Chat log/incident channel: [link]
- Related incidents: [link]

---

If you share your actual timeline, metrics, and architecture, I can tailor this to the real incident.
~~~

# Incident Postmortem: Checkout API Outage (Connection Pool Exhaustion)

*Template with illustrative details. Swap in real service names, times, and numbers.*

**Severity:** SEV-1 | **Duration:** 4h 05m (09:12 to 13:17 UTC) | **Status:** Resolved
**Authors:** On-call + service owners | **Review:** Blameless

## Summary
A slow downstream dependency (`inventory-svc`) raised p99 latency from ~80 ms to 30+ s. `orders-api` held DB and HTTP connections while it waited, so its pools filled. New requests queued, then timed out. Health checks shared the same pool, so the load balancer pulled healthy instances out of rotation and capacity shrank further. About 92% of checkout requests failed at peak.

## Impact
- ~4 hours of near-total checkout failure
- ~310k failed requests; est. 18k abandoned orders
- Search and browse stayed up, but degraded
- No data loss. 2,100 orders stuck in `pending` were reconciled afterward.

## Timeline (UTC)
| Time | Event |
|---|---|
| 09:02 | `inventory-svc` deploy begins. A new query lacks an index. |
| 09:12 | `inventory-svc` p99 climbs past 5 s. No alert (threshold only on error rate). |
| 09:20 | `orders-api` pool utilization hits 100%. Request queue grows. |
| 09:26 | First page: checkout 5xx > 20%. On-call acknowledges. |
| 09:40 | Suspect `orders-api` itself. Restart of 1/3 instances. Pool refills within 2 min. No improvement. |
| 10:05 | Healthcheck failures eject instances from LB. Capacity drops to 1/3. 5xx > 80%. |
| 10:15 | Incident escalated. Incident commander assigned. |
| 10:50 | Thread dumps show threads blocked on `inventory-svc` calls. Root cause area identified. |
| 11:10 | Attempt to scale `orders-api` 3x. Makes things worse: more instances hammer the already slow `inventory-svc`. |
| 11:35 | `inventory-svc` team engaged. Link to their 09:02 deploy found. |
| 12:10 | Rollback of `inventory-svc` started. Slow because it needs a DB migration revert. |
| 12:45 | Rollback complete. `inventory-svc` p99 back to normal. |
| 12:55 | `orders-api` pools drain, but retry storm from clients keeps load high. |
| 13:05 | Rate limit on checkout raised gradually, instances re-added to LB. |
| 13:17 | Error rate < 0.5%. Incident declared resolved. |

## Root Cause
**Trigger:** unindexed query in the `inventory-svc` deploy made it slow.

**Why it became an outage (contributing causes):**
1. **No timeout on inventory calls.** Client default was effectively 60 s, so each request held a connection for a long time.
2. **Shared, unbounded blast radius.** One pool served all endpoints, including ones that did not need inventory. A single slow dependency starved everything.
3. **Healthcheck depended on the pool.** `/health` needed a pooled connection. Under exhaustion it failed, the LB ejected healthy instances, and load concentrated on the survivors.
4. **No circuit breaker or load shedding.** Calls kept going to a failing dependency and requests piled up.
5. **Retries without backoff** at the client and gateway multiplied load, and slowed recovery after the fix.
6. **Alerting gap.** Alerts fired on errors, not on dependency latency or pool saturation. Detection took 14 min, and diagnosis took 1h 40m.
7. **Slow rollback.** The migration was not safely reversible, so the revert took ~35 min.

## What Went Well
- Paging worked, and the incident commander was assigned within 1 hour.
- Order data stayed consistent. Reconciliation was straightforward.

## What Went Poorly
- Restart and scale-out were applied before a diagnosis, and scale-out worsened the load.
- No dashboard showed pool wait time or per-dependency latency.
- The two teams were engaged late (~2h 10m after the first page).

## Remediation
| # | Action | Type | Owner | Due |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Set strict timeouts on all `inventory-svc` calls (connect 200 ms, read 1 s) | Prevent | orders | +1 wk |
| 2 | Add circuit breaker + fallback (cached stock, soft-fail on non-critical paths) | Mitigate | orders | +3 wk |
| 3 | Bulkhead: separate pool per dependency and per critical endpoint | Mitigate | orders | +4 wk |
| 4 | Make `/health` independent of pools (liveness vs readiness split) | Prevent | orders/infra | +1 wk |
| 5 | Retry policy: capped, exponential backoff + jitter, retry budget | Prevent | platform | +3 wk |
| 6 | Alerts: dependency p99 latency, pool wait time, pool utilization > 80% | Detect | SRE | +1 wk |
| 7 | Dashboard: per-dependency latency and pool metrics, linked from runbook | Detect | SRE | +2 wk |
| 8 | Require query-plan review and load test for `inventory-svc` schema changes | Prevent | inventory | +2 wk |
| 9 | Canary + auto-rollback on latency regression for `inventory-svc` | Prevent | inventory/platform | +6 wk |
| 10 | Expand-contract migrations so rollbacks stay fast | Mitigate | inventory | +6 wk |
| 11 | Runbook: "Pool exhaustion". Check dependency latency first. Do not scale out blindly. | Respond | SRE | +1 wk |
| 12 | Game day: inject dependency latency in staging | Verify | SRE | +8 wk |

## Lessons Learned
- A slow dependency is often worse than a dead one: failures are fast, slowness holds resources.
- Every outbound call needs a timeout, and every pool needs a bound and isolation.
- Healthchecks must not fail for the same reason as the load they protect.
- Under saturation, scaling out can push more load onto the real bottleneck.

## Open Questions
- Why did the pre-deploy checks not catch the missing index?
- Should checkout degrade gracefully (accept orders, verify stock async) when inventory is slow?
~~~

# Postmortem: Checkout API Outage Caused by Connection Pool Exhaustion

*Template with illustrative details. Replace bracketed values with your real data.*

**Date:** [YYYY-MM-DD] | **Duration:** 4h 02m (14:07-18:09 UTC) | **Severity:** SEV-1
**Status:** Resolved | **Author:** [name] | **Reviewers:** [names]

## Summary

For about four hours, [Service A] returned errors or timed out for ~85% of requests. A downstream dependency, [Service B], degraded and began responding in 20-30s instead of ~80ms. Service A's database/HTTP connection pool filled with requests waiting on B. No connections were left for any request, including endpoints that don't depend on B. Recovery took four hours because the trigger was misdiagnosed and restarts were ineffective while B was still slow.

## Impact

- ~85% of requests failed (5xx/timeouts) at peak.
- ~[N] users affected, ~[N] failed transactions, ~$[X] estimated revenue impact.
- Unrelated endpoints (login, health checks, read-only pages) also failed because they share the pool.
- No data loss. [Confirm.]

## Timeline (UTC)

| Time | Event |
|------|-------|
| 13:52 | [Service B] deploys a change that adds an unindexed query; p99 latency rises from 80ms to 25s. |
| 14:07 | A's pool utilization hits 100%. Latency and 5xx rates climb. |
| 14:12 | Alert "A error rate > 5%" pages on-call. Acknowledged 14:14. |
| 14:25 | On-call suspects A's own deploy from that morning and rolls it back. No improvement. |
| 14:50 | A's pods restarted. Errors drop briefly, then return within ~3 min as the pool refills. |
| 15:20 | Incident escalated to SEV-1. Database team engaged; DB CPU and connections look normal, ruling out A's own DB. |
| 15:45 | Thread dumps from A show most threads blocked on calls to B. |
| 16:10 | B's team engaged. Their dashboards show slow responses, but no alert had fired (alerting was on error rate, not latency). |
| 16:40 | B's deploy identified as a candidate cause. |
| 17:05 | B's change rolled back. |
| 17:20 | B's latency recovers. A's pool drains slowly because in-flight requests still have 30s timeouts. |
| 17:40 | A's pods restarted to clear stuck connections; error rate drops below 1%. |
| 18:09 | Metrics stable for 30 min. Incident closed. |

## Root Cause

**Trigger:** B shipped an unindexed query, which pushed its latency from ~80ms to ~25s.

**Root cause:** A had no effective protection against a slow dependency:

1. **Timeout too long.** The HTTP client timeout to B was 30s (the default), about 375x normal latency.
2. **Shared pool.** One pool of [50] connections served all endpoints, so B-dependent requests starved everything else. At ~200 req/s with 25s calls, the pool was exhausted in under a second.
3. **No circuit breaker, bulkhead, or load shedding.** A kept sending requests to B and queued new ones until they too timed out.
4. **Unbounded wait for a connection.** Requests queued for a pool slot with no short acquisition timeout, which increased latency and held upstream resources.

## Why It Took Four Hours (Contributing Factors)

- **Misleading symptoms.** A's errors were pool-exhaustion timeouts, not obviously tied to B. We spent ~90 minutes on A's deploy and A's DB.
- **Restarts gave false hope.** Restarts briefly cleared the pool, which reinforced the wrong hypothesis.
- **Monitoring gap.** No alert on B's latency or on A's pool utilization.
- **No dependency visibility.** No tracing or per-dependency latency dashboard on A.
- **Slow rollback correlation.** B's deploy was not surfaced in the incident channel or on a shared change calendar.

## What Went Well

- Alert paged within 5 minutes of impact.
- No data corruption; the incident was cleanly isolated once identified.
- B's rollback was fast once decided (~25 min).

## Remediations

| # | Action | Type | Owner | Priority | Due |
|---|--------|------|-------|----------|-----|
| 1 | Cut A→B timeout to 1s (p99 × ~10) with a connect timeout of 200ms | Prevent | [A] | P0 | [date] |
| 2 | Separate connection pools/bulkheads per dependency, with a short pool acquire timeout (~250ms) | Prevent | [A] | P0 | [date] |
| 3 | Add a circuit breaker on A→B with a fallback or fast-fail | Prevent | [A] | P1 | [date] |
| 4 | Alert on pool utilization >80% and on per-dependency p99 latency | Detect | [A] | P0 | [date] |
| 5 | Alert on B's latency SLO, not just error rate | Detect | [B] | P0 | [date] |
| 6 | Dashboard and tracing showing per-dependency latency on A | Detect | [A] | P1 | [date] |
| 7 | Require index and query-plan review plus a canary rollout for B's DB changes | Prevent | [B] | P1 | [date] |
| 8 | Post deploys from all services to a shared channel and show recent changes in incident tooling | Respond | [SRE] | P2 | [date] |
| 9 | Add "check downstream latency and thread dumps" to the runbook for pool-exhaustion symptoms | Respond | [SRE] | P1 | [date] |
| 10 | Run a game day injecting latency into B to validate remediations 1-3 | Verify | [SRE] | P2 | [date] |

## Lessons Learned

- A slow dependency is more dangerous than a failed one: failures return fast, slowness holds resources.
- Defaults (30s timeouts, one shared pool) are not safe settings.
- Restarting is not a fix when the cause is upstream. A quick recurrence after a restart should point to an external trigger.
- Alert on latency and saturation, not only errors.

*Blameless format: this review focuses on systems and process, not individuals.*

# Postmortem: Checkout API outage, pool exhaustion from slow downstream

*Illustrative draft: service names, times, and numbers are placeholders. Replace with real data.*

**Date:** 2026-11-20 | **Duration:** 4h 05m (09:12-13:17 UTC) | **Severity:** SEV-1 | **Status:** Resolved

## Summary
`orders-api` returned 5xx/timeouts for ~92% of requests for 4 hours. `inventory-svc` (downstream) degraded: p99 went from 80ms to 30s. `orders-api` held DB/HTTP pool connections for the full wait, pool (size 50) exhausted in <2 min, and all requests queued → timeouts → retries → more load. Impact: ~18k failed checkouts, est. $X lost revenue.

## Impact
- Checkout and order-status endpoints unavailable. Read-only catalog unaffected.
- Retry storms from mobile clients tripled inbound traffic.
- No data loss; ~600 orders in "pending" needed reconciliation.

## Timeline (UTC)
| Time | Event |
|---|---|
| 09:02 | `inventory-svc` deploy v2.41 begins; adds unindexed query on stock lookup |
| 09:08 | `inventory-svc` p99 climbs past 5s. No alert (threshold is on error rate, not latency) |
| 09:12 | `orders-api` pool hits 50/50, request queue grows. **Impact starts** |
| 09:19 | Synthetic checkout check fails, pages on-call |
| 09:27 | On-call acks, sees `orders-api` 5xx; suspects own service, restarts pods |
| 09:40 | Restart gives ~3 min relief, then pool re-exhausts. Scaled pods 6→20: worsens, since more pods → more load on `inventory-svc` |
| 10:15 | Escalated; `inventory-svc` team engaged |
| 10:50 | Traces show 95% of `orders-api` time is in `inventory-svc` calls (client timeout was 60s) |
| 11:30 | `inventory-svc` team identifies v2.41 query regression; rollback attempted, blocked by a migration that is not backward compatible |
| 12:20 | Index added manually in prod; `inventory-svc` p99 recovers to 200ms |
| 12:45 | `orders-api` pools drain; traffic still elevated from client retries. Rate limiting enabled at gateway |
| 13:17 | Error rate <0.5%. **Resolved** |

## Root cause
1. **Trigger:** `inventory-svc` v2.41 introduced a query without an index → latency spike.
2. **Why it became an outage:** `orders-api` called `inventory-svc` synchronously inside a request holding a pooled connection, with a 60s timeout, no circuit breaker, and no bulkhead. Slow dependency × concurrency = pool exhaustion (Little's law: 50 conns / 30s ≈ 1.7 req/s capacity vs ~400 req/s demand).
3. **Amplifiers:** client retries without backoff/jitter; scaling pods increased pressure on the already-slow dependency.

## Why it lasted 4 hours
- No latency alert on downstream; detection by synthetic check only (7 min).
- Misdiagnosis: dashboards showed `orders-api` symptoms, not dependency latency. ~80 min lost to restarts/scaling.
- Rollback blocked by non-reversible migration.
- No runbook for "dependency slow".

## What went well
- Synthetic check paged within 7 min.
- Cross-team escalation worked once triggered.
- Gateway rate limit stopped the retry storm.

## Remediations
| # | Action | Type | Owner | Due |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Client timeout on `inventory-svc` calls: 60s → 1s (p99 ×3), fail fast | Prevent | orders | 12-01 |
| 2 | Circuit breaker + fallback (cached stock / optimistic reserve) | Mitigate | orders | 12-08 |
| 3 | Separate pools/bulkheads per dependency; don't hold DB connection during outbound calls | Prevent | orders | 12-15 |
| 4 | Alert on downstream p99 latency and pool saturation (>80% for 2 min) | Detect | SRE | 11-30 |
| 5 | Dependency-latency panel on `orders-api` dashboard, trace-based top-dependency view | Detect | SRE | 12-05 |
| 6 | Client retries: exponential backoff + jitter, retry budget | Prevent | mobile/web | 12-20 |
| 7 | Require backward-compatible migrations; rollback test in CI | Recover | inventory | 12-15 |
| 8 | Query-plan check / slow-query gate in `inventory-svc` pre-prod; canary deploys with latency SLO auto-rollback | Prevent | inventory | 12-22 |
| 9 | Runbook: "slow dependency → check downstream latency before restart/scale; don't scale callers" | Respond | SRE | 11-30 |
| 10 | Game day: inject 30s latency into `inventory-svc` in staging | Verify | SRE | Q1 |

## Lessons
- A slow dependency is worse than a dead one: dead fails fast, slow holds resources.
- Timeouts, breakers, and bulkheads are the contract between services, not optional tuning.
- Scaling callers of a degraded dependency makes things worse.
~~~

## large-s2 / statemachine

> Implement an order state machine covering created, paid, shipped, delivered, cancelled and refunded, with the legal transitions enforced and invalid ones rejected. Show the code.

### vanilla: 2080 answer tokens, 2080 billed, 149 lines

~~~
Here's a self-contained Python implementation.

**Transition rules**

```
created   -> paid, cancelled
paid      -> shipped, cancelled, refunded
shipped   -> delivered
delivered -> refunded
cancelled -> (terminal)
refunded  -> (terminal)
```

Reasoning behind the choices: a paid order can still be cancelled (before shipping) and refunded. Once shipped, it can't be cancelled, only delivered, and after delivery it can be refunded (a return). Cancelled and refunded are terminal.

```python
from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from datetime import datetime, timezone
from enum import Enum


class OrderState(str, Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"


S = OrderState

# Single source of truth for legal transitions.
TRANSITIONS: dict[OrderState, frozenset[OrderState]] = {
    S.CREATED:   frozenset({S.PAID, S.CANCELLED}),
    S.PAID:      frozenset({S.SHIPPED, S.CANCELLED, S.REFUNDED}),
    S.SHIPPED:   frozenset({S.DELIVERED}),
    S.DELIVERED: frozenset({S.REFUNDED}),
    S.CANCELLED: frozenset(),
    S.REFUNDED:  frozenset(),
}


class InvalidTransitionError(Exception):
    def __init__(self, current: OrderState, target: OrderState):
        allowed = sorted(s.value for s in TRANSITIONS[current])
        super().__init__(
            f"Cannot transition from '{current.value}' to '{target.value}'. "
            f"Allowed: {allowed or 'none (terminal state)'}"
        )
        self.current = current
        self.target = target


@dataclass
class Order:
    id: str
    state: OrderState = OrderState.CREATED
    history: list[tuple[OrderState, OrderState, datetime]] = field(default_factory=list)

    def can_transition_to(self, target: OrderState) -> bool:
        return target in TRANSITIONS[self.state]

    def transition_to(self, target: OrderState | str) -> None:
        target = OrderState(target)  # raises ValueError on unknown state
        if not self.can_transition_to(target):
            raise InvalidTransitionError(self.state, target)
        self.history.append((self.state, target, datetime.now(timezone.utc)))
        self.state = target

    # Intent-revealing helpers
    def pay(self) -> None:       self.transition_to(S.PAID)
    def ship(self) -> None:      self.transition_to(S.SHIPPED)
    def deliver(self) -> None:   self.transition_to(S.DELIVERED)
    def cancel(self) -> None:    self.transition_to(S.CANCELLED)
    def refund(self) -> None:    self.transition_to(S.REFUNDED)

    @property
    def is_terminal(self) -> bool:
        return not TRANSITIONS[self.state]
```

**Usage and tests**

```python
import pytest


def test_happy_path():
    o = Order("A1")
    o.pay(); o.ship(); o.deliver()
    assert o.state is S.DELIVERED
    assert len(o.history) == 3


def test_cancel_before_shipping():
    o = Order("A2")
    o.pay()
    o.cancel()
    assert o.is_terminal


def test_refund_after_delivery():
    o = Order("A3")
    o.pay(); o.ship(); o.deliver(); o.refund()
    assert o.state is S.REFUNDED


@pytest.mark.parametrize("setup, action", [
    ([], "ship"),                          # created -> shipped
    ([], "refund"),                        # created -> refunded
    (["pay", "ship"], "cancel"),           # shipped -> cancelled
    (["pay", "ship"], "refund"),           # shipped -> refunded
    (["cancel"], "pay"),                   # cancelled is terminal
    (["pay", "refund"], "ship"),           # refunded is terminal
])
def test_invalid_transitions_rejected(setup, action):
    o = Order("X")
    for step in setup:
        getattr(o, step)()
    before = o.state
    with pytest.raises(InvalidTransitionError):
        getattr(o, action)()
    assert o.state is before               # state unchanged on failure


def test_exhaustive_matrix():
    for src in OrderState:
        for dst in OrderState:
            o = Order("M", state=src)
            if dst in TRANSITIONS[src]:
                o.transition_to(dst)
                assert o.state is dst
            else:
                with pytest.raises(InvalidTransitionError):
                    o.transition_to(dst)
```

**Design notes**

- **Table-driven:** the `TRANSITIONS` dict is the only place rules live, so changing policy (e.g., allowing `shipped -> refunded` for lost parcels) is a one-line edit.
- **Atomic failure:** validation happens before any mutation, so a rejected transition leaves state and history untouched.
- **Self-transitions** (e.g., `paid -> paid`) are rejected, which also gives you idempotency protection against duplicate payment webhooks if you catch `InvalidTransitionError` there.
- **Persistence/concurrency:** in a real system, enforce this at the database level too, e.g. `UPDATE orders SET state = :new WHERE id = :id AND state = :expected` and check the affected row count, so two concurrent requests can't both apply conflicting transitions.
- **Unknown input:** passing a string that isn't a valid state raises `ValueError` from the enum, distinct from an illegal-but-known transition.

If your business rules differ (e.g., partial refunds, cancel-after-ship, or a `returned` state), tell me and I'll adjust the table.
~~~

Python impl. Transition table is single source of truth. Terminal states have no outgoing edges.

```python
from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from datetime import datetime, timezone
from enum import Enum


class OrderState(str, Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"


S = OrderState

# Legal transitions. Anything not listed is rejected.
TRANSITIONS: dict[OrderState, frozenset[OrderState]] = {
    S.CREATED:   frozenset({S.PAID, S.CANCELLED}),
    S.PAID:      frozenset({S.SHIPPED, S.CANCELLED, S.REFUNDED}),
    S.SHIPPED:   frozenset({S.DELIVERED}),
    S.DELIVERED: frozenset({S.REFUNDED}),
    S.CANCELLED: frozenset(),  # terminal
    S.REFUNDED:  frozenset(),  # terminal
}


class InvalidTransitionError(Exception):
    def __init__(self, current: OrderState, target: OrderState):
        allowed = sorted(s.value for s in TRANSITIONS[current])
        super().__init__(
            f"Cannot move order from '{current.value}' to '{target.value}'. "
            f"Allowed: {allowed or 'none (terminal state)'}"
        )
        self.current = current
        self.target = target


@dataclass
class Order:
    id: str
    state: OrderState = OrderState.CREATED
    history: list[tuple[OrderState, OrderState, datetime]] = field(default_factory=list)

    def can_transition(self, target: OrderState) -> bool:
        return target in TRANSITIONS[self.state]

    def transition(self, target: OrderState) -> None:
        target = OrderState(target)  # raises ValueError on unknown state
        if not self.can_transition(target):
            raise InvalidTransitionError(self.state, target)
        self.history.append((self.state, target, datetime.now(timezone.utc)))
        self.state = target

    # Intent-named helpers
    def pay(self) -> None:      self.transition(S.PAID)
    def ship(self) -> None:     self.transition(S.SHIPPED)
    def deliver(self) -> None:  self.transition(S.DELIVERED)
    def cancel(self) -> None:   self.transition(S.CANCELLED)
    def refund(self) -> None:   self.transition(S.REFUNDED)

    @property
    def is_terminal(self) -> bool:
        return not TRANSITIONS[self.state]
```

Tests (pytest):

```python
import pytest

def test_happy_path():
    o = Order("1")
    o.pay(); o.ship(); o.deliver(); o.refund()
    assert o.state is S.REFUNDED and o.is_terminal
    assert len(o.history) == 4

def test_cancel_before_payment():
    o = Order("2"); o.cancel()
    assert o.state is S.CANCELLED

@pytest.mark.parametrize("start,target", [
    (S.CREATED, S.SHIPPED),
    (S.CREATED, S.DELIVERED),
    (S.PAID, S.DELIVERED),
    (S.SHIPPED, S.CANCELLED),
    (S.DELIVERED, S.CANCELLED),
    (S.CANCELLED, S.PAID),
    (S.REFUNDED, S.PAID),
    (S.PAID, S.PAID),
])
def test_invalid_rejected(start, target):
    o = Order("x", state=start)
    with pytest.raises(InvalidTransitionError):
        o.transition(target)
    assert o.state is start      # state unchanged on failure
    assert o.history == []

def test_every_state_in_table():
    assert set(TRANSITIONS) == set(OrderState)
```

Design notes:
- **Table-driven**: add/change rule = edit `TRANSITIONS` only.
- **Failure atomic**: check before mutate; bad transition leave state and history untouched.
- **Policy choices** (change if business differ): `paid → cancelled` and `paid → refunded` allowed; `shipped` cannot cancel (must deliver, then refund); `cancelled`/`refunded` terminal.
- **Not included**: persistence. In real DB, do transition as conditional update (`UPDATE orders SET state=:new WHERE id=:id AND state=:expected`) and check rows affected, else concurrent requests race past the in-memory check.

```python
from enum import Enum


class State(Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"


# ponytail: a dict is the whole machine; no state classes until states carry behavior.
TRANSITIONS = {
    State.CREATED:   {State.PAID, State.CANCELLED},
    State.PAID:      {State.SHIPPED, State.CANCELLED, State.REFUNDED},
    State.SHIPPED:   {State.DELIVERED},
    State.DELIVERED: {State.REFUNDED},
    State.CANCELLED: set(),   # terminal
    State.REFUNDED:  set(),   # terminal
}


class InvalidTransition(Exception):
    pass


class Order:
    def __init__(self):
        self.state = State.CREATED

    def transition(self, new: State) -> None:
        if new not in TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise InvalidTransition(f"{self.state.value} -> {new.value}")
        self.state = new


def demo():
    o = Order()
    o.transition(State.PAID)
    o.transition(State.SHIPPED)
    o.transition(State.DELIVERED)
    o.transition(State.REFUNDED)
    assert o.state is State.REFUNDED

    for bad in (State.PAID, State.CANCELLED):  # terminal: nothing leaves
        try:
            o.transition(bad)
            assert False, "should have raised"
        except InvalidTransition:
            pass

    o = Order()
    try:
        o.transition(State.SHIPPED)  # can't skip payment
        assert False, "should have raised"
    except InvalidTransition:
        pass
    assert o.state is State.CREATED  # failed transition leaves state untouched


if __name__ == "__main__":
    demo()
    print("ok")
```

Transition rules I chose:
- created → paid | cancelled
- paid → shipped | cancelled | refunded
- shipped → delivered (no cancel or refund mid-flight; handle returns after delivery)
- delivered → refunded
- cancelled, refunded: terminal

Skipped: transition hooks/events, persistence, and concurrency control. Add when needed; for concurrent writers, make the update a compare-and-set on the current state (`UPDATE ... WHERE state = :old`). If your business allows cancelling shipped orders, add `State.CANCELLED` to that row.

Python, stdlib only:

```python
from enum import Enum


class State(Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"


S = State
TRANSITIONS: dict[State, frozenset[State]] = {
    S.CREATED:   frozenset({S.PAID, S.CANCELLED}),
    S.PAID:      frozenset({S.SHIPPED, S.REFUNDED}),
    S.SHIPPED:   frozenset({S.DELIVERED}),
    S.DELIVERED: frozenset({S.REFUNDED}),
    S.CANCELLED: frozenset(),  # terminal
    S.REFUNDED:  frozenset(),  # terminal
}


class InvalidTransition(Exception):
    def __init__(self, src: State, dst: State):
        super().__init__(f"illegal transition: {src.value} -> {dst.value}")
        self.src, self.dst = src, dst


class Order:
    def __init__(self, state: State = State.CREATED):
        self.state = state

    def transition(self, dst: State) -> None:
        if dst not in TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise InvalidTransition(self.state, dst)
        self.state = dst

    # convenience wrappers
    def pay(self):     self.transition(State.PAID)
    def ship(self):    self.transition(State.SHIPPED)
    def deliver(self): self.transition(State.DELIVERED)
    def cancel(self):  self.transition(State.CANCELLED)
    def refund(self):  self.transition(State.REFUNDED)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    o = Order()
    o.pay(); o.ship(); o.deliver(); o.refund()
    assert o.state is State.REFUNDED

    try:
        o.ship()
    except InvalidTransition as e:
        print(e)  # illegal transition: refunded -> shipped
```

Design choices (my assumptions, adjust the table if your rules differ):
- Cancel only from `created`; once paid, the exit is `refunded` (money must move back).
- No refund after `shipped` until `delivered` (in-transit returns would need a new state).
- `cancelled`/`refunded` terminal.

Skipped: persistence/concurrency. When storing in a DB, make the update atomic: `UPDATE orders SET state=:dst WHERE id=:id AND state=:src`, and treat 0 rows affected as an invalid/raced transition. Otherwise two concurrent requests can both pass the in-memory check.

## large-s3 / apidesign

> Design the REST API for a multi-tenant document store: resources, auth model, pagination, versioning and error format. Explain the decisions.

### vanilla: 3678 answer tokens, 3678 billed, 174 lines

~~~
# Multi-Tenant Document Store REST API

## 1. Tenancy model

**Decision: tenant is a path prefix, derived from and checked against the credential.**

```
https://api.example.com/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/...
```

- Explicit in the URL, so it's visible in logs, caches, rate limiters and routing.
- The server still verifies that the token is authorized for `{tenant_id}`. The path is never trusted by itself.
- Return **404, not 403**, for tenants/resources the caller can't see, so existence isn't leaked across tenants.
- Alternative (subdomain per tenant, `acme.api.example.com`) is fine for custom domains, but the path form is simpler for clients and TLS.

## 2. Resources

```
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections                      GET, POST
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}                  GET, PATCH, DELETE
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents        GET (list/filter), POST (create)
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}    GET, PUT, PATCH, DELETE
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}/versions        GET
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}/versions/{n}    GET
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents/{d}/content         GET, PUT   (binary blobs)
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents:search              POST
/v1/tenants/{t}/collections/{c}/documents:batchWrite          POST
/v1/tenants/{t}/api-keys                         GET, POST
/v1/tenants/{t}/api-keys/{k}                     DELETE
/v1/tenants/{t}/members                          GET, POST, PATCH, DELETE
/v1/tenants/{t}/usage                            GET
```

Decisions:
- **Collections** group documents and carry settings (optional JSON schema, retention, default ACL). Documents are nested so authorization and quotas are scoped naturally.
- **Metadata vs content split.** The document resource is JSON metadata plus the body; large binaries go to `/content` (supports `Range`, `Content-Type`, and presigned-URL redirects).
- **Custom methods with `:verb`** (Google style) for actions that aren't CRUD: `:search` (complex queries need a body) and `:batchWrite`.
- **Versions are read-only sub-resources.** Every write creates an immutable version; restore by `POST .../versions/{n}:restore`.
- **DELETE is soft by default** (`deleted_at`, recoverable for the retention window); `?permanent=true` requires an elevated scope.
- **Client-supplied IDs are allowed** via `PUT` on a known URL (idempotent upsert); `POST` generates a server ID (ULID/UUIDv7, sortable).

Document representation:

```json
{
  "id": "doc_01J9ZK...",
  "collection": "contracts",
  "title": "MSA 2026",
  "content_type": "application/json",
  "data": { "...": "..." },
  "labels": { "status": "draft" },
  "version": 7,
  "etag": "\"7-9f2c\"",
  "created_at": "2026-11-23T10:00:00Z",
  "updated_at": "2026-11-23T10:05:00Z",
  "created_by": "usr_123"
}
```

## 3. Auth model

**Authentication**
- **OAuth 2.0 / OIDC bearer tokens (JWT)** for users and apps: `Authorization: Bearer <token>`.
- **API keys** (`sk_live_...`) for server-to-server use, scoped to one tenant, stored hashed, shown once, revocable, with optional expiry.
- JWT claims include `sub`, `tenant_id` (or a list of tenants for users in multiple), `scope`, and `exp`. Short TTL (≈15 min) with refresh tokens.

**Authorization (two layers)**
1. **Scopes** on the token, coarse: `documents:read`, `documents:write`, `collections:admin`, `tenant:admin`.
2. **RBAC/ACLs** inside the tenant: roles (viewer, editor, admin) assigned per tenant or per collection, with optional per-document overrides.

**Isolation enforcement**
- Every query is filtered by `tenant_id` at the data layer (row-level security, or a tenant-scoped repository), never only in handlers.
- Storage keys and encryption keys are namespaced per tenant (optionally per-tenant KMS keys for enterprise).
- Rate limits and quotas are per tenant, with a per-key sub-limit, so one noisy tenant can't starve the rest (`429` + `Retry-After`).
- Audit log of every authz decision and write, queryable at `/audit-events`.

## 4. Pagination, filtering, sorting

**Decision: cursor-based pagination.** Offset pagination skips or duplicates rows under concurrent writes and gets slow at depth.

```
GET .../documents?limit=50&page_token=eyJ...
```

```json
{
  "items": [ ... ],
  "next_page_token": "eyJ0IjoiMDFKOVpL...",
  "has_more": true
}
```

- `limit` default 50, max 200. Tokens are opaque (base64 of the sort key plus tie-breaker `id`, signed, and bound to the tenant and query so they can't be replayed elsewhere).
- Stable ordering: default `sort=-created_at,id`. Allowed sort fields are whitelisted and indexed.
- Filtering: simple equality via query params (`labels.status=draft`, `created_after=...`); anything richer goes to `:search` with a JSON body.
- No total count by default (expensive); `?include_total=true` returns an approximate or exact `total`.
- Link headers (`rel="next"`) are optional; the body field is the primary mechanism.

## 5. Versioning

Two distinct concepts:

**API versioning:** major version in the URL path (`/v1`).
- Visible, cacheable, easy to route and debug.
- Within a major version, only **additive** changes (new fields, endpoints, optional params). Clients must ignore unknown fields.
- Breaking changes mean `/v2`, with `Deprecation` and `Sunset` headers on `/v1` and at least 12 months of overlap.
- Optional date-pinned behavior tweaks via a header (`API-Version: 2026-11-01`) for minor behavioral changes without a new major.

**Document versioning:** optimistic concurrency.
- Every document has a monotonically increasing `version` and an `ETag`.
- `PUT`/`PATCH`/`DELETE` require `If-Match: "<etag>"` (or return `428 Precondition Required` if the collection is configured strict). A mismatch returns `412 Precondition Failed`.
- `PATCH` uses JSON Merge Patch (`application/merge-patch+json`) by default, JSON Patch (RFC 6902) when a client needs array operations.
- `GET` supports `If-None-Match` → `304`.

**Idempotency:** `POST` and `:batchWrite` accept an `Idempotency-Key` header. The server stores the response per (tenant, key) for 24 hours and replays it for retries.

## 6. Error format

**Decision: RFC 9457 Problem Details** (`application/problem+json`). It's a standard, so clients and tooling already understand it, and it's extensible.

```json
{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/errors/version-conflict",
  "title": "Version conflict",
  "status": 412,
  "detail": "Document was modified; current version is 8, you sent 7.",
  "instance": "/v1/tenants/acme/collections/contracts/documents/doc_01J9ZK",
  "code": "version_conflict",
  "request_id": "req_8f3a...",
  "errors": [
    { "pointer": "/data/amount", "code": "invalid_type", "message": "must be a number" }
  ]
}
```

- `code` is a stable machine-readable string; `title`/`detail` are for humans and may change.
- `errors[]` carries per-field validation failures, using JSON Pointers.
- `request_id` is also returned in an `X-Request-Id` response header, for support and tracing.
- Never leak internals (stack traces, other tenants' info).

Status code map:

| Status | Use |
|---|---|
| 400 | Malformed request |
| 401 | Missing or invalid credentials |
| 403 | Authenticated but lacks scope/role (within a visible tenant) |
| 404 | Not found, **or** not visible to this caller |
| 409 | Conflict (duplicate ID, state conflict) |
| 412 / 428 | ETag mismatch / precondition required |
| 413 | Document or batch too large |
| 415 | Unsupported content type |
| 422 | Schema/semantic validation failure |
| 429 | Rate limit or quota exceeded (`Retry-After`) |
| 5xx | Server errors (`503` with `Retry-After` for overload) |

## 7. Other cross-cutting choices

- **Batch writes:** `:batchWrite` returns `207`-style per-item results inside a `200` body (`results: [{index, status, document|error}]`), max 100 items, optionally `atomic: true`.
- **Async operations:** long jobs (bulk export, reindex) return `202` with `Location: /v1/tenants/{t}/operations/{op}`, which can be polled.
- **Webhooks/change feed:** `GET .../documents:changes?since=<cursor>` for sync clients, plus tenant-configured webhooks signed with HMAC.
- **Transport:** TLS only, gzip/br compression, `application/json; charset=utf-8`, RFC 3339 UTC timestamps, snake_case field names.
- **Docs:** publish an OpenAPI 3.1 spec as the source of truth, generate SDKs and contract tests from it.

## Summary of the key tradeoffs

| Decision | Chosen | Why | Cost |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tenant location | Path prefix | Explicit, routable, loggable | Longer URLs |
| Pagination | Opaque cursors | Stable, scalable | No random page access |
| API version | URL major + additive evolution | Simple and visible | Occasional big migrations |
| Concurrency | ETag + `If-Match` | Prevents lost updates, standard HTTP | Clients must track ETags |
| Errors | RFC 9457 + stable `code` | Standard, extensible | Slightly more verbose |
| Cross-tenant misses | 404 over 403 | No existence leak | Harder to debug for callers |
~~~
