# Multi-tenant document store REST API

## Resources

```
/v1/tenants/{tenant}                                  GET, PATCH
/v1/tenants/{tenant}/members                          GET, POST
/v1/tenants/{tenant}/members/{member}                 GET, PATCH, DELETE
/v1/tenants/{tenant}/api-keys                         GET, POST
/v1/tenants/{tenant}/api-keys/{key}                   GET, DELETE
/v1/tenants/{tenant}/collections                      GET, POST
/v1/tenants/{tenant}/collections/{coll}               GET, PATCH, DELETE
/v1/tenants/{tenant}/collections/{coll}/documents     GET (list/filter), POST (create)
/v1/tenants/{tenant}/collections/{coll}/documents/{id}            GET, PUT, PATCH, DELETE
/v1/tenants/{tenant}/collections/{coll}/documents/{id}/revisions  GET
/v1/tenants/{tenant}/collections/{coll}/documents/{id}/revisions/{rev}  GET
/v1/tenants/{tenant}/collections/{coll}/documents:search          POST
/v1/tenants/{tenant}/collections/{coll}/documents:batch           POST
/v1/tenants/{tenant}/webhooks                         GET, POST
```

**Decisions**
- **Tenant in path, not only in token.** Visible in logs, cache keys, routing, rate limits. Server checks path tenant == token tenant. Mismatch → `404`, not `403`. No tenant enumeration.
- **Collections as middle level.** Gives per-collection schema, permissions, quotas.
- **Nouns for resources, `:verb` for non-CRUD** (`:search`, `:batch`). Search takes complex body; GET with giant query string breaks.
- **Revisions = read-only subresource.** Document history is separate from API versioning. Don't conflate.
- **PATCH = JSON Merge Patch** (`application/merge-patch+json`). Simple. Use JSON Patch only if clients need array-element edits.
- **Large binaries** not inline. Separate `/attachments` with presigned upload URLs if needed.

## Auth model

- **Humans:** OAuth2/OIDC, short-lived JWT access token. Claims: `sub`, `tenant`, `scope`, `role`.
- **Services:** API keys, hashed at rest, shown once, scoped, expirable, revocable. Sent as `Authorization: Bearer <key>`. Prefix key (`dk_live_…`) for leak scanning.
- **AuthZ:** RBAC (`owner`, `admin`, `editor`, `viewer`) at tenant level, optional per-collection grants. Scopes like `documents:read`, `documents:write` cap what a token can do regardless of role.
- **Isolation defense in depth:** tenant ID from verified token, never from body. Enforce in the data layer too (Postgres row-level security, or per-tenant keyspace/prefix). One missed `WHERE tenant_id=` must not leak data.
- **Status codes:** `401` no/bad credential. `403` authenticated but lacks permission within own tenant. `404` for cross-tenant.
- **Limits:** per-tenant rate limit and quota, `429` + `Retry-After`, `RateLimit-*` headers. Stops noisy neighbor.

## Concurrency and idempotency

- Every document has `ETag` (revision hash). `PUT`/`PATCH`/`DELETE` require `If-Match`. Stale → `412 Precondition Failed`. Missing → `428`. Prevents lost updates.
- `POST` accepts `Idempotency-Key`. Safe client retry, no duplicate docs. Store key + response 24h.
- Client-supplied ID allowed via `PUT /documents/{id}` (create-or-replace with `If-None-Match: *` for create-only).

## Pagination

Cursor-based, not offset.

```
GET /v1/tenants/acme/collections/inv/documents?limit=50&cursor=eyJ...&sort=-created_at
```
```json
{
  "data": [ ... ],
  "page": { "next_cursor": "eyJ...", "has_more": true }
}
```
Also `Link: <...>; rel="next"`.

- **Why:** offset is slow on deep pages and skips/duplicates rows when data changes. Cursor encodes last sort key + id (opaque, signed, tenant-bound).
- `limit` default 25, max 100.
- **No total count by default.** Expensive at scale. Opt in with `?include=total` (may be approximate).
- Sort must have a unique tiebreaker (`id`) for stable cursors.
- Filtering: `?filter[status]=active&filter[created_at][gte]=...`. Complex queries go to `:search`.

## Versioning

- **Major version in URL** (`/v1`). Easy to route, cache, debug, and pin per client.
- **Additive changes are non-breaking** within a version: new fields, endpoints, optional params. Clients must ignore unknown fields.
- Breaking change → `/v2`, run both. Announce with `Deprecation` and `Sunset` headers plus changelog. Minimum 6–12 month window.
- Optional per-tenant pinning for gradual rollouts is possible but adds cost. Skip until needed.
- Document schema versioning (per collection) is separate. Stored as `schema_version` on collection.

## Error format

RFC 9457 `application/problem+json`:

```json
{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/errors/validation-failed",
  "title": "Validation failed",
  "status": 422,
  "code": "validation_failed",
  "detail": "2 fields invalid.",
  "instance": "/v1/tenants/acme/collections/inv/documents",
  "request_id": "req_8f3a1c",
  "errors": [
    { "pointer": "/price", "code": "type_mismatch", "message": "Expected number." },
    { "pointer": "/sku",   "code": "required",      "message": "Missing field." }
  ]
}
```

**Decisions**
- Standard format → existing client libs and tooling work.
- Stable machine-readable `code` for branching. `detail` for humans, may change.
- `request_id` also in `X-Request-Id` header, for support.
- Never leak internals (stack traces, other tenants' IDs, SQL).

| Status | Use |
|---|---|
| 400 | Malformed request |
| 401 / 403 / 404 | Authn / authz / missing or cross-tenant |
| 409 | Conflict (duplicate ID, unique violation) |
| 412 / 428 | ETag mismatch / missing `If-Match` |
| 413 | Payload too large |
| 422 | Valid JSON, fails schema or validation |
| 429 | Rate/quota exceeded |
| 5xx | Server fault. Retryable with backoff |

## Other notes

- JSON only, `snake_case`, RFC 3339 UTC timestamps.
- Soft delete by default (`deleted_at`), purge endpoint for compliance (GDPR erase).
- Batch returns per-item status, `207`-style body, so one failure doesn't void the batch.
- Webhooks signed with HMAC, per-tenant secret.
- Publish OpenAPI spec as source of truth, generate SDKs from it.

Open questions that would change design: schema-less vs schema-enforced docs, expected doc size, need for cross-collection queries, need for per-tenant data residency.
~~~

### ponytail: 2495 answer tokens, 3004 billed, 108 lines

~~~
# Multi-tenant document store REST API

## 1. Tenancy model

**The tenant comes from the credential, not the URL.** Every token or API key is bound to exactly one tenant, so there is no `/tenants/{id}/...` prefix on the data plane.

- A client can't reach another tenant's data by editing an ID in the path.
- Storage queries always get `WHERE tenant_id = <from token>`, enforced in one middleware (plus row-level security in the DB if you can).
- A resource in another tenant returns **404, not 403**, so IDs can't be probed for existence.
- Users who belong to several tenants get a separate token per tenant, issued at login with `tenant` as a parameter.

A separate **platform/admin plane** (`/v1/admin/tenants`) is for your own operators. It uses different credentials and a different audience claim.

## 2. Resources

```
/v1/collections                         GET, POST
/v1/collections/{cid}                   GET, PATCH, DELETE
/v1/collections/{cid}/documents         GET (list/filter), POST (create)
/v1/documents/{did}                     GET, PATCH (JSON Merge Patch), PUT, DELETE
/v1/documents/{did}/content             GET, PUT          (raw bytes, any media type)
/v1/documents/{did}/revisions           GET
/v1/documents/{did}/revisions/{rev}     GET
/v1/documents/{did}/revisions/{rev}:restore   POST
/v1/documents/{did}/permissions         GET, POST
/v1/documents/{did}/permissions/{pid}   DELETE
/v1/search?q=...                        GET
/v1/members                             GET, POST          (tenant users and roles)
/v1/api-keys                            GET, POST
/v1/api-keys/{kid}                      DELETE
```

Decisions:
- **Documents are top-level (`/documents/{did}`) and listed under a collection.** Document IDs are globally unique opaque IDs (ULID/UUID). This avoids deep nesting, and moving a document between collections is just `PATCH {"collection_id": ...}`.
- **Metadata and content are separate.** The JSON resource holds `id, name, collection_id, content_type, size, etag, revision, created_at, updated_at, labels`. Bytes live at `/content`, so listings stay small and big files can be streamed or redirected to object storage (`302` to a presigned URL on GET, and the same pattern for PUT).
- **Revisions are immutable and read-only.** Every write creates one. Restore is a custom `:action` verb because it's not CRUD.
- **Soft delete.** `DELETE` returns 204 and the document stays recoverable for a retention window. `?permanent=true` requires an admin scope.
- **Filtering** uses plain query params: `?collection_id=&label=k:v&updated_after=&sort=-updated_at`.

## 3. Auth model

- **Authentication:** OAuth2/OIDC bearer JWTs for humans and apps, and API keys (`Authorization: Bearer sk_...`, stored hashed, shown once) for service accounts. Both resolve to a principal `{tenant_id, subject, roles, scopes}`.
- **Authorization** has two layers:
  1. **Scopes** on the token (`documents:read`, `documents:write`, `members:admin`, ...) cap what a credential can do at all.
  2. **RBAC plus ACLs** decide per resource. Tenant roles (`owner`, `admin`, `editor`, `viewer`) apply by default, and per-collection or per-document permissions grant or restrict further.
- The effective permission is the **intersection** of scope and role/ACL. A read-only API key owned by an admin is still read-only.
- **Audit log:** every mutating call records `tenant, principal, action, resource, request_id`.
- **Rate limits and quotas** are per tenant, so one noisy tenant can't starve the others. They're reported in `RateLimit-*` headers, with `429` and `Retry-After` when exceeded.

## 4. Concurrency and idempotency

- Every document returns an `ETag`. `PUT`, `PATCH` and `DELETE` require `If-Match`, otherwise **428**. A mismatch returns **412**. This prevents lost updates between concurrent editors.
- `POST` accepts an `Idempotency-Key` header. The server stores the response per `(tenant, key)` for 24h so client retries don't create duplicates.

## 5. Pagination

**Cursor-based** on every list endpoint:

```
GET /v1/collections/c_1/documents?limit=50&cursor=eyJ...
→ { "items": [...], "next_cursor": "eyJ..." | null }
```

- `limit` defaults to 50 and is capped at 200.
- The cursor is opaque (base64 of the sort key plus the last ID, signed). Clients can't depend on its structure, and you can change the internals later.
- Offset pagination is rejected because it's slow on big tenants and skips or duplicates rows when data changes mid-scan.
- There's no total count by default, since it's expensive. `?include_total=true` is available where it's cheap.

## 6. Versioning

- **Major version in the URL (`/v1`).** It's visible, cacheable, easy to route and easy to debug.
- **Within a major version, only additive changes are allowed:** new fields, endpoints and optional params. Clients must ignore unknown fields.
- A breaking change means `/v2`, run side by side. Deprecated endpoints send `Deprecation` and `Sunset` headers and a `Link` to the migration guide.
- Header or media-type versioning was rejected as harder to test with curl and a common source of caching bugs.

## 7. Error format

[RFC 9457](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9457) `application/problem+json`, with a stable machine-readable `code`:

```json
{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/errors/revision-conflict",
  "title": "Revision conflict",
  "status": 412,
  "code": "revision_conflict",
  "detail": "Document d_01H... was modified; current ETag is \"7\".",
  "request_id": "req_8f2c",
  "errors": [{ "field": "name", "code": "too_long", "message": "max 255 chars" }]
}
```

| Status | Use |
|---|---|
| 400 / 422 | Malformed request / failed validation (`errors[]` populated) |
| 401 | Missing or invalid credential |
| 403 | Authenticated but lacks permission (only for resources the caller can already see) |
| 404 | Not found, **or in another tenant** |
| 409 | Name or state conflict |
| 412 / 428 | `If-Match` mismatch / missing |
| 413 | Content too large |
| 429 | Rate limit or quota, with `Retry-After` |

Clients branch on `code`, never on `detail`. Internal errors return 500 with only the `request_id` and no stack traces. The same `request_id` goes in an `X-Request-Id` header on every response.

## Skipped, add when needed
- **Webhooks and event feeds:** add when clients need change notifications.
- **Batch endpoints:** add when per-request overhead shows up in profiles.
- **Per-tenant custom domains and regional data residency:** these come from deployment, not API shape, and can be layered on later.
~~~

### chisle: 2042 answer tokens, 2516 billed, 78 lines

~~~
## Resources

Tenant is a path prefix: `/v1/tenants/{tenant_id}/...`

```
/tenants/{t}                                  GET, PATCH (admin)
/tenants/{t}/members                          GET, POST; /{user_id} PATCH, DELETE
/tenants/{t}/api-keys                         GET, POST; /{id} DELETE
/tenants/{t}/collections                      GET, POST; /{id} GET, PATCH, DELETE
/tenants/{t}/documents                        GET (?collection=&q=&updated_after=), POST
/tenants/{t}/documents/{id}                   GET, PATCH, DELETE
/tenants/{t}/documents/{id}/content           GET, PUT (blob)
/tenants/{t}/documents/{id}/revisions         GET; /{rev} GET
/tenants/{t}/documents/{id}/revisions/{rev}:restore   POST
/tenants/{t}/documents/{id}/permissions       GET, PUT
/tenants/{t}/webhooks                         GET, POST; /{id} DELETE
```

- **Metadata and content are split.** `POST /documents` creates metadata (name, collection, content type, tags) and returns an `upload_url` (presigned object-store URL) for large files. Small files can `PUT .../content` directly. This keeps API servers out of the byte path.
- **Revisions are read-only and immutable.** Every content change creates one. Restore creates a new revision rather than rewriting history.
- **Custom actions use `:verb` suffixes** (`:restore`) instead of verbs in paths.
- **DELETE is a soft delete** with a retention window. `?permanent=true` is admin-only.

## Auth model

- **Users:** OIDC/OAuth2 authorization code + PKCE. The access token is a JWT carrying `sub`, `tenant_ids`, and scopes (`docs:read`, `docs:write`, `admin`).
- **Services:** per-tenant API keys (`Authorization: Bearer sk_...`), stored hashed, scoped, and revocable. Each is bound to exactly one tenant.
- **Tenant isolation:** middleware checks that the `{tenant_id}` path segment is in the token's tenant claim. On mismatch, return **404, not 403**, so tenant and resource existence doesn't leak. Every DB query is also scoped by `tenant_id` (row-level security in Postgres as a backstop), so a missing check can't cause a cross-tenant read.
- **Authorization:** role per member (owner/admin/editor/viewer) plus optional per-collection or per-document ACLs via `/permissions`. Deny by default, with the most specific grant winning.

**Why the tenant is in the path rather than only in the token:** it's explicit, cacheable, visible in logs and metrics, and lets one user work across several tenants without token juggling. Path and token are cross-checked, so it can't be spoofed.

## Pagination

Cursor-based: `?limit=50&cursor=<opaque>`, default 50, max 100.

```json
{ "data": [...], "next_cursor": "eyJ...", "has_more": true }
```

Offset pagination breaks under concurrent writes (skips and duplicates) and gets slow on deep pages. The cursor encodes the sort key plus ID, so it's stable and index-friendly. Sort is fixed or allowlisted (`sort=-updated_at`) and the cursor is only valid for the same query. Don't return total counts by default (expensive). Offer `?include_total=true` if needed.

## Versioning

- **API:** major version in the URL (`/v1`). Only breaking changes bump it. Adding fields, endpoints, or optional params is non-breaking, so clients must ignore unknown fields. Deprecations are signaled with `Deprecation` and `Sunset` headers, with at least 6 months' notice.
- **Documents:** optimistic concurrency. Responses carry `ETag: "<rev>"`, and `PATCH`/`PUT`/`DELETE` require `If-Match`. A stale value returns `412 Precondition Failed`. A missing header returns `428`. This prevents silent lost updates.
- **Idempotency:** `POST` accepts an `Idempotency-Key` header, stored 24h per tenant, so retries don't create duplicates.

## Error format

RFC 9457 `application/problem+json`:

```json
{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/errors/validation",
  "title": "Validation failed",
  "status": 422,
  "code": "validation_failed",
  "detail": "2 fields invalid",
  "request_id": "req_8f3a...",
  "errors": [
    { "field": "name", "code": "required", "message": "name is required" }
  ]
}
```

- Clients switch on the stable machine-readable `code`, never on `title` or `detail`.
- Status mapping: 400 malformed, 401 no or bad credentials, 403 authenticated but not permitted (within a tenant), 404 not found or cross-tenant, 409 conflict (e.g. duplicate name), 412/428 concurrency, 413 too large, 422 validation, 429 rate limited (with `Retry-After`), 5xx server.
- `request_id` is also returned as an `X-Request-Id` header and logged, so support can trace any failure.

## Other decisions

- **Rate limits:** per tenant plus per key, with `RateLimit-*` headers. This stops one noisy tenant from starving the others.
- **Filtering:** simple query params (`collection`, `tag`, `updated_after`) and full-text via `q`. Avoid a custom query language until it's needed.
- **Webhooks:** signed with HMAC and retried with backoff, so clients can sync via events instead of polling.
- **Audit log:** a read-only `/audit-events` resource (admin) is worth adding early, since tenants will ask for it.

**Skipped:** GraphQL/bulk endpoints, resumable multipart upload details, and sharing links. Add them when a concrete client need appears.
~~~

## large-s3 / architecture

> Explain how you would migrate a monolith to services without a big-bang rewrite, covering ordering, data ownership, and how to keep it shippable throughout.

### vanilla: 2536 answer tokens, 2536 billed, 77 lines

~~~
# Migrating a monolith to services incrementally

The core approach is the strangler fig: grow new services around the monolith, route traffic to them one slice at a time, and shrink the monolith until it can be retired (or left as a small, healthy core). Every step should be independently deployable and reversible.

## 0. Prerequisites (do these first)

- **Fix the deploy pipeline and test coverage on the monolith.** If you can't ship the monolith safely and often, you can't ship services safely either.
- **Add observability**: distributed tracing, structured logs with correlation IDs, per-endpoint metrics. You need a baseline to prove a migrated slice behaves the same.
- **Make the monolith modular internally.** Enforce module boundaries inside the codebase (package visibility, dependency-rule linters, separate schemas per module). A "modular monolith" is often the real goal; extraction is then mostly mechanical. If a boundary can't hold inside one process, it won't survive a network.
- **Put a routing seam in front**: an API gateway, reverse proxy, or facade layer. This is what lets you shift traffic per route without clients noticing.

## 1. Ordering: what to extract and when

Pick candidates by scoring on value vs. risk:

1. **First extraction: low-risk, loosely coupled, with a clear edge.** Think notifications, document rendering, search, auth/identity, or a read-heavy catalog. The goal is to build the platform muscle (CI/CD templates, service discovery, secrets, on-call, tracing) with low blast radius, not to win the biggest architectural prize.
2. **Then extract by pain**: the parts that need independent scaling, deploy cadence far faster than the rest, different tech, or are owned by a distinct team. Churn data from version control (hot spots) plus incident history shows where the pain is.
3. **Leave the tangled core for last**: the highly connected domain center (e.g. orders/pricing) is where most coupling lives. By the time you reach it, the periphery is gone and the seams are clearer. Some of it may never need extraction.
4. **Extract leaves before roots**: prefer components with few inbound dependencies first (others depend on them less), or ones that depend on little (they call out rarely).

Align boundaries to **business capabilities / bounded contexts** and team ownership (Conway's law), not technical layers. A "database service" or "utils service" is a distributed monolith.

## 2. Per-slice extraction pattern

For each candidate, repeat the same loop:

1. **Define the contract** (API or events) while the code is still in the monolith; make the monolith call its own module through that interface.
2. **Build the service behind that interface**, initially as a thin wrapper or copy of the module's logic.
3. **Dark launch / shadow traffic**: send mirrored requests to the new service, compare responses, don't serve them.
4. **Canary via feature flag or gateway routing**: 1% → 10% → 50% → 100%, per tenant or per route, with automatic rollback on error/latency regression.
5. **Delete the old code** from the monolith once stable. Skipping this leaves two sources of truth and is the most common way migrations stall.

Use an **anti-corruption layer** where the new service must talk to legacy models, so the monolith's messy concepts don't leak into the new design.

## 3. Data ownership (the hard part)

The rule: **each table has exactly one owning service; others access it only through that service's API or published events.** Code extraction is easy; data extraction is where migrations fail. Do it in stages:

1. **Logical separation first**: within the shared database, give the module its own schema and forbid cross-schema joins and foreign keys from other modules (enforce in CI). Fix the violations while everything is still one transaction boundary.
2. **Replace joins with explicit calls or replicated read models.** Consumers that need another domain's data either call its API or keep a local, eventually consistent copy populated by events.
3. **Physically split the database** only after the service is live and no one else touches its tables. Typical sequence:
   - Service reads/writes the shared DB (own schema only).
   - Introduce change data capture (CDC) or the **outbox pattern** to publish changes reliably, and replicate to the new store.
   - **Dual-write or CDC-sync** with reconciliation checks, then flip reads to the new store, then flip writes, then drop the old tables. Each flip is reversible until the last.
4. **Handle transactions that spanned modules**: replace with sagas (orchestrated or choreographed), idempotent operations, and compensating actions. Accept eventual consistency deliberately; for each cross-service invariant decide whether it truly needs strong consistency (if yes, the boundary is probably wrong, so merge those pieces).
5. **Reporting/analytics**: don't let people query service DBs directly. Stream to a warehouse or lake.
6. **Migrate IDs carefully**: keep existing primary keys, or maintain a mapping, so references in other data and external systems stay valid.

## 4. Keeping it shippable throughout

- **Trunk-based development with small, reversible changes.** No long-lived "migration branch." Everything merges behind flags.
- **Feature flags and gateway routing as the rollback mechanism.** Rolling back is a config change, not a redeploy. Keep the old path working until the new one has proven itself over a full business cycle (e.g. month-end).
- **Expand/contract (parallel change) for every schema and API change**: add new, migrate consumers, remove old. Never a breaking change in one step.
- **Contract tests (e.g. Pact) and backward-compatible API versioning** so services and monolith can deploy independently without lockstep releases.
- **Parity verification**: shadow traffic diffs, data reconciliation jobs, and SLO-based canary gates before each ramp-up.
- **One migration in flight per team at a time.** Finish and delete before starting the next; limit work-in-progress so you never have five half-extracted modules.
- **Keep delivering features.** Do extractions as part of feature work where possible (build the new feature in the new service) rather than as a freeze. A freeze kills stakeholder support and the migration with it.

## 5. Platform and operational concerns

- Standardize a **service template** (logging, metrics, health checks, auth, CI/CD, retries/timeouts/circuit breakers) so the 5th service costs a fraction of the 1st.
- Treat **timeouts, retries with backoff, idempotency, and bulkheads** as mandatory; a synchronous call chain turns one slow service into an outage.
- Prefer **async events** for cross-domain notification and **sync APIs** for queries that need an immediate answer. Don't build deep synchronous call chains.
- Establish **ownership and on-call** per service before it takes production traffic.

## 6. Know when to stop

Measure outcomes: deploy frequency, lead time, change failure rate, incident blast radius. If extracting another piece doesn't improve those or unblock a team, stop. A monolith with several well-bounded services around it is a perfectly good end state. The goal is independent delivery and fewer coordination costs, not a service count.

## Common failure modes

- **Distributed monolith**: services that must deploy together or share a database.
- **Extracting by technical layer** instead of business capability.
- **Shared database "temporarily"** that becomes permanent.
- **Never deleting the old code**, leaving two implementations to maintain.
- **Starting with the hardest, most central module** and burning out the team's credibility before the platform exists.
- **No metrics**, so no one can tell whether the migration helped.
~~~

### caveman: 1749 answer tokens, 1809 billed, 67 lines

~~~
# Monolith → services, no big bang

**Core idea:** strangler fig. Build new around old, route traffic piece by piece, shrink monolith until gone (or good enough). Every step ship to prod.

## 0. Prep (before any extraction)
- Add tests/observability to monolith first: tracing, per-endpoint metrics, error rates. No baseline → can't prove migration safe.
- Fix modular boundaries *inside* monolith first (modular monolith). Cheap to refactor in-process, costly across network.
- Put routing layer in front (gateway/proxy/feature flags). Lets traffic shift per route, per tenant, per percent.

## 1. Ordering: what to extract first

Pick by **value × ease**, not by size.

- **First: easy, low-risk, leaf-like.** Few inbound deps, own clear data, tolerable failure. Examples: notifications, search, file/media, auth-adjacent read paths. Goal = prove pipeline (CI/CD, deploy, observability, on-call), not business win.
- **Next: high-pain seams.** Part that need independent scale, deploy cadence, or team ownership. Part that change most often (git churn + merge conflicts = signal).
- **Last: core, tangled domain** (orders, billing, ledger). Highest coupling, strongest consistency needs. Maybe never extract.
- Find seams with: dependency graph, call counts, co-change analysis. Extract what has few *incoming* calls first; many-callers modules are hard.
- Don't extract by technical layer (UI/service/DB). Extract by business capability (bounded context).

## 2. Data ownership: hard part

Rule: **each table has one owning service. Others get data via API or events, never direct SQL.**

Steps per extraction:
1. **Inside monolith**, wrap capability behind interface. Kill cross-module joins and foreign tables access. Callers go through interface only.
2. **Separate schema** in same DB first (logical split). Enforce with DB permissions. Cheap, reversible.
3. **Stand up service**, still reading/writing same data via owner module.
4. **Sync data** to service's own store: backfill + CDC/outbox. Dual-write is risky (partial failure); prefer transactional outbox or CDC.
5. **Shadow/compare:** send reads to both, diff results, serve old. Fix mismatches.
6. **Flip reads**, then **flip writes** (single writer at a time). Never two writers to same data.
7. **Remove** old code and old tables only after soak period.

Handling joins/consistency:
- Cross-service join → API composition, or local read-replica via events (denormalized copy, owner stays source of truth).
- Cross-service transaction → no 2PC. Use sagas/compensation, idempotent handlers, eventual consistency. If business need strict atomicity, those things belong in same service. Boundary wrong → move it.
- Shared reference data → owned by one service, cached/replicated elsewhere.
- IDs: keep stable, don't renumber.

## 3. Keep shippable throughout

- **Trunk-based, small steps.** Each PR deployable. No long-lived migration branch.
- **Expand/contract** (parallel change) for every schema and API change: add new → dual support → migrate callers → remove old.
- **Feature flags / routing rules** control cutover. Rollback = flip flag, not redeploy.
- **Progressive rollout:** shadow traffic → internal users → 1% → 10% → 100%. Compare error rate, latency, business metrics at each step.
- **Contract tests** between monolith and service (consumer-driven). Catch breakage in CI, not prod.
- **Anti-corruption layer** at boundary: translate old model to new, so monolith mess don't leak into new service.
- **Keep monolith working during extraction.** Old path stays until new proven. Delete is last, separate step.
- **Feature work continue.** New features in new service when they touch extracted area; don't freeze roadmap. Freeze = migration dies politically.

## 4. Guardrails against common failures

- **Distributed monolith:** services chatty, deploy together, share DB. Test: can one service deploy alone? If no, boundary wrong.
- **Too many services too early.** Start coarse. Split further only with evidence.
- **Platform debt:** need CI/CD, tracing, service discovery, auth, retries/timeouts/circuit breakers *before* service #2, not after service #20.
- **Latency and failure:** in-process calls become network calls. Set timeouts, retries with backoff, idempotency keys, fallbacks.
- **No end date pressure to finish:** define done per service (traffic 100%, old code deleted, old tables dropped). Stop when remaining monolith is cheap to own.

## Per-service checklist
1. Seam identified, interface in monolith, tests pass
2. Schema split, cross-joins gone
3. Service deployed, observability in place
4. Data synced (CDC/outbox), shadow reads diffed clean
5. Reads flipped, then writes flipped, behind flag
6. Soak, then delete old code + data
7. Retro: what slowed us, update playbook

Repeat. Each loop ship value and shrink monolith.
~~~

### ponytail: 2187 answer tokens, 2187 billed, 63 lines

~~~
# Migrating a monolith to services without a big-bang rewrite

**Core approach: strangler fig.** Carve off one capability at a time behind a stable seam, route traffic to it gradually, and delete the old code only after the new path has proven itself. The monolith stays deployable the whole time.

## 0. Prerequisites (do first, cheap, high payoff)
- **Deploy pipeline, observability, and tests on the monolith.** If you can't ship the monolith safely today, you can't ship ten services.
- **Make it a modular monolith first.** Enforce module boundaries in-process (package visibility rules, import-lint, no cross-module table joins). This is most of the design work, and it's reversible. Many boundaries turn out wrong; finding that out in-process costs a refactor, not a distributed-systems incident.
- **Ask whether you need services at all** for each module. Reasons that justify extraction: independent scaling, independent release cadence/team ownership, fault isolation, different runtime needs. "Microservices are modern" doesn't.

## 1. Ordering: what to extract, in what sequence
Score candidates on **value** (pain it causes, team that wants autonomy, scaling need) vs. **coupling** (how many tables, callers, and transactions it touches).

1. **First: a low-risk, loosely coupled, but real capability** (notifications, PDF/report generation, search, auth-adjacent edge things). Goal is to build the paved road: service template, CI/CD, tracing, service-to-service auth, contract testing. Mistakes here are cheap.
2. **Then: high-value, moderately coupled domains** (e.g. catalog, pricing, billing), one at a time, each owned by a team.
3. **Last, or never: the tangled core** (the god-object `User`/`Order` domain with transactions everywhere). Often the right end state is a smaller monolith that remains the system of record, not zero monolith.
4. **Extract leaves before roots**: things that others call but that call little themselves are easiest, since dependencies point inward.

## 2. The mechanics of one extraction
1. **Define the seam in the monolith**: an interface/facade that all callers use. Callers never touch the module's tables directly.
2. **Build the service behind that interface**, initially as a thin implementation that can call the monolith's code or share its data.
3. **Route through the seam** (a proxy/API gateway, or a feature flag in the facade) with the old in-process implementation still available.
4. **Verify before cutover**: shadow traffic / dark launch, compare responses (parallel run), then ramp 1% → 10% → 100% per tenant or cohort.
5. **Keep rollback one flag-flip away** until confidence is high.
6. **Delete the old code** in a separate, later change. Dead code left in place becomes the next migration's confusion.

Use an **anti-corruption layer** where the new service's model differs from the monolith's, so legacy concepts don't leak into the new domain.

## 3. Data ownership (the hard part)
Rule of the end state: **one service owns each table; everyone else uses its API or consumes its events.** Getting there is staged:

1. **Identify ownership per table.** Shared tables that multiple modules write to must be assigned a single owner or split.
2. **Stage 1: shared DB, separate schema/ownership by convention.** Remove cross-module joins and direct writes in the monolith first. Replace joins with API calls or locally cached copies.
3. **Stage 2: new service gets its own database**, populated by:
   - **Dual write** (risky: no atomicity across two stores) or, preferably,
   - **Change data capture / outbox pattern**: the owner writes its DB and an outbox row in one transaction, a relay publishes events. Backfill historical data, then keep in sync via CDC.
4. **Cutover reads first, then writes.** Move reads to the new store (verified against the old), then flip write ownership, then stop syncing back.
5. **Handle what you lose**:
   - Cross-service joins → API composition or read-model replicas built from events.
   - Cross-service transactions → sagas with compensating actions, idempotent consumers, and explicit eventual consistency in the product behavior. If a workflow genuinely needs strict atomicity, that's a signal the two things belong in the same service.
6. **Reporting/analytics**: stream into a warehouse rather than querying service DBs across boundaries.

## 4. Keeping it shippable throughout
- **Trunk-based development, small increments**: every step (add seam, add flag, build service, ramp, delete old) is its own deployable change. No long-lived migration branch.
- **Feature flags and gradual rollout** control exposure; deploys are decoupled from releases.
- **Expand/contract (parallel change) for schemas and APIs**: add new, support both, migrate consumers, remove old. Never a breaking change in one step.
- **Contract tests** (e.g. consumer-driven) between monolith and each service so either side can deploy independently.
- **Backward-compatible, versioned APIs and events**; consumers tolerate unknown fields.
- **Observability from day one**: distributed tracing, correlation IDs, per-service SLOs, and a dashboard comparing old vs. new path (latency, error rate, result diffs).
- **Timeouts, retries with backoff, circuit breakers, and fallbacks** on every new network hop. A call that was a function is now a failure mode.
- **Freeze scope on the extracted slice**: during migration, new features for that domain go in one place only (ideally the new service) to avoid porting a moving target.
- **Feature parity first, improvements later.** Don't redesign the domain while moving it; you won't be able to tell regressions from intentional changes.

## 5. Organizational and pacing guidance
- **One extraction in flight per team**, with a definition of done that includes deleting the old code, the data cut over, and the runbook/on-call in place.
- **Align services to team ownership** (Conway's law); a service nobody owns is worse than a module.
- **Re-evaluate after each extraction**: did it deliver the expected benefit? Stop when the remaining monolith is healthy enough, since there is no obligation to finish.

## Common failure modes
- Extracting before boundaries are clean (resulting in a distributed monolith with synchronous call chains).
- Shared database kept indefinitely "temporarily".
- Dual writes without reconciliation.
- Skipping the paved road, so every service reinvents deploy, auth, and logging.
- Never deleting the old code, ending up maintaining both.
~~~

### chisle: 1061 answer tokens, 1145 billed, 25 lines

~~~
Strangler fig: put a seam in front of the monolith, move one capability at a time behind it, delete the old code once traffic is fully shifted. The monolith keeps shipping the whole time.

**Prep first.** Modularize inside the monolith before extracting anything. Enforce module boundaries (package visibility, import lint rules, no cross-module table joins). If you can't draw a clean boundary in-process, a network hop will make it worse. Also add a routing layer (gateway or proxy) and basic observability (tracing, per-endpoint metrics). Without these you can't see regressions or roll back.

**Ordering.**
- Start with a low-risk, loosely coupled, well-understood edge: notifications, search, media processing, auth. This builds the pipeline (CI/CD, deploy, tracing, on-call) at low cost.
- Next, extract what has the strongest business payoff: the parts with different scaling profiles, a high change rate, or a team bottleneck. Extract along business capabilities, not technical layers.
- Leave the entangled core (orders, billing, user model) for last, or never. Often it just stays as a smaller monolith, and that's a fine outcome.
- Extract leaves (few inbound dependencies) before hubs. A dependency graph or call/commit-coupling analysis picks the order better than intuition.

**Data ownership.** This is the hard part. Each service must own its data exclusively; a shared database is a distributed monolith.
1. Carve the module's tables out logically inside the shared DB. Remove cross-module joins and foreign keys, replacing them with API calls or ID references.
2. Route all access through the module's interface, so nothing else touches its tables.
3. Move the service to its own schema or database. Migrate in steps: dual-write (or CDC/outbox) to the new store, backfill, verify by comparing reads, flip reads, then stop writing to the old store.
4. Where others need your data, publish events (outbox pattern) and let them keep read-only local copies. Accept eventual consistency, and replace cross-service transactions with sagas or idempotent compensations. If two things truly need atomicity, they probably belong in the same service.

**Keeping it shippable.**
- Every step is independently deployable and reversible. Never have a half-migrated state that blocks releases.
- Shift traffic with feature flags or weighted routing: shadow traffic (mirror requests and diff responses), then 1%, 10%, 100%. Rollback is a flag flip.
- Run old and new paths in parallel until outputs match. Only then delete the old code. Skipping deletion leaves you with two systems.
- Use contract tests on the interfaces and version the APIs, so the monolith and services can deploy independently.
- Use an anti-corruption layer where the monolith's model is ugly, so it doesn't leak into the new service.
- Feature work continues on the monolith. Freeze changes only on the specific module mid-extraction, and keep that window short.

**Pitfalls.** Too many services too early means operational overhead outruns the benefit. Chatty synchronous calls between services mean the boundary is wrong. Treat "no migration" as a valid outcome for any module whose extraction doesn't pay for itself.
~~~

## large-s3 / authflow

> Implement email-and-password signup and login with password hashing, session issuing, and rate limiting on failed attempts. Show the code.

### vanilla: 3441 answer tokens, 3733 billed, 240 lines

~~~
This is a single-file Flask + SQLite implementation using `argon2-cffi`. It's meant as a solid starting point.

```bash
pip install flask argon2-cffi
```

```python
# auth.py
import hashlib
import re
import secrets
import sqlite3
import time

from argon2 import PasswordHasher
from argon2.exceptions import VerifyMismatchError, InvalidHashError
from flask import Flask, g, jsonify, request

app = Flask(__name__)
DB_PATH = "auth.db"

# ---- config ----
SESSION_TTL = 60 * 60 * 24 * 7          # 7 days
MAX_FAILS_PER_ACCOUNT = 5               # per email within window
MAX_FAILS_PER_IP = 20                   # per IP within window
WINDOW = 15 * 60                        # 15 min sliding window
COOKIE_NAME = "session"

ph = PasswordHasher()                   # argon2id with sane defaults
DUMMY_HASH = ph.hash("dummy-password-for-timing")  # equalizes timing for unknown emails
EMAIL_RE = re.compile(r"^[^@\s]+@[^@\s]+\.[^@\s]+$")


# ---- db ----
def db():
    if "db" not in g:
        g.db = sqlite3.connect(DB_PATH)
        g.db.row_factory = sqlite3.Row
    return g.db

@app.teardown_appcontext
def close_db(_):
    conn = g.pop("db", None)
    if conn:
        conn.close()

def init_db():
    conn = sqlite3.connect(DB_PATH)
    conn.executescript("""
    CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (
        id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
        email TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
        password_hash TEXT NOT NULL,
        created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
    );
    CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sessions (
        token_hash TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
        user_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
        expires_at INTEGER NOT NULL
    );
    CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS failed_logins (
        id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
        email TEXT NOT NULL,
        ip TEXT NOT NULL,
        at INTEGER NOT NULL
    );
    CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS idx_fail_email ON failed_logins(email, at);
    CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS idx_fail_ip ON failed_logins(ip, at);
    """)
    conn.commit()
    conn.close()


# ---- helpers ----
def normalize_email(email: str) -> str:
    return email.strip().lower()

def valid_password(pw: str) -> bool:
    return isinstance(pw, str) and 12 <= len(pw) <= 128

def hash_token(token: str) -> str:
    # Store only a hash so a DB leak doesn't expose live sessions.
    return hashlib.sha256(token.encode()).hexdigest()

def is_rate_limited(email: str, ip: str) -> bool:
    since = int(time.time()) - WINDOW
    c = db()
    by_email = c.execute(
        "SELECT COUNT(*) FROM failed_logins WHERE email=? AND at>?", (email, since)
    ).fetchone()[0]
    by_ip = c.execute(
        "SELECT COUNT(*) FROM failed_logins WHERE ip=? AND at>?", (ip, since)
    ).fetchone()[0]
    return by_email >= MAX_FAILS_PER_ACCOUNT or by_ip >= MAX_FAILS_PER_IP

def record_failure(email: str, ip: str):
    db().execute(
        "INSERT INTO failed_logins(email, ip, at) VALUES (?,?,?)",
        (email, ip, int(time.time())),
    )
    db().commit()

def clear_failures(email: str):
    db().execute("DELETE FROM failed_logins WHERE email=?", (email,))
    db().commit()

def issue_session(user_id: int):
    token = secrets.token_urlsafe(32)
    db().execute(
        "INSERT INTO sessions(token_hash, user_id, expires_at) VALUES (?,?,?)",
        (hash_token(token), user_id, int(time.time()) + SESSION_TTL),
    )
    db().commit()
    resp = jsonify({"ok": True})
    resp.set_cookie(
        COOKIE_NAME, token,
        max_age=SESSION_TTL,
        httponly=True,       # not readable from JS
        secure=True,         # HTTPS only
        samesite="Lax",      # basic CSRF mitigation
    )
    return resp

def current_user():
    token = request.cookies.get(COOKIE_NAME)
    if not token:
        return None
    row = db().execute(
        """SELECT u.id, u.email FROM sessions s JOIN users u ON u.id = s.user_id
           WHERE s.token_hash=? AND s.expires_at>?""",
        (hash_token(token), int(time.time())),
    ).fetchone()
    return row


# ---- routes ----
@app.post("/signup")
def signup():
    data = request.get_json(silent=True) or {}
    email = normalize_email(str(data.get("email", "")))
    password = data.get("password", "")

    if not EMAIL_RE.match(email) or len(email) > 254:
        return jsonify(error="Invalid email"), 400
    if not valid_password(password):
        return jsonify(error="Password must be 12-128 characters"), 400

    try:
        cur = db().execute(
            "INSERT INTO users(email, password_hash, created_at) VALUES (?,?,?)",
            (email, ph.hash(password), int(time.time())),
        )
        db().commit()
    except sqlite3.IntegrityError:
        # Note: this reveals whether an email is registered. To avoid that,
        # respond identically and send a "you already have an account" email.
        return jsonify(error="Could not create account"), 400

    return issue_session(cur.lastrowid), 201


@app.post("/login")
def login():
    data = request.get_json(silent=True) or {}
    email = normalize_email(str(data.get("email", "")))
    password = data.get("password", "")
    ip = request.remote_addr or "unknown"

    if not isinstance(password, str) or len(password) > 128:
        return jsonify(error="Invalid credentials"), 401

    if is_rate_limited(email, ip):
        resp = jsonify(error="Too many attempts. Try again later.")
        resp.headers["Retry-After"] = str(WINDOW)
        return resp, 429

    user = db().execute(
        "SELECT id, password_hash FROM users WHERE email=?", (email,)
    ).fetchone()

    # Always run a verify so response time doesn't reveal whether the email exists.
    stored = user["password_hash"] if user else DUMMY_HASH
    try:
        ph.verify(stored, password)
        ok = user is not None
    except (VerifyMismatchError, InvalidHashError):
        ok = False

    if not ok:
        record_failure(email, ip)
        return jsonify(error="Invalid credentials"), 401

    clear_failures(email)
    if ph.check_needs_rehash(user["password_hash"]):
        db().execute(
            "UPDATE users SET password_hash=? WHERE id=?",
            (ph.hash(password), user["id"]),
        )
        db().commit()
    return issue_session(user["id"])


@app.post("/logout")
def logout():
    token = request.cookies.get(COOKIE_NAME)
    if token:
        db().execute("DELETE FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=?", (hash_token(token),))
        db().commit()
    resp = jsonify(ok=True)
    resp.delete_cookie(COOKIE_NAME)
    return resp


@app.get("/me")
def me():
    user = current_user()
    if not user:
        return jsonify(error="Unauthenticated"), 401
    return jsonify(id=user["id"], email=user["email"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    init_db()
    app.run()   # use gunicorn behind HTTPS in production
```

**Design notes**

- **Hashing:** Argon2id via `argon2-cffi`, with a per-hash random salt built in. `check_needs_rehash` upgrades hashes transparently if you raise the parameters later.
- **Sessions:** a 256-bit random token goes in an `HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax` cookie. Only its SHA-256 is stored server-side, so a DB leak doesn't hand out live sessions.
- **Rate limiting:** failures are counted over a 15-minute window, per account (5) and per IP (20). The per-account limit stops targeted guessing and the per-IP limit stops credential stuffing across many accounts. Success clears the account's counter.
- **Timing and enumeration:** the dummy hash verify and the identical error message for "no such user" and "wrong password" keep responses uniform.

**What to add before production**

1. Serve over HTTPS only. If you're behind a proxy, configure `ProxyFix` so `request.remote_addr` is the real client IP, not the proxy's.
2. Add CSRF tokens for state-changing routes if you ever relax `SameSite`, or use cross-site requests.
3. Use Redis or similar for rate-limit counters if you run multiple instances. Also purge old `failed_logins` and expired `sessions` rows periodically.
4. Add email verification and password reset, and consider checking passwords against a breached-password list.
5. Note that a per-account lockout lets an attacker deliberately lock out a victim. Add CAPTCHA or exponential backoff if that matters for your threat model.
~~~

### caveman: 3334 answer tokens, 3571 billed, 192 lines

Stack: Node + Express + SQLite + argon2id. One file, easy to port.

```bash
npm i express better-sqlite3 argon2 cookie-parser
```

```js
// server.js
const crypto = require("crypto");
const express = require("express");
const cookieParser = require("cookie-parser");
const argon2 = require("argon2");
const Database = require("better-sqlite3");

const db = new Database("auth.db");
db.exec(`
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  password_hash TEXT NOT NULL,
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sessions (
  token_hash TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  expires_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS failed_logins (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  key TEXT NOT NULL,          -- "email:<addr>" or "ip:<addr>"
  at INTEGER NOT NULL
);
CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS idx_failed_key_at ON failed_logins(key, at);
`);

const SESSION_TTL_MS = 7 * 24 * 3600 * 1000;
const WINDOW_MS = 15 * 60 * 1000;
const MAX_FAILS_PER_EMAIL = 5;
const MAX_FAILS_PER_IP = 20;

const ARGON_OPTS = { type: argon2.argon2id, memoryCost: 19456, timeCost: 2, parallelism: 1 };
// Dummy hash so unknown-email logins cost the same as real ones (timing defense).
const DUMMY_HASH = argon2.hash("dummy-password", ARGON_OPTS);

const sha256 = (s) => crypto.createHash("sha256").update(s).digest("hex");
const normEmail = (e) => String(e || "").trim().toLowerCase();
const validEmail = (e) => e.length <= 254 && /^[^\s@]+@[^\s@]+\.[^\s@]+$/.test(e);
const validPassword = (p) => typeof p === "string" && p.length >= 10 && p.length <= 128;

// ---- rate limiting ----
function failCount(key) {
  return db.prepare("SELECT COUNT(*) c FROM failed_logins WHERE key=? AND at>?")
    .get(key, Date.now() - WINDOW_MS).c;
}
function recordFail(...keys) {
  const ins = db.prepare("INSERT INTO failed_logins(key, at) VALUES (?,?)");
  for (const k of keys) ins.run(k, Date.now());
}
function clearFails(key) {
  db.prepare("DELETE FROM failed_logins WHERE key=?").run(key);
}
function retryAfterSec(key) {
  const oldest = db.prepare("SELECT MIN(at) t FROM failed_logins WHERE key=? AND at>?")
    .get(key, Date.now() - WINDOW_MS).t;
  return Math.max(1, Math.ceil((oldest + WINDOW_MS - Date.now()) / 1000));
}

// ---- sessions ----
function issueSession(res, userId) {
  const token = crypto.randomBytes(32).toString("base64url");
  db.prepare("INSERT INTO sessions(token_hash, user_id, expires_at) VALUES (?,?,?)")
    .run(sha256(token), userId, Date.now() + SESSION_TTL_MS);
  res.cookie("sid", token, {
    httpOnly: true,
    secure: process.env.NODE_ENV === "production",
    sameSite: "lax",
    maxAge: SESSION_TTL_MS,
    path: "/",
  });
}

function requireAuth(req, res, next) {
  const token = req.cookies.sid;
  if (!token) return res.status(401).json({ error: "unauthenticated" });
  const row = db.prepare(
    "SELECT user_id FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=? AND expires_at>?"
  ).get(sha256(token), Date.now());
  if (!row) return res.status(401).json({ error: "unauthenticated" });
  req.userId = row.user_id;
  next();
}

// ---- app ----
const app = express();
app.set("trust proxy", 1); // only if behind exactly one reverse proxy; else remove
app.use(express.json({ limit: "10kb" }));
app.use(cookieParser());

app.post("/signup", async (req, res) => {
  const email = normEmail(req.body.email);
  const { password } = req.body;
  if (!validEmail(email)) return res.status(400).json({ error: "invalid email" });
  if (!validPassword(password))
    return res.status(400).json({ error: "password must be 10-128 chars" });

  const hash = await argon2.hash(password, ARGON_OPTS);
  try {
    const info = db.prepare(
      "INSERT INTO users(email, password_hash, created_at) VALUES (?,?,?)"
    ).run(email, hash, Date.now());
    issueSession(res, info.lastInsertRowid);
    res.status(201).json({ ok: true });
  } catch (e) {
    if (e.code === "SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_UNIQUE")
      return res.status(409).json({ error: "email already registered" });
    throw e;
  }
});

app.post("/login", async (req, res) => {
  const email = normEmail(req.body.email);
  const password = req.body.password;
  const emailKey = `email:${email}`;
  const ipKey = `ip:${req.ip}`;

  // Check lockout BEFORE doing any hashing work.
  if (failCount(emailKey) >= MAX_FAILS_PER_EMAIL || failCount(ipKey) >= MAX_FAILS_PER_IP) {
    const wait = Math.max(
      failCount(emailKey) >= MAX_FAILS_PER_EMAIL ? retryAfterSec(emailKey) : 0,
      failCount(ipKey) >= MAX_FAILS_PER_IP ? retryAfterSec(ipKey) : 0
    );
    res.set("Retry-After", String(wait));
    return res.status(429).json({ error: "too many attempts, try later" });
  }

  const user = typeof password === "string" && password.length <= 128
    ? db.prepare("SELECT id, password_hash FROM users WHERE email=?").get(email)
    : undefined;

  const ok = await argon2.verify(user ? user.password_hash : await DUMMY_HASH, String(password || ""))
    .catch(() => false);

  if (!user || !ok) {
    recordFail(emailKey, ipKey);
    return res.status(401).json({ error: "invalid email or password" });
  }

  clearFails(emailKey); // keep IP counter; it throttles credential stuffing
  issueSession(res, user.id);
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

app.post("/logout", requireAuth, (req, res) => {
  db.prepare("DELETE FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=?").run(sha256(req.cookies.sid));
  res.clearCookie("sid", { path: "/" });
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

app.get("/me", requireAuth, (req, res) => {
  const u = db.prepare("SELECT id, email FROM users WHERE id=?").get(req.userId);
  res.json(u);
});

// Housekeeping
setInterval(() => {
  db.prepare("DELETE FROM sessions WHERE expires_at<?").run(Date.now());
  db.prepare("DELETE FROM failed_logins WHERE at<?").run(Date.now() - WINDOW_MS);
}, 10 * 60 * 1000).unref();

app.listen(3000, () => console.log("listening :3000"));
```

Key decisions:

- **Hashing**: argon2id, salt auto-embedded in hash string. Params follow OWASP minimum (19 MiB, t=2, p=1); raise if latency budget allows.
- **Sessions**: 256-bit random token. DB store only SHA-256 of it, so DB leak doesn't give live sessions. Cookie is `httpOnly`, `secure` in prod, `sameSite=lax`.
- **Rate limit**: sliding 15-min window, two counters.
  - Per email (5 fails): stops targeted guessing. Downside: attacker can lock out a victim. Mitigate with the IP counter, CAPTCHA, or email unlock.
  - Per IP (20 fails): slows credential stuffing across many emails.
  - Lockout check runs before hashing, so locked attempts cost no CPU.
  - Success clears email counter only.
- **Enumeration**: login returns one generic error, and hashes a dummy when the user doesn't exist, so timing is equal. Signup returns 409 for a duplicate email, which leaks existence. Fix: always return "check your email" and send a verification mail.

Missing for production:

- CSRF token on state-changing routes (`sameSite=lax` covers most, not all).
- Email verification and password reset.
- Rotate session on privilege change; revoke all sessions on password change.
- Password breach check (HIBP k-anonymity).
- Shared store (Redis/Postgres) for rate limits if you run multiple instances.
- HTTPS and security headers (`helmet`).
- Rate limit `/signup` too.

```python
# auth.py  (pip install flask)
import hashlib, hmac, os, secrets, sqlite3, time
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify, make_response

DB = os.environ.get("AUTH_DB", "auth.db")
SECURE_COOKIES = os.environ.get("DEV") is None   # Secure cookie needs HTTPS
SESSION_TTL = 7 * 86400
WINDOW, MAX_PER_EMAIL, MAX_PER_IP = 900, 5, 20   # 15 min window

app = Flask(__name__)

def db():
    c = sqlite3.connect(DB)
    c.executescript("""
      CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users(id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, email TEXT UNIQUE, pw TEXT);
      CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sessions(token TEXT PRIMARY KEY, user_id INT, expires REAL);
      CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS fails(key TEXT, ts REAL);
      CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS fails_key ON fails(key, ts);""")
    return c

def hash_pw(pw, salt=None):
    salt = salt or os.urandom(16)
    h = hashlib.scrypt(pw.encode(), salt=salt, n=2**14, r=8, p=1)
    return salt.hex() + ":" + h.hex()

def check_pw(pw, stored):
    salt, h = stored.split(":")
    got = hashlib.scrypt(pw.encode(), salt=bytes.fromhex(salt), n=2**14, r=8, p=1)
    return hmac.compare_digest(got, bytes.fromhex(h))

DUMMY = hash_pw("x")  # burn equal time for unknown emails

def creds():
    d = request.get_json(silent=True) or {}
    email, pw = str(d.get("email", "")).strip().lower(), str(d.get("password", ""))
    return email, pw

def issue_session(c, user_id):
    token = secrets.token_urlsafe(32)
    # store only the hash: a DB leak doesn't leak live sessions
    c.execute("INSERT INTO sessions VALUES(?,?,?)",
              (hashlib.sha256(token.encode()).hexdigest(), user_id, time.time() + SESSION_TTL))
    c.commit()
    r = make_response(jsonify(ok=True))
    r.set_cookie("sid", token, max_age=SESSION_TTL, httponly=True,
                 samesite="Lax", secure=SECURE_COOKIES)
    return r

@app.post("/signup")
def signup():
    email, pw = creds()
    if "@" not in email or len(email) > 254 or not 8 <= len(pw) <= 1024:
        return jsonify(error="invalid email or password (8-1024 chars)"), 400
    c = db()
    try:
        cur = c.execute("INSERT INTO users(email,pw) VALUES(?,?)", (email, hash_pw(pw)))
    except sqlite3.IntegrityError:
        return jsonify(error="email already registered"), 409
    c.commit()
    return issue_session(c, cur.lastrowid)

@app.post("/login")
def login():
    email, pw = creds()
    ip = request.remote_addr or "?"
    keys = (f"e:{email}", f"i:{ip}")
    c, now = db(), time.time()
    c.execute("DELETE FROM fails WHERE ts < ?", (now - WINDOW,))
    n = lambda k: c.execute("SELECT COUNT(*) FROM fails WHERE key=?", (k,)).fetchone()[0]
    if n(keys[0]) >= MAX_PER_EMAIL or n(keys[1]) >= MAX_PER_IP:
        return jsonify(error="too many attempts, try later"), 429
    row = c.execute("SELECT id, pw FROM users WHERE email=?", (email,)).fetchone()
    ok = check_pw(pw[:1024], row[1] if row else DUMMY) and row is not None
    if not ok:
        c.executemany("INSERT INTO fails VALUES(?,?)", [(k, now) for k in keys])
        c.commit()
        return jsonify(error="invalid credentials"), 401   # same message either way
    c.execute("DELETE FROM fails WHERE key=?", (keys[0],))
    return issue_session(c, row[0])

@app.get("/me")
def me():
    tok = request.cookies.get("sid", "")
    row = db().execute(
        "SELECT u.email FROM sessions s JOIN users u ON u.id=s.user_id "
        "WHERE s.token=? AND s.expires>?",
        (hashlib.sha256(tok.encode()).hexdigest(), time.time())).fetchone()
    return (jsonify(email=row[0]), 200) if row else (jsonify(error="unauthenticated"), 401)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    import tempfile
    os.environ["DEV"] = ""; SECURE_COOKIES = False
    DB = os.path.join(tempfile.mkdtemp(), "t.db")
    t = app.test_client()
    j = lambda e, p: {"email": e, "password": p}
    assert t.post("/signup", json=j("A@x.com", "hunter2hunter2")).status_code == 200
    assert t.get("/me").get_json()["email"] == "a@x.com"            # session works
    assert t.post("/signup", json=j("a@x.com", "whatever123")).status_code == 409
    t2 = app.test_client()
    assert t2.get("/me").status_code == 401
    for _ in range(5):
        assert t2.post("/login", json=j("a@x.com", "wrong")).status_code == 401
    assert t2.post("/login", json=j("a@x.com", "hunter2hunter2")).status_code == 429  # locked out
    print("ok")
```

Run `python auth.py` for the self-check; for real use, run under `flask --app auth run` behind HTTPS.

→ Skipped: CSRF tokens (SameSite=Lax covers most cases; add if you accept cross-site POSTs), email verification, password reset, session revocation or logout, and a proper rate-limit store. Add Redis or similar when you run more than one process. The `fails` table also trusts `remote_addr`, so configure proxy headers correctly if you sit behind a proxy.

Assumed stack: Node 20+, Express, better-sqlite3. Hashing uses stdlib `crypto.scrypt`, so no extra dep.

```js
// auth.js — npm i express better-sqlite3
import express from "express";
import Database from "better-sqlite3";
import { scrypt, randomBytes, timingSafeEqual, createHash } from "node:crypto";
import { promisify } from "node:util";

const scryptP = promisify(scrypt);
const db = new Database("auth.db");
db.exec(`
  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users(
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, email TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL, pw TEXT NOT NULL);
  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sessions(
    token_hash TEXT PRIMARY KEY, user_id INTEGER NOT NULL, expires_at INTEGER NOT NULL);
  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS attempts(
    key TEXT PRIMARY KEY, count INTEGER NOT NULL, first_at INTEGER NOT NULL);
`);

const MAX_FAILS = 5, WINDOW_MS = 15 * 60_000, SESSION_MS = 7 * 86_400_000;
const sha = (s) => createHash("sha256").update(s).digest("hex");

async function hashPw(pw) {
  const salt = randomBytes(16);
  const dk = await scryptP(pw, salt, 64);
  return `${salt.toString("hex")}:${dk.toString("hex")}`;
}
async function verifyPw(pw, stored) {
  const [s, h] = stored.split(":");
  const dk = await scryptP(pw, Buffer.from(s, "hex"), 64);
  return timingSafeEqual(dk, Buffer.from(h, "hex"));
}
const DUMMY = await hashPw("dummy"); // equalize timing for unknown emails

function issueSession(res, userId) {
  const token = randomBytes(32).toString("base64url");
  db.prepare("INSERT INTO sessions VALUES(?,?,?)")
    .run(sha(token), userId, Date.now() + SESSION_MS);
  res.cookie("sid", token, {
    httpOnly: true, secure: true, sameSite: "lax", maxAge: SESSION_MS,
  });
}

// rate limit: key = email+ip
function blocked(key) {
  const r = db.prepare("SELECT * FROM attempts WHERE key=?").get(key);
  if (!r) return false;
  if (Date.now() - r.first_at > WINDOW_MS) {
    db.prepare("DELETE FROM attempts WHERE key=?").run(key);
    return false;
  }
  return r.count >= MAX_FAILS;
}
const recordFail = (key) =>
  db.prepare(`INSERT INTO attempts VALUES(?,1,?)
    ON CONFLICT(key) DO UPDATE SET count=count+1`).run(key, Date.now());
const clearFails = (key) => db.prepare("DELETE FROM attempts WHERE key=?").run(key);

const validEmail = (e) => typeof e === "string" && e.length <= 254 && /^[^@\s]+@[^@\s]+\.[^@\s]+$/.test(e);
const validPw = (p) => typeof p === "string" && p.length >= 10 && p.length <= 128;

const app = express();
app.use(express.json({ limit: "10kb" }));

app.post("/signup", async (req, res) => {
  const email = String(req.body.email ?? "").trim().toLowerCase();
  const { password } = req.body;
  if (!validEmail(email) || !validPw(password))
    return res.status(400).json({ error: "Invalid email or password (min 10 chars)" });
  try {
    const { lastInsertRowid } = db
      .prepare("INSERT INTO users(email,pw) VALUES(?,?)")
      .run(email, await hashPw(password));
    issueSession(res, lastInsertRowid);
    res.status(201).json({ ok: true });
  } catch (e) {
    if (e.code === "SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_UNIQUE")
      return res.status(409).json({ error: "Email already registered" });
    throw e;
  }
});

app.post("/login", async (req, res) => {
  const email = String(req.body.email ?? "").trim().toLowerCase();
  const { password } = req.body;
  if (typeof password !== "string" || password.length > 128)
    return res.status(400).json({ error: "Invalid credentials" });

  const key = `${email}|${req.ip}`;
  if (blocked(key))
    return res.status(429).set("Retry-After", String(WINDOW_MS / 1000))
      .json({ error: "Too many attempts, try later" });

  const user = db.prepare("SELECT * FROM users WHERE email=?").get(email);
  const ok = await verifyPw(password, user ? user.pw : DUMMY);
  if (!user || !ok) {
    recordFail(key);
    return res.status(401).json({ error: "Invalid credentials" });
  }
  clearFails(key);
  issueSession(res, user.id);
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

// session middleware for protected routes
export function requireAuth(req, res, next) {
  const m = /(?:^|;\s*)sid=([^;]+)/.exec(req.headers.cookie ?? "");
  const row = m && db.prepare(
    "SELECT user_id FROM sessions WHERE token_hash=? AND expires_at>?").get(sha(m[1]), Date.now());
  if (!row) return res.status(401).json({ error: "Unauthenticated" });
  req.userId = row.user_id;
  next();
}

app.listen(3000);
```

Notes:
- Sessions: stored as SHA-256 of the token → DB leak doesn't yield usable cookies.
- Unknown email runs a dummy scrypt + same 401 → no user enumeration via timing or message. Signup's 409 does leak existence; fix with email verification flow if that matters.
- Behind a proxy, set `app.set("trust proxy", 1)` or `req.ip` is the proxy's.
- Per email+IP key stops brute force from one IP, not a distributed attack on one account. Add a per-email cap if needed.

skipped: CSRF tokens (`sameSite: lax` covers basic cases, add for cross-site POST needs), logout and expired-session cleanup, email verification, password reset. Add when going to production.
~~~

## large-s3 / csvpipeline

> Write a script that reads a large CSV of transactions, validates each row, aggregates totals per customer, and writes a report, handling malformed rows without dying. Show the code.

### vanilla: 2432 answer tokens, 2526 billed, 158 lines

~~~
Here is a streaming Python script (standard library only). It reads one row at a time, so memory scales with the number of customers rather than the file size. Rows that fail validation go to a rejects file with the reason.

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Aggregate transaction totals per customer from a large CSV.

Expected columns: transaction_id, customer_id, amount, currency, timestamp
  - amount:    decimal number (parsed with Decimal to avoid float drift)
  - timestamp: ISO 8601 (e.g. 2026-01-31 or 2026-01-31T12:00:00)

Usage:
  python aggregate.py transactions.csv report.csv --rejects rejects.csv
"""
import argparse
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
from datetime import datetime
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation

REQUIRED = ["transaction_id", "customer_id", "amount", "currency", "timestamp"]
VALID_CURRENCIES = {"USD", "EUR", "GBP", "INR"}


class RowError(ValueError):
    """Raised when a single row fails validation."""


def validate(row: dict) -> tuple[str, str, Decimal]:
    # DictReader puts extra fields under key None and fills missing ones with None/restval
    if None in row:
        raise RowError("too many fields")
    if any(row.get(c) is None for c in REQUIRED):
        raise RowError("too few fields")

    txn_id = row["transaction_id"].strip()
    cust = row["customer_id"].strip()
    if not txn_id:
        raise RowError("empty transaction_id")
    if not cust:
        raise RowError("empty customer_id")

    currency = row["currency"].strip().upper()
    if currency not in VALID_CURRENCIES:
        raise RowError(f"unknown currency {currency!r}")

    try:
        amount = Decimal(row["amount"].strip())
    except InvalidOperation:
        raise RowError(f"bad amount {row['amount']!r}") from None
    if not amount.is_finite():
        raise RowError("non-finite amount")

    try:
        datetime.fromisoformat(row["timestamp"].strip())
    except ValueError:
        raise RowError(f"bad timestamp {row['timestamp']!r}") from None

    return txn_id, cust, amount, currency


def process(in_path, rejects_path):
    # totals[customer][currency] = [sum, count]
    totals = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(lambda: [Decimal(0), 0]))
    seen_ids = set()  # for duplicate detection; drop if the id set is too large for memory
    stats = {"read": 0, "ok": 0, "rejected": 0, "duplicates": 0}

    # newline="" is required by the csv module; errors="replace" survives bad bytes
    with open(in_path, newline="", encoding="utf-8", errors="replace") as fin, \
         open(rejects_path, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as frej:

        reader = csv.DictReader(fin)
        missing = [c for c in REQUIRED if c not in (reader.fieldnames or [])]
        if missing:
            sys.exit(f"Header missing required columns: {missing}")

        rej = csv.writer(frej)
        rej.writerow(["line", "reason", "raw"])

        line_no = 1  # header
        while True:
            try:
                row = next(reader)
            except StopIteration:
                break
            except csv.Error as e:  # e.g. NUL byte, field larger than limit
                line_no = reader.line_num
                stats["read"] += 1
                stats["rejected"] += 1
                rej.writerow([line_no, f"csv parse error: {e}", ""])
                continue

            line_no = reader.line_num
            stats["read"] += 1
            try:
                txn_id, cust, amount, currency = validate(row)
                if txn_id in seen_ids:
                    stats["duplicates"] += 1
                    raise RowError(f"duplicate transaction_id {txn_id!r}")
                seen_ids.add(txn_id)
            except RowError as e:
                stats["rejected"] += 1
                rej.writerow([line_no, str(e), "|".join(str(v) for v in row.values())])
                continue

            bucket = totals[cust][currency]
            bucket[0] += amount
            bucket[1] += 1
            stats["ok"] += 1

    return totals, stats


def write_report(totals, out_path):
    with open(out_path, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        w = csv.writer(f)
        w.writerow(["customer_id", "currency", "transaction_count", "total_amount"])
        for cust in sorted(totals):
            for cur in sorted(totals[cust]):
                total, count = totals[cust][cur]
                w.writerow([cust, cur, count, f"{total:.2f}"])


def main():
    p = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=__doc__,
                                formatter_class=argparse.RawDescriptionHelpFormatter)
    p.add_argument("input")
    p.add_argument("report")
    p.add_argument("--rejects", default="rejects.csv")
    args = p.parse_args()

    csv.field_size_limit(10_000_000)  # guard against runaway quoted fields
    totals, s = process(args.input, args.rejects)
    write_report(totals, args.report)

    print(f"Read {s['read']:,} rows: {s['ok']:,} ok, {s['rejected']:,} rejected "
          f"({s['duplicates']:,} duplicates). Customers: {len(totals):,}.")
    print(f"Report: {args.report}  Rejects: {args.rejects}")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

**How it handles failure**

- **Per-row validation errors** (missing or extra fields, bad amount, bad date, unknown currency, empty IDs, duplicates) raise `RowError`. The row is logged to the rejects file with its line number and reason, and processing continues.
- **CSV-level errors** (NUL bytes, oversized fields) are caught around `next(reader)`, so one corrupt record doesn't abort the run.
- **Bad encoding** is replaced rather than crashing (`errors="replace"`).
- **A bad header** is the one case that exits, because nothing downstream could be trusted.

**Design choices to adjust**

- `Decimal` is used instead of `float` so money sums are exact. Totals are kept per currency, because summing across currencies would be meaningless.
- The `seen_ids` set is the only structure that grows with row count. With hundreds of millions of rows, drop it, or dedupe upstream (e.g. `sort -u` or a database).
- For a faster run on very large files, `pandas.read_csv(chunksize=...)` with `on_bad_lines` is an option. It gives less precise per-row rejection reasons, though.
- Adapt `REQUIRED`, `VALID_CURRENCIES`, and the rules in `validate()` to your actual schema (e.g. reject negative amounts, or treat them as refunds).
~~~

### caveman: 1718 answer tokens, 1833 billed, 118 lines

~~~
Python, streaming, constant memory. Assumed columns: `transaction_id,customer_id,date,amount`. Adjust `REQUIRED`.

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Aggregate transactions per customer. Bad rows go to a reject file, not a crash.

Usage: python report.py transactions.csv report.csv [rejects.csv]
"""
import csv
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
from datetime import datetime
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation

REQUIRED = ["transaction_id", "customer_id", "date", "amount"]


class RowError(ValueError):
    pass


def validate(row):
    """Return (customer_id, amount) or raise RowError."""
    if None in row:  # DictReader puts extra fields under key None
        raise RowError("too many fields")
    if any(row.get(c) is None for c in REQUIRED):  # missing fields -> None
        raise RowError("too few fields")

    txn_id = row["transaction_id"].strip()
    cust = row["customer_id"].strip()
    if not txn_id:
        raise RowError("empty transaction_id")
    if not cust:
        raise RowError("empty customer_id")

    try:
        datetime.strptime(row["date"].strip(), "%Y-%m-%d")
    except ValueError:
        raise RowError(f"bad date: {row['date']!r}")

    try:
        amount = Decimal(row["amount"].strip())
    except InvalidOperation:
        raise RowError(f"bad amount: {row['amount']!r}")
    if not amount.is_finite():  # rejects NaN / Infinity
        raise RowError(f"non-finite amount: {row['amount']!r}")

    return txn_id, cust, amount


def main(src, out, rejects_path):
    totals = defaultdict(lambda: [Decimal("0"), 0])  # cust -> [sum, count]
    seen = set()  # dedupe txn ids; drop this if memory matters at huge scale
    good = bad = 0

    # newline="" required by csv module; errors="replace" survives bad bytes
    with open(src, newline="", encoding="utf-8", errors="replace") as fin, \
         open(rejects_path, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as frej:

        reader = csv.DictReader(fin)
        missing = [c for c in REQUIRED if c not in (reader.fieldnames or [])]
        if missing:
            sys.exit(f"missing required columns: {missing}")

        rej = csv.writer(frej)
        rej.writerow(["line", "reason", "raw"])

        while True:
            try:
                row = next(reader)
            except StopIteration:
                break
            except csv.Error as e:  # malformed quoting, NUL bytes, etc.
                bad += 1
                rej.writerow([reader.line_num, f"csv error: {e}", ""])
                continue

            try:
                txn_id, cust, amount = validate(row)
                if txn_id in seen:
                    raise RowError(f"duplicate transaction_id: {txn_id}")
            except RowError as e:
                bad += 1
                rej.writerow([reader.line_num, str(e), repr(row)])
                continue

            seen.add(txn_id)
            totals[cust][0] += amount
            totals[cust][1] += 1
            good += 1

    with open(out, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as fout:
        w = csv.writer(fout)
        w.writerow(["customer_id", "transaction_count", "total_amount"])
        for cust in sorted(totals):
            total, n = totals[cust]
            w.writerow([cust, n, f"{total:.2f}"])

    print(f"valid rows:    {good}")
    print(f"rejected rows: {bad} -> {rejects_path}")
    print(f"customers:     {len(totals)}")
    print(f"report:        {out}")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) < 3:
        sys.exit(__doc__)
    main(sys.argv[1], sys.argv[2],
         sys.argv[3] if len(sys.argv) > 3 else "rejects.csv")
```

Design notes:
- **Streaming**: `DictReader` reads row by row. File size no matter. Only per-customer totals (and `seen` set) stay in RAM.
- **`Decimal` not `float`**: money sums exact.
- **Bad rows**: field count mismatch, bad date/amount, NaN/Inf, empty IDs, duplicates → rejects file with line number + reason. Parser-level `csv.Error` caught per row, loop continues.
- **Exit code**: only dies on missing header columns. Could add nonzero exit if reject rate passes a threshold.
- **Memory caveat**: `seen` grows with unique txn IDs. Billions of rows → drop dedupe or use sqlite/Bloom filter.
- **Possible extensions**: currency column (key totals by `(cust, currency)`), negative-amount policy (refunds), date range checks.
