```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Usage: report.py transactions.csv report.csv rejects.csv
Expects columns: customer_id, amount, [date as YYYY-MM-DD]. Streams the file, so memory is O(customers)."""
import csv, sys
from collections import defaultdict
from datetime import date
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation


def parse(row):
    if None in row:
        raise ValueError("too many fields")
    cust = (row.get("customer_id") or "").strip()
    if not cust:
        raise ValueError("missing customer_id")
    try:
        amt = Decimal((row.get("amount") or "").strip())
    except InvalidOperation:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount {row.get('amount')!r}")
    if not amt.is_finite():
        raise ValueError(f"non-finite amount {amt}")
    if (d := (row.get("date") or "").strip()):
        try:
            date.fromisoformat(d)
        except ValueError:
            raise ValueError(f"bad date {d!r}")
    return cust, amt


def run(src, report, rejects):
    totals = defaultdict(lambda: [0, Decimal(0)])
    good = bad = 0
    with open(src, newline="", encoding="utf-8", errors="replace") as f, \
         open(rejects, "w", newline="") as rj:
        reader = csv.DictReader(f)
        rw = csv.writer(rj)
        rw.writerow(["line", "reason"])
        it = iter(reader)
        while True:
            try:
                row = next(it)
            except StopIteration:
                break
            except csv.Error as e:  # e.g. NUL byte, field too large
                bad += 1
                rw.writerow([reader.line_num, f"csv error: {e}"])
                continue
            try:
                cust, amt = parse(row)
            except ValueError as e:
                bad += 1
                rw.writerow([reader.line_num, e])
                continue
            good += 1
            totals[cust][0] += 1
            totals[cust][1] += amt
    with open(report, "w", newline="") as out:
        w = csv.writer(out)
        w.writerow(["customer_id", "transactions", "total"])
        for cust in sorted(totals):
            n, t = totals[cust]
            w.writerow([cust, n, t])
    return good, bad


def demo():
    import os, tempfile
    d = tempfile.mkdtemp()
    src = os.path.join(d, "in.csv")
    with open(src, "w") as f:
        f.write("customer_id,amount,date\n"
                "a,10.10,2024-01-01\n"
                "a,0.20,\n"
                "b,abc,2024-01-01\n"       # bad amount
                ",5,2024-01-01\n"          # missing customer
                "c,5,2024-13-45\n"         # bad date
                "b,1,2024-01-01,extra\n"   # too many fields
                "b,NaN,2024-01-01\n"       # non-finite
                "b,7,2024-01-02\n")
    good, bad = run(src, os.path.join(d, "r.csv"), os.path.join(d, "x.csv"))
    assert (good, bad) == (3, 5), (good, bad)
    rows = list(csv.reader(open(os.path.join(d, "r.csv"))))
    assert rows[1:] == [["a", "2", "10.30"], ["b", "1", "7"]], rows


if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) == 1:
        demo(); print("ok")
    else:
        g, b = run(*sys.argv[1:4])
        print(f"{g} good rows, {b} rejected (see {sys.argv[3]})")
```

Skipped: duplicate-transaction detection, multi-currency, and parallelism. Add duplicates when the source can resend rows (a `set` of IDs costs memory, so use a DB or sort for billions of rows). Add currency grouping when amounts aren't all in one currency.

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""usage: report.py transactions.csv report.csv [rejects.csv]
Expected columns: txn_id, customer_id, amount, date (YYYY-MM-DD)"""
import csv, sys
from collections import defaultdict
from datetime import date
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation

REQUIRED = ("txn_id", "customer_id", "amount", "date")

def validate(row):
    if None in row or None in row.values():  # extra / missing fields
        raise ValueError("wrong column count")
    cust = row["customer_id"].strip()
    if not cust or not row["txn_id"].strip():
        raise ValueError("empty id")
    try:
        amt = Decimal(row["amount"].strip())
    except InvalidOperation:
        raise ValueError(f"bad amount {row['amount']!r}")
    if not amt.is_finite():
        raise ValueError("non-finite amount")
    try:
        date.fromisoformat(row["date"].strip())
    except ValueError:
        raise ValueError(f"bad date {row['date']!r}")
    return cust, amt

def main(src, out, rej_path="rejects.csv"):
    totals = defaultdict(lambda: [Decimal(0), 0])
    seen, ok, bad = set(), 0, 0
    with open(src, newline="", encoding="utf-8-sig", errors="replace") as f, \
         open(rej_path, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as rf:
        rej = csv.writer(rf)
        rej.writerow(["line", "reason", "raw"])
        rd = csv.DictReader(f)
        missing = set(REQUIRED) - set(rd.fieldnames or ())
        if missing:
            sys.exit(f"missing columns: {sorted(missing)}")
        while True:
            try:
                row = next(rd)
            except StopIteration:
                break
            except csv.Error as e:  # unterminated quote, NUL byte, etc.
                bad += 1
                rej.writerow([rd.line_num, f"csv error: {e}", ""])
                continue
            try:
                cust, amt = validate(row)
                tid = row["txn_id"].strip()
                if tid in seen:
                    raise ValueError("duplicate txn_id")
            except ValueError as e:
                bad += 1
                rej.writerow([rd.line_num, str(e), "|".join(map(str, row.values()))])
                continue
            seen.add(tid)
            t = totals[cust]
            t[0] += amt
            t[1] += 1
            ok += 1

    with open(out, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as of:
        w = csv.writer(of)
        w.writerow(["customer_id", "txn_count", "total"])
        for c in sorted(totals):
            w.writerow([c, totals[c][1], totals[c][0]])
    print(f"ok={ok} rejected={bad} customers={len(totals)}", file=sys.stderr)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) < 3:
        sys.exit(__doc__)
    main(*sys.argv[1:4])
```

Notes:
- Errors during parsing (`csv.Error`) are caught around `next()`, so a broken quote doesn't kill the run.
- `seen` set grows with unique txn_ids → ~100 bytes/id; for hundreds of millions of rows, drop dedupe or use sqlite.
- Negative amounts (refunds) are accepted; add `if amt <= 0` in `validate` if unwanted.
- skipped: parallelism, currency handling. Add when CSV exceeds what one core handles in acceptable time or multi-currency appears.
~~~

## large-s3 / migration

> Write a database migration script that moves a users table from a single full_name column to first_name/last_name, backfills existing rows, and is safely re-runnable. Show the code.

### vanilla: 1747 answer tokens, 2929 billed, 99 lines

~~~
Here's a PostgreSQL migration using the expand → backfill → contract pattern. I assumed Postgres and a table named `users`; adjust the syntax for other databases.

## `001_split_full_name.up.sql` (expand + backfill)

```sql
BEGIN;

-- 1. Expand: add new columns (no-op if they already exist)
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS first_name text;
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS last_name  text;

-- 2. Backfill: only runs if full_name still exists, and only touches
--    rows that haven't been migrated yet, so re-runs are harmless.
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF EXISTS (
    SELECT 1
    FROM information_schema.columns
    WHERE table_schema = current_schema()
      AND table_name   = 'users'
      AND column_name  = 'full_name'
  ) THEN
    UPDATE users
    SET first_name = split_part(regexp_replace(btrim(full_name), '\s+', ' ', 'g'), ' ', 1),
        last_name  = CASE
          WHEN strpos(regexp_replace(btrim(full_name), '\s+', ' ', 'g'), ' ') = 0 THEN ''
          ELSE substr(
                 regexp_replace(btrim(full_name), '\s+', ' ', 'g'),
                 strpos(regexp_replace(btrim(full_name), '\s+', ' ', 'g'), ' ') + 1
               )
        END
    WHERE first_name IS NULL
      AND last_name  IS NULL
      AND full_name IS NOT NULL
      AND btrim(full_name) <> '';
  END IF;
END
$$;

COMMIT;
```

## `002_drop_full_name.up.sql` (contract, run later)

Run this only after the application is deployed and reading/writing `first_name`/`last_name`.

```sql
BEGIN;

-- Safety check: refuse to drop if any row has a name that wasn't backfilled
DO $$
DECLARE missing bigint;
BEGIN
  IF EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns
    WHERE table_schema = current_schema()
      AND table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'full_name'
  ) THEN
    EXECUTE $q$
      SELECT count(*) FROM users
      WHERE btrim(coalesce(full_name, '')) <> '' AND first_name IS NULL
    $q$ INTO missing;

    IF missing > 0 THEN
      RAISE EXCEPTION '% rows not backfilled; aborting drop', missing;
    END IF;
  END IF;
END
$$;

ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN IF EXISTS full_name;

COMMIT;
```

## `001_split_full_name.down.sql` (rollback, only valid before step 002)

```sql
BEGIN;
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS full_name text;
UPDATE users
SET full_name = btrim(concat_ws(' ', first_name, nullif(last_name, '')))
WHERE full_name IS NULL AND first_name IS NOT NULL;
-- Only drop the new columns if you're sure nothing has written to them since.
-- ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN IF EXISTS first_name, DROP COLUMN IF EXISTS last_name;
COMMIT;
```

## Why it's re-runnable
- `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` makes the expand step idempotent.
- The backfill is wrapped in an `information_schema` check, so it does nothing after `full_name` is dropped.
- The `WHERE first_name IS NULL AND last_name IS NULL` filter means already-migrated rows (and any edited since) are never overwritten.
- Everything runs in a transaction, so a failure leaves no partial state.

## Decisions to review
- **Splitting rule:** the first token becomes `first_name` and the rest becomes `last_name` ("Mary Jane Watson" → "Mary" / "Jane Watson"). Mononyms get `last_name = ''`. Names don't split cleanly in general, so if you need "last token is the surname", change the expression accordingly.
- **Large tables:** the single `UPDATE` holds locks and generates a lot of WAL for the whole run. For millions of rows, backfill in batches outside one transaction, e.g. loop on `WHERE id IN (SELECT id FROM users WHERE first_name IS NULL AND full_name IS NOT NULL AND btrim(full_name) <> '' LIMIT 10000)`, committing between batches.
- **Dual-write period:** between steps 001 and 002, new rows written by old app code will have `full_name` only. Either run the backfill again right before the drop (it's safe to) or add a temporary trigger that syncs the columns.
- **NOT NULL:** I left the new columns nullable. Add `SET NOT NULL` after the backfill is verified if your schema requires it.
~~~

### caveman: 1106 answer tokens, 1748 billed, 67 lines

~~~
Assumes PostgreSQL. Expand/contract: add columns, backfill, drop `full_name` later in a separate step.

```sql
-- 001_split_full_name.sql
BEGIN;

-- 1. Add new columns (no-op if present)
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS first_name text;
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS last_name  text;

-- 2. Backfill. Skip entirely if full_name already dropped.
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns
    WHERE table_schema = current_schema()
      AND table_name   = 'users'
      AND column_name  = 'full_name'
  ) THEN
    EXECUTE $q$
      UPDATE users u
      SET first_name = split_part(n.norm, ' ', 1),
          last_name  = NULLIF(regexp_replace(n.norm, '^\S+\s*', ''), '')
      FROM (
        SELECT id,
               btrim(regexp_replace(full_name, '\s+', ' ', 'g')) AS norm
        FROM users
        WHERE full_name IS NOT NULL
          AND first_name IS NULL
          AND last_name  IS NULL
      ) n
      WHERE u.id = n.id
        AND n.norm <> ''
    $q$;
  END IF;
END $$;

COMMIT;
```

Contract step, run only after app deploys using new columns and you verify data:

```sql
-- 002_drop_full_name.sql
ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN IF EXISTS full_name;
```

Why re-runnable:
- `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` → no error on second run.
- Backfill only touches rows where `first_name` and `last_name` both `NULL` → never overwrite data app wrote after first run.
- Column-exists check → script still passes after `full_name` dropped.
- One transaction → partial failure rolls back clean.

Behavior notes:
- `"Ada Lovelace"` → `Ada` / `Lovelace`.
- `"Mary Jane Watson"` → `Mary` / `Jane Watson` (first token only is first name).
- `"Cher"` → `Cher` / `NULL`.
- `NULL` or blank `full_name` → left `NULL`.
- Whitespace trimmed and collapsed.

Caveats:
- Naive split is wrong for some names (`"van der Berg"`, mononyms, name order in some cultures). Review sample before dropping `full_name`.
- Big table: single `UPDATE` holds long lock and bloats. Batch instead, e.g. loop `... WHERE id IN (SELECT id ... LIMIT 10000)` with commit between batches (outside the single transaction, so move `BEGIN/COMMIT` out).
- Using `id` as primary key; adjust if different.
- Want `NOT NULL` on `first_name`? Add after backfill, with `CHECK ... NOT VALID` then `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` to avoid long lock.

Need MySQL, SQLite, or Alembic/Django/Rails version? Say which.

```sql
BEGIN;

ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS first_name text;
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS last_name  text;

-- Backfill only if full_name still exists; only rows not yet migrated.
DO $$
BEGIN
  IF EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM information_schema.columns
    WHERE table_schema = current_schema()
      AND table_name = 'users' AND column_name = 'full_name'
  ) THEN
    UPDATE users
    SET first_name = split_part(btrim(full_name), ' ', 1),
        last_name  = NULLIF(btrim(substr(btrim(full_name),
                       length(split_part(btrim(full_name), ' ', 1)) + 1)), '')
    WHERE full_name IS NOT NULL
      AND first_name IS NULL
      AND last_name  IS NULL;
  END IF;
END $$;

COMMIT;

-- Later, separate migration, after app code stops reading/writing full_name:
-- ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN IF EXISTS full_name;
```

Behavior:
- `"Ada Lovelace"` → `Ada` / `Lovelace`
- `"Mary Jane Watson"` → `Mary` / `Jane Watson` (first token = first name, rest = last)
- `"Cher"` → `Cher` / `NULL`
- Blank or NULL `full_name` is skipped (blank gives an empty `first_name`; filter with `btrim(full_name) <> ''` if you want NULL instead).

Re-run safety: `IF NOT EXISTS` on the columns, the `first_name IS NULL AND last_name IS NULL` guard means already-migrated rows are never overwritten, and the `information_schema` check means it still works after `full_name` is dropped.

Caveats:
- Space-splitting is lossy for multi-word first names ("Mary Jane Smith") and for names where the family name comes first. Review the output.
- Large table: the single `UPDATE` holds row locks for its whole duration. Batch it (e.g. `WHERE id IN (SELECT id ... LIMIT 10000)` in a loop) and run it outside one big transaction.
- Dual-write from the app during the transition, or new rows created mid-deploy will lack the new columns. A re-run picks them up.

## large-s3 / postmortem

> Write an incident postmortem for a four-hour outage caused by a connection pool exhausted by a slow downstream dependency, including timeline, root cause and remediations.

### vanilla: 2869 answer tokens, 2869 billed, 109 lines

~~~
# Incident Postmortem: Checkout API Outage Caused by Connection Pool Exhaustion

**Status:** Resolved
**Severity:** SEV-1
**Duration:** 4 hours 5 minutes (illustrative figures; replace with your actuals)
**Authors:** [Name], [Name]
**Date of incident:** [YYYY-MM-DD]
**Blameless review:** Yes. This document focuses on systems and process, not individuals.

---

## 1. Summary

On [date], the Orders API became unavailable for roughly four hours. A downstream dependency, the Inventory Service, began responding slowly after a database index was dropped during a routine migration. Orders API threads held database and HTTP connections open while waiting on Inventory, and the connection pool filled. Once it was exhausted, every request, including those that never touched Inventory, queued and then timed out. Retries from clients and an upstream gateway amplified load and delayed recovery.

## 2. Impact

- **User-facing:** ~92% of checkout requests failed or timed out for 4h 5m. Roughly [N] orders were failed or abandoned.
- **Business:** Estimated revenue loss of [$X]; [N] support tickets.
- **Internal:** Dependent services (Notifications, Reporting) saw elevated errors due to cascading timeouts.
- **Data integrity:** No data loss. [N] orders were left in a "pending" state and were reconciled manually.

## 3. Timeline (all times UTC)

| Time | Event |
|---|---|
| 13:52 | Inventory DB migration deployed; an index on `sku_id` is inadvertently dropped. |
| 14:03 | Inventory p99 latency rises from 80 ms to 6 s. No alert fires (alert threshold is on error rate, not latency). |
| 14:10 | Orders API pool utilization climbs from 30% to 100%. Threads block waiting for Inventory responses. |
| 14:14 | Orders API begins returning 503s; error rate crosses 50%. |
| 14:18 | Pager fires on elevated 5xx rate. On-call acknowledges. |
| 14:30 | On-call suspects Orders API itself, restarts pods. Pool refills within minutes and exhausts again. Incident declared SEV-1. |
| 14:55 | Gateway and client retries push request volume to ~3x normal. Orders API CPU is idle, which rules out compute saturation. |
| 15:25 | Thread dumps show most threads blocked on Inventory calls. Focus shifts to the dependency. |
| 15:50 | Inventory team engaged. Slow query log identifies full table scans on `sku_id`. |
| 16:20 | Team debates rolling back the migration vs. recreating the index; chooses to recreate the index online. |
| 16:55 | Index build in progress; Orders API traffic is shed at the gateway to reduce load on Inventory. |
| 17:40 | Index build completes. Inventory p99 returns to 90 ms. |
| 17:55 | Orders API pools drain; gateway rate limits gradually lifted. |
| 18:05 | Error rate returns to baseline. Incident resolved. |
| 18:30 | Reconciliation of pending orders begins. |

## 4. Root Cause

**Direct cause:** The Orders API called Inventory synchronously on the request path with no effective timeout (the default was 30 s) and no isolation between the connection pool used for Inventory-bound work and the pool used by everything else. When Inventory slowed, in-flight requests accumulated faster than they completed (Little's Law: concurrency = arrival rate × latency). With latency up ~75x, the pool of 100 connections saturated within minutes.

**Triggering event:** A migration on the Inventory database dropped an index, causing full table scans under production load.

**Contributing factors:**
1. **No timeout or circuit breaker** on the Inventory client. Slow responses were held rather than failed fast.
2. **Shared pool.** Endpoints that didn't need Inventory (health checks, order lookup) failed too, which turned a degraded dependency into a total outage.
3. **Retry amplification.** The gateway and mobile clients retried immediately, without backoff or jitter, tripling load.
4. **Monitoring gaps.** Alerts keyed on errors, not latency or pool saturation. There was no dashboard showing pool utilization.
5. **Migration review.** The migration was not tested against production-scale data, and the index drop was not flagged in review.
6. **Misleading first hypothesis.** Restarting pods gave temporary relief, which reinforced the wrong theory and cost about 70 minutes before attention turned to the dependency.

## 5. Detection

The incident was detected by a 5xx-rate alert at 14:18, 15 minutes after the first latency degradation. No alert covered Inventory latency or connection pool saturation, which would have fired at ~14:05 to 14:10.

## 6. Response: What Went Well and What Didn't

**Went well**
- Rapid pager acknowledgement and clear incident-channel communication.
- Gateway load shedding effectively protected Inventory during the index rebuild.
- Reconciliation of pending orders was completed with no data loss.

**Went poorly**
- Almost 90 minutes passed before the dependency was identified as the source.
- No runbook existed for "pool exhausted, CPU idle."
- Inventory owners weren't paged automatically; engagement took 90 minutes.
- Rollback vs. forward-fix decision took 30 minutes with no clear owner.

**Where we got lucky**
- The table was small enough that an online index build finished in under an hour.

## 7. Remediation / Action Items

| # | Action | Type | Owner | Priority | Due |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Set explicit timeouts (connect 200 ms, read 1 s) on all Inventory calls | Prevent | Orders team | P0 | [date] |
| 2 | Add circuit breaker with fallback (e.g., cached stock levels) for Inventory | Mitigate | Orders team | P0 | [date] |
| 3 | Isolate connection pools per dependency (bulkheads); keep health checks and non-Inventory endpoints on separate resources | Mitigate | Orders team | P0 | [date] |
| 4 | Add exponential backoff with jitter and retry budgets to gateway and mobile clients | Mitigate | Platform | P1 | [date] |
| 5 | Alert on dependency p99 latency and pool utilization >80%; build a pool saturation dashboard | Detect | SRE | P0 | [date] |
| 6 | Add migration checks: run against production-scale data, and flag dropped indexes in CI | Prevent | Inventory team | P1 | [date] |
| 7 | Write a runbook for "high latency, idle CPU, pool exhausted" including thread-dump steps | Respond | SRE | P1 | [date] |
| 8 | Add service-ownership paging so dependency owners are auto-engaged for SEV-1s | Respond | SRE | P2 | [date] |
| 9 | Run a game day injecting dependency latency to validate timeouts, breakers and alerts | Verify | SRE + Orders | P2 | [date] |
| 10 | Define rollback-vs-forward-fix guidance for migrations, with a named decision owner | Respond | Eng management | P2 | [date] |

## 8. Lessons Learned

- **Slow is worse than down.** A dependency that fails fast is easier to survive than one that hangs, because hanging consumes resources while failing releases them.
- **Bound everything.** Every outbound call needs a timeout, and every pool needs isolation so one dependency can't take down unrelated functionality.
- **Retries are load.** Uncoordinated retries turn partial degradation into total failure.
- **Alert on saturation and latency, not just errors.** Errors are a lagging indicator.
- **Idle CPU plus failing requests** suggests blocked threads on a dependency, not a compute problem.

## 9. Appendix

- Graphs: Inventory p99 latency, Orders pool utilization, gateway request rate [attach]
- Thread dump excerpt [attach]
- Slow query log and migration diff [attach]
- Incident channel transcript [link]

---

If you share the real details (services, timings, numbers, tooling), I can tailor this to your incident, or produce a shorter executive-summary version.

# Incident Postmortem: Checkout API Outage from Connection Pool Exhaustion

**Status:** Draft | **Severity:** SEV-1 | **Duration:** 4h 07m | **Authors:** [names] | **Date:** [YYYY-MM-DD]

*Template with illustrative details. Replace bracketed values with real data.*

## Summary

Between 09:12 and 13:19 UTC, `orders-api` returned errors or timed out for ~85% of requests. A downstream dependency, `inventory-service`, became slow (p99 from 120 ms to 30 s). `orders-api` held DB and HTTP connections while waiting on it. Pools drained, threads blocked, and healthy endpoints that didn't need inventory also failed. Restarts and scaling gave only brief relief.

## Impact

- ~4h of degraded checkout; ~[N] failed orders, ~[N] affected users
- Est. revenue impact: [$X]
- [N] support tickets; SLA breach for [customer tier]
- No data loss. No duplicate charges confirmed (payments are idempotent).

## Timeline (UTC)

| Time | Event |
|------|-------|
| 08:40 | `inventory-service` deploy v2.31 rolls out. Adds un-indexed query on stock-reservation path. |
| 09:05 | `inventory-service` p99 latency starts climbing. No alert (threshold only on error rate). |
| 09:12 | `orders-api` pool utilization hits 100%. Request queue grows. Error rate >50%. |
| 09:18 | Page fires on `orders-api` 5xx. On-call acks. |
| 09:30 | Suspected `orders-api` bug or DB issue. DB dashboards look healthy, so DB ruled out wrongly as "not the cause." |
| 09:45 | `orders-api` restarted. Recovery ~3 min, then pool exhausted again. |
| 10:20 | Scaled `orders-api` 6 → 18 pods. Brief improvement. Then worse: more pods amplify load on slow `inventory-service`. |
| 10:50 | Thread dumps show most threads blocked in `InventoryClient.reserve()`. Dependency identified. |
| 11:10 | `inventory-service` team engaged. Latency traced to slow query. |
| 11:40 | Attempted `inventory-service` rollback. Blocked by schema migration already applied. |
| 12:25 | Index added manually in production; `inventory-service` latency begins recovering. |
| 12:50 | `orders-api` still saturated due to retry storm and queued requests. Traffic shed at gateway to 25%. |
| 13:19 | Pools drain, error rate <1%. Traffic restored gradually. Incident resolved. |
| 14:00 | Monitoring period ends. All-clear. |

## Root Cause

Direct trigger: `inventory-service` v2.31 introduced a slow query, raising latency from ~120 ms to ~30 s.

Root cause: `orders-api` had no effective isolation from dependency slowness:

1. **Client timeout of 60 s** (default), far above the normal 120 ms. Slow calls held connections for a minute.
2. **Shared connection/thread pool** across all endpoints. Inventory slowness starved unrelated endpoints (health, order lookup).
3. **No circuit breaker or bulkhead.** Failing calls kept being attempted at full rate.
4. **Aggressive retries** (3x, no backoff or jitter) tripled load on the already-slow dependency.
5. **Autoscaling on CPU.** Blocked threads use little CPU, so scaling was neither automatic nor useful; manual scale-up increased load on the dependency.

Little Law view: concurrency = arrival rate × latency. At ~400 req/s and 30 s latency, ~12,000 concurrent calls needed vs. a pool of 200. Exhaustion was certain within seconds.

## Contributing Factors

- Alerts on error rate only, none on dependency latency or pool saturation (detection delay ~13 min, diagnosis ~100 min).
- Dashboards lacked per-dependency latency and pool-wait metrics.
- Rollback of `inventory-service` blocked by non-reversible migration.
- Index issue not caught in staging (data volume ~1% of prod).
- No runbook for "dependency slow" scenario.

## What Went Well

- Page fired within 6 min of customer-visible errors.
- Gateway load shedding worked and enabled final recovery.
- Cross-team escalation was fast once dependency was identified.

## What Went Poorly

- ~100 min to identify the dependency; early focus on DB and `orders-api` code.
- Scale-up and restart made or kept things worse.
- Migration prevented clean rollback.

## Remediation / Action Items

| # | Action | Type | Owner | Priority | Due |
|---|--------|------|-------|----------|-----|
| 1 | Set inventory client timeout to 1s (connect 200ms), based on p99.9 × margin | Prevent | [orders] | P0 | [date] |
| 2 | Add circuit breaker around `InventoryClient` with fallback (soft-reserve / queue for later) | Prevent | [orders] | P0 | [date] |
| 3 | Bulkhead: separate pool per dependency; reserve capacity for health and read endpoints | Prevent | [orders] | P0 | [date] |
| 4 | Retries: max 1, exponential backoff with jitter, retry budget (≤10% of traffic) | Prevent | [orders] | P1 | [date] |
| 5 | Alert on dependency p99 latency, pool wait time, and pool utilization >80% | Detect | [SRE] | P0 | [date] |
| 6 | Dashboard: per-dependency latency, in-flight calls, pool saturation | Detect | [SRE] | P1 | [date] |
| 7 | Autoscale on in-flight requests / queue depth, with max cap to avoid amplifying downstream load | Mitigate | [SRE] | P1 | [date] |
| 8 | Pre-built gateway load-shedding switch + runbook for "slow dependency" | Mitigate | [SRE] | P1 | [date] |
| 9 | Require backward-compatible, reversible migrations (expand/contract) in `inventory-service` | Prevent | [inventory] | P1 | [date] |
| 10 | Add query-plan check and prod-scale dataset to staging perf tests; canary deploys with latency-based auto-rollback | Prevent | [inventory] | P1 | [date] |
| 11 | Add `inventory-service` server-side timeouts and concurrency limits | Prevent | [inventory] | P2 | [date] |
| 12 | Quarterly game day: inject latency into each critical dependency | Verify | [SRE] | P2 | [date] |

## Lessons Learned

- A slow dependency is more dangerous than a dead one: failures are fast, slowness holds resources.
- Timeouts must be set from observed latency, not left at defaults.
- Scaling out a blocked service can worsen a downstream overload.
- "Healthy DB" does not mean healthy request path. Check thread dumps and dependency latency early.

## Appendix

- Dashboards/graphs: [links]
- Thread dump sample: [link]
- Slack incident channel log: [link]
- Related incidents: [IDs]

# Postmortem: Checkout API Outage Caused by Connection Pool Exhaustion

**Status:** Resolved | **Severity:** SEV-1 | **Duration:** 4h 05m | **Author:** [name] | **Review date:** [date]

*Illustrative template: the service names, times, and numbers below are placeholders. Replace them with your real data.*

## Summary

On [date], from 09:12 to 13:17 UTC, the Orders API returned errors or timed out for roughly 85% of requests. A downstream dependency, the Inventory service, became slow after a database migration. Orders API threads held database and HTTP connections far longer than normal, the shared connection pool was exhausted, and every endpoint, including ones that never call Inventory, began failing. Recovery took four hours because the symptom (pool exhaustion) was initially misdiagnosed as a capacity problem, and restarts only bought minutes of relief.

## Impact

- ~85% of requests failed at peak (HTTP 503 / timeouts)
- ~[N] orders failed or were delayed; ~[N] customers affected
- Estimated revenue impact: [$X]
- [N] support tickets; SLA breach for [customers/tier]
- No data loss. Some orders were retried and double-submitted, and were reconciled manually.

## Timeline (UTC)

| Time | Event |
|------|-------|
| 08:40 | Inventory team deploys a schema migration that drops an index. Query latency rises from ~50 ms to ~8 s. No alert fires. |
| 09:12 | Orders API p99 latency crosses 5 s. Connection pool saturation reaches 100%. Error rate climbs. |
| 09:18 | Page fires on Orders API 5xx rate. On-call acknowledges. |
| 09:30 | On-call sees pool exhaustion errors and suspects traffic growth. Pool size is raised from 50 to 100. No improvement. |
| 09:55 | Orders API pods restarted. Recovery lasts ~4 minutes, then pools fill again. |
| 10:20 | Incident escalated; second responder joins. Hypothesis shifts to a DB problem on the Orders side. Primary DB checked and found healthy. |
| 11:05 | Thread dumps show most threads blocked on Inventory HTTP calls. Inventory is identified as the bottleneck. |
| 11:25 | Inventory team engaged. They are unaware of the migration's impact. |
| 12:10 | Migration identified as the trigger. Rollback decision made. |
| 12:50 | Index restored on Inventory DB (index rebuild is slow). |
| 13:05 | Inventory latency returns to normal. |
| 13:17 | Orders API error rate below 0.1%. Incident declared resolved. |

## Root Cause

**Trigger:** An Inventory migration dropped an index, making a hot query ~160x slower.

**Root cause:** The Orders API had no effective isolation from a slow dependency:

1. **No timeout on Inventory calls.** The HTTP client used a default 60 s timeout, so each slow call held a thread and connection for a very long time.
2. **Single shared pool.** All endpoints drew from one pool. Slow Inventory calls starved endpoints that never use Inventory.
3. **No circuit breaker or load shedding.** The service kept sending traffic to a failing dependency, and queued requests piled up behind the exhausted pool.
4. **Retries amplified load.** Clients and the Orders API retried on timeout, multiplying demand on Inventory and prolonging its slowness.

## Contributing Factors

- No latency alert on Inventory; only an error-rate alert on Orders, which fired ~35 minutes after the trigger.
- Migration was deployed without a performance review or staged rollout.
- Dashboards showed pool utilization but not *what* held the connections, which delayed diagnosis.
- The runbook for "pool exhausted" prescribed scaling and restarting, which was the wrong action here.

## What Went Well

- The page fired and was acknowledged within 6 minutes of the error spike.
- Escalation brought in the right people once the cause was identified.
- No data loss; duplicate orders were reconcilable.

## What Went Poorly

- 1h 55m passed before anyone looked at what the blocked threads were waiting on.
- The initial fix (a larger pool) made nothing better and delayed the real diagnosis.
- Rollback was slow because index rebuilds on a large table take ~40 minutes.

## Remediation

| # | Action | Type | Owner | Due |
|---|--------|------|-------|-----|
| 1 | Set explicit connect/read timeouts on all Inventory calls (e.g. 500 ms connect, 2 s read) | Prevent | Orders | [date] |
| 2 | Add a circuit breaker and fallback (cached stock levels or graceful degradation) for Inventory | Prevent | Orders | [date] |
| 3 | Separate pools or bulkheads per downstream dependency | Prevent | Orders | [date] |
| 4 | Retries limited to 1 with jittered backoff and a retry budget | Prevent | Orders | [date] |
| 5 | Alert on downstream latency (p95/p99) and pool wait time, not just error rate | Detect | SRE | [date] |
| 6 | Dashboard panel showing connections held per dependency; add thread-dump tooling to the runbook | Detect | SRE | [date] |
| 7 | Rewrite the "pool exhausted" runbook: check downstream latency *before* scaling | Respond | SRE | [date] |
| 8 | Require query-plan review and a canary rollout for schema migrations touching hot tables | Prevent | Inventory | [date] |
| 9 | Game day: inject latency into Inventory in staging and verify Orders degrades gracefully | Verify | Both | [date] |

## Lessons Learned

- A slow dependency is more dangerous than a dead one: failures return fast, slowness holds resources.
- Increasing pool size under a latency problem only delays exhaustion.
- Every outbound call needs a timeout shorter than the caller's own SLO.

*This review is blameless. The goal is to fix the system, not to assign fault to individuals.*

# Postmortem: Checkout API outage, connection pool exhaustion

Illustrative draft: all names, times, and numbers are placeholders. Replace with real data.

**Status:** Resolved | **Severity:** SEV-1 | **Duration:** 4h 05m (02:10-06:15 UTC) | **Author:** `<name>` | **Review date:** `<date>`

## Summary
`orders-api` returned 5xx for ~92% of requests for 4h. Downstream `inventory-svc` degraded (p99 200ms → 30s+) after a bad index migration. `orders-api` held DB/HTTP pool connections for the full downstream latency → pool (max 50) exhausted → all requests, including ones not touching inventory, queued and timed out. ~`<N>` failed orders, est. `<$>` revenue impact. No data loss.

## Impact
- Checkout and order-status endpoints unavailable. Health-check endpoint stayed green (it didn't touch the pool), so the LB kept routing traffic.
- Retries from mobile clients amplified load ~3x.

## Timeline (UTC)
| Time | Event |
|---|---|
| 01:50 | `inventory-svc` migration deployed; adds index on large table, locks reads intermittently |
| 02:10 | `inventory-svc` p99 latency climbs past 5s. No alert (threshold: error rate only) |
| 02:18 | `orders-api` pool utilization hits 100%; request queue grows |
| 02:25 | Error-rate alert fires on `orders-api`; on-call paged |
| 02:40 | On-call restarts `orders-api` pods → recovers ~3 min, then pool exhausts again |
| 03:05 | Suspected DB problem; `orders-api` DB checked, healthy. Hours lost here |
| 03:50 | Escalation; `inventory-svc` team engaged; traces show 30s waits on inventory calls |
| 04:30 | Pool size raised 50 → 200. Worsens: `inventory-svc` load up, DB connection limit hit |
| 05:20 | Root cause confirmed: inventory latency + no timeout on client call (default: infinite) |
| 05:40 | Migration rolled back on `inventory-svc`; latency recovering |
| 05:55 | Pool size reverted; `orders-api` pods restarted |
| 06:15 | Error rate < 0.1%; incident closed |

## Root cause
1. **Immediate:** `inventory-svc` slowed → each `orders-api` request held a pooled connection while waiting.
2. **Missing timeout:** the inventory HTTP client had no read timeout; requests waited until the 30s+ server-side limit.
3. **Shared pool:** inventory-dependent and independent endpoints shared one pool, so one slow dependency starved everything (no bulkhead).
4. **Retry storm:** client and service retries with no backoff/jitter multiplied load.
5. **Detection gap:** no latency alert on the dependency, and the health check didn't reflect pool saturation.

Little Law check: 50 conns / 30s hold ≈ 1.7 req/s capacity vs. ~400 req/s normal load.

## What went well
- Rollback of the migration was fast once identified.
- Runbook for pod restarts worked.

## What went poorly
- ~3h to identify the downstream dependency. Pool-wait metrics weren't on the dashboard.
- Scaling the pool treated the symptom and made things worse.
- The migration shipped at off-peak with no canary or latency gate.

## Remediations
| # | Action | Type | Owner | Due |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Set connect/read timeouts on all outbound clients (inventory: 500ms connect, 2s read) | Prevent | `<owner>` | `<date>` |
| 2 | Circuit breaker on inventory calls with fallback (cached stock, degraded "check later" path) | Prevent | `<owner>` | `<date>` |
| 3 | Separate pools/bulkheads per dependency | Prevent | `<owner>` | `<date>` |
| 4 | Retries: exponential backoff + jitter, retry budget (≤10%) | Prevent | `<owner>` | `<date>` |
| 5 | Alerts: dependency p99 latency, pool utilization >80%, pool wait time | Detect | `<owner>` | `<date>` |
| 6 | Readiness check fails on pool saturation so LB sheds the pod | Detect | `<owner>` | `<date>` |
| 7 | Dashboards: pool in-use/wait, per-dependency latency on the service overview | Detect | `<owner>` | `<date>` |
| 8 | Migration process: staging load test, canary, auto-rollback on latency regression | Prevent | `<owner>` | `<date>` |
| 9 | Runbook: "pool exhausted → check downstream first; don't raise pool size" | Mitigate | `<owner>` | `<date>` |
| 10 | Game day: inject 30s latency into inventory, verify the degradation path | Verify | `<owner>` | `<date>` |

## Lessons
Pool size doesn't fix slow dependencies; timeouts and isolation bound the blast radius. Pool capacity ≈ concurrency / hold time, so unbounded hold time means unbounded failure.

*Blameless format: no individual fault assigned.*

## large-s3 / statemachine

> Implement an order state machine covering created, paid, shipped, delivered, cancelled and refunded, with the legal transitions enforced and invalid ones rejected. Show the code.

### vanilla: 2278 answer tokens, 2278 billed, 175 lines

~~~
Here's a self-contained TypeScript implementation.

**Legal transitions**

```
created   -> paid, cancelled
paid      -> shipped, cancelled, refunded
shipped   -> delivered
delivered -> refunded
cancelled -> (terminal)
refunded  -> (terminal)
```

Rationale: a paid order can still be cancelled (before shipping), which implies a refund, though here I model that as `cancelled` and leave the payment reversal to the caller. A shipped order can't be cancelled, only delivered. A delivered order can be refunded (returns).

```ts
export type OrderState =
  | "created"
  | "paid"
  | "shipped"
  | "delivered"
  | "cancelled"
  | "refunded";

export type OrderEvent =
  | "pay"
  | "ship"
  | "deliver"
  | "cancel"
  | "refund";

// event -> (from state -> to state)
const TRANSITIONS: Readonly<
  Record<OrderState, Partial<Record<OrderEvent, OrderState>>>
> = {
  created:   { pay: "paid", cancel: "cancelled" },
  paid:      { ship: "shipped", cancel: "cancelled", refund: "refunded" },
  shipped:   { deliver: "delivered" },
  delivered: { refund: "refunded" },
  cancelled: {},
  refunded:  {},
};

export class InvalidTransitionError extends Error {
  constructor(
    public readonly from: OrderState,
    public readonly event: OrderEvent,
  ) {
    super(`Invalid transition: cannot "${event}" an order in state "${from}"`);
    this.name = "InvalidTransitionError";
  }
}

export interface HistoryEntry {
  from: OrderState;
  to: OrderState;
  event: OrderEvent;
  at: Date;
}

export class Order {
  private _state: OrderState;
  private _history: HistoryEntry[] = [];

  constructor(public readonly id: string, initial: OrderState = "created") {
    this._state = initial;
  }

  get state(): OrderState {
    return this._state;
  }

  get history(): readonly HistoryEntry[] {
    return this._history;
  }

  get isTerminal(): boolean {
    return Object.keys(TRANSITIONS[this._state]).length === 0;
  }

  can(event: OrderEvent): boolean {
    return TRANSITIONS[this._state][event] !== undefined;
  }

  availableEvents(): OrderEvent[] {
    return Object.keys(TRANSITIONS[this._state]) as OrderEvent[];
  }

  /** Applies the event or throws InvalidTransitionError; state is unchanged on failure. */
  apply(event: OrderEvent): this {
    const next = TRANSITIONS[this._state][event];
    if (next === undefined) {
      throw new InvalidTransitionError(this._state, event);
    }
    this._history.push({ from: this._state, to: next, event, at: new Date() });
    this._state = next;
    return this;
  }

  pay()     { return this.apply("pay"); }
  ship()    { return this.apply("ship"); }
  deliver() { return this.apply("deliver"); }
  cancel()  { return this.apply("cancel"); }
  refund()  { return this.apply("refund"); }
}
```

**Usage**

```ts
const order = new Order("A-1001");
order.pay().ship().deliver();
console.log(order.state);          // "delivered"

order.refund();                    // ok -> "refunded"
order.cancel();                    // throws InvalidTransitionError

const o2 = new Order("A-1002");
o2.can("ship");                    // false (not paid yet)
o2.ship();                         // throws, state stays "created"
```

**Tests (Vitest/Jest style)**

```ts
import { Order, InvalidTransitionError, OrderState, OrderEvent } from "./order";

const ALL_STATES: OrderState[] = ["created","paid","shipped","delivered","cancelled","refunded"];
const ALL_EVENTS: OrderEvent[] = ["pay","ship","deliver","cancel","refund"];

const LEGAL: Array<[OrderState, OrderEvent, OrderState]> = [
  ["created","pay","paid"],
  ["created","cancel","cancelled"],
  ["paid","ship","shipped"],
  ["paid","cancel","cancelled"],
  ["paid","refund","refunded"],
  ["shipped","deliver","delivered"],
  ["delivered","refund","refunded"],
];

describe("Order state machine", () => {
  test.each(LEGAL)("%s --%s--> %s", (from, event, to) => {
    const o = new Order("x", from);
    o.apply(event);
    expect(o.state).toBe(to);
  });

  test("every other (state, event) pair is rejected and leaves state unchanged", () => {
    const legal = new Set(LEGAL.map(([f, e]) => `${f}:${e}`));
    for (const s of ALL_STATES) {
      for (const e of ALL_EVENTS) {
        if (legal.has(`${s}:${e}`)) continue;
        const o = new Order("x", s);
        expect(() => o.apply(e)).toThrow(InvalidTransitionError);
        expect(o.state).toBe(s);
        expect(o.history).toHaveLength(0);
      }
    }
  });

  test("terminal states", () => {
    expect(new Order("x", "cancelled").isTerminal).toBe(true);
    expect(new Order("x", "refunded").isTerminal).toBe(true);
    expect(new Order("x", "paid").isTerminal).toBe(false);
  });
});
```

**Design notes**

- **Table-driven:** the transition map is the single source of truth. Changing the rules means editing one table, and the exhaustive test catches drift.
- **Atomic failure:** `apply` validates before mutating, so a rejected event never changes state or history.
- **Events vs. states:** transitions are keyed by event, so `refund` from `paid` and from `delivered` are the same event with different guards by source state.
- **Policy choices to confirm:** whether `shipped` orders may be cancelled (I disallowed it), whether `paid -> cancelled` should instead go through `refunded`, and whether `delivered -> refunded` needs a return window. All are one-line changes in the table.
- **Persistence/concurrency:** if you store orders in a database, do the transition as a conditional update (`UPDATE ... SET state = :next WHERE id = :id AND state = :from`) so two concurrent requests can't both pass validation against stale state.
~~~

### caveman: 1457 answer tokens, 1707 billed, 111 lines

~~~
Python impl. Table-driven: one dict holds all legal moves, one method enforces it.

```python
from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from datetime import datetime, timezone
from enum import Enum


class OrderState(str, Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"


class InvalidTransition(Exception):
    def __init__(self, current: OrderState, target: OrderState):
        self.current = current
        self.target = target
        super().__init__(f"Illegal transition: {current.value} -> {target.value}")


S = OrderState

# Single source of truth. Missing key or empty set = terminal.
TRANSITIONS: dict[OrderState, frozenset[OrderState]] = {
    S.CREATED:   frozenset({S.PAID, S.CANCELLED}),
    S.PAID:      frozenset({S.SHIPPED, S.CANCELLED, S.REFUNDED}),
    S.SHIPPED:   frozenset({S.DELIVERED}),
    S.DELIVERED: frozenset({S.REFUNDED}),
    S.CANCELLED: frozenset(),
    S.REFUNDED:  frozenset(),
}


@dataclass
class Order:
    id: str
    state: OrderState = OrderState.CREATED
    history: list[tuple[OrderState, OrderState, datetime]] = field(default_factory=list)

    def can_transition(self, target: OrderState) -> bool:
        return target in TRANSITIONS[self.state]

    def transition(self, target: OrderState) -> None:
        target = OrderState(target)  # reject junk values early (ValueError)
        if not self.can_transition(target):
            raise InvalidTransition(self.state, target)
        self.history.append((self.state, target, datetime.now(timezone.utc)))
        self.state = target

    # Named helpers for readability
    def pay(self):     self.transition(S.PAID)
    def ship(self):    self.transition(S.SHIPPED)
    def deliver(self): self.transition(S.DELIVERED)
    def cancel(self):  self.transition(S.CANCELLED)
    def refund(self):  self.transition(S.REFUNDED)

    @property
    def is_terminal(self) -> bool:
        return not TRANSITIONS[self.state]
```

Tests (pytest):

```python
import itertools
import pytest

def test_happy_path():
    o = Order("1")
    o.pay(); o.ship(); o.deliver(); o.refund()
    assert o.state is S.REFUNDED and o.is_terminal
    assert len(o.history) == 4

def test_cancel_from_created():
    o = Order("2"); o.cancel()
    assert o.state is S.CANCELLED

@pytest.mark.parametrize("src,dst", [
    (a, b) for a, b in itertools.product(S, S) if b not in TRANSITIONS[a]
])
def test_all_illegal_rejected(src, dst):
    o = Order("x", state=src)
    with pytest.raises(InvalidTransition):
        o.transition(dst)
    assert o.state is src      # state unchanged
    assert o.history == []     # nothing recorded

def test_bad_value():
    with pytest.raises(ValueError):
        Order("y").transition("teleported")
```

Rules I assumed (edit `TRANSITIONS` to change, nothing else needs touching):

- `created → paid | cancelled`
- `paid → shipped | cancelled | refunded`
- `shipped → delivered`. No cancel or refund mid-shipment.
- `delivered → refunded`
- `cancelled`, `refunded` terminal.

Notes:

- Failed transition leaves state and history untouched.
- Not thread-safe. In a real DB, do the check and update atomically: `UPDATE orders SET state=:new WHERE id=:id AND state=:old`, and treat 0 rows affected as a conflict. This stops two concurrent requests both passing the check.
- Side effects (charge, email, restock) belong outside the state machine, triggered after a successful transition.

### ponytail: 854 answer tokens, 1141 billed, 71 lines

~~~
```python
from enum import Enum


class State(Enum):
    CREATED = "created"
    PAID = "paid"
    SHIPPED = "shipped"
    DELIVERED = "delivered"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    REFUNDED = "refunded"


S = State
TRANSITIONS = {
    S.CREATED:   {S.PAID, S.CANCELLED},
    S.PAID:      {S.SHIPPED, S.CANCELLED, S.REFUNDED},
    S.SHIPPED:   {S.DELIVERED},
    S.DELIVERED: {S.REFUNDED},
    S.CANCELLED: set(),  # terminal
    S.REFUNDED:  set(),  # terminal
}


class InvalidTransition(Exception):
    pass


class Order:
    def __init__(self):
        self.state = S.CREATED

    def transition(self, new: State) -> None:
        if new not in TRANSITIONS[self.state]:
            raise InvalidTransition(f"{self.state.value} -> {new.value}")
        self.state = new


if __name__ == "__main__":
    o = Order()
    for s in (S.PAID, S.SHIPPED, S.DELIVERED, S.REFUNDED):
        o.transition(s)
    assert o.state is S.REFUNDED

    # every pair not in the table must be rejected
    for src in State:
        for dst in State:
            o = Order()
            o.state = src
            if dst in TRANSITIONS[src]:
                o.transition(dst)
                assert o.state is dst
            else:
                try:
                    o.transition(dst)
                    raise AssertionError(f"{src} -> {dst} should fail")
                except InvalidTransition:
                    assert o.state is src  # state unchanged on rejection
    print("ok")
```

The rules live in the `TRANSITIONS` table. `transition()` raises `InvalidTransition` for anything not listed and leaves the state unchanged. The self-check tries all 36 state pairs.

Assumptions I made:
- **Cancel:** it is allowed before shipping, from created or paid.
- **Refund:** it is allowed from paid or delivered.
- **Terminal states:** cancelled and refunded are terminal.

If a cancelled paid order should still pass through refunded, add `S.REFUNDED` to the cancelled entry.

Skipped: persistence, timestamps/history, and events or hooks on transitions. Add them when you need an audit trail or side effects.

### chisle: 684 answer tokens, 923 billed, 53 lines

~~~
TypeScript, table-driven:

```ts
export type OrderState =
  | "created" | "paid" | "shipped" | "delivered" | "cancelled" | "refunded";

const TRANSITIONS: Record<OrderState, readonly OrderState[]> = {
  created:   ["paid", "cancelled"],
  paid:      ["shipped", "cancelled", "refunded"],
  shipped:   ["delivered"],
  delivered: ["refunded"],
  cancelled: [],
  refunded:  [],
};

export class InvalidTransitionError extends Error {
  constructor(public from: OrderState, public to: OrderState) {
    super(`Illegal order transition: ${from} -> ${to}`);
    this.name = "InvalidTransitionError";
  }
}

export const canTransition = (from: OrderState, to: OrderState) =>
  TRANSITIONS[from].includes(to);

export class Order {
  private _state: OrderState = "created";
  get state() { return this._state; }

  transition(to: OrderState): this {
    if (!canTransition(this._state, to)) {
      throw new InvalidTransitionError(this._state, to);
    }
    this._state = to;
    return this;
  }
}
```

Usage:

```ts
const o = new Order();
o.transition("paid").transition("shipped").transition("delivered");
o.transition("shipped"); // throws InvalidTransitionError: delivered -> shipped
```

Assumptions (edit `TRANSITIONS` to change):
- `cancelled` and `refunded` are terminal.
- `paid → cancelled` and `paid → refunded` are both allowed. Drop one if cancel-after-pay must always go through refund.
- `shipped → refunded` is disallowed (refund only after delivery). Add it if returns in transit are allowed.

skipped: persistence/concurrency. When storing in a DB, make the update conditional (`UPDATE ... WHERE id=? AND state=?`) so concurrent transitions can't both succeed.
~~~
